[
CK24
]
a problem call
DAG-like SSSP
was defined, that abstracts the above problem. They also provided an algorithm for it, which is essentially identical to the algorithm of
[
BGWN20
]
; a similar algorithm was used in
[
BGS20
]
.
We now provide the definition of the
DAG-like SSSP
problem from
[
CK24
]
, and summarize their algorithm for it.

2.4
SSSP in DAG-like Graphs

The presentation in this section is almost identical to the definition of the
DAG-like SSSP
problem from
[
CK24
]
.
The input to the
DAG-like SSSP
problem is a directed multigraph
G=(V,E)
, with two designated vertices
s
and
t
, a distance parameter
d>0
, a precision parameter
0<\epsilon\leq 1/8
, such that
1/\epsilon
is an integer, and another parameter
\Gamma>0
. Graph
G
may have parallel edges, but no self-loops. Every edge
e\in E
of
G
is associated with an integral
length

\ell(e)>0
, and an additional
weight

w(e)
, that must be an integral power of
2
between
1
and
2^{\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil}
. The graph undergoes the following two types of updates:

•

Edge deletion:
given an edge
e\in E
, delete
e
from
G
; and

•

Vertex splitting:
in this operation, we are given as input a vertex
v\not\in\left\{s,t\right\}
in the current graph, and new vertices
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
that need to be inserted into
G
; we may sometimes say that vertices
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
are
split-off from
v
. In addition to vertex
v
and vertices
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
, we are given a new collection
E^{\prime}
of edges that must be inserted into
G
, and for every edge
e\in E^{\prime}
, we are given an integral length
\ell(e)>0
and a weight
w(e)
that must be an integral power of
2
between
1
and
2^{\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil}
. For each such edge
e\in E^{\prime}
, either both endpoints of
e
lie in
\left\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k},v\right\}
; or one endpoint lies in
\left\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\right\}
, and the other in
V(G)\setminus\left\{v,u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\right\}
. In the latter case, if
e=(u_{i},x)
, then edge
e^{\prime}=(v,x)
must currently lie in
G
, and both
w(e^{\prime})=w(e)
and
\ell(e^{\prime})=\ell(e)
must hold. Similarly, if
e=(x,u_{i})
, then edge
e^{\prime}=(x,v)
must currently lie in
G
, and both
w(e^{\prime})=w(e)
and
\ell(e^{\prime})=\ell(e)
must hold.

We let
{\mathcal{T}}
be the
time horizon
of the algorithm – that is, the time interval during which the graph
G
undergoes all updates.
We denote by
n
and by
m
the total number of vertices and edges, respectively, that were ever present in
G
during
{\mathcal{T}}
. We assume w.l.o.g. that for every vertex
v
that was ever present in
G
, some edge was incident to
v
at some time during the update sequence, so
n\leq O(m)
.

For every vertex
v\in V(G)\setminus\left\{t\right\}
and integer
0\leq i\leq\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
, we define a set:

U_{i}(v)=\left\{u\in V(G)\mid\exists e=(v,u)\in E(G)\mbox{ with }w(e)=2^{i}\right\}.

Note that as the algorithm progresses, each such set
U_{i}(v)
may change, with vertices both leaving and joining the set. We require that
the following property must hold at all times
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
:

S1.

For every vertex
v\in V(G)\setminus\left\{t\right\}
, for every integer
0\leq i\leq\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
,
|U_{i}(v)|\leq 2^{i+2}
.

As graph
G
undergoes edge-deletion and vertex-splitting updates, at any time the algorithm may be asked a query
path-query
. It is then required to return a
simple
path
P
connecting
s
to
t
, whose length
\sum_{e\in E(P)}\ell(e)
is bounded by
(1+10\epsilon)d
, in time
O(|E(P)|)
.

Lastly, the algorithm may return FAIL at any time and halt. It may only do so if the current graph
G
does not contain any
s
-
t
path
P
with
\sum_{e\in E(P)}\ell(e)\leq d
, and
\sum_{e\in E(P)}w(e)\leq\Gamma
.

The following theorem provides an algorithm for the
DAG-like SSSP
problem. Its formal proof, presented in
[
CK24
]
, follows almost immediately from the results of
[
BGWN20
]
. Specifically, Theorem 5.1 of
[
BGWN20
]
provides a similar result with a slightly different setting of parameters, but their arguments can be easily extended to prove the theorem below, as was shown in
[
CK24
]
.

Theorem 2.7
(Theorem 5.1 in the full version of
[
CK24
]
).

There is a deterministic algorithm for the
DAG-like SSSP
problem with total update time
\tilde{O}\left(\frac{n^{2}+m+\Gamma\cdot n}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)
; here,
m
and
n
are the total number of edges and vertices, respectively, that were ever present in
G
.

3
High-Level Overview of the Algorithm

In this section we provide the definitions of well-structured graphs and well-structured cuts, that are used throughout the paper. We also define a new problem, called
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
, which is one of the main building blocks of our algorithm. We then state the main theorem for this section, that provides an efficient algorithm for the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, and we show that our main result – the proof of
Theorem

1.1
follows from it. The proof of the theorem that provides an algorithm for the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem is by induction, and is provided in the remainder of this paper. In this section we also provide a simple and somewhat inefficient algorithm for the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, that will be used as the induction base. We also define an intermediate problem, called
r
-Restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem, and show that an algorithm for this problem implies an algorithm for the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem. We start by defining well-structured graphs and well-structured cuts.

3.1
Well-Structured Graphs and Well-Structured Cuts

Throughout the paper we will work with special kinds of directed graphs, that for convenience we call
well-structured graphs
, and define below.

Definition 3.1
(Well-Structured Graphs).

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be a bipartite directed graph. We call the edges of
E_{G}(L,R)

regular
and the edges of
E_{G}(R,L)

special
. We say that
G
is a
well-structured graph
, if every vertex of
G
is incident to at most one special edge. If every vertex of
G
is incident to
exactly
one special edge, then we say that
G
is a
perfect well-structured graph
.

If
G
is a well-structured graph, then we assume that the partition
(L,R)
of its vertices is given as part of the description of
G
.
We will sometimes consider well-structured graphs with lengths on their edges, and we will sometimes restrict our attention to assignments of lengths to edges that have special properties, that we define below. We call such assignments of lengths to edges
proper
.

Definition 3.2
(Proper Assignment of Edge Lengths).

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be a well-structured directed graph, and let
\ell(e)\geq 0
be an assignment of lengths to the edges
e\in E
. We say that
\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E}
is a
proper
assignment of edge lengths, if, for every regular edge
e\in E
,
\ell(e)=0
, and for every special edge
e\in E
,
\ell(e)
is an integral power of
2
with
\ell(e)\geq 1
. If, for every special edge
e\in E
,
\ell(e)=1
holds, then we say that
\left\{\ell(e)\right\}_{e\in E}
is a
proper uniform assignment of edge lengths
.

Recall that a
cut
in a graph
G
is an ordered pair
(A,B)
of non-empty subsets of
V(G)
with
A\cap B=\emptyset
and
A\cup B=V
.
We will often use the notion of a well-structured cut, that is defined next.

Definition 3.3
(Well-Structured Cut).

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be a well-structured graph, and let
(A,B)
be a cut in
G
. We say that
(A,B)
is a
weakly well-structured cut
, or just a
well-structured cut
, if all edges of
E_{G}(A,B)
are special edges. If, additionally, all edges in
E_{G}(B,A)
are regular edges, then we say that
(A,B)
is a
strongly
well-structured cut.

3.2
The RouteAndCut problem

In this subsection we define the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, which is one of the main building blocks of our algorithm. Before we do so, we need to define the notion of
routing
.

Definition 3.4
(Routing).

Let
G=(V,E)
be a directed graph, and let
A,B
be two disjoint subsets of its vertices. A
partial routing
, or just a
routing
, from
A
to
B
is a collection
{\mathcal{Q}}
of paths in
G
, such that every path in
{\mathcal{Q}}
connects a vertex of
A
to a vertex of
B
, and the endpoints of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
are all disjoint. The
congestion
of the routing is the edge-congestion that the set
{\mathcal{Q}}
of paths causes in graph
G
.
Vertices of
A\cup B
may serve both as endpoints and as inner vertices of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
simultaneously. A partial routing of cardinality
|A|
is called a
complete
routing.

We are now ready to define the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, and its special case, called
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
. Intuitively, we use the notion of the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem in order to discretize the problem instances: our algorithm for
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
will consider, by induction,
r
-restricted instances of the problem, from smaller to larger values of
r
.

Definition 3.5
(
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem).

The input to the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem is a well-structured
n
-vertex graph
G=(L,R,E)
, that is given in the adjacency-list representation,
parameters
N\geq n
,
\Delta\geq 1
, and
1\leq\eta\leq\Delta
, and two disjoint subsets
A,B
of vertices of
G
, with
|A|,|B|\geq\Delta
.

The output of the problem is a (possibly partial) routing
{\mathcal{Q}}
from
A
to
B
, whose congestion is bounded by
4\eta\log N
. Additionally, if
|{\mathcal{Q}}|<\Delta
, the output must contain a cut
(X,Y)
in
G
with
|E_{G}(X,Y)|\leq\frac{64\Delta}{\eta\log^{4}n}+\frac{256|{\mathcal{Q}}|}{\eta}
, such that, if

A^{\prime}\subseteq A,B^{\prime}\subseteq B
denote the subsets of vertices that do not serve as endpoints of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
, then
A^{\prime}\subseteq X
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq Y
hold. The algorithm for the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem is also allowed to return “FAIL” without producing any output, but the probability of the algorithm doing so must be bounded by
1/2
.

We say that an instance
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem is
r
-restricted
, for an integer
1\leq r\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
, iff
\frac{(n-|B|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\leq 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N}}
holds.

We provide a brief intuition for the parameters
\Delta
and
N
in the definition of the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, and for the definition of
r
-restricted instances. Consider an arbitrary well-structured
n
-vertex graph
G
, two disjoint sets
A,B
of its vertices and a parameter
\eta
, and assume that we need to compute a large routing from
A
to
B
in
G
, with congestion at most
\eta
. Typically, we will set
\Delta
to be the cardinality of the desired routing (and if it is not known, we can set
\Delta=\min\left\{|A|,|B|\right\}
). Initially, we will also set
N=n
, and consider the resulting instance
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem.
Now suppose we compute a routing from
A
to
B
in graph
G
of cardinality
\Delta
that causes edge-congestion at most
\eta
. Assume w.l.o.g. that, if
b\in B
is an inner vertex on some path of the routing, then it serves as an endpoint of some other path of the routing. Then it is not hard to show that most of the paths in the routing must have length at most
d=O\left(\frac{(n-|B|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\right)
.
The definition or
r
-restricted instances requires that this parameter
d
is roughly bounded by
2^{r\sqrt{\log N}}
.
It is easy to see that our starting instance is
r^{*}
-restricted, for
r^{*}=\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
. In order to solve this problem instance, we will need to solve the problem recursively on smaller subgraphs
G^{\prime}
of
G
, but it is important for us to ensure that the resulting instances are
r^{\prime}
-restricted, for
r^{\prime}<r^{*}
. In order to do so, we will let the parameter
N
in the resulting subinstances correspond to the number of vertices in the original graph
G
(we may need to slightly adjust it for technical reasons but the adjustments are minor). By appropriately setting the parameters
\Delta
and
\eta
for the resulting subinstances, we can then ensure that they are indeed
r^{\prime}
-restricted, for some
r^{\prime}<r^{*}
. Overall, the parameter
N
can be thought of as roughly the number of vertices in the original instance of the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem that we try to solve, and it is used mostly to define the notion of
r
-restricted instances. The notion of
r
-restricted instances allows us to construct algorithms for the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem inductively, by going from smaller to larger values of
r
.

The following theorem provides one of our main technical results, namely an efficient algorithm for the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem.

Theorem 3.6
.

There is a randomized algorithm for the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, that, on an input
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
, has running time at most
O\left(n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{O(\sqrt{\log N}\cdot\log\log N)}\right)
, where
n=|V(G)|
.

The remainder of this paper is largely dedicated to the proof of
Theorem

3.6
, but first we show that the proof of
Theorem

1.1
follows from it.

3.3
Completing the Proof of
Theorem

1.1

Recall that in the
Maximum Bipartite Matching
problem, the input is a simple undirected
n
-vertex bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
, and the goal is to compute a matching of maximum cardinality in
G
.

We can assume w.l.o.g. that we are given a target integral value
C^{*}>0
. If
C^{*}\leq\mathsf{OPT}
, then our algorithm must either produce a matching of cardinality
C^{*}
, or terminate with a “FAIL”, but we require that the algorithm terminates with a “FAIL” with probability at most
1/\operatorname{poly}(n)
in this case. If
C^{*}>\mathsf{OPT}
, then our algorithm may return an arbitrary matching, or terminate with a “FAIL”.

We can make this assumption w.l.o.g. since we can use a standard technique of performing a binary search over the value
\mathsf{OPT}
of the optimal solution.
We use bounds
W<W^{\prime}
on the value of the optimal solution, initializing
W=1
and
W^{\prime}=n
, and we start with
C^{*}=\left\lfloor n/2\right\rfloor
.
Given the current guess
C^{*}
on
\mathsf{OPT}
, we run the algorithm with this target value
C^{*}
. If the algorithm successfully computes a matching of cardinality at least
C^{*}
, then we set
W=C^{*}
, and
otherwise, we set
W^{\prime}=C^{*}
. In either case, we
select the next value
C^{*}=\left\lfloor\frac{W^{\prime}-W}{2}\right\rfloor
and continue. Once
W^{\prime}-W\leq 1
holds, we run the algorithm once with
C^{*}=W^{\prime}
and once with
C^{*}=W
, and then output the better of the two solutions.
We say that an algorithm
errs
in an iteration, if it returns “FAIL”, but
C^{*}\leq\mathsf{OPT}
holds in that iterations. Notice that, if the algorithm never errs, then after
O(\log n)
iterations it is guaranteed to terminate with an optimal matching. Since the probability that the algorithm errs in a single iteration is bounded by
1/\operatorname{poly}(n)
, and the number of iterations is bounded by
O(\log n)
, the algorithm is guaranteed to return an optimal matching with probability at least
1-1/\operatorname{poly}(n)
.

From now on, we assume that we are given a target value
C^{*}
, and that
G
contains a matching of cardinality
C^{*}
. Our algorithm must either compute a matching of cardinality
C^{*}
, or to return “FAIL”, but the probability for returning “FAIL” must be bounded by
1/\operatorname{poly}(n)
. For convenience, we denote
C^{*}
by
\mathsf{OPT}
.
We can assume that
n
is greater than a large enough constant, since otherwise the problem can be solved in
O(1)
time.

It is well known that the
Maximum Bipartite Matching
problem can be reduced to computing maximum
s
-
t
flow in a directed flow network with unit edge capacities. In order to do so, we start with the graph
G
, and direct all its edges from the vertices of
L
towards the vertices of
R
. We then add a source vertex
s
, that connects with an edge to every vertex of
L
, and a destination vertex
t
, to which every vertex of
R
is connected. All edge capacities are set to
1
. Let
G^{\prime}
denote the resulting directed flow network. It is easy to verify that the value of the maximum
s
-
t
flow in
G^{\prime}
is equal to the cardinality of maximum matching in
G
. Moreover, given an integral flow
f
in
G^{\prime}
, we can compute a matching
M
of cardinality
\operatorname{val}(f)
in
G
, in time
O(n)
: we simply include in
M
all edges of
G
that carry
1
flow unit.

Our algorithm consists of
O(\log^{3}n)
phases. Throughout the algorithm, we maintain a matching
M
in
G
, starting with
M=\emptyset
, and we denote
\Delta^{*}=\mathsf{OPT}-|M|
. If
M
is the matching at the beginning of a phase, and
M^{\prime}
is the matching obtained at the end of the phase, then we require that
|M^{\prime}|\geq|M|+\Omega(\Delta^{*}/\log^{2}n)
, where
\Delta^{*}=\mathsf{OPT}-|M|
. We show a combinatorial algorithm, that, given an initial matching
M
, either returns “FAIL”, or computes such a matching
M^{\prime}
, in time
O\left(n^{2}\cdot 2^{O(\sqrt{\log n}\log\log n)}\right)
, where the probability that the algorithm returns “FAIL” is bounded by
1/\operatorname{poly}(n)
. This will ensure that the number of the phases is bounded by
O(\log^{3}n)
, and the total running time of the algorithm is
O\left(n^{2}\cdot 2^{O(\sqrt{\log n}\log\log n)}\right)
.
From now on we focus on the description of a single phase.

Implementation of a Single Phase.

We assume that we are given some matching
M
in the input graph
G
, and we denote
\Delta^{*}=\mathsf{OPT}-|M|
. Our goal is to compute a matching
M^{\prime}
of cardinality at least
|M|+\Omega(\Delta^{*}/\log^{2}n)
.
As observed already, matching
M
defines a flow
f
of value
|M|
in the directed flow network
G^{\prime}
with unit edge capacities. We denote by
H=G^{\prime}_{f}
the corresponding residual flow network, and we say that
H
is the
residual flow network of
G
with respect to matching
M
. Observe that
H
is a directed flow network with unit edge capacities, and that the value of the maximum
s
-
t
flow in
H
is at least
\mathsf{OPT}-|M|=\Delta^{*}
.

Next, we will define an instance of the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem. Consider first the graph
H^{\prime}=H\setminus\left\{s,t\right\}
. Notice that, for every edge
e=(u,v)
of
G
with
u\in L
and
v\in R
, if
e\in M
, then edge
(v,u)
is present in
H^{\prime}
, and otherwise edge
(u,v)
is present in
H^{\prime}
. Therefore, all edges of
H^{\prime}
that are directed from vertices of
R
to vertices of
L
correspond to the edges of the current matching
M
. Clearly, every vertex of
H^{\prime}
may be incident to at most one edge of
E_{H^{\prime}}(R,L)
, and so graph
H^{\prime}
is a well-structured graph.

We let
A\subseteq L
be the set of vertices
v
, such that edge
(s,v)
is present in graph
H
, and we let
B\subseteq R
be the set of vertices
u
, such that edge
(u,t)
is present in
H
. We also define parameters
\eta=1
,
N=n
, and
\Delta=\Delta^{*}
. It is easy to verify that
(H^{\prime},A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
is a valid input to the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, and we can compute an adjacency-list representation of
H^{\prime}
in time
O(|E(G)|)
.
Recall that there is an
s
-
t
flow
f
of value
\Delta^{*}
in graph
H
, and, from the integrality of maximum flow in integer-capacity networks, we can assume that this flow is integral. This flow naturally defines a routing
{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
from
A
to
B
, that causes congestion
\eta=1
, with
|{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}|=\Delta^{*}
.

We apply the algorithm from
Theorem

3.6
to instance
(H^{\prime},A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem.
As long as the algorithm returns “FAIL”, we keep executing it, for up to
\left\lceil 100\log n\right\rceil
iterations. If the algorithm from
Theorem

3.6
returned “FAIL” in all
\left\lceil 100\log n\right\rceil
consecutive iterations, we terminate our algorithm and return “FAIL”. It is easy to verify that this may happen with probability at most
1/n^{100}
. Otherwise, the algorithm from
Theorem

3.6
must return a routing
{\mathcal{Q}}
from
A
to
B
with congestion at most
4\log n
. We show that
|{\mathcal{Q}}|\geq\frac{\Delta^{*}}{\log n}
must hold in the next observation.

Observation 3.7
.

If
{\mathcal{Q}}
is the routing returned by the algorithm from
Theorem

3.6
, then
|{\mathcal{Q}}|\geq\frac{\Delta^{*}}{\log n}
.

Proof.

Let
A^{\prime}\subseteq A,B^{\prime}\subseteq B
denote the subsets of vertices that do not serve as endpoints of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
.
Assume for contradiction that
|{\mathcal{Q}}|<\frac{\Delta^{*}}{\log n}
.

Recall that the algorithm from
Theorem

3.6
must also return a cut
(X,Y)
in
H^{\prime}
with
|E_{H^{\prime}}(X,Y)|\leq\frac{64\Delta^{*}}{\log^{4}n}+256|{\mathcal{Q}}|\leq\frac{512\Delta^{*}}{\log n}
, such that
A^{\prime}\subseteq X
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq Y
. Also, recall that we have established that there is a routing
{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
from
A
to
B
in
H^{\prime}
of cardinality at least
\Delta^{*}
, that causes edge-congestion
1
. Since
|{\mathcal{Q}}|<\frac{\Delta^{*}}{\log n}
, at least
\frac{\Delta^{*}}{2}
of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{*}
must originate at vertices of
A^{\prime}
and terminate at vertices of
B^{\prime}
. Each such path must contain an edge of
E_{H^{\prime}}(X,Y)
, so
|E_{H^{\prime}}(X,Y)|\geq\frac{\Delta^{*}}{2}
must hold, a contradiction.

Notice that the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
naturally define a collection
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
of at least
\frac{\Delta^{*}}{\log n}

s
-
t
paths in graph
H
(the residual flow network with respect to
G
and the current matching
M
), and they cause congestion at most
4\log n
(since the endpoints of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
are disjoint).
Next, we show an algorithm that computes a collection
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}
of
\Omega\left(\frac{\Delta^{*}}{\log^{2}n}\right)
edge-disjoint
s
-
t
paths in graph
H
. We will then use the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}
in order to augment the current flow
f
in graph
G^{\prime}
, which, in turn, will allow us to compute the new augmented matching
M
.

In order to compute the collection
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}
of paths,
we construct a directed graph
H^{\prime\prime}\subseteq H
, that consists of all vertices and edges that participate in the paths of
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
.
The capacity of every edge in
H^{\prime\prime}
remains unit – the same as its capacity in
H
. Next, we show that
|E(H^{\prime\prime})|\leq O(n\log n)
, and that there is an
s
-
t
flow of value at least
\frac{\Delta^{*}}{4\log^{2}n}
in
H^{\prime\prime}
.

Observation 3.8
.

|E(H^{\prime\prime})|\leq O(n\log n)
, and there is an
s
-
t
flow of value at least
\frac{\Delta^{*}}{4\log^{2}n}
in
H^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

We start by showing that every vertex
v\in V(H^{\prime\prime})\setminus\left\{s,t\right\}
participates in at most
8\log n
paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
. Indeed, consider any vertex
v\in V(H^{\prime\prime})\setminus\left\{s,t\right\}
, and let
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
be a path in which vertex
v
participates. Assume first that
v\in L
. Then either edge
(s,v)
belongs to
H
and lies on path
Q
, or there is a special edge
(u,v)
incident to
v
in
H
, that lies on path
Q
. Since the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
cause congestion at most
4\log n
in
H
, there may be at most
8\log n
paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
that contain either of the edges
(s,v)
or
(u,v)
, and so
v
participates in at most
8\log n
paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
. Using a similar argument, every vertex
u\in R
participates in at most
8\log n
paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
. Therefore,
|E(H^{\prime\prime})|\leq O(n\log n)
.

Consider now a flow in
H^{\prime\prime}
, where we send
\frac{1}{4\log n}
flow units along every path in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
. Since the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
cause congestion at most
4\log n
, this is a valid
s
-
t
flow in
H^{\prime\prime}
, and the value of this flow is
\frac{|{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}|}{4\log n}\geq\frac{\Delta^{*}}{4\log^{2}n}
.

Next, we compute an integral maximum
s
-
t
flow in
H^{\prime\prime}
that obeys the edge capacities in
H^{\prime\prime}
, using the standard Ford-Fulkerson algorithm. We start by setting the flow
f^{\prime}(e)
on every edge
e\in E(H^{\prime})
to
0
, and then perform iterations. In every iteration, we use the current flow
f^{\prime}
in
H^{\prime\prime}
in order to compute a residual flow network
H^{\prime\prime}_{f^{\prime}}
. If there is no
s
-
t
path in
H^{\prime\prime}_{f^{\prime}}
, then the algorithm terminates. We are then guaranteed that the value of the current flow
f^{\prime}
is optimal, so
\operatorname{val}(f^{\prime})\geq\frac{\Delta^{*}}{4\log^{2}n}
. Otherwise, we compute an arbitrary
s
-
t
path
P
in
H^{\prime\prime}_{f^{\prime}}
, and we augment the flow
f^{\prime}
along the path
P
. Notice that every iteration of the algorithm can be implemented in time
O(|E(H^{\prime\prime}_{f^{\prime}})|)=\widetilde{O}(n)
, and the number of iterations is bounded by
n
. Therefore, the total running time of the algorithm that computes the set
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}
of paths in
H^{\prime\prime}
is
\widetilde{O}(n^{2})
. The final integral flow
f^{\prime}
can be decomposed, in time
O(|E(H^{\prime\prime})|)=\widetilde{O}(n)
, into a collection
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}
of at least
\Omega\left(\frac{\Delta^{*}}{\log^{2}n}\right)
edge-disjoint paths connecting
s
to
t
in
H^{\prime\prime}
. Since
H^{\prime\prime}\subseteq H
, we have now obtained the desired collection
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}
of at least
\Omega\left(\frac{\Delta^{*}}{\log^{2}n}\right)
edge-disjoint
s
-
t
paths in
H
.

We note that we could also directly round the initial fractional
s
-
t
flow in
H^{\prime\prime}
, in expected time
\widetilde{O}(|E(H^{\prime\prime})|)=\widetilde{O}(n)
, e.g. by using the algorithm from Theorem 5 in
[
LRS13
]
, that builds on the results of
[
GKK10
]
. But since their running time is only bounded in expectation, and since the bottlenecks in the running time of our algorithm lie elsewhere, we instead use the above simple deterministic algorithm.

We can now augment the current flow in
G^{\prime}
via the collection
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}
of augmenting paths, obtaining a new integral flow in graph
G^{\prime}
of value
|M|+|{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}|
, which, in turn, defines a new matching
M^{\prime}
with
|M^{\prime}|\geq|M|+|{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}|\geq|M|+\Omega\left(\frac{\Delta^{*}}{\log^{2}n}\right)
.

We now bound the running time of a single phase.
Recall that we may execute the algorithm from
Theorem

3.6
at most
O(\log n)
times per phase.
The running time of a single execution of the algorithm from
Theorem

3.6
is bounded by
O\left(n^{2}\cdot 2^{O(\sqrt{\log N}\cdot\log\log N)}\right)\leq O\left(n^{2}\cdot 2^{O(\sqrt{\log n}\cdot\log\log n)}\right)
.
Additionally, the time required to compute the graph
H^{\prime\prime}
, and to compute the maximum flow in it is bounded by
\widetilde{O}(n^{2})
.

Overall, the running time of a single phase is bounded by
O\left(n^{2}\cdot 2^{O(\sqrt{\log n}\cdot\log\log n)}\right)
. Since the number of phases is bounded by
O(\log^{3}n)
, the total running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O\left(n^{2}\cdot 2^{O(\sqrt{\log n}\cdot\log\log n)}\right)
, and the probability that the algorithm ever returns “FAIL” is bounded by
1/\operatorname{poly}(n)
.

3.4
Proof of
Theorem

3.6
– High Level Overview

In order to prove
Theorem

3.6
, we use two large enough constants
c_{1}\gg c_{2}
, whose values we set later, and we employ the following theorem.

Theorem 3.9
.

For all
r\geq 1
, there is a randomized algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, that, on an input
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
with
|V(G)|=n
, has running time at most:

c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}}.

Note that, if
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
is an instance of the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, and
|V(G)|=n
, then, from the problem definition,
\eta\leq\Delta
and
\frac{(n-|B|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\leq n
holds. Therefore, any instance of the problem is also an instance of
r^{*}
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, for
r^{*}=\left\lceil\sqrt{\log n}\right\rceil
. By using
Theorem

3.9
with parameter
r^{*}=\left\lceil\sqrt{\log n}\right\rceil
, we obtain an algorithm for the general
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem whose running time is:

O\left(n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{O(\sqrt{\log N}\cdot\log\log N)}\right).

Therefore, in order to prove
Theorem

3.6
, it is enough to prove
Theorem

3.9
. In the remainder of this paper we focus on the proof of
Theorem

3.9
. The proof is by induction on
r
, and it relies on a slight modification of the Multiplicative Weight Update (
MWU
) framework of
[
GK98
,
Fle00
]
, that essentially reduces the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem to a special case of the directed
SSSP
problem, that we call
r
-Restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
. Before we describe this approach, we describe an algorithm that, given an instance
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, constructs a subgraph
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
with
V(G^{\prime})=V(G)
and
|E(G^{\prime})|\leq O((n-|B|)\cdot n)
, such that a solution to the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem instance
(G^{\prime},A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
immediately provides a solution to the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem instance
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
.

3.4.1
An Auxiliary Graph
G^{\prime}
and its Properties

Consider an instance
(G=(L,R,E),A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, for an integer
1\leq r\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
. Let
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
be a graph, that is constructed as follows. We let
V(G^{\prime})=V(G)
, and we include in
G^{\prime}
all special edges of
G
. Additionally, for every vertex
v\in L
, if
|\delta^{+}_{G}(v)|\leq 512(n-|B|)
, then we add all regular edges that leave
v
in
G
to graph
G^{\prime}
, and otherwise, we select an arbitrary subset
E^{\prime}(v)\subseteq\delta^{+}_{G}(v)
of
512(n-|B|)
such edges, and include these edges in
G^{\prime}
.

Note that
G^{\prime}
is a well-structured
n
-vertex graph, and
(G^{\prime},A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
is a valid instance of the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem. Moreover, the out-degree of every vertex
v\in L
is bounded by
512(n-|B|)
, and so
|E(G^{\prime})|\leq O((n-|B|)\cdot n)
. Given the adjacency-list representation
of graph
G
, graph
G^{\prime}
can be constructed explicitly in time
O((n-|B|)\cdot n)
.

Since
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
, any routing
{\mathcal{Q}}
from
A
to
B
with congestion at most
4\eta\log N
in
G^{\prime}
defines a routing from
A
to
B
with congestion at most
4\eta\log N
in
G
. Assume now that we are given a routing
{\mathcal{Q}}
from
A
to
B
in
G^{\prime}
with congestion at most
4\eta\log N
, such that
|{\mathcal{Q}}|<\Delta
. Let
A^{\prime}\subseteq A,B^{\prime}\subseteq B
denote the subsets of vertices that do not serve as endpoints of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
. Assume, additionally, that we are given a cut
(X,Y)
in
G^{\prime}
with
|E_{G^{\prime}}(X,Y)|\leq\frac{64\Delta}{\eta\log^{4}n}+\frac{256|{\mathcal{Q}}|}{\eta}
, such that
A^{\prime}\subseteq X
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq Y
. Clearly,
(X,Y)
is also a cut in graph
G
. We claim that
E_{G}(X,Y)=E_{G^{\prime}}(X,Y)
. Indeed, since
|{\mathcal{Q}}|<\Delta
, we get that
|B^{\prime}|>|B|-\Delta
, and so
|X|\leq n-|B^{\prime}|<n-|B|+\Delta\leq 2(n-|B|)
(here, we have used the fact that
\Delta\leq|A|\leq(n-|B|)
). Assume for contradiction that
E_{G}(X,Y)\neq E_{G^{\prime}}(X,Y)
. Since
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
, there must be an edge
e=(x,y)\in E_{G}(X,Y)
, such that
e\not\in E(G^{\prime})
. This can only happen if
x\in L
, and graph
G^{\prime}
contains a subset
E^{\prime}(x)\subseteq\delta^{+}_{G}(x)
of
512(n-|B|)
edges. Since
|X|\leq 2(n-|B|)
, at least
510(n-|B|)
edges of
E^{\prime}(x)
must lie in
E_{G^{\prime}}(X,Y)
. Therefore,
|E_{G^{\prime}}(X,Y)|\geq 510(n-|B|)
must hold. However,
|E_{G^{\prime}}(X,Y)|\leq\frac{64\Delta}{\eta\log^{4}n}+\frac{256|{\mathcal{Q}}|}{\eta}\leq 509\Delta\leq 509(n-|B|)
, a contradiction. From the above discussion, we obtain the following claim.

Claim 3.10
.

There is a deterministic algorithm, that, given an instance
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem (where graph
G
is given as an adjacency list), constructs another instance
(G^{\prime},A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem with
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
, such that
|E(G^{\prime})|\leq O(n\cdot(n-|B|))
, and moreover, if
({\mathcal{Q}},(X,Y))
is a valid solution to instance
(G^{\prime},A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
, then it is also a valid solution to instance
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
. The running time of the algorithm is
O(n\cdot(n-|B|))
.

In our algorithms for the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, we will use
Claim

3.10
in order to ensure that the number of edges in the input graph is bounded by
O(n\cdot(n-|B|))
.
We now turn to describe the
MWU
-based approach for solving
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
.

3.5
Solving
r
-Restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
via the Modified MWU Framework and a Reduction to
r
-Restricted
s
-
t
-
SP

In this subsection we provide a high-level description of our algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, including the modified
MWU
framework that we use, and a reduction to a problem that we call
r
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
, that can be viewed as a special case of the
SSSP
problem. We show that an algorithm for the
r
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem implies an algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, via the modified
MWU
framework. Lastly, we provide a simple though not very efficient algorithm for
r
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
, that will be used as the induction base in the proof of
Theorem

3.9
.

Let
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
be the input to the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem instance, where
G=(L,R,E)
is a well-structured graph with
|V(G)|=n
, that is given as an adjancency list,
A
and
B
are disjoint subsets of
V(G)
, and
N\geq n
,
\Delta\geq 1
, and
1\leq\eta\leq\Delta
are parameters, with
|A|,|B|\geq\Delta
. Since the instance is
r
-restricted,
\frac{(n-|B|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\leq 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N}}
holds. By using the algorithm from
Claim

3.10
, we convert this instance into another instance
(G^{\prime},A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
with
|E(G^{\prime})|\leq O(n\cdot(n-|B|))
, in time
O(n\cdot(n-|B|))
. From now on we focus on solving instance
(G^{\prime},A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
, and, for convenience, we denote
G^{\prime}
by
G
.

Recall that our goal is to compute a routing
{\mathcal{Q}}
from
A
to
B
, whose congestion is bounded by
4\eta\log N
. Additionally, if
|{\mathcal{Q}}|<\Delta
, we need to compute a cut
(X,Y)
in
G
with
|E_{G}(X,Y)|\leq\frac{64\Delta}{\eta\log^{4}n}+\frac{256|{\mathcal{Q}}|}{\eta}
, such that, if

A^{\prime}\subseteq A,B^{\prime}\subseteq B
denote the subsets of vertices that do not serve as endpoints of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
, then
A^{\prime}\subseteq X
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq Y
hold.
Recall also that the edges of
E_{G}(R,L)
are called special edges, and the remaining edges of
G
are called regular edges.
From the definition of well-structured graphs, every vertex of
G
is incident to at most one special edge.
We start with a simple preprocessing step.

A Preprocessing Step.

As a preprocessing step, we construct a maximal collection
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}
of disjoint paths, where every path connects a distinct vertex of
A
to a distinct vertex of
B
, and consists of a single regular edge. We do so by employing a simple greedy algorithm: Start with
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}=\emptyset
. While there is a regular edge
e=(a,b)
with
a\in A
and
b\in B
, add a path
Q=(e)
to
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}
, and delete
a
and
b
from
A
and
B
, respectively. Clearly, this preprocessing step can be executed in time
O(|E(G)|)\leq O(n\cdot(n-|B|))
. If
|{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}|\geq\Delta
, then we terminate the algorithm and return
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}
. We assume from now on that
|{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}|<\Delta
.
Let
A_{1}\subseteq A
,
B_{1}\subseteq B
denote the sets of vertices that do not serve as endpoints of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}
. Since
|{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}|<\Delta
, we get that
|B_{1}|\geq|B|-\Delta
, and so:

n-|B_{1}|\leq n-|B|+\Delta\leq 2(n-|B|),

(1)

since
n-|B|\geq|A|\geq\Delta
from the problem definition. The preprocessing step ensures that there is no regular edge in
G
connecting a vertex of
A_{1}
to a vertex of
B_{1}
, so every path connecting a vertex of
A_{1}
to a vertex of
B_{1}
must contain at least one special edge.

Next, we describe a modified
MWU
framework that will allow us to compute a solution to the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem instance. The modified
MWU
framework is designed in such a way that it reduces the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem to a special case of
SSSP
, that we call
r
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
, which seems more tractable than the general decremental
SSSP
problem in directed graphs. We will then design an algorithm for the
r
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem, that will allow us to obtain the desired algorithm for
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
. The algorithm for
r
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
will in turn rely on an algorithm for the
(r-1)
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem.

3.5.1
The Modified
MWU
Framework

We now describe an algorithm that is based on the modified
MWU
framework, but ignore the issue of the efficient implementation of the algorithm. We address this issue later, by reducing the problem to the
r
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem.

Throughout the algorithm, we use a parameter
{\Lambda=(n-|B_{1}|)\cdot\frac{\eta\log^{5}n}{\Delta}}
. While the set
B_{1}
of vertices may change over the course of the algorithm, the value of the parameter
\Lambda
is set at the beginning of the algorithm and remains unchanged throughout the algorithm.
Our algorithm maintains an assignment
\ell(e)\geq 0
of
lengths
to the edges of
G
. At the beginning of the algorithm, we assign an initial length
\ell(e)
to every edge
e\in E(G)
, as follows. If
e
is a regular edge, we set
\ell(e)=0
, and if it is a special edge, we set
\ell(e)=\frac{1}{\Lambda}
.

As the algorithm progresses, the lengths of the special edges may grow, but the lengths of the regular edges remain unchanged.
Whenever we talk about distances between vertices and lengths of paths, it is always with respect to the current lengths
\ell(e)
of edges
e\in E(G)
.

The algorithm gradually constructs a routing
{\mathcal{Q}}
from
A_{1}
to
B_{1}
in
G
, starting with
{\mathcal{Q}}=\emptyset
. It then performs at most
\Delta
iterations, and in every iteration, a single path
P
, connecting some vertex
a\in A_{1}
to some vertex
b\in B_{1}
, is added to
{\mathcal{Q}}
.
We add
P
to
{\mathcal{Q}}
, and delete
a
from
A_{1}
and
b
from
B_{1}
. Additionally, we may double the lengths of
some
special edges on the path
P
. Specifically, our algorithm maintains, for every special edge
e
of
G
, a counter
n(e)
, which, intuitively, counts the number of paths that were added to
{\mathcal{Q}}
that contained
e
, since
\ell(e)
was last doubled (or since the beginning of the algorithm, if
\ell(e)
was never doubled yet). At the beginning of the algorithm, we set
n(e)=0
for every special edge
e
. Whenever a path
P
is added to
{\mathcal{Q}}
, we increase the counter
n(e)
of every special edge
e\in E(P)
. If, for any such edge
e
, the counter
n(e)
reaches
\eta
, then we double the length of edge
e
, and reset the counter
n(e)
to be
0
.

The Oracle

At the heart of our algorithm is an
oracle
– an algorithm that, in every iteration, either computes a path
P
of length at most
1
in the current graph
G
connecting a vertex of
A_{1}
to a vertex of
B_{1}
, or produces a new assignment
\ell^{\prime}(e)
for edges
e\in E(G)
, that we call a
cut-witness
, and define next. Intuitively, the cut-witness is designed in such a way that it can be easily transformed into the desired cut
(X,Y)
in
G
with
|E_{G}(X,Y)|\leq\frac{64\Delta}{\eta\log^{4}n}+\frac{256|{\mathcal{Q}}|}{\eta}
, such that, if

A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
denote the current sets
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
respectively, then
A^{\prime}\subseteq X
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq Y
hold. Once the oracle produces a cut-witness, the algorithm terminates. We now define the cut-witness formally.

Definition 3.11
(Cut-witness).

A
cut-witness
is an assignment of lengths
\ell^{\prime}(e)\geq 0
to every edge
e\in E(G)
, such that, if we denote by
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
the current sets
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
of vertices respectively, and by
E^{*}
the set of all special edges with both endpoints in
B^{\prime}
, then:

•

\sum_{e\in E(G)}\ell^{\prime}(e)\leq\frac{\Delta}{2\eta\log^{4}n}+\sum_{e\in E(G)\setminus E^{*}}\ell(e)
; and

•

the distance in graph
G
, with respect to edge lengths
\ell^{\prime}(\cdot)
, from
A^{\prime}
to
B^{\prime}
is at least
\frac{1}{64}
.

Next, we define the notion of an
acceptable path
in
G
, and we will require that the oracle, in every iteration, either returns an acceptable path, or returns a cut-witness.

Definition 3.12
(Acceptable Path).

A path
P
in the current graph
G
is called
acceptable
if
P
is a simple path, connecting a vertex of
A_{1}
to a vertex of
B_{1}
, the length of
P
with respect to the current edge lengths
\ell(\cdot)
is at most
1
, and no inner vertices of
P
belong to
B_{1}
.

We are now ready to define the oracle.

Definition 3.13
(The Oracle).

An oracle for the
MWU
framework is an algorithm that, in every iteration, either returns an acceptable path
P
, or returns a cut-witness, or returns “FAIL”. The probability that the oracle ever returns “FAIL” must be bounded by
1/2
.

The
MWU
-Based Algorithm.

We describe the modified
MWU
-based algorithm, denoted by
ALG-MWU
in Figure
2
; the description excludes the implementation of the oracle, that will be discussed later.

Alg-MWU

•

Let
A_{1}\subseteq A
and
B_{1}\subseteq B
be the sets of vertices that do not serve as endpoints of any path in the set
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}
constructed in the preprocessing step.

•

Initialize the data structures:

–

Set
{\mathcal{Q}}=\emptyset
;

–

For every edge
e\in E(G)
, if
e
is a regular edge, set
\ell(e)=0
, otherwise set
\ell(e)=\frac{1}{\Lambda}
.

–

For every special edge
e
, set
n(e)=0
.

•

Perform at most
\Delta-|{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}|
iterations, where in each iteration we apply the oracle.

–

If the oracle returned “FAIL”, then return “FAIL” and terminate the algorithm;

–

If the oracle returned a cut-witness, return the cut-witness and terminate the algorithm;

–

Otherwise, the oracle must have returned an acceptable path
P
connecting a vertex
a\in A_{1}
to a vertex
b\in B_{1}
.

*

remove
a
from
A_{1}
and
b
from
B_{1}
;

*

add
P
to
{\mathcal{Q}}
;

*

for each special edge
e\in E(P)
, increase
n(e)
by
1
, and, if
n(e)
reaches
\eta
, double
\ell(e)
and set
n(e)=0
.

Figure 2:
Alg-MWU

Before we discuss the implementation of the oracle, and an efficient implementation of Algorithm
ALG-MWU
, we first show that we can use the algorithm in order to solve the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem.
Observe first that, if the total running time of the oracle, over the course of all iterations, is bounded by
T
, then the total running time of Algorithm
ALG-MWU
is bounded by
O(|T|)+O(|E(G)|)\leq O(|T|)+O(n\cdot(n-|B|))
. Moreover, since the probability that the oracle ever returns “FAIL” is at most
1/2
, the probability that algorithm
ALG-MWU
terminates with a “FAIL” is bounded by
1/2
.

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}
be the set of paths obtained when Algorithm
ALG-MWU
terminates, and let
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{Q}}\cup{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}
be the final set of paths that we obtain. Clearly, every path in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
connects a vertex of
A
to a vertex of
B
. We start by showing that the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
cause congestion at most
4\eta\log n
, and that the endpoints of all paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
are disjoint, in the following simple observation.

Observation 3.14
.

The endpoints of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
are disjoint, and the congestion caused by the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
in
G
is bounded by
4\eta\log n
.

Proof.

It is easy to verify that the endpoints of all paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
are disjoint. First, the preprocessing step, in every iteration selects a regular edge
e=(a,b)
with
a\in A
and
b\in B
, adds the path
Q=(e)
to
Q_{1}
, and then deletes
a
from
A
and
b
from
B
. This ensures that vertices
a
and
b
cannot serve as endpoints of any paths that are subsequently added to
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}
, or to
{\mathcal{Q}}
. Additionally, whenever Algorithm
ALG-MWU
adds a new path
P
connecting a vertex
a^{\prime}\in A_{1}
to a vertex
b^{\prime}\in B_{1}
to
{\mathcal{Q}}
, we delete
a^{\prime}
from
A_{1}
and
b^{\prime}
from
B_{1}
. Therefore,
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
may not serve as endpoints of paths that are added subsequently to
{\mathcal{Q}}
. Hence the endpoints of all paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
are disjoint.

Next, we show that every
special edge
of
G
belongs to at most
2\eta\log n
paths of
{\mathcal{Q}}
. At the beginning of the algorithm, we set
\ell(e)=\frac{1}{\Lambda}=\frac{\Delta}{(n-|B_{1}|)\cdot\eta\cdot\log^{5}n}\geq\frac{\Delta}{n\cdot\eta\cdot\log^{5}n}
. Let
i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{q}
denote the indices of the iterations when
\ell(e)
was doubled. Notice that, once
\ell(e)
grows above
1
, it may no longer be used by any paths that are added to
{\mathcal{P}}
, so
q\leq\left\lceil\log\Lambda\right\rceil+1\leq 2\log n
must hold. Moreover, for all
1\leq j<q
, the number of paths that were added to
{\mathcal{Q}}
after iteration
i_{j}
but until iteration
i_{j+1}
(inclusive), that contained
e
, is bounded by
\eta
.
Similarly, at most
\eta
paths containing
e
were added to
{\mathcal{Q}}
until iteration
i_{1}
(inclusive).
Altogether,
e
may belong to at most
2\eta\log n
paths of
{\mathcal{Q}}
.
Since paths of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}
do not contain special edges,
every special edge belongs to at most
2\eta\log n
paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
.

Consider now some
regular edge

e=(u,v)\in E(G)
. Recall that no regular edge in
G
may connect a vertex of
A_{1}
to a vertex of
B_{1}
. Therefore, if a path
P\in{\mathcal{Q}}
contains
e
, then it must contain either the unique special edge that is incident to
u
(if it exists), or the unique special edge that is incident to
v
(if it exists). Moreover, if a path
Q=(e)
belongs to
{\mathcal{Q}}_{0}
, then every path
Q^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Q}}
that contains
e
must contain both the unique special edge incident to
u
and the unique special edge incident to
v
(since
u\not\in A_{1}
and
v\not\in B_{1}
must hold). Therefore, every regular edge
e
belongs to at most
4\eta\log n
paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
.

Let
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
denote the sets
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
, respectively, at the end of Algorithm
ALG-MWU
. Notice that
A^{\prime}
is a set of all vertices
a\in A
that do not serve as endpoints of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
, and similarly,
B^{\prime}
contains all vertices
b\in B
that do not serve as endpoints of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
.
We now show an algorithm, that, given a cut-witness
\left\{\ell^{\prime}(e)\right\}_{e\in E(G)}
, computes a cut
(X,Y)
in
G
with
|E_{G}(X,Y)|\leq\frac{64\Delta}{\eta\log^{4}n}+\frac{256|{\mathcal{Q}}|}{\eta}
, such that
A^{\prime}\subseteq X
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq Y
hold.

Claim 3.15
.

There is a deterministic algorithm, that, given a cut-witness
\left\{\ell^{\prime}(e)\right\}_{e\in E(G)}
for
G
, obtained at the end of Algorithm
ALG-MWU
, computes a cut
(X,Y)
in
G
with
|E_{G}(X,Y)|\leq\frac{64\Delta}{\eta\log^{4}n}+\frac{256|{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}|}{\eta}
, such that
A^{\prime}\subseteq X
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq Y
hold. The running time of the algorithm is
O(|E(G)|)\leq O(n\cdot(n-|B|))
.

Proof.

Consider the cut-witness
\left\{\ell^{\prime}(e)\right\}_{e\in E(G)}
. We start by bounding
\sum_{e\in E(G)}\ell^{\prime}(e)
in the following simple observation.

Observation 3.16
.

\sum_{e\in E(G)}\ell^{\prime}(e)\leq\frac{\Delta}{\eta\log^{4}n}+\frac{2|{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}|}{\eta}
.

Proof.

For every special edge
e\in E(G)
, let
\ell^{0}(e)
denote the length of
e
at the beginning of Algorithm
ALG-MWU
, and let
\ell^{*}(e)
denote the length of
e
at the end of the algorithm.
We denote by
E^{\prime}
the set of all special edges
e\in E(G)
, such that, at the beginning of Algorithm
ALG-MWU
, both endpoints of
E^{\prime}
lied in
B_{1}
, but
e\not\in E^{*}
. For every edge
e\in E^{\prime}
, at least one endpoint of
e
was deleted from
B_{1}
over the course of the algorithm, and
e
is the only special edge incident to that endpoint, so
|E^{\prime}|\leq\Delta
must hold. Recall that, at the beginning of the algorithm, every special edge was assigned the length
\frac{1}{\Lambda}
. Let
E^{\mathsf{spec}}
denote the set of all special edges in
G
. For each edge
e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}
, if
e\not\in E^{\prime}\cup E^{*}
, then, at the beginning of Algorithm
ALG-MWU
,
e
was incident to some vertex
x\in V(G)\setminus B_{1}
, and moreover,
e
is the only special edge incident to
x
. Therefore,
|E(G)\setminus(E^{\prime}\cup E^{*})|\leq n-|B_{1}|
, and
|E(G)\setminus E^{*}|\leq n-B_{1}+\Delta\leq 2(n-|B_{1}|)
, since
\Delta\leq|A|\leq n-|B_{1}|
. Overall, we get that:

\sum_{e\in E(G)\setminus E^{*}}\ell^{0}(e)\leq\frac{2(n-|B_{1}|)}{\Lambda}\leq\frac{2\Delta}{\eta\cdot\log^{5}n},

since
\Lambda=(n-|B_{1}|)\cdot\frac{\eta\log^{5}n}{\Delta}
.

Let
\hat{E}
denote the subset of special edge
e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}\setminus E^{*}
with
\ell^{*}(e)>\ell^{0}(e)
. Clearly,
\sum_{e\in E(G)\setminus E^{*}}\ell^{*}(e)\leq\sum_{e\in E(G)\setminus E^{*}}\ell^{0}(e)+\sum_{e\in\hat{E}}\ell^{*}(e)
.

Let
q
be the total number of iterations Algorithm
ALG-MWU
, so
|{\mathcal{Q}}|=q
holds, and denote
{\mathcal{Q}}=\left\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{q}\right\}
, where the paths are indexed in the order in which they were added to
{\mathcal{Q}}
. For all
1\leq i\leq q
, let
C_{i}
denote the length of path
P_{i}
when it was added to
{\mathcal{Q}}
. Clearly,
C_{i}\leq 1
, and
\sum_{i=1}^{q}C_{i}\leq q
.

Consider now some edge
e\in\hat{E}
, and recall that
\ell(e)
was doubled at least once during the algorithm. Let
\tau
be the last time when the length of
e
was doubled. Then, prior to time
\tau
, there were at least
\eta
iterations
i
, during which the path
P_{i}
that was added to
{\mathcal{Q}}
contained
e
, and the length of
e
during the corresponding iteration was
\frac{\ell^{*}(e)}{2}
. In other words, edge
e
contributes at least
\frac{\ell^{*}(e)\eta}{2}
to
\sum_{i=1}^{q}C_{i}
.
Therefore, we get that:

\sum_{e\in\hat{E}}\ell^{*}(e)\leq\frac{2\sum_{i=1}^{q}C_{i}}{\eta}\leq\frac{2q}{\eta}\leq\frac{2|{\mathcal{Q}}|}{\eta}.

Altogether, we get that:

\sum_{e\in E(G)\setminus E^{*}}\ell^{*}(e)\leq\sum_{e\in E(G)\setminus E^{*}}\ell^{0}(e)+\sum_{e\in\hat{E}}\ell^{*}(e)\leq\frac{2\Delta}{\eta\cdot\log^{5}n}+\frac{2|{\mathcal{Q}}|}{\eta}.

Lastly, from the definition of the cut-witness, we get that:

\sum_{e\in E(G)}\ell^{\prime}(e)\leq\frac{\Delta}{2\eta\log^{4}n}+\sum_{e\in E(G)\setminus E^{*}}\ell(e)\leq\frac{\Delta}{\eta\log^{4}n}+\frac{2|{\mathcal{Q}}|}{\eta}\leq\frac{\Delta}{\eta\log^{4}n}+\frac{2|{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}|}{\eta}.

Consider the graph
G
with lengths
\ell^{\prime}(e)
on its edges
e\in E(G)
. From the definition of a cut-witness, the length of a shortest-path (with respect to edge-lengths
\ell^{\prime}(\cdot)
) connecting a vertex of
A^{\prime}
to a vertex of
B^{\prime}
in
G^{\prime}
is at least
\frac{1}{64}
.

Consider a thought experiment, where we select a threshold
\rho\in(0,\frac{1}{64})
uniformly at random, and then let
X
be the set of all vertices
v\in V(G)
, such that the distance from
A^{\prime}
to
v
, with respect to the length
\ell^{\prime}(\cdot)
of edges, is at most
\rho
. Let
Y=V(G)\setminus X
. It is easy to verify that, since the distance from
A^{\prime}
to
B^{\prime}
in
G
is at least
\frac{1}{64}
,
A^{\prime}\subseteq X
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq Y
must hold. Moreover, the probability that an edge
e\in E(G)
lies in
E_{G}(X,Y)
is bounded by
64\ell^{\prime}(e)
. Therefore, the expectation of
|E_{G}(X,Y)|
is bounded by
64\sum_{e\in E(G)}\ell^{\prime}(e)\leq\frac{64\Delta}{\eta\log^{4}n}+\frac{128|{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}|}{\eta}
.

By performing a standard ball-growing procedure from the set
A^{\prime}
of vertices in graph
G
, we can compute, in time
O(|E(G)|)
, a cut
(X,Y)
in
G
with
|E_{G}(X,Y)|\leq\frac{64\Delta}{\eta\log^{4}n}+\frac{256|{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}|}{\eta}
, such that
A^{\prime}\subseteq X
and
B^{\prime}\subseteq Y
.
The total running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O(|E(G)|)\leq O(n\cdot(n-|B|))
.

To summarize, we provided an algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, that assumes the existence of an oracle.

We now discuss an efficient implementation of our algorithm for the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, given an efficient implementation of the oracle. Recall that the time required for the preprocessing step, and for computing the final cut
(X,Y)
by the algorithm from
Claim

3.15
, is bounded by
O(n\cdot(n-|B|))
. If the running time of the oracle is bounded by
T
, then the running time of Algorithm
ALG-MWU
is bounded by
O(T+n\cdot(n-B))
. Therefore, the total running time of the algorithm for the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem is bounded by
O(T+n\cdot(n-|B|))
. We now focus on implementing the oracle.

Implementing the Oracle: Intuition.

Below we define a problem called
r
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
. The problem is designed to implement the oracle from
Definition

3.13
. However, for convenience, we
multiply all edge lengths by factor
\Lambda
, and then round them up to the next integer. We denote the new edge lengths by
\hat{\ell}(e)
for
e\in E(G)
. Notice that, throughout the algorithm, the length
\hat{\ell}(e)
of every edge
e\in E(G)
is either
0
, or it is an integer between
1
and
\left\lceil\Lambda\right\rceil
. We now define the analogues of an acceptable path and a cut-witness with respect to the new edge lengths, that we refer to as
modified acceptable path
and
modified cut-witness
, respectively.

Definition 3.17
(Modified Acceptable Path).

A path
P
in the current graph
G
is called a
modified acceptable path for
G
with respect to
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
, if
P
is a simple path, connecting a vertex of
A_{1}
to a vertex of
B_{1}
, the length of
P
with respect to the current edge lengths
\hat{\ell}(\cdot)
is at most
\Lambda
, and no inner vertices of
P
belong to
B_{1}
.

Definition 3.18
(Modified Cut-witness).

A
modified cut-witness
for graph
G
with respect to
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
is an assignment of lengths
\ell^{\prime\prime}(e)\geq 0
to every edge
e\in E(G)
, such that if we denote by
A^{\prime},B^{\prime}
the current sets
A_{1},B_{1}
of vertices, and by
E^{*}
the set of all special edges with both endpoints in
B^{\prime}
, then:

•

\sum_{e\in E(G)}\ell^{\prime\prime}(e)\leq\frac{\Lambda\cdot\Delta}{\eta\log^{4}n}+\sum_{e\in E(G)\setminus E^{*}}\hat{\ell}(e)
; and

•

the distance in graph
G
, with respect to edge lengths
\ell^{\prime\prime}(\cdot)
, from
A^{\prime}
to
B^{\prime}
is at least
\frac{\Lambda}{32}
.

It is immediate to verify that, if
\left\{\ell^{\prime\prime}(e)\right\}_{e\in E(G)}
is a modified cut-witness for
G
, then, by setting the length
\ell^{\prime}(e)=\frac{\ell^{\prime\prime}(e)}{2\Lambda}
for every edge
e\in E(G)
, we obtain a valid cut-witness for
G
. Additionally, if
P
is a valid modified acceptable path for
G
, then it is also an acceptable path for
G
.

In order to implement the oracle, it is now enough to design an algorithm that, in every iteration, either produces a modified acceptable path in
G
, or produces a modified cut-witness for
G
, or returns “FAIL”, such that the probability that the algorithm ever returns “FAIL” is bounded by
1/2
.
Recall that
n-|B_{1}|\leq 2(n-|B|)
from Inequality
1
, and so, from the definition of
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem,

\frac{(n-|B_{1}|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\leq\frac{2(n-|B|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\leq 2\cdot 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N}}
holds.

We now provide a formal definition of the
r
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem, that is designed specifically to provide such an algorithm. For convenience, in the definition of the problem, we will denote the sets
A_{1},B_{1}
of vertices by
A
and
B
, respectively, and we will denote the edge lengths
\hat{\ell}(e)
by
\ell(e)
.

3.5.2
The
r
-Restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
Problem

The input to the
r
-Restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem is a well-structured
n
-vertex graph
G=(L,R,E)
, parameters
N\geq n
,
\Delta\geq 1
, and
1\leq\eta\leq\Delta
, and two disjoint subsets
A,B
of vertices of
G
, such that no regular edge of
G
connects a vertex of
A
to a vertex of
B
in
G
,
\Delta\leq n-|B|
, and
\frac{(n-|B|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\leq 2\cdot 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N}}
holds.
We also require that
|E|\leq O(n\cdot(n-|B|))
and that
1\leq r\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
.

Initially, every edge
e\in E(G)
is assigned a length
\ell(e)
as follows: if
e
is a regular edge then
\ell(e)=0
, and otherwise
\ell(e)=1
.
We use a parameter
\Lambda=\frac{(n-|B|)\eta\cdot\log^{5}n}{\Delta}
, and we initialize
{\mathcal{Q}}=\emptyset
. While the set
B
of vertices may change over the course of the algorithm, parameter
\Lambda
remains unchanged.

The execution of the algorithm for the
r
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem consists of at most
\Delta
iterations. In every iteration
i
, the algorithm must do one of the following:

•

either return FAIL;

•

or return a modified cut-witness for
G
with respect to
A
and
B
(see
Definition

3.18
);

•

or return a modified acceptable path
P
for
G
with respect to
A
and
B
(see
Definition

3.17
).

In the former two cases, the algorithm terminates. In the last case, let
a\in A
,
b\in B
denote the endpoints of path
P
. We remove
a
from
A
and
b
from
B
and add
P
to
{\mathcal{Q}}
. Additionally, the lengths of some of the special edges on
P
will be doubled; we assume that the algorithm receives a list of all such edges.

For every special edge
e\in E(G)
, the length of
e
is doubled once
e
participates in exactly
\eta
paths that the algorithm returned, since the last time the length of
e
was doubled, or since the beginning of the algorithm – whatever happened last.

Lastly, we require that the probability that the algorithm ever returns “FAIL” is bounded by
\frac{1}{2}
.
This concludes the definition of the
r
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem.

When an algorithm for the problem computes a modified acceptable path
P
, we may say, for convenience, that it responds to a
short-path
query.
We note that the running time of an algorithm for the
s
-
t
-
SP
problem includes both the time required to maintain its data structures (total update time), and time required to compute and output the modified acceptable paths in every iteration (sometimes referred to as query time).

The following theorem is immediate from the discussion in Section
3.5.1
, the definition of the
r
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem, and Inequality
1
.

Theorem 3.19
.

Suppose there is an algorithm for the
r
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem, that, given an input
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
with
|V(G)|=n
, has running time at most
n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot T(n,\Delta,\eta,N)
. Then there is an algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, that, on input
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
with
|V(G)|=n
, has running time at most
O\left(n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot T(n,\Delta,\eta,N)\right)+O(n\cdot(n-|B|))
.

Lastly, we show a simple but not very efficient algorithm for
s
-
t
-
SP
, that, combined with
Theorem

3.19
, will be used as the induction base in the proof of
Theorem

3.9
.

3.5.3
A Simple Algorithm for
s
-
t
-
SP

In this subsection we provide the following simple algorithm for the
r
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem.

Theorem 3.20
.

For all
r\geq 1
, there is a deterministic algorithm for the
r
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem, that, on input
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
with
|V(G)|=n
, has running time:

O\left(n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot\frac{(n-|B|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\cdot\log^{6}N\right)
.

Proof.

We define a dynamic graph
H
, and we will eventually apply the algorithm for maintaining the
ES-Tree
data structure from
Theorem

2.6
to graph
H
, with the distance parameter
d=\Lambda
.

We initialize graph
H
as follows. We let
V(H)=V(G)\cup\left\{s,t\right\}
. For every regular edge
e=(u,v)\in E(G)
, we include edge
e
with length
\tilde{\ell}(e)=1
in
H
. For every special edge
e^{\prime}=(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in E(G)
, we include
\left\lceil\log\Lambda\right\rceil+1
parallel edges
e_{1},\ldots,e_{\left\lceil\log\Lambda\right\rceil+1}
, where for all
1\leq i\leq\left\lceil\log\Lambda\right\rceil+1
, the length
\tilde{\ell}(e_{i})=2^{i-1}
. Lastly, for every vertex
a\in A
, we add an edge
(s,a)
of length
1
, and for every vertex
b\in B
, we add an edge
(b,t)
of length
1
to
H
.
Note that graph
H
can be initialized in time
O(|E(G)|\cdot\log n)\leq O(n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot\log n)
.
We then initialize the algorithm for maintaining the
ES-Tree
data structure from
Theorem

2.6
on graph
H
, with the distance threshold
d=\Lambda
.

Our algorithm consists of at most
\Delta
iterations. In every iteration, we check whether
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(s,t)\leq\Lambda
holds, in time
O(1)
, using the
ES-Tree
data structure. Assume first that this is the case. Then we use the
ES-Tree
data structure to compute the shortest
s
-
t
path
P
in the current graph
H
, of length at most
\Lambda
. From our construction, for every vertex
b\in B
, there is an edge
(b,t)
of length
1
in
H
. This ensures that path
P
may not contain vertices of
B
as inner vertices. Let
P^{\prime}
be the path obtained from
P
by deleting its first and last vertex. It is then easy to verify that
P^{\prime}
is a modified acceptable path, that we return as an output of the current iteration. Let
a\in A
,
b\in B
be the endpoints of path
P^{\prime}
. Recall that following the current iteration,
a
is deleted from
A
and
b
is deleted from
B
. Additionally, the lengths of some special edges of
E(P^{\prime})
may be doubled.
We modify graph
H
, in order to reflect these changes as follows. We delete the edges
(s,a)
and
(b,t)
from
H
. Additionally, for every special edge
e^{\prime}\in E(P)
whose length was doubled in
G
, we delete the shortest-length copy of
e^{\prime}
from
H
.

Assume now that, at the beginning of some iteration, we discover that
\mbox{\sf dist}_{H}(s,t)>\Lambda
holds. Then, in the current graph
H
, the length of the shortest path connecting a vertex of
A
to a vertex of
B
is at least
\Lambda-2\geq\frac{\Lambda}{2}
. We now define the lengths
\ell^{\prime}(e)
of edges
e\in E(G)
as follows. If
e
is a regular edge that lies in
H
, then we set
\ell^{\prime}(e)=0
. If
e=(x,y)
is a special edge, then we let
\ell^{\prime}(e)
be the length of the shortest parallel edge
(x,y)
in
H
.

Let
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
denote the sets
A
and
B
of vertices at the end of the algorithm, respectively.
Clearly,
\sum_{e\in E(G)}\ell^{\prime}(e)\leq\sum_{e\in E(G)}\ell(e)
holds. Next, we claim that, for every path
P
connecting a vertex
a\in A^{\prime}
to a vertex
b\in B^{\prime}
in
G
, the length
\sum_{e\in E(P)}\ell^{\prime}(e)\geq\frac{\Lambda}{32}
. Indeed, recall that we have established that the length of such a path
P
in graph
H
is at least
\frac{\Lambda}{2}
. The only difference between the edge lengths
\left\{\tilde{\ell}(e)\right\}_{e\in E(P)}
and
\left\{\ell^{\prime}(e)\right\}_{e\in E(P)}
is that the length
\tilde{\ell}(e)
of every regular edge
e\in E(H)
is
1
, and
\ell^{\prime}(e)=0
. However, since no regular edge may connect a vertex of
A
to a vertex of
B
in
G
, regular and special edges must alternate on path
P
, and, since the length of every special edge in
H
is at least
1
, the total length
\tilde{\ell}(e)
of all special edges
e\in E(P)
is at least
\frac{\sum_{e^{\prime}\in E(P)}\hat{\ell}(e^{\prime})}{4}\geq\frac{\Lambda}{32}
.

Let
E^{*}
be the set of all special edges with both endpoints in
B^{\prime}
. In order to turn the current assignment
\left\{\ell^{\prime}(e)\right\}_{e\in E(G)}
of edge lengths to a valid modified cut-witness, we set the length
\ell^{\prime}(e)
of every edge
e\in E^{*}
to
0
. It is easy to verify that, for every path
P
connecting a vertex of
A
to a vertex of
B
in
G
, its length remains at least
\frac{\Lambda}{32}
, and
\sum_{e\in E(G)}\ell^{\prime}(e)\leq\sum_{e\in E(G)\setminus E^{*}}\ell(e)
now holds. Therefore, we obtain a valid modified cut-witness for
G
.

It now remains to analyze the running time of the algorithm. We start by analyzing the
total update time
– the time required to maintain all data structures, which excludes the
query time
– the time required to compute the modified acceptable paths in every iteration.

Let
m
denote the total number of edges that are ever present in
H
, and recall that
m\leq O(n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot\log N)
. Once graph
H
is initialized, the additional update of the algorithm is bounded by
O(m\cdot\Lambda)
. Therefore, the total update time that is required in order to maintain the data structures (excluding the time required to return the paths in every iteration) is bounded by:

O(n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot\Lambda\cdot\log N)\leq O\left(n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot\frac{(n-|B|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\cdot\log^{6}N\right).

Assume now that the number of iterations in which the algorithm returned a modified acceptable path is
q
, and let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{q}
denote the paths that the algorithm returned. Then the time that the algorithm spent on returning these paths is bounded by
\sum_{i=1}^{q}O(|E(P_{i})|)
. Recall that no regular edge connects a vertex of
A
to a vertex of
B
in
G
, and so regular and special edges alternate on each such path
P_{i}
. Therefore, for all
1\leq i\leq q
,
|E(P_{i})|
is asymptotically bounded by the number of special edges on
P_{i}
.

Consider now some special edge
e
of
G
. When
e
is first added to
G
, we include at most
O(\log n)
copies of
e
in
H
. Every time edge
e
participates in
\eta
of the paths that the algorithm returns, one of the copies of
e
is deleted from
H
. Therefore, copies of a special edge
e
of
G
may participate in at most
O(\eta\log n)
paths that the algorithm returns, and, overall,
\sum_{i=1}^{q}|E(P_{i})|\leq O(n\eta\log n)
(since there are at most
n
special edges in
G
). We conclude that the total running time of the algorithm is bounded by:

O\left(n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot\frac{(n-|B|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\cdot\log^{6}N\right)+O(n\eta\log N)\leq O\left(n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot\frac{(n-|B|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\cdot\log^{6}N\right),

since
\eta\leq\Delta\leq n-|B|
from the problem definition.

By combining
Theorem

3.20
with
Theorem

3.19
,
and recalling that, in the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem,
\frac{(n-|B|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\leq 2\cdot 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N}}
holds, we obtain the following immediate corollary.

Corollary 3.21
.

There is a deterministic algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, that, on input
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
with
|V(G)|=n
, has running time at most
O\left(n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot\log^{6}N\right)
.

We will use this corollary in our inductive proof of
Theorem

3.9
, for the base case when
r=1
, and also for general values of
r
, when the value of the parameter
N
is sufficiently small.

4
Tools for Expander-Like Graphs and the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
Problem

In this section we introduce some tools for expander-like graphs, and define the second main problem that serves as a subroutine in our algorithm for
Maximum Bipartite Matching
– the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem. We start by definining subdivided graphs, and providing a new definition of cut sparsity, that extends the standard definition to graphs with edge lengths in a natural way.

4.1
A Subdivided Graph and a New Definition of Cut Sparsity

We first recall a standard definition of cut sparsity in directed graphs. Let
G
be an arbitrary directed graph, and assume that the graph is unweighted, so all edge lengths and capacities are unit. Under the standard definition, the sparsity of the cut
(A,B)
in
G
is defined as
\frac{|E_{G}(A,B)|}{\min\left\{|A|,|B|\right\}}
.

Suppose now that we are given a well-structured graph
G=(L,R,E)
, with a proper
uniform
assignment of lengths to its edges, so the lengths of all special edges in
G
are unit. In this case, for a well-structured cut
(A,B)
in
G
, it would be natural to define its sparsity as
\frac{|E_{G}(A,B)|}{\min\left\{|A|,|B|\right\}}
, to be consistent with the standard definition of sparsity.

Assume now that we are given a well-structured graph
G=(L,R,E)
, with a proper assignment of lengths to its edges, but now the lengths of the special edges may no longer be unit. Several of the expander-based tools that we define below work best when the underlying graph is unweighted; in the context of well-structured graphs, this corresponds to having a proper uniform assignment of edge lengths. A natural way to handle graphs with arbitrary lengths of special edges in this setting is to suitably
subdivide
its edges, so that the lengths of the special edges become unit, but the lengths of all paths are preserved. We now define a subdivided graph of a well-structured graph, a notion that we will use later, that will also motivate our modified definition of cut sparsity.

Definition 4.1
(Subdivided Graph).

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be a perfect well-structured graph with a proper assignment of lengths
\ell(e)
to its edges
e\in E
, and let
G^{\prime}=(L^{\prime},R^{\prime},E^{\prime})
be another graph. We say that
G^{\prime}
is the
subdivided graph of
G
, and denote
G^{\prime}=G^{+}
, if
G^{\prime}
can be obtained from
G
via the following process. We start with
G^{\prime}=G
,
L^{\prime}=L,R^{\prime}=R
and
E^{\prime}=E
, and then consider every special edge
e\in E
one by one. Let
e=(u,v)
be any such edge, and denote its length
\ell(e)
by
q
. We replace edge
e
with a path
P(e)=(u=x_{1},y_{1},x_{2},y_{2},\ldots,x_{q},y_{q}=v)
. Note that
u\in R^{\prime}
and
v\in L^{\prime}
must hold. We add vertices
x_{2},\ldots,x_{q}
to
R^{\prime}
, and vertices
y_{1},\ldots,y_{q-1}
to
L^{\prime}
. Edges
(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{q},y_{q})
become special edges, and edges
(y_{1},x_{2}),\ldots,(y_{q-1},x_{q})
become regular edges of
G^{\prime}
. The lengths of the resulting special edges are set to
1
, while the lengths of the resulting regular edges are set to
0
, so the length of the path
P(e)
is
q=\ell(e)
.

Note that, if
G
is a perfect well-structured graph with proper edge lengths, then its subdivided graph
G^{+}
is a perfect well-structured graph with proper uniform edge lengths. Therefore, we can use the definition of cut sparsity for well-structured graphs with proper uniform edge lengths from above in graph
G^{+}
. Every cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in graph
G^{+}
naturally also defines a cut
(A,B)
in
G
, where we set
A=V(G)\cap A^{\prime}
and
B=V(G)\cap B^{\prime}
. We will define the sparsity of cuts in perfect well-structured graphs with proper edge lengths in a natural way, so that, if a well-structured cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in
G^{+}
is
\varphi
-sparse according to the standard definition, then the sparsity of the corresponding cut
(A,B)
in
G
is at most
O(\varphi)
. Notice that, since graph
G^{+}
is obtained from
G
by subdividing some of its edges, every edge of
G
may contribute a number of vertices to
A^{\prime}
and/or to
B^{\prime}
. We will assign a
weight
to every vertex of
G
in order to reflect this contribution. We use the following definition of vertex weights throughout the paper.

Definition 4.2
(Vertex Weights).

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be a perfect well-structured graph with a proper assignment of lengths to its edges. For every vertex
v\in L\cup R
, the
weight

w(v)
is defined to be the length of the unique special edge that is incident to
v
. For a subset
S
of vertices of
G
, we define
w(S)=\sum_{v\in S}w(v)
, and for a subgraph
G^{\prime}
of
G
we define
w(G^{\prime})=w(V(G^{\prime}))
.

We are now ready to define the sparsity of cuts in perfect well-structured graphs.

Definition 4.3
(Cut Sparsity).

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be a perfect well-structured graph with a proper assignment of lengths on its edges, and let
(A,B)
be a well-structured cut in
G
. The
sparsity
of cut
(A,B)
is:

\Phi_{G}(A,B)=\frac{|E_{G}(A,B)|}{\min\left\{w(A),w(B)\right\}}.

We will use the following simple observation whose proof can be found in Section
A.1
of the Appendix.

Observation 4.4
.

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be a perfect well-structured graph with proper lengths
\ell(e)
on its edges
e\in E
, and let
G^{+}
be its subdivided graph. Assume further that we are given a parameter
0<\varphi<1
, and that the length of every edge in
G
is at most
\frac{1}{4\varphi}
. Let
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
be a well-structured cut in
G^{+}
of sparsity at most
\varphi
, and let
(A,B)
be the corresponding cut in
G
, where
A=A^{\prime}\cap V(G)
and
B=B^{\prime}\cap V(G)
. Then
(A,B)
is a well-structured cut in
G
,
w(A)\geq|A^{\prime}|/8
,
w(B)\geq|B^{\prime}|/8
, and
\Phi_{G}(A,B)\leq 8\varphi
.

The next observation allows us to transform a weakly well-structured cut into a strongly well-structured cut in a perfect well-structured graph, while roughly preserving its sparsity. The proof appears in Section
A.2
of the Appendix.

Observation 4.5
.

Let
G=(L,R,E)
be a perfect well-structured graph with a proper assignment of lengths
\ell(e)
to its edges
e\in E
, and let
0<\varphi\leq 1
be a parameter. Let
W=\sum_{e\in E}\ell(e)
, and assume that, for all
e\in E
,
\ell(e)\leq\frac{W}{2}
. Let
(A,B)
be a
\frac{\varphi}{2}
-sparse weakly well-structured cut in
G
. Then there is a
\varphi
-sparse strongly well-structured cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in
G
with
w(A^{\prime})\geq w(A)/4
and
w(B^{\prime})\geq w(B)/4
. Moreover, there is a deterministic algorithm, that, given
G
,
\varphi
, and cut
(A,B)
in
G
, computes cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in
G
with the above properties in time
O(\min\left\{\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(A),\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(B)\right\})
.

4.2
The
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
Problem

We abstract the central technical tool that we use in order to maintain expander-like graphs by the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, that we define next.

As an input to the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, we are given a
perfect
well-structured graph
G
with a proper assignment of lengths
\ell(e)
to its edges
e\in E(G)
, that subsequently undergoes some updates, though
G
remains a perfect well-structured graph throughout the algorithm.
Recall that we have defined vertex weights for perfect well-structured graphs, where the weight of a vertex
v
is the length of the unique special edge incident to
v
. For every vertex
v\in V(G)
, we denote by
w^{0}(v)
its initial weight in the initial input graph
G
, and we denote
W^{0}(G)=\sum_{v\in V(G)}w^{0}(v)
.
We are now ready to define the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem.

Definition 4.6
.

The input to the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem consists of a perfect well-structured graph
G=(L,R,E)
with
|V(G)|=n
, together with a proper assignment of lengths
\ell(e)
to its edges
e\in E
, and parameters
N\geq W^{0}(G)
,
1\leq\Delta\leq N
,
1\leq\eta\leq\Delta
, and
d\geq(\log N)^{64}
, such that
\frac{\Delta\cdot d}{\eta}\leq n
, and
N
is greater than a sufficiently large constant, so that, for example,
N>2^{2^{c_{2}}}
holds, where
c_{2}
is the constant from
Theorem

3.9
.

The algorithm consists of at most
\Delta
iterations.
At the beginning of every iteration
i
, the algorithm is given two vertices
x,y\in V(G)
, and it must return a simple path
P_{i}
in
G
connecting
x
to
y
of length at most
d
. (We may sometimes say that the algorithm is given a
short-path
query between the pair
(x,y)
of vertices, and it returns the path
P_{i}
in response to the query). After that the length of some special edges on path
P_{i}
may be doubled. We are guaranteed that the following property holds:

P1.

If
\tau^{\prime}>\tau
are two times at which the length of some edge
e\in E(G)
is doubled, then the algorithm returned at least
\eta
paths in response to queries during the time interval
(\tau,\tau^{\prime}]
that contained
e
.

The algorithm may, at any time, produce a strongly well-structured cut
(X,Y)
in
G
of sparsity
\Phi_{G}(X,Y)\leq\frac{(\log N)^{64}}{d}
.
If
w(X)\leq w(Y)
, then let
Z=X
and
Z^{\prime}=Y
; otherwise, let
Z=Y
and
Z^{\prime}=X
. We also let
J^{\prime}\subseteq Z^{\prime}
denote the set of vertices that serve as endpoints of the edges of
E_{G}(X,Y)
. The vertices of
Z\cup J^{\prime}
are then deleted from
G
, and the algorithm continues with the resulting graph
G=G[Z^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}]
. Note that we are guaranteed that
G
remains a perfect well-structured graph after this modification. Once
|V(G)|\leq n/2
holds, the algorithm terminates, even if fewer than
\Delta
iterations have passed.

The algorithm may also, at any time, return “FAIL”. The probability that the algorithm ever returns “FAIL” must be bounded by
1/N^{4}
.

For an integer
1\leq r\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
, we say that an instance
(G,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
of the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem is
r
-restricted, if
d\leq 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N}}
.

We prove the following theorem that provides an algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem. Though we do not use the theorem directly, it easily follows from our results and we believe that it is interesting in its own right.

Theorem 4.7
.

There is a randomized algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, that, on an instance
(G,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
of the problem, has running time at most
O\left(W^{0}(G)\cdot(W^{0}(G)+\Delta)\cdot 2^{O(\sqrt{\log N}\cdot\log\log N)}\right)
.

4.3
The
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
Problem

We will sometimes need to use a slight variation of the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, that we refer to as the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem. In this problem, as before, we are given a perfect well-structured graph
G
with proper lengths
\ell(e)
on its edges
e\in E(G)
, that subsequently undergoes some updates, though
G
remains a perfect well-structured graph throughout the algorithm.
Graph
G
is given in the adjacency-list representation, and initially all special edges have length
1
.
For every vertex
v\in V(G)
, we define its initial weight
w^{0}(v)
exactly as before, and we denote
W^{0}(G)=\sum_{v\in V(G)}w^{0}(v)
as before.
In addition to the perfect well-structured graph
G
, the algorithm is given as input a subset
\beta
of vertices of
G
. Over the course of the algorithm, some vertices may be deleted from the set
\beta
, but we will ensure that, throughout the algorithm, every special edge incident to a vertex in
\beta
has length
1
. For intuition, the vast majority of the vertices of the input graph
G
lie in
\beta
, and this property is preserved throughout the entire algorithm. Unlike the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, in a
short-path
query, we are now given a single vertex
x\in V(G)
, and we need to return a path
P
in the current graph
G
that connects
x
to any vertex of
\beta
, such that no inner vertices of
P
lie in
\beta
. The last endpoint of the path
P
is then deleted from
\beta
, and the lengths of some edges of the path
P
may be doubled, like in the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem. Like in the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, the algorithm may, at any time, return a sparse strongly well-structured cut
(X,Y)
in
G
, but now we require that
w(X)\geq 1.8w(\beta\cap X)=1.8|\beta\cap X|
, that is, much of the weight of the vertices in
X
must come from vertices that do not lie in
\beta
. The vertices of
X
, and all endpoints of the edges in
E_{G}(X,Y)
are then deleted from
G
, and the algorithm continues.
We are now ready to define the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem formally.

Definition 4.8
.

The input to the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem consists of a perfect well-structured graph
G=(L,R,E)
with a proper assignment of lengths
\ell(e)
to its edges
e\in E
, that are given in the adjacency-list representation, such that the length of every special edge is
1
. Additionally, we are given a subset
\beta\subseteq V(G)
of vertices with
