|\beta|\geq\frac{99|V(G)|}{100}
, and parameters
N\geq W^{0}(G)
,
1\leq\Delta\leq\frac{|\beta|}{(\log N)^{128}}
,
1\leq\eta\leq\Delta
, and
d\geq(\log N)^{64}
, such that
\frac{\Delta\cdot d}{\eta}\leq|V(G)|-|\beta|+\Delta\cdot(\log N)^{64}
, and
N
is greater than a sufficiently large constant. Over the course of the algorithm, vertices may be deleted from set
\beta
, and the lengths of some special edges may be increased, but the length of every special edge incident to a vertex that currently lies in
\beta
is always
1
.

The algorithm consists of at most
\Delta
iterations.
At the beginning of every iteration
i
, the algorithm is given a vertex
x\in V(G)
, and it must return a simple path
P_{i}
in
G
connecting
x
to any vertex
y\in\beta
, such that the length of the path is at most
d
, and no inner vertices on the path lie in
\beta
(if
x\in\beta
, that we require that path
P_{i}
only consists of the vertex
x
). After that, the length of some special edges on path
P_{i}
may be doubled, and vertex
y
is deleted from
\beta
. We are guaranteed that Property
P1
from the definition of the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem holds.

The algorithm may, at any time, produce a strongly well-structured cut
(X,Y)
in
G
of sparsity
\Phi_{G}(X,Y)\leq\frac{(\log N)^{64}}{d}
, such that
w(X)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap X|
holds. Let
J^{\prime}\subseteq Y
denote the set of vertices that serve as endpoints of the edges of
E_{G}(X,Y)
. The vertices of
X\cup J^{\prime}
are then deleted from
G
, and the algorithm continues with the resulting graph
G=G[Y\setminus J^{\prime}]
. Note that we are guaranteed that
G
remains a perfect well-structured graph after this modification.

The algorithm may also, at any time, return “FAIL”. The probability that the algorithm ever returns “FAIL” must be bounded by
1/4
. Lastly, if the number of vertices in
\beta
decreases by a factor
2
, the algorithm terminates.

For an integer
1\leq r\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
, we say that an instance
(G,\beta,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
of the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem is
r
-restricted, if
d\leq 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N}}
.

We prove the following theorem that provides an algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem.

Theorem 4.9
.

There is a randomized algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem, that, on an instance
(G,\beta,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
of the problem, has running time at most:

O\left(W^{0}(G)\cdot(W^{0}(G)-|\beta|+\Delta)\cdot 2^{O(\sqrt{\log N}\cdot\log\log N)}\right).

In order to prove Theorems
4.7
and
4.9
, we use induction. Specifically, we will consider
r
-restricted instances of both problems problem, from smaller to larger values of
r
. The following theorem summarizes our main result for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
and
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problems. It uses the large enough constants
c_{1}\gg c_{2}
that also appear in the statment of
Theorem

3.9
.

Theorem 4.10
.

For all
r\geq 1
, there is a randomized algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, that, on an instance
(G,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
of the problem, has running time at most:

c_{1}\cdot(W^{0}(G))^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r}+c_{1}W^{0}(G)\cdot\Delta\cdot\log^{4}N,

and there is a randomized algorithm for the the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem, that, on an instance
(G,\beta,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
of the problem, has running time at most:

c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(G)\cdot(W^{0}(G)-|\beta|+\Delta)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r}.

Note that the proof of
Theorem

4.7
easily follows from
Theorem

4.10
. Indeed, consider any instance
(G,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
of the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem. From the definition of the problem,
d\leq\frac{n\eta}{\Delta}\leq n\leq N
must hold, and so in particular
d\leq 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N}}
, for
r=\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
. Therefore, any instance of the problem is
r
-restricted for
r=\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
. By applying
Theorem

4.10
with
r=\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
, we obtain an algorithm for the given instance of the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, whose running time is bounded by:

\begin{split}&O\left(W^{0}(G)\cdot(W^{0}(G)+\Delta)\cdot 2^{O(\sqrt{\log N})}\cdot(\log N)^{O(\sqrt{\log N})}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left(W^{0}(G)\cdot(W^{0}(G)+\Delta)\cdot 2^{O(\sqrt{\log N}\cdot\log\log N)}\right),\end{split}

as required.

Similarly, the proof of
Theorem

4.9
easily follows from
Theorem

4.10
. Indeed, consider any instance
(G,\beta,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
of the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem, and denote
n=|V(G)|
. As before, from the definition of the problem,
d\leq\frac{n\eta}{\Delta}\leq n\leq N
must hold, and so in particular
d\leq 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N}}
, for
r=\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
. Therefore, any instance of the problem is
r
-restricted for
r=\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
. By applying
Theorem

4.10
with
r=\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
, we obtain an algorithm for the given instance of the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem, whose running time is bounded by:

\begin{split}O\left(W^{0}(G)\cdot(W^{0}(G)-|\beta|+\Delta)\cdot 2^{O(\sqrt{\log N})}\cdot(\log N)^{O(\sqrt{\log N})}\right)&\\
\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left(W^{0}(G)\cdot(W^{0}(G)-|\beta|+\Delta)\cdot 2^{O(\sqrt{\log N}\cdot\log\log N)}\right).\end{split}

It now remains to complete the proofs of Theorems
3.9
and
4.10
.

4.4
Completing the Proofs of
Theorem

3.9
and
Theorem

4.10

We prove the two theorems together, by induction on
r
. Specifically, we first show that, given an efficient algorithms for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
and
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problems, we can obtain an efficient algorithm for the
(r+1)
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem. This result is summarized in the following theorem, whose proof is deferred to
Section

5
.

Theorem 4.11
.

Suppose that, for some parameter
r\geq 1
,
there exists a randomized algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, that, given as input an instance

(G,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
of the problem, has running time at most:

c_{1}\cdot(W^{0}(G))^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r}+c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(G)\cdot\Delta\cdot\log^{4}N
.
Assume further that there also exists a randomized algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem, that, on an instance

(G,\beta,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
of the problem, has running time at most:

c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(G)\cdot(W^{0}(G)-|\beta|+\Delta)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r}
. Then there is a randomized algorithm for the
(r+1)
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, that, given as input
an instance
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of the problem with
|V(G)|=n
,
has running time at most
c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+8c_{2}}
.

Next, we show that an efficient algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem implies an efficient algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
and
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problems, in the following theorem, whose proof is deferred to Sections
6
and
7
.

Theorem 4.12
.

Suppose that, for some parameter
r\geq 1
, there exists a randomized algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, that, given as an input an instance
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of the problem with
|V(G)|=n
,
has running time at most
c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}}
. Then there exists a randomized algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, that, on input
(G,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
has running time at most:
c_{1}\cdot(W^{0}(G))^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r}+c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(G)\cdot\Delta\cdot\log^{4}N
, and there exists a randomized algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem, that, on input
(G,\beta,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
has running time at most:
c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(G)\cdot(W^{0}(G)-|\beta|+\Delta)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r}
.

The majority of the remainder of this paper is dedicated to the proofs of

Theorem

4.11
and
Theorem

4.12
. The proofs of
Theorem

3.9
and
Theorem

4.10
easily follow from these theorems, as we show next.

Recall that, from
Corollary

3.21
, there is an algorithm for the
1
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, that, given as input an instance
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of the problem with
|V(G)|=n
, has running time at most
O\left(n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot\log^{6}N\right)\leq c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{8c_{2}}
, since we can assume that
c_{1}
is a large enough constant. This proves the induction base for the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem. From
Theorem

4.12
we then conclude that there exists a randomized algorithm for the
1
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, that, on input
(G,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
has running time at most:

c_{1}\cdot(W^{0}(G))^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}}+c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(G)\cdot\Delta\cdot\log^{4}N
, and that there is the
1
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem, that, on input
(G,\beta,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
has running time at most:

c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(G)\cdot(W^{0}(G)-|\beta|+\Delta)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}}
.
This proves the induction base for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
and the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problems.

For the induction step, consider an integer
r\geq 1
, and assume that both
Theorem

3.9
and
Theorem

4.10
hold for
r
-restricted instances. Then from

Theorem

4.11
, there is a randomized algorithm for the
(r+1)
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, that, given as input
an instance
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of the problem with
|V(G)|=n
,
has running time at most
c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+8c_{2}}
, proving
Theorem

3.9
for
(r+1)
-restricted instances. By applying

Theorem

4.12
with integer
(r+1)
, we then conclude that there exists a randomized algorithm for the
(r+1)
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, that, on input
(G,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
has running time at most:

c_{1}\cdot(W^{0}(G))^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r+1)}+c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(G)\cdot\Delta\cdot\log^{4}N
,
and that there exists a randomized algorithm for the
(r+1)
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem, that, on input
(G,\beta,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
has running time at most:

c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(G)\cdot(W^{0}(G)-|\beta|+\Delta)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r+1)}
.
This proves
proving
Theorem

4.10
for
(r+1)
-restricted instances of
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
and
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
.
This completes the proofs of Theorems
3.9
and
4.10
from Theorems

4.11
and
4.12
. In the next three sections we prove

Theorem

4.11
and
Theorem

4.12
.

5
From
r
-Restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
and
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
to
(r+1)
-Restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
: Proof of
Theorem

4.11

The proof of
Theorem

4.11
follows from the following theorem:

Theorem 5.1
.

Suppose that, for some parameter
r\geq 1
, there exists a randomized algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, that, given as input an instance

(G,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
of the problem, has running time at most:

c_{1}\cdot(W^{0}(G))^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r}+c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot\Delta\cdot\log^{4}N
.
Suppose further that there exists a randomized algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem, that, given as input an instance

(G,\beta,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
of the problem, has running time at most:

c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(G)\cdot(W^{0}(G)-|\beta|+\Delta)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r}
. Then there is a randomized algorithm for the
(r+1)
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem, that, given an instance

(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of the problem with
|V(G)|=n
, has running running time at most
c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+7c_{2}}
.

Before we provide the proof of
Theorem

5.1
, we show that the proof of
Theorem

4.11
follows from it.

Proof of
Theorem

4.11
:

Consider a parameter
r\geq 1
, and assume that there exists a randomized algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, that, on input
(G,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
, has running time at most:

c_{1}\cdot(W^{0}(G))^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r}+c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot\Delta\cdot\log^{4}N
.
Assume further that there exists a randomized algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem, that, on input
(G,\beta,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
, has running time at most:

c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(G)\cdot(W^{0}(G)-|\beta|+\Delta)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r}
. From
Theorem

5.1
, there exists a randomized algorithm for the
(r+1)
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem, that, on input
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
with
|V(G)|=n
, has running running time at most
c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+7c_{2}}
. From
Theorem

3.19
, there exists a randomized algorithm for the
(r+1)
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, that, on input
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta)
with
|V(G)|=n
, has running time is at most:

\begin{split}&O\left(c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+7c_{2}}\right)+O(n\cdot(n-|B|))\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+8c_{2}},\end{split}

since
c_{2}
is a sufficiently large constant.

From now on we focus on the proof of
Theorem

5.1
. We denote the randomized algorithms for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
and
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problems from the theorem statement by
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
, respectively.

We denote the input to the
(r+1)
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem by
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
, where
G=(L,R,E)
is an
n
-vertex well-structured graph,
A
and
B
are two disjoint subsets of vertices of
G
, and
N\geq n
,
\Delta\geq 1
, and
1\leq\eta\leq\Delta
are parameters. Recall that no regular edge of
G
connects a vertex of
A
to a vertex of
B
,
\Delta\leq n-|B|
,
|E|\leq O(n\cdot(n-|B|))
, and
\frac{(n-|B|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\leq 2\cdot 2^{(r+1)\sqrt{\log N}}
hold.

Recall that, as the algorithm progresses, we maintain lengths
\ell(e)
for edges
e\in E(G)
, and, additionally, some vertices may leave the sets
A
and
B
. Since the number of iterations is bounded by
\Delta
, at most
\Delta
vertices may be deleted from
A
and from
B
.
For convenience, we denote by
A^{0}
and
B^{0}
the initial sets
A
and
B
, respectively, to distinguish them from the sets that are maintained during the algorithm.

Note that we can assume that
N
is greater than a sufficiently large constant, so that, for example,
N>2^{2^{c_{2}}}
holds. Indeed, if this is not the case, then, from
Theorem

3.20
, we obtain a determinisic algorithm that solves the input instance of
s
-
t
-
SP
in time:

\begin{split}O\left(n\cdot(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\cdot\log^{6}N\right)&\leq O\left(n\cdot(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot N\cdot\log^{6}N\right)\\
&\leq c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+7c_{2}},\end{split}

since
n\leq N\ll c_{1}
, and
\eta\leq\Delta
(recall that, by definition,
c_{1}\gg c_{2}
holds). Therefore, we assume from now on that
N
is sufficiently large.

Additionally, we can assume that
\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}>2^{4\sqrt{\log N}}
, since otherwise, from
Theorem

3.20
, we obtain a determinisic algorithm that solves the input instance of
s
-
t
-
SP
in time:

\begin{split}O\left(n\cdot(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\cdot\log^{6}N\right)&\leq O\left(n\cdot(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot 2^{4\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot\log^{6}N\right)\\
&\leq c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+7c_{2}}.\end{split}

Therefore, we assume from now on that:

\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}>2^{4\sqrt{\log N}}

(2)

Throughout the algorithm, we use the parameter
\Lambda=(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\frac{\eta\cdot\log^{5}n}{\Delta}
.
We let
H
be the graph that is obtained from
G
, by adding a source vertex
s
, that connects to every vertex
a\in A
with a length-
0
edge, and a destination vertex
t
, to which every vertex
b\in B
connects with a length-
0
edge. If an edge
e\in E(G)
is a special edge for graph
G
, then
e
remains a special edge for
H
. All other edges of
H
, including edges incident to
s
and
t
, are called regular edges.
For every edge
e\in E(G)
, its length in
H
remains the same as in
G
, so for convenience, we denote the length of every edge
e\in E(H)
by
\ell(e)
.

At the beginning of the algorithm, for each edge
e\in E(H)
, its length
\ell(e)
is set as follows: if
e
is a regular edge, then
\ell(e)=0
, and if
e
is a special edge, then
\ell(e)=1
.
The algorithm proceeds as follows. We start by setting
{\mathcal{Q}}=\emptyset
, and then perform at most
\Delta
iterations. In every iteration, the algorithm must do one of the following:

R1.

either return FAIL;

R2.

or return a simple
s
-
t
path
P
of length at most
\Lambda
in the current graph
H
, such that
P
contains exactly one vertex of
B
– the penultimate vertex on the path.

R3.

or compute an assignment
\ell^{\prime}(e)\geq 0
of lengths to all edges
e\in E(H)
, such that, if we denote by
\tilde{E}
the set of all special edges of
G
with both endpoints in
B
, then
\sum_{e\in E(H)}\ell^{\prime}(e)\leq\frac{\Lambda\cdot\Delta}{\eta\log^{4}n}+\sum_{e\in E(H)\setminus\tilde{E}}\ell(e)
; the length
\ell^{\prime}(e)
of every edge
e
that is incident to
s
or to
t
is
0
; and the distance from
s
to
t
in
H
, with respect to edge lengths
\ell^{\prime}(\cdot)
, is at least
\frac{\Lambda}{32}
;

Note that, if the algorithm returns assignments of lengths
\ell^{\prime}(e)
to edges
e\in E(H)
as in Outcome
R3
, then these assignments of edge lengths define a modified cut-witness for
G
(see
Definition

3.18
). In this case, the algorithm terminates, and returns the lengths
\ell^{\prime}(e)
of all edges
e\in E(H\setminus\left\{s,t\right\})
. Slightly abusing the notation, for the sake of simplicity, we will call an assignment of lengths
\ell^{\prime}(e)
to the edges
e\in E(H)
as described in Outcome
R3
a
cut-witness
.

If the algorithm returns an
s
-
t
path
P
as in Outcome
R2
, then
P\setminus\left\{s,t\right\}
is a modified acceptable path for
G
(see
Definition

3.17
). To simplify the notation, we say that a path
P
described in Outcome
R2
is an
acceptable path
. If the algorithm returns an acceptable path
P
, then we let
P^{\prime}=P\setminus\left\{s,t\right\}
, and we let
a\in A
,
b\in B
be the endpoints of path
P^{\prime}
. We remove
a
from
A
and
b
from
B
, add
P^{\prime}
to
{\mathcal{Q}}
, and delete the edges
(s,a)
and
(b,t)
from
H
. Additionally, the lengths of some of the special edges on
P
may be doubled; we assume that the algorithm receives the list of all such edges.

From the problem definition, for every special edge
e\in E(H)
, the length of
e
is doubled once
e
participates in exactly
\eta
paths that the algorithm returned since the last time the length of
e
was doubled, or since the beginning of the algorithm – whatever happened last.

Lastly, in the case of Outcome
R1
, we terminate the algorithm and return “FAIL”, but we require that the probability that the algorithm ever returns “FAIL” is bounded by
\frac{1}{2}
.

Some Useful Bounds

We denote by
E^{\mathsf{spec}}
the set of all special edges in graph
G
, and we denote by
E^{*}\subseteq E^{\mathsf{spec}}
the set of all special edges
e=(u,v)
, such that
u,v\in B
holds at the end of the algorithm. Recall that the length of a special edge
e
may only be increased once it participates in some path that the algorithm returns, and, if a vertex
x\in B
lies on any path that the algorithm returns, then
x
is deleted from
B
. Therefore, every edge
e\in E^{*}
has length
1
throughout the algorithm.
We start by bounding
|E^{\mathsf{spec}}\setminus E^{*}|
in the following simple observation.

Observation 5.2
.

|E^{\mathsf{spec}}\setminus E^{*}|\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)
.

Proof.

We partition the set
E^{\mathsf{spec}}\setminus E^{*}
if edges into two subsets: set
E_{1}
contains all edges
e
, such that at least one endpoint of
e
lies in
V(G)\setminus B^{0}
, and set
E_{2}
contains all remaining edges of
E^{\mathsf{spec}}\setminus E^{*}
. Since every edge of
E_{1}
is incident to a vertex of
V(G)\setminus B^{0}
, and every vertex of
G
may serve as an endpoint of at most one special edge,
|E_{1}|\leq n-|B^{0}|
must hold. Consider now some edge
e\in E_{2}
. From the definition, over the course of the algorithm, at least one endpoint of
e
was deleted from
B
. Since at most
\Delta
vertices are deleted from
B
over the course of the algorithm, and since each such vertex may serve as an endpoint of at most one special edge, we get that
|E_{2}|\leq\Delta\leq n-|B^{0}|
, from the definition of the
s
-
t
-
SP
problem. Altogether, we get that

|E^{\mathsf{spec}}\setminus E^{*}|\leq|E_{1}|+|E_{2}|\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)
.

For each edge
e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}
, let
\ell^{*}(e)
be the length of
e
at the end of the algorithm. In the next simple observation we bound
\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}}\ell^{*}(e)
.

Observation 5.3
.

\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}}\ell^{*}(e)\leq|E^{\mathsf{spec}}|+2(n-|B^{0}|)\log^{5}n
, and
\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}\setminus E^{*}}\ell^{*}(e)\leq 4(n-|B^{0}|)\log^{5}n
.

Proof.

Recall that, at the beginning of the algorithm, for every edge
e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}
, we set
\ell(e)=1
. Let
E^{\prime}\subseteq E^{\mathsf{spec}}
be the set of all edges
e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}
with
\ell^{*}(e)>1
. Clearly,
\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}}\ell^{*}(e)\leq|E^{\mathsf{spec}}|+\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}}\ell^{*}(e)
.

Let
q
be the total number of iterations of the algorithm, so that, at the end of the algorithm,
|{\mathcal{Q}}|=q
holds, and let
{\mathcal{Q}}=\left\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{q}\right\}
, where the paths are indexed in the order in which they were added to
{\mathcal{Q}}
. For all
1\leq i\leq q
, let
C_{i}
denote the length of path
P_{i}
when it was added to
{\mathcal{Q}}
. Clearly,
C_{i}\leq\Lambda
, and
\sum_{i=1}^{q}C_{i}\leq q\cdot\Lambda\leq\Delta\cdot\Lambda
.

Consider now some edge
e\in E^{\prime}
, and recall that
\ell(e)
was doubled at least once during the algorithm. Let
\tau
be the last time when the length of
e
was doubled. Then, prior to time
\tau
, there were at least
\eta
iterations
i
, during which the path
P_{i}
that was added to
{\mathcal{Q}}
contained
e
, and the length of
e
during the corresponding iteration was
\frac{\ell^{*}(e)}{2}
. In other words, edge
e
contributes at least
\frac{\ell^{*}(e)\eta}{2}
to
\sum_{i=1}^{q}C_{i}
. Altogether, we get that
\sum_{i=1}^{q}C_{i}\geq\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}}\frac{\ell^{*}(e)\eta}{2}
. Therefore:

\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}}\ell^{*}(e)\leq\frac{2\sum_{i=1}^{q}C_{i}}{\eta}\leq\frac{2\Delta\Lambda}{\eta}\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\log^{5}n,

since
\Lambda=(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\frac{\eta\cdot\log^{5}n}{\Delta}
.
Altogether, we get that:

\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}}\ell^{*}(e)\leq|E^{\mathsf{spec}}|+\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}}\ell^{*}(e)\leq|E^{\mathsf{spec}}|+2(n-|B^{0}|)\log^{5}n.

Lastly, from
Observation

5.2
,

|E^{\mathsf{spec}}|-|E^{*}|\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)
, and so:

\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}\setminus E^{*}}\ell^{*}(e)\leq\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}}\ell^{*}(e)-|E^{*}|\leq|E^{\mathsf{spec}}|+2(n-|B^{0}|)\log^{5}n-|E^{*}|\leq 4(n-|B^{0}|)\log^{5}n.

Throughout the algorithm, we use a parameter
N^{\prime}=\max\left\{N,8n\cdot\log^{5}n\right\}
. Let
\tau
be any time in the algorithm’s execution, and let
H^{\prime}
be any subgraph of
H\setminus\left\{s,t\right\}
, that is a perfect well-structured graph. Recall that the weight of every vertex
v\in V(H^{\prime})
is the length of the unique special edge incident to
v
. From
Observation

5.3
, and since
|E^{\mathsf{spec}}|\leq n
, the total weight of all vertices of
H^{\prime}
must be bounded by
N^{\prime}
. Notice also that, since
N\geq n
, we get that
N^{\prime}\leq 8N\log^{5}n\leq N\log^{6}N
. It is then easy to see that:

\log N^{\prime}\leq\log N+6\log\log N\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{64c_{2}r}\right)\log N,

(3)

since
r\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
and
N
is large enough. Additionally,
\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}\leq\sqrt{\log N+6\log\log N}\leq\sqrt{\log N}+\sqrt{6\log\log N}
. Therefore:

2^{\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}\leq 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot\log^{6}N\leq 2^{2\sqrt{\log N}}.

(4)

We will use these facts later.

Algorithm for the
(r+1)
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem:

The description of our algorithm for the
(r+1)
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem consists of two parts. In the first part, we provide an algorithm for maintaining an
\mathcal{ATO}
for graph
H
. This algorithm in turn exploits algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem and Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem. In the second part, we use the algorithm from
Theorem

2.7
for the
DAG-like SSSP
problem on the corresponding contracted graph.

5.1
Part 1 - Maintaining the
\mathcal{ATO}

In this part we describe our algorithm for maintaining the
\mathcal{ATO}

({\mathcal{X}},\rho,U,\gamma)
for graph
H
. Notice that
|V(H)|=|V(G)|+2=n+2
.
Throughout, we use a parameter
{d=\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\eta}{4\Delta\cdot 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}}}
.
Notice that:

2^{2\sqrt{\log N}}\leq d\leq 2^{r\sqrt{\log N}-1}.

(5)

Here, the first inequality follows from Inequality
2
.
The second inequality follows since the input instance of
s
-
t
-
SP
is
(r+1)
-restricted, so
\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\leq 2\cdot 2^{(r+1)\sqrt{\log N}}
holds.

Let
U\subseteq V(H)\setminus\left\{s,t\right\}
be the collection of all vertices
v
, such that some special edge of
E^{\mathsf{spec}}
is incident to
v
, and let
U^{\prime}=V(H)\setminus U
. Notice that
H[U]
is a perfect well-structured graph (see
Definition

3.1
).
We let the initial collection
{\mathcal{X}}
of vertex subsets contain the set
U
, and, for every vertex
v\in U^{\prime}
, a set
X_{v}=\left\{v\right\}
. For convenience, we denote
S=\left\{s\right\}
and
T=\left\{t\right\}
.
We distinguish between two cases. Case 1 happens when
|U|>2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
. In this case, we let
U
be the special cluster of the
\mathcal{ATO}
, and we set
\gamma=|U|-256(n-|B^{0}|)(\log N)^{64}
.
Otherwise, Case 2 happens, the special cluster of the
\mathcal{ATO}
is undefined, and we set
\gamma=1
.

Next, we assign, to every resulting set
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
, an interval
I_{X}\subseteq\left\{1,\ldots,|V(H)|-\gamma+1\right\}
, so that, for each regular cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
,
|I_{X}|=|X|
, and, if the special cluster is defined, then
|I_{U}|=|U|-\gamma+1
. In order to do so, it is sufficient to define an ordering
\sigma
of the sets in
{\mathcal{X}}
. We let
S
appear first in the ordering, followed by the sets in
\left\{X_{v}\mid v\in U^{\prime}\cap L\right\}
in an arbitrary order, followed by set
U
, which is followed by the sets in
\left\{X_{v}\mid v\in U^{\prime}\cap R\right\}
in an arbitrary order, followed by
T
. Since vertices of
U^{\prime}
are not incident to special edges, it is easy to verify that no edge of
H
is a right-to-left edge with respect to this initial
\mathcal{ATO}
.
Throughout the algorithm, we refer to each set
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
of vertices as a
cluster
. If
|X|=1
, then we say that
X
is a
singleton
cluster.
As the algorithm progresses, cluster
U
may be partitioned into smaller and smaller clusters, with each such cluster assigned an interval that is contained in the initial interval
I_{U}
, but the clusters in
\left\{X_{v}\mid v\in U^{\prime}\right\}
, together with their corresponding intervals
I_{X_{v}}
, remain unchanged. For convenience, we denote the initial set
U
by
U^{0}
.
We will ensure that, throughout the algorithm, the following invariants hold:

I1.

If
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
is a non-singleton cluster, then
H[X]
is a perfect well-structured graph;

I2.

If the special cluster is defined, then
U\in{\mathcal{X}}
holds throughout the algorithm, and moreover,
|U|\geq\gamma
always holds; and

I3.

If
e=(x,y)
is a right-to-left edge with respect to the current
\mathcal{ATO}
, and
X,Y\in{\mathcal{X}}
are the clusters that contain
x
and
y
, respectively, so that
Y
appears before
X
in the ordering
\sigma
of clusters of
{\mathcal{X}}
defined by the
\mathcal{ATO}
, then
e
must be a special edge, and moreover,
X=\left\{x\right\}
and
Y=\left\{y\right\}
must hold; in other words,
X
and
Y
are both singleton clusters.

It is easy to verify that the invariant holds for the initial
\mathcal{ATO}
.

For each cluster
X
that is ever added to
{\mathcal{X}}
, we denote by
X^{0}
the set
X
when it was just added to
{\mathcal{X}}
. If
|X^{0}|>1
(that is,
X
is not a singleton cluster when it is added to
{\mathcal{X}}
), then, from Invariant
I1
,
H[X]
is a perfect well-structured graph, so weights of vertices of
X
are well defined. In such a case, we denote by
W^{0}(X)
the total weight of all vertices of
X
when
X
is added to
{\mathcal{X}}
. Whenever a non-singleton regular cluster
X
is added to set
{\mathcal{X}}
, we define the parameter
{d_{X}=\min\left\{\frac{|X^{0}|\cdot\eta}{\Delta},d\right\}}
. We may sometimes refer to
d_{X}
as
d_{X^{0}}
.
Next, we partition all clusters that are ever added to
{\mathcal{X}}
into
leaf
and
non-leaf
clusters.

Definition 5.4
(Leaf and Non-Leaf Clusters).

Let
X
be a cluster that was just added to
{\mathcal{X}}
. We say that
X
is a
leaf cluster
, if
|X^{0}|\leq\frac{\Delta\cdot(\log N)^{128}}{\eta}
.
If
X
is not a leaf cluster, then we call it a
non-leaf
cluster.

Recall that, if
U
is a special cluster, then
|U^{0}|>2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
. Since
\Delta\leq n-|B^{0}|
from the definition of the
s
-
t
-
SP
problem, we get that a special cluster may not be a leaf cluster.

Recall that in general, once a cluster
X
is added to the collection
{\mathcal{X}}
of clusters of an
\mathcal{ATO}
, it must remain in
{\mathcal{X}}
throughout the algorithm, though we may delete some vertices from
X
. A new cluster
Y
may only be added to the set
{\mathcal{X}}
if it is a subset of some cluster
X
that currently lies in
{\mathcal{X}}
. When we describe our algorithm for maintaining the
\mathcal{ATO}
, it will be convenient for us to sometimes remove a cluster
X
from the set
{\mathcal{X}}
, and then immediately reinsert it into
{\mathcal{X}}
. This is only done for clarity of exposition and convenience of analysis, and we do not need to do so in order to maintain the
\mathcal{ATO}
. Additionally, we will sometimes replace a cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
with a new collection of clusters, each of which contains a single vertex of
X
. In such a case we could simply identify
X
with one of the new clusters and implement this update to
{\mathcal{X}}
via a sequence of cluster splitting operations, though for clarity of exposition we will treat all newly inserted clusters as new clusters, and we will think of
X
as being removed from
{\mathcal{X}}
.

Next, we describe an algorithm for maintaining each cluster
X
that is added to
{\mathcal{X}}
. We employ different algorithms for leaf clusters, non-leaf regular clusters, and the special cluster, if it is defined. We now describe each of the three algorithms in turn.

5.1.1
Leaf Clusters

Let
X
be a leaf cluster that was just added to
{\mathcal{X}}
. If
X
is a singleton cluster, then no further processing of this cluster is needed. Assume now that
X
is a non-singleton cluster.
We immediately remove
X
from
{\mathcal{X}}
, and instead add, for every vertex
v\in X
, a new singleton cluster
\left\{v\right\}
to
{\mathcal{X}}
. We assign, to each such new cluster
\left\{v\right\}
, an interval
I_{\left\{v\right\}}\subseteq I_{X}
of length
1
, so that all intervals corresponding to the vertices of
X\cap L
appear before all intervals corresponding to the vertices of
X\cap R
. Notice that the only new right-to-left edges that are introduced by this transformation are the edges of
E_{H}(X\cap R,X\cap L)
, which are all special edges. The number of such edges is
|X\cap R|=|X\cap L|\leq|X|
. We say that these new right-to-left edges are
bad edges
, and that they are
owned
by the leaf cluster
X
. We denote the set of these edges by
E^{\mathsf{bad}}(X)
. From the above discussion,
|E^{\mathsf{bad}}(X)|\leq|X|
, and each edge
e\in E^{\mathsf{bad}}(X)
has
\operatorname{span}(e)\leq|X|
.

5.1.2
Non-Leaf Regular Clusters

Whenever a new non-leaf regular cluster
X
is added to
{\mathcal{X}}
, we initialize Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem on graph
H[X^{0}]
, with parameters
\Delta
and
\eta
that remain unchanged, parameter
d
replaced with
d_{X}
, and parameter
N
replaced with
N^{\prime}
. We denote this instantiation of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
by
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
. We now verify that
(H[X^{0}],\Delta,\eta,d_{X},N^{\prime})
is indeed a valid input instance for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, in the following simple but technical observation, whose proof is deferred to Section
B.1
of Appendix.

Observation 5.5
.

(H[X^{0}],\Delta,\eta,d_{X},N^{\prime})
is a valid input to the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem.

Recall that Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
may, at any time,
produce a strongly well-structured cut
(Y,Z)
in
H[X]
of sparsity
\Phi_{H[X]}(Y,Z)\leq\frac{(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{d_{X}}
.
Below, we provide an algorithm for processing such a cut.
But before we do so, we provide the remaining details for processing cluster
X
.

Recall that the algorithm for the
s
-
t
-
SP
problem consists of at most
\Delta
iterations. At the beginning of every iteration
i
, we will compute, for every non-leaf regular cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
, a pair
x,y\in V(X)
of vertices (where possibly
x=y
). The corresponding algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
is then required to compute a simple path
P_{X}
connecting
x
to
y
in graph
H[X]
, whose length is at most
d_{X}
. We will then process the resulting paths
P_{X}
for all non-leaf clusters
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
, in order to obtain a short
s
-
t
path
P_{i}
in
H
, as required. For each non-leaf cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
, we will ensure that
E(P_{i})\cap E(H[X])\subseteq E(P_{X})
. Following that, the lengths of some special edges on path
P_{i}
may be doubled; we assume that our algorithm is given the list of all such edges. In particular, for each non-leaf regular cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
, we are given a subset of the special edges on path
P_{X}
, whose lengths must be doubled.
From the definition of the
s
-
t
-
SP
problem, we are guaranteed that, for every non-leaf regular cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
, for every special edge
e\in E(H[X])
, if
\tau^{\prime}>\tau
are two times at which the length of
e
is doubled, then the number of paths
P_{X}
that Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
returned in response to queries during the time interval
(\tau,\tau^{\prime}]
, which contain
e
, is at least
\eta
, so Property
P1
holds for the instance of the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem associated with
X
. If Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
ever returns “FAIL”, then we terminate our algorithm for the
s
-
t
-
SP
problem, and return “FAIL” as well.

Lastly, whenever, for a non-leaf regular cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
,
|X|
falls below
\frac{|X^{0}|}{2}
, we terminate Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
, and we say that cluster
X
is
destroyed
. We then think of the resulting set
X
of vertices as a new cluster that was just added to
{\mathcal{X}}
, and treat it as such (so we think that, once cluster
X
is destroyed, it is deleted from
{\mathcal{X}}
, and instead a new cluster, that is identical to
X
, is inserted into
{\mathcal{X}}
).
It now remains to describe the algorithm to process sparse cuts produced by Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
for non-leaf regular clusters
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
.

Processing a Cut
(Y,Z)
in Cluster
X

We assume that we are given a non-leaf regular cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
, and a strongly well-structured cut
(Y,Z)
in
H[X]
of sparsity
\Phi_{H[X]}(Y,Z)\leq\frac{(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{d_{X}}
.
We denote by
J\subseteq Y
and
J^{\prime}\subseteq Z
the sets of vertices that serve as endpoints of the edges of
E_{H}(Y,Z)
. Since the edges of
E_{H}(Y,Z)
are special edges, it must be the case that
J\subseteq R
and
J^{\prime}\subseteq L
. Denote
Y^{\prime}=Y\setminus J
and
Z^{\prime}=Z\setminus J^{\prime}
.
We start with the following simple observation, whose proof is deferred to Section
B.2
of Appendix.

Observation 5.6
.

Each of the graphs
H[Y^{\prime}],H[Z^{\prime}]
is a perfect well-structured graph.

Assume first that
w(Y)\leq w(Z)
.
We update the set
X
of vertices, by deleting the vertices of
Y\cup J^{\prime}
from it; in other words, we set
X=Z^{\prime}
. We then add
Y^{\prime}
as a new cluster to
{\mathcal{X}}
, and additionally, for every vertex
v\in J\cup J^{\prime}
, we add a new singleton cluster
\left\{v\right\}
to
{\mathcal{X}}
. We define the intervals associated with the new clusters, as follows. We assign, to every vertex
v\in J^{\prime}\cup J
an interval
I_{\left\{v\right\}}\subseteq I_{X}
of length
1
, and additionally we define two intervals:
I_{Y^{\prime}}\subseteq I_{X}
of length
|Y^{\prime}|
and
I_{Z^{\prime}}\subseteq I_{X}
of length
|Z^{\prime}|
, so that all resulting intervals
I_{Y^{\prime}},I_{Z^{\prime}},\left\{I_{\left\{v\right\}}\right\}_{v\in J\cup J^{\prime}}
are mutually disjoint, and the intervals are ordered as follows: we place the intervals corresponding to the vertices of
J^{\prime}
first, followed by interval
I_{Z^{\prime}}
, then
I_{Y^{\prime}}
, and lastly the intervals corresponding to the vertices of
J
. Since we have set
X=Z^{\prime}
, we let
I_{X}=I_{Z^{\prime}}
. From
Observation

5.6
, Invariant
I1
continues to hold.
We now show that Invariant
I3
continues to hold, in the following observation, whose proof is deferred to Section
B.3
of Appendix.

Observation 5.7
.

If
e=(u,v)
is a right-to-left edge that was added due to the above transformation of
\mathcal{ATO}
, then
u\in J
and
v\in J^{\prime}
holds, and so
e\in E_{H}(Y,Z)
, and
e
is a special edge.

Since we have defined a separate singleton cluster for every vertex of
J\cup J^{\prime}
, from
Observation

5.7
, Invariant
I3
continues to hold. Moreover, the only right-to-left edges that we have introduced during this update are the edges of
E_{H}(Y,Z)
. Notice that the
\operatorname{span}(e)
value of each such edge is at most
|X|
. We call all edges of
E_{H}(Y,Z)

bad edges
, and we say that they are
owned
by cluster
X
.

Recall that so far we have assumed that
w(Y)\leq w(Z)
. If
w(Y)>w(Z)
, then the algorithm remains the same, except that at the end we update cluster
X
by deleting the vertices of
Z\cup J
from it; or equivalently, we set
X=Y^{\prime}
. We also set
I_{X}=I_{Y^{\prime}}
. This concludes the algorithm for processing the cut
(Y,Z)
of a non-leaf regular cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
.

5.1.3
The Special Cluster

Recall that Case 1 happens if

|U^{0}|>2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
holds. In this case, we let
U
be the special cluster of the
\mathcal{ATO}
, and we set
\gamma=|U^{0}|-256(n-|B^{0}|)(\log N)^{64}
.
We now show an algorithm for processing the special cluster
U
if Case 1 happened. We initialize Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem on graph
H[U^{0}]
, with parameters
\Delta
and
\eta
that remain unchanged, parameter
d
replaced by parameter
d_{U}=\max\left\{(\log N^{\prime})^{64},\left(|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+\Delta\right)\frac{\eta}{4\Delta\cdot 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}}\right\}
, and parameter
N
replaced with
N^{\prime}
. We also initially set
\beta=B^{0}\cap U^{0}
, and we ensure that, throughout the algorithm,
\beta=B\cap U
holds. In other words, whenever a vertex of
\beta
is deleted from
B
or from
U
, it is also deleted from
\beta
.

We denote this instantiation of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
by
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(U)
. We now verify that
(H[U^{0}],\beta,\Delta,\eta,d,N^{\prime})
is indeed a valid input instance for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem, in the following simple observation, whose proof is deferred to Section
B.4
of Appendix.

Observation 5.8
.

(H[U^{0}],\beta,\Delta,\eta,d_{U},N^{\prime})
is a valid input to the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem.

Recall that Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(U)
may, at any time,
produce a strongly well-structured cut
(Y,Z)
in
H[U]
of sparsity
\Phi_{H[U]}(Y,Z)\leq\frac{(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{d_{U}}
, such that
w(Y)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap Y|
holds.
Below, we provide an algorithm for processing such a cut.
But before we do so, we provide the remaining details for processing cluster
U
.
We will show later that, throughout the algorithm,
|\beta|\geq\frac{|B^{0}\cap U^{0}|}{2}
holds.

Recall that the algorithm for the
s
-
t
-
SP
problem consists of at most
\Delta
iterations. At the beginning of every iteration
i
, we may compute a single vertex
x\in U
, that is sent as a query to Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(U)
. Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(U)
is then required to compute a simple path
P(x)
connecting
x
to some vertex
y\in\beta
in graph
H[U]
, whose length is at most
d_{U}
, such that no inner vertex of
P(x)
lies in
\beta
(if
x\in\beta
, then we require that path
P(x)
only consists of the vertex
x
).
Our algorithm will then compute a short
s
-
t
path
P_{i}
in
H
, as required, so that
E(P_{i})\cap E(H[U])\subseteq E(P(x))
. Following that, the lengths of some special edges on path
P_{i}
may be doubled; we assume that our algorithm is given the list of all such edges. In particular, we are given a subset of the special edges on path
P(x)
, whose lengths must be doubled. Additionally, vertex
y
that serves as an endpoint of path
P(x)
is deleted from set
B
and from set
\beta
. This ensures that the lengths of the special edges that are incident to vertices of
\beta
always remain
1
. We note that in some of the iterations the algorithm for the
s
-
t
-
SP
problem may not ask any queries to algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(U)
, and in such iterations the lengths of the special edges of
H[U]
are not doubled.
From the definition of the
s
-
t
-
SP
problem, we are guaranteed that, for every special edge
e\in E(H[U])
, if
\tau^{\prime}>\tau
are two times at which the length of
e
is doubled, then the number of paths
P(x)
that Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(U)
returned in response to queries during the time interval
(\tau,\tau^{\prime}]
, which contain
e
, is at least
\eta
, so Property
P1
holds for the instance of the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem associated with
U
. If Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(U)
ever returns “FAIL”, then we terminate our algorithm for the
s
-
t
-
SP
problem, and return “FAIL” as well.

Finally, we provide an algorithm for processing the sparse cuts that Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(U)
may produce. Suppose Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(U)
returns a strongly well-structured cut
(Y,Z)
in
H[U]
of sparsity
\Phi_{H[U]}(Y,Z)\leq\frac{(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{d_{U}}
, such that
w(Y)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap Y|
holds.
We denote by
J\subseteq Y
and
J^{\prime}\subseteq Z
the sets of vertices that serve as endpoints of the edges of
E_{H}(Y,Z)
, and we let
Y^{\prime}=Y\setminus J
and
Z^{\prime}=Z\setminus J^{\prime}
.
Using the same reasoning as in the proof of
Observation

5.6
,
each of the graphs
H[Y^{\prime}],H[Z^{\prime}]
is a perfect well-structured graph.
We will show below that
|Z^{\prime}|\geq\gamma
must hold. We delete the vertices of
Y\cup J^{\prime}
from
U
, so that
U=Z^{\prime}
now holds, and we add
Y^{\prime}
as a new cluster to
{\mathcal{X}}
. Additionally, for every vertex
v\in J\cup J^{\prime}
, we add a new singleton cluster
\left\{v\right\}
to
{\mathcal{X}}
. We define the intervals associated with the new clusters as follows. We assign, to every vertex
v\in J^{\prime}\cup J
an interval
I_{\left\{v\right\}}\subseteq I_{U}
of length
1
, and additionally we define two intervals:
I_{Y^{\prime}}\subseteq I_{U}
of length
|Y^{\prime}|
and
I_{Z^{\prime}}\subseteq I_{U}
of length
|Z^{\prime}|-\gamma+1
, so that all resulting intervals
I_{Y^{\prime}},I_{Z^{\prime}},\left\{I_{\left\{v\right\}}\right\}_{v\in J\cup J^{\prime}}
are mutually disjoint, and the intervals are ordered as follows: we place the intervals corresponding to the vertices of
J^{\prime}
first, followed by interval
I_{Z^{\prime}}
, then
I_{Y^{\prime}}
, and lastly the intervals corresponding to the vertices of
J
. Since we have set
U=Z^{\prime}
, we let
I_{U}=I_{Z^{\prime}}
. As before, Invariant
I1
continues to hold. Using the same reasoning as in the proof of
Observation

5.7
, if
e=(u,v)
is a right-to-left edge that was added due to the above transformation of
\mathcal{ATO}
, then
u\in J
and
v\in J^{\prime}
holds, and so
e\in E_{H}(Y,Z)
, and
e
is a special edge. In particular, Invariant
I3
continues to hold.
Moreover, the only right-to-left edges that we have introduced during this update are the edges of
E_{H}(Y,Z)
. Notice that the
\operatorname{span}(e)
value of each such edge is at most
|U^{0}|-\gamma+1
. We call all edges of
E_{H}(Y,Z)

bad edges
, and we say that they are
owned
by cluster
U
.
We denote by
E^{\mathsf{bad}}(U)
the set of all bad edges that are owned by cluster
U
over the course of the algorithm. In the following observation we show that
|U|\geq\gamma
holds throughout the algorithm, and we bound
|E^{\mathsf{bad}}(U)|
.

Observation 5.9
.

Over the course of the algorithm, at most
64|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+128\Delta\cdot(\log N)^{64}\leq 256(n-|B^{0}|)(\log N)^{64}
vertices are deleted from
U
, and so throughout the algorithm,
|U|\geq\gamma
and
|\beta|\geq\frac{|U^{0}\cap B^{0}|}{2}
hold. Moreover,
|E^{\mathsf{bad}}(U)|\leq\frac{\Delta\cdot 2^{6\sqrt{\log N}}}{\eta}
.

Proof.

Recall that we denoted by
E^{*}\subseteq E^{\mathsf{spec}}
the set of all special edges
e=(u,v)
, such that
u,v\in B
holds at the end of the algorithm. Let
B^{*}\subseteq B
the set of vertices containing all endpoints of the edges in
E^{*}
, and let
\tilde{U}=U^{0}\setminus B^{*}
. For every vertex
v\in U^{0}
, we define a weight
\tilde{w}(v)
as follows: if, at the end of the algorithm,
v\in U
, then
\tilde{w}(v)
is the weight of
v
at the end of the algorithm; otherwise,
\tilde{w}(v)
is the weight of
v
just before it is deleted from
U
. We use the following observation, whose proof is very similar to the proof of
Observation

5.3
, and deferred to Section
B.5
of Appendix.

Observation 5.10
.

\sum_{v\in\tilde{U}}\tilde{w}(v)\leq 8|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+8\Delta\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{64}
.

Consider now some cut
(Y,Z)
of
U
that Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(U)
produced. Recall that
|E_{H}(Y,Z)|\leq\frac{(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{d_{U}}\cdot w(Y)
, and that
w(Y)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap Y|
holds. Since
B^{*}\cap Y\subseteq\beta\cap Y
, we get that
w(Y)\geq 1.8|B^{*}\cap Y|
. Since the weight of every vertex in
B^{*}
remains
1
throughout the algorithm, we get that
w(Y\setminus B^{*})=w(Y)-|B^{*}\cap Y|\geq w(Y)-\frac{w(Y)}{1.8}\geq 0.44w(Y)
. In particular,
|Y\cap B^{*}|\leq\frac{w(Y)}{1.8}\leq\frac{w(Y\setminus B^{*})}{1.8\cdot 0.44}\leq 1.3w(Y\setminus B^{*})
.

Moreover,
|Y|\leq w(Y)=w(Y\setminus B^{*})+|Y\cap B^{*}|\leq 2.3w(Y\setminus B^{*})
holds. Recall that all vertices of
Y
, and also the endpoints of the special edges in
E_{H}(Y,Z)
are deleted from cluster
U
following the cut
(Y,Z)
. Therefore, the total number of vertices deleted from
U
following the cut
(Y,Z)
is at most
2|Y|\leq 4.6w(Y\setminus B^{*})\leq 4.6\sum_{v\in Y\cap\tilde{U}}\tilde{w}(v)
. Lastly:

|E_{H}(Y,Z)|\leq\frac{(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{d_{U}}\cdot w(Y)\leq\frac{(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{d_{U}}\cdot 2.3w(Y\setminus B^{*})\leq\frac{2.3\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{d_{U}}\cdot\sum_{v\in Y\cap\tilde{U}}\tilde{w}(v).

Consider now all cuts
(Y_{1},Z_{1}),\ldots,(Y_{r},Z_{r})
that Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(U)
ever produced, in the order in which they were produced. For all
1\leq i\leq r
, following the cut
(Y_{i},Z_{i})
, the vertices of
Y_{i}
, along with some additional vertices, were deleted from
U
. From the above discussion, the total number of vertices deleted from
U
following the cut is bounded by
4.6\sum_{v\in Y_{i}\cap\tilde{U}}\tilde{w}(v)
, and
|E_{H}(Y_{i},Z_{i})|\leq\frac{2.3\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{d_{U}}\cdot\sum_{v\in Y\cap\tilde{U}}\cdot\sum_{v\in Y_{i}\cap\tilde{U}}\tilde{w}(v)
. We conclude that the total number of vertices deleted from
U
over the course of the algorithm is bounded by:

\begin{split}4.6\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{v\in Y_{i}\cap\tilde{U}}\tilde{w}(v)&\leq 4.6\sum_{v\in\tilde{U}}\tilde{w}(v)\\
&\leq 64|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+64\Delta\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{64}\\
&\leq 64|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+128\Delta\cdot(\log N)^{64}\\
&\leq 256(n-|B^{0}|)(\log N)^{64},\end{split}

from Inequality
3
.
Since, at the beginning of the algorithm,
|U|>2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
holds, and since

\gamma=|U^{0}|-256(n-|B^{0}|)(\log N)^{64}
, we get that
|U|\geq\gamma
holds throughout the algorithm.

Next, we show that, throughout the algorithm,
|\beta|\geq\frac{|U^{0}\cap B^{0}|}{2}
holds. Recall again that
|U^{0}|>2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
, and that
|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|\leq n-|B^{0}|
. Therefore,
|U^{0}\cap B^{0}|\geq|U^{0}|-|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|\geq(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
. Set
\beta
contains all vertices of
U^{0}\cap B^{0}
, except for the vertices that were deleted from
B
(whose number is bounded by
\Delta\leq n-|B^{0}|
), and the vertices that were deleted from
U
(whose number is bounded by
256(n-|B^{0}|)(\log N)^{64}
). Therefore, throughout the algorithm,
|\beta|\geq|U^{0}\cap B^{0}|-257(n-|B^{0}|)(\log N)^{64}
must hold. Since
|U^{0}\cap B^{0}|\geq(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
, we get that
|\beta|\geq\frac{|U^{0}\cap B^{0}|}{2}
holds throughout the algorithm.

Lastly, we get that:

\begin{split}|E^{\mathsf{bad}}(U)|&\leq\sum_{i=1}^{r}|E_{H}(Y_{i},Z_{i})|\\
&\leq\frac{2.3\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{d_{U}}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{v\in Y\cap\tilde{U}}\tilde{w}(v)\\
&\leq\frac{10\Delta\cdot 2^{2\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}}{\left(|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+\Delta\right)\cdot\eta}\cdot\sum_{v\in\tilde{U}}\tilde{w}(v)\\
&\leq\frac{10\Delta\cdot 2^{2\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}}{\left(|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+\Delta\right)\cdot\eta}\cdot\left(8|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+8\Delta(\log N^{\prime})^{64}\right)\\
&\leq\frac{\Delta\cdot 2^{6\sqrt{\log N}}}{\eta},\end{split}

since
d_{U}=\max\left\{(\log N^{\prime})^{64},\left(|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+\Delta\right)\frac{\eta}{4\Delta\cdot 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}}\right\}\geq\left(|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+\Delta\right)\frac{\eta}{4\Delta\cdot 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}}
; we have also used Inequality
4
.

5.1.4
Child Clusters, Descendant Clusters, and a Useful Bound

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
denote the set of all clusters that ever belonged to
{\mathcal{X}}
over the course of the algorithm. We partition
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
into three subsets: set
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
contains all singleton clusters, set
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
contains all non-singleton leaf clusters, and set
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}
contains all non-leaf clusters. If the special cluster is defined, then we set
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\prime}=\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}\setminus\left\{U\right\}
, and otherwise we let
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\prime}=\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}
.

Let
E^{\mathsf{bad}}
be the set of all edges of
G
that ever served as right-to-left edges of the
\mathcal{ATO}
. For every cluster
X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{1}\cup\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}
, we denote by
E^{\mathsf{bad}}(X)
the set of all bad edges that cluster
X
owns. Recall that, once an edge
e=(x,y)
is added to the set
E^{\mathsf{bad}}(X)
of edges, its initial value
\operatorname{span}(e)
is bounded by
|X|
. From Invariant
I3
,
once edge
e
was added to set
E^{\mathsf{bad}}
, if
Y,Y^{\prime}
were the clusters of
{\mathcal{X}}
containing
x
and
y
respectively, then
Y=\left\{x\right\}
and
Y^{\prime}=\left\{y\right\}
holds. In other words, when
e
was added to
E^{\mathsf{bad}}
,
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
were singleton clusters. Therefore, these clusters, and their corresponding intervals
I_{Y}
and
I_{Y^{\prime}}
, remain unchanged for the remainder of the algorithm. In particular, the value
\operatorname{span}(e)
also remains unchanged for the remainder of the algorithm.

Recall that, from Invariant
I1
, for every cluster
X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\prime}
, graph
H[X]
remains a perfect well-structured graph for as long as
X
lies in
{\mathcal{X}}
. Recall also that we have defined weights of vertices for such graphs: for every vertex
v\in X
, its weight
w(v)
is the length of the unique special edge incident to
v
in
H[X]
. As the algorithm progresses, the lengths of some special edges grow, and so do the weights of the vertices in
H[X]
.
For every vertex
v\in X^{0}
, we denote by
w^{0}_{X}(v)
the weight of
v
when cluster
X
was first added to set
{\mathcal{X}}
, and algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
was initialized. If
v
belongs to set
X
when algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
is terminated (e.g. because
|X|
fell below
|X^{0}|/2
), then we let
w^{*}_{X}(v)
denote the weight of
v
in graph
H[X]
when algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
terminated. Otherwise, we denote by
w^{*}_{X}(v)
the weight of
v
just before it was deleted from
X
.
We also denote
W^{0}(X)=\sum_{v\in X^{0}}w^{0}_{X}(v)
, and
W^{*}(X)=\sum_{v\in X^{0}}w^{*}_{X}(v)
.
From
Observation

5.3
, the total length of all edges in
E^{\mathsf{spec}}
at the end of the algorithm is bounded by
|E^{\mathsf{spec}}|+2(n-|B^{0}|)\log^{5}n\leq 4n\log^{5}n
, and so, for every cluster
X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}
:

W^{0}(X)\leq W^{*}(X)\leq 8n\log^{5}n.

(6)

Recall that, at the beginning of the algorithm,
{\mathcal{X}}
may contain at most one non-leaf cluster – the cluster
U
. A new non-leaf cluster
X^{\prime}
may only be inserted into
{\mathcal{X}}
in one of the following two cases: (i) there is some existing non-leaf cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
, for which
|X|
fell below
|X^{0}|/2
, and
X^{\prime}=X
; or (ii) there is some existing non-leaf cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
, for which Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
or
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(X)
just computed a sparse well-structured cut
(Y,Z)
, and
X^{\prime}
is contained in one of the sets
Y
or
Z
. In the latter case, the vertices of
X^{\prime}
(along, possibly, with some additional vertices) are deleted from
X
. In either one of the above two cases, we say that
X
is the
parent-cluster
of
X^{\prime}
, and
X^{\prime}
is the
child-cluster
of
X
. We can now naturally define a descendant-ancestor relationship between clusters: Cluster
X^{\prime}
is a descendant-cluster of another cluster
X
, iff there is a sequence
X^{\prime}=X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{r}=X
of
r\geq 1
clusters, such that, for all
1\leq i<r
,
X_{i}
is a child-cluster of
X_{i+1}
. If
X^{\prime}
is a descendant-cluster of
X
, then
X
is the ancestor-cluster of
X^{\prime}
. It is easy to verify that, for every pair
X_{1},X_{2}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}
of clusters, either
X^{0}_{1}\cap X^{0}_{2}=\emptyset
, or
X_{1}^{0}\subseteq X^{0}_{2}
(in which case
X_{1}
is a descendant cluster of
X_{2}
), or
X_{2}^{0}\subseteq X^{0}_{1}
(in which case
X_{2}
is a descendant cluster of
X_{1}
).
From the above discussion, if
X_{1}
is a child-cluster of some cluster
X_{2}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}_{2}
, then either
|X_{1}^{0}|<|X_{2}^{0}|/2
; or, at the time when
X_{1}
was created,
\sum_{v\in X_{1}}w(v)\leq\sum_{v\in X_{2}}w(v)/2
held, following which the vertices of
X_{1}
were deleted from
X_{2}
.

Consider now a pair
X_{1},X_{2}\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\prime}
of clusters, where
X_{2}
is a descendant cluster of
X_{1}
. We say that
X_{2}
is a
close descendant of
X_{1}
iff
|X_{2}^{0}|>|X_{1}^{0}|/2
. In the following claim, we bound the number of close descendants of every cluster in
\tilde{X}_{2}^{\prime}
. The proof is somewhat technical and deferred to Section
B.6
of Appendix.

Claim 5.11
.

Every cluster
X\in\tilde{X}_{2}^{\prime}
has at most
64\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
close descendants.

For the sake of analysis, we need to bound
\sum_{X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\prime}}W^{*}(X)
. This bound will later be used both in order to bound the running time of the algorithm, and in order to bound
\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{bad}}}\operatorname{span}(e)
.

Observation 5.12
.

\sum_{X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\prime}}W^{*}(X)\leq(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{133}
.

Proof.

Recall that for every cluster
X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}_{2}
,
W^{0}(X)\leq 8n\log^{5}n
holds (see Inequality
6
).
For all
0\leq i<\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
and
0\leq j\leq 4\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
, we denote by
{\mathcal{X}}_{i,j}
the set of all clusters
X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}_{2}
with
2^{i}\leq|X^{0}|<2^{i+1}
and
2^{j}\leq W^{0}(X)<2^{j+1}
.

Fix a pair
0\leq i<\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
and
0\leq j\leq 4\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
of indices, and consider the collection
{\mathcal{X}}_{i,j}
of clusters. Notice that, if
X,X^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{X}}_{i,j}
, and
X^{\prime}
is a descendant-cluster of
X
, then
X^{\prime}
is a close descendant of
X
. From
Claim

5.11
, for every cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}_{i,j}
, at most
64\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
descendant clusters of
X
lie in
{\mathcal{X}}_{i,j}
. Using a simple greedy algorithm, we can then paratition the clusters in
{\mathcal{X}}_{i,j}
into
z=64\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil+1
subsets
{\mathcal{Y}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{Y}}_{z}
, such that, for all
1\leq z^{\prime}\leq z
, for every pair
X,X^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Y}}_{z^{\prime}}
of clusters, neither of the two clusters is a descendant of the other. In order to do so, we consider the clusters
X\in{\mathcal{X}}_{i,j}
one by one, in the non-decreasing order of
|X^{0}|
, breaking ties arbitrarily. When cluster
X
is considered, there must be some index
1\leq z^{\prime}\leq z
, such that no descendant of
X
lies in
{\mathcal{Y}}_{z^{\prime}}
, and we add
X
to such a set
{\mathcal{Y}}_{z^{\prime}}
.
For all
1\leq z^{\prime}\leq z
, we now bound
\sum_{X\in{\mathcal{Y}}_{z^{\prime}}}W^{*}(X)
separately.

Fix any index
1\leq z^{\prime}\leq z
, and denote
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}=\left\{X^{0}\mid X\in{\mathcal{Y}}_{z^{\prime}}\right\}
. Since, for every pair
X,X^{\prime}
of clusters in
{\mathcal{Y}}_{z^{\prime}}
, neither is the descendant of the other, all sets in
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}
are mutually disjoint.

Assume first that Case 2 happened, so the special cluster is undefined, and
|U^{0}|\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
holds. From the construction of the set
U
of vertices at the beginning of the algorithm, for every special edge
e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}
, both endpoints of
e
lie in
U^{0}
. Therefore,
|E^{\mathsf{spec}}|\leq\frac{|U^{0}|}{2}\leq(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
. From
Observation

5.3
, the total length of all edges in
E^{\mathsf{spec}}
at the end of the algorithm is bounded by
|E^{\mathsf{spec}}|+2(n-|B^{0}|)\log^{5}n\leq(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{129}
. Since the clusters in
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}
are mutually disjoint, it is easy to verify that
\sum_{X\in{\mathcal{Y}}_{z^{\prime}}}W^{*}(X)\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{129}
.

Next, we consider Case 1, where the special cluster is defined. Let
U^{*}
denote the set
U
of vertices at the end of the algorithm. Notice that
U^{*}
is disjoint from all sets of vertices in
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}
. For every vertex
v\in U^{*}
, let
w^{*}(v)
be the weight of
v
at the end of the algorithm, and let
W^{*}(U)=\sum_{v\in U^{*}}w^{*}(v)
. From
Observation

5.3
, the total length of all edges in
E^{\mathsf{spec}}
at the end of the algorithm is bounded by
|E^{\mathsf{spec}}|+2(n-|B^{0}|)\log^{5}n
, and so:

\sum_{X\in{\mathcal{Y}}_{z^{\prime}}}W^{*}(X)+W^{*}(U)\leq 2|E^{\mathsf{spec}}|+4(n-|B^{0}|)\log^{5}n.

(7)

Recall that all vertices that serve as endpoints of the edges in
E^{\mathsf{spec}}
lie in
U^{0}
. From
Observation

5.9
,
|U^{0}\setminus U^{*}|\leq 256(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{64}
. Therefore,
|U^{*}|\geq 2|E^{\mathsf{spec}}|-256(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{64}
, and
W^{*}(U)\geq|U^{*}|\geq 2|E^{\mathsf{spec}}|-256(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{64}
.
Combining this with Inequality
7
, we get that:

\sum_{X\in{\mathcal{Y}}_{z^{\prime}}}W^{*}(X)\leq 2|E^{\mathsf{spec}}|+4(n-|B^{0}|)\log^{5}n-W^{*}(U)\leq 260(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{64}\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{129}.

To conclude, we have shown that, for every index
1\leq z^{\prime}\leq z
:

\sum_{X\in{\mathcal{Y}}_{z^{\prime}}}W^{*}(X)\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{129}.

Summing this up over all indices
1\leq z^{\prime}\leq z
, and recalling that
z=64\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil+1\leq 128\log n
, we get that:

\sum_{X\in{\mathcal{X}}_{i,j}}W^{*}(X)\leq 256(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{130}.

Lastly, by summing up the above inequality over all indices
0\leq i<\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
and
0\leq j\leq 4\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
, we get that:

\sum_{X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}_{2}}W^{*}(X)\leq 2^{12}(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{132}\leq(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{133}.

We obtain the following corollary of
Observation

5.12
, that allows us to bound the total span of all bad edges owned by the clusters in
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\prime}
.

Corollary 5.13
.

\sum_{X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\prime}}\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{bad}}(X)}\operatorname{span}(e)\leq\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\Delta\cdot 2^{4\sqrt{\log N}}}{\eta}
.

Proof.

Consider a cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\prime}
, and let
(Y,Z)
be a cut that Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
returned. Recall that
|E_{H}(Y,Z)|\leq\frac{(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{d_{X}}\cdot\min\left\{w(Y),w(Z)\right\}
. If
w(Y)\leq w(Z)
, then let
Z^{\prime}=Y
, and otherwise let
Z^{\prime}=Z
. Then
|E_{H}(Y,Z)|\leq\frac{(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{d_{X}}\cdot w(Z^{\prime})\leq\frac{(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{d_{X}}\cdot\sum_{v\in Z^{\prime}}w^{*}_{X}(v)
holds. Moreover, all vertices of
Z^{\prime}
are deleted from
X
following the cut
(Y,Z)
, and the edges of
E_{H}(Y,Z)
are added to the set
E^{\mathsf{bad}}(X)
of bad edges that
X
owns. We assign to every vertex
v\in Z^{\prime}
a
charge
of
\frac{(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{d_{X}}\cdot w^{*}_{X}(v)
units, so that the total charge assigned to the vertices of
Z^{\prime}
is at least
|E_{H}(Y,Z)|
. It is then easy to see that, at the end of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
, the total number of edges in
E^{\mathsf{bad}}(X)
is bounded by the total charge to all vertices of
X^{0}
, which, in turn, is bounded by:

\frac{(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{d_{X}}\cdot\sum_{v\in X^{0}}w^{*}_{X}(v)=\frac{(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{d_{X}}\cdot W^{*}(X).

Next, we show that
d_{X}\geq\frac{|X^{0}|\cdot\eta}{\Delta\cdot 2^{2\sqrt{\log N}}}
. Indeed, recall that
d=\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\eta}{4\Delta\cdot 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}}
and so:

d_{X}=\min\left\{\frac{|X^{0}|\cdot\eta}{\Delta},d\right\}=\min\left\{\frac{|X^{0}|\cdot\eta}{\Delta},\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\eta}{4\Delta\cdot 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}}\right\}.

(8)

If Case 2 happened, then
|U^{0}|\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
, and, since
X^{0}\subseteq U^{0}
, we get that
|X^{0}|\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
. If Case 1 happened, then, from
Observation

5.9
,
|X^{0}|\leq 256(n-|B^{0}|)(\log N)^{64}
. Therefore, in any case,
|X^{0}|\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
must hold. Substituting the bound
n-|B^{0}|\geq\frac{|X^{0}|}{2(\log N)^{128}}
into Inequality
8
, we get that
d_{X}\geq\frac{|X^{0}|\cdot\eta}{\Delta\cdot 2^{2\sqrt{\log N}}}
. Altogether, we get that:

|E^{\mathsf{bad}}(X)|\leq\frac{(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{d_{X}}\cdot W^{*}(X)\leq\frac{\Delta\cdot 2^{2\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{|X^{0}|\cdot\eta}\cdot W^{*}(X)\leq\frac{\Delta\cdot 2^{3\sqrt{\log N}}}{|X^{0}|\cdot\eta}\cdot W^{*}(X).

Since for every edge
e\in E^{\mathsf{bad}}(X)
,
\operatorname{span}(e)\leq|X^{0}|
, we get that:

\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{bad}}(X)}\operatorname{span}(e)\leq|X^{0}|\cdot|E^{\mathsf{bad}}(X)|\leq\frac{\Delta\cdot 2^{3\sqrt{\log N}}}{\eta}\cdot W^{*}(X).

Finally, from
Observation

5.12
:

\begin{split}\sum_{X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\prime}}\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{bad}}(X)}\operatorname{span}(e)&\leq\frac{\Delta\cdot 2^{3\sqrt{\log N}}}{\eta}\cdot\sum_{X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\prime}}W^{*}(X)\\
&\leq\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\Delta\cdot 2^{3\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{133}}{\eta}\\
&\leq\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\Delta\cdot 2^{4\sqrt{\log N}}}{\eta}.\end{split}

5.1.5
Bounding the Running Time of Part 1 and Probability of Failure

Recall that, for every cluster
X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}_{2}
, the total running time of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
is bounded by:

\begin{split}&c_{1}\cdot(W^{0}(X))^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{16c_{2}r}+c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(X)\cdot\Delta\cdot\log^{4}N^{\prime}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(X)\cdot(W^{0}(X)+\Delta)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+6}\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{64c_{2}r}\right)^{16c_{2}r}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(X)\cdot(W^{0}(X)+\Delta)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+7}.\end{split}

(We have used the fact that, from Inequality
4
,

2^{\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}\leq 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot\log^{6}N
, and from Inequality
3
,
\log N^{\prime}\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{64c_{2}r}\right)\cdot\log N
. Additionally, we used the assumption that
N
is sufficiently large.).

Summing up over all clusters
X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\prime}
, from
Observation

5.12
, we get that the total running time of all Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
for clusters
X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}_{2}
is bounded by:

\begin{split}&\sum_{X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\prime}}c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(X)\cdot(W^{0}(X)+\Delta)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+7}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq\left(\Delta\cdot\sum_{X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}_{2}}W^{0}(X)+\left(\sum_{X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}_{2}}W^{0}(X)\right)^{2}\right)\cdot c_{1}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+7}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq c_{1}\cdot(n-|B^{0}|)^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+273}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+273}.\end{split}

(We have used the fact that
\Delta\leq n-|B^{0}|
from the problem definition).

Assume now that Case 1 happened. In this case, the running time of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(U)
is bounded by:

\begin{split}&c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(U)\cdot(W^{0}(U)-|\beta|+\Delta)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{16c_{2}r}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(U)\cdot(W^{0}(U)-|\beta|+\Delta)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+6}\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{64c_{2}r}\right)^{16c_{2}r}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(U)\cdot(W^{0}(U)-|\beta|+\Delta)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+7}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq 2c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+7}.\end{split}

(As before, we have used the fact that, from Inequality
4
,

2^{\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}\leq 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot\log^{6}N
, and from Inequality
3
,
\log N^{\prime}\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{64c_{2}r}\right)\cdot\log N
. Additionally, since, at the beginning of the algorithm, the length of every special edge is
1
,
W^{0}(U)=|U^{0}|\leq n
. From the problem definition,
\Delta\leq n-|B^{0}|
, and it is easy to see that
W^{0}(U)-|\beta|=|U^{0}|-|\beta|\leq n-|B^{0}|
must hold).

Additional time that is required in order to maintain the
\mathcal{ATO}
is asymptotically bounded by the total number of edges that are ever present in
H
, which, from the problem definition, is bounded by
O(|E(H)|)\leq O(n\cdot(n-|B^{0}|))
. Overall, since we can assume that
c_{2}
is a large enough constant, the running time of Part 1 of the algorithm is bounded by:

c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+c_{2}}.

Notice that, since
N\geq n
is large enough, the above running time is also bounded by
N^{3}
. Therefore, the total number of clusters that ever lie in
{\mathcal{X}}
is also bounded by
N^{3}
. For each regular non-leaf cluster
X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}
, the probability that Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
returns “FAIL” is bounded by
\frac{1}{(N^{\prime})^{4}}\leq\frac{1}{N^{4}}
. If Case 1 happens, then the probability that Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(U)
returns “FAIL” is bounded by
\frac{1}{4}
. From the union bound, the total probability that our algorithm ever returns “FAIL” is bounded by
\frac{1}{2}
, as required.

5.1.6
Bounding the Total Span of Bad Edges

Recall that, from
Corollary

5.13
,

\sum_{X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\prime}}\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{bad}}(X)}\operatorname{span}(e)\leq\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\Delta\cdot 2^{4\sqrt{\log N}}}{\eta}
.

If Case 1 happened, then, from
Observation

5.9
,
|E^{\mathsf{bad}}(U)|\leq\frac{\Delta\cdot 2^{6\sqrt{\log N}}}{\eta}
. It is easy to verify that, for every edge
e\in E^{\mathsf{bad}}(U)
,
\operatorname{span}(e)\leq|U^{0}|-\gamma\leq 256(n-|B^{0}|)(\log N)^{64}
, since
\gamma=|U^{0}|-256(n-|B^{0}|)(\log N)^{64}
. Therefore,
\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{bad}}(U)}\operatorname{span}(e)\leq\frac{\Delta\cdot 2^{6\sqrt{\log N}}}{\eta}\cdot 256(n-|B^{0}|)(\log N)^{64}\leq\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\Delta\cdot 2^{7\sqrt{\log N}}}{\eta}
.

Lastly, consider a leaf cluster
X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
, and recall that
|X^{0}|\leq\frac{\Delta\cdot(\log N)^{128}}{\eta}
.
Recall that
|E^{\mathsf{bad}}(X)|\leq|X^{0}|
, and so
\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{bad}}(X)}\operatorname{span}(e)\leq|X^{0}|^{2}\leq|X^{0}|\cdot\frac{\Delta\cdot(\log N)^{128}}{\eta}
.

Note that all clusters in
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{1}
are mutually disjoint, and, for each such cluster
X
,
X^{0}\subseteq U^{0}
holds. If Case 2 happened, then
|U^{0}|\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
, and so
\sum_{X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{1}}|X^{0}|\leq|U^{0}|\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
. Otherwise, from
Observation

5.9
,
\sum_{X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{1}}|X^{0}|\leq 256(n-|B^{0}|)(\log N)^{64}
. Therefore, in either case,
\sum_{X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{1}}|X^{0}|\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
holds, and so overall:

\sum_{X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{1}}\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{bad}}(X)}\operatorname{span}(e)\leq\frac{\Delta\cdot(\log N)^{128}}{\eta}\cdot\sum_{X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{1}}|X^{0}|\leq\frac{2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\Delta\cdot(\log N)^{256}}{\eta}.

Summing up over the three types of clusters, we get that:

\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{bad}}}\operatorname{span}(e)\leq\frac{4(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\Delta\cdot 2^{7\sqrt{\log N}}}{\eta}.

(9)

5.2
Part 2: Solving
DAG-like SSSP
on the Contracted Graph

The second part of our algorithm, we will define a dynamic graph
\hat{H}
that our algorithm will maintain, which in turn will be used to define an instance of the
DAG-like SSSP
problem (see
Section

2.4
). We will then apply the algorithm for
DAG-like SSSP
from
Theorem

2.7
to this instance of the problem.
We start by defining the dynamc graph
\hat{H}
, and by providing an algorithm for maintaining it.

Definition of the Dynamic Graph
\hat{H}

Consider any time
\tau
during the time horizon
{\mathcal{T}}
, the graph
H
and the collection
{\mathcal{X}}
of clusters at time
\tau
. We say that a cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
is
distinguished
, if it contains some vertex that currently lies in
B
.

Graph
\hat{H}
at time
\tau
is defined as follows. We start with graph
H
, and, for every cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
that is not a distinguished cluster, we contract all vertices in
X
into a single vertex
v_{X}
(that we refer to as a
supernode
). Additionally, all vertices that lie in distinguished clusters, together with the vertex
t
, are contracted into a single supernode, that for convenience we continue to denote by
t
. We delete all loops but keep parallel edges. We also denote the supernode
v_{S}
corresponding to cluster
S=\left\{s\right\}
by
s
. Consider now any edge
e
in the resulting graph. This edge corresponds to some edge in
H
whose endpoints lie in different clusters; we do not distinguish between these two edges.
We replace every edge
e=(v_{X},v_{Y})
in the resulting graph by a collection
J(e)
of
O(\log^{2}n)
parallel edges
(v_{X},v_{Y})
, as follows. Let
2^{j}
be the smallest power of
2
that is greater than
\operatorname{skip}(e)
. If
e
is a regular edge in
H
, then set
J(e)
contains, for all
j\leq j^{\prime}\leq\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
, a copy
e_{j^{\prime}}
of
e
, whose length is
\hat{\ell}(e_{j^{\prime}})=1
, and weight
w(e_{j^{\prime}})=2^{j^{\prime}}
.
Assume now that
e
is a special edge of
H
, and denote its length in
H
by
\ell(e)=2^{i}
. For all
i\leq i^{\prime}\leq\left\lceil\log\Lambda\right\rceil
and
j\leq j^{\prime}\leq\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
, set
J(e)
contains a copy
e_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
of
e
with length
\hat{\ell}(e_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})=2^{i^{\prime}}
and weight
w(e_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})=2^{j^{\prime}}
. This completes the definition of graph
\hat{H}
.

Consider now some vertex
v\in V(\hat{H})\setminus\left\{t\right\}
, and some integer
0\leq i\leq\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
. Consider the set of vertices
U_{i}(v)=\left\{u\in V(\hat{H})\mid\exists e=(v,u)\in E(\hat{H})\mbox{ with }w(e)=2^{i}\right\}
.
Notice that set
U_{i}(v)
may only contain the nodes
s,t
, and all nodes
v_{Y}\in V(\hat{H})
, such that some edge
e
of
H
connects a vertex of
X
to a vertex of
Y
, and has value
\operatorname{skip}(e)\leq 2^{i}
. In the latter case, the distance between intervals
I_{X}
and
I_{Y}
in the
\mathcal{ATO}
is at most
2^{i}
, and so at most
2^{i}
other intervals of the
\mathcal{ATO}
may lie between them. Therefore, it is immediate to verify that
|U_{i}(v)|\leq 2^{i+2}
always holds, establishing Property
S1
from the definition of the
DAG-like SSSP
problem.
Let
n^{\prime}
be the total number of vertices that are ever present in the dynamic graph
\hat{H}
. We bound
n^{\prime}
in the following simple observation.

Observation 5.14
.

n^{\prime}\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)+2
.

Proof.

Let
B^{*}
be the set of vertices that lie in
B
at the end of the algorithm. Clearly,
n^{\prime}\leq n-|B^{*}|+2
. On the other hand, since the algorithm consists of
\Delta\leq n-|B^{0}|
iterations, and in every iteration at most one vertex is deleted from
B
, we get that
|B^{*}|\geq|B^{0}|-\Delta\geq 2|B^{0}|-n
. Therefore,
n^{\prime}\leq n-|B^{*}|+2\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)+2
.

Maintaining the Graph
\hat{H}

At the beginning of the algorithm, we construct the initial graph
\hat{H}
from graph
H
as described above. It is easy to verify that this can be done in time
O(|E(H)|\cdot\log^{2}n)\leq O(n(n-|B^{0}|)\log^{2}n)
.

Next, we show an algorithm that maintains the graph
\hat{H}
, as graph
H
and the collection
{\mathcal{X}}
of clusters evolve over time. We will ensure that the only updates that
\hat{H}
undergoes are edge deletions and vertex splitting, as is required from the input to the
DAG-like SSSP
problem.

Recall that the only updates that graph
H
undergoes are (i) doubling of the lengths of its edges; and (ii) deletion of edges incident to
s
and
t
. Additionally, some vertices may be deleted from set
B
.

If an edge
e
that is incident to
s
or
t
is deleted from
H
, then we delete all copies of
e
from
\hat{H}
. If the length of some edge
e
is doubled, and the initial length of edge
e
was
2^{i}
, then we delete all copies of
e
whose length is
2^{i}
from graph
\hat{H}
.

Assume now that some vertex
v
is deleted from set
B
. Let
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
be the cluster that contains
v
. If
X
remains a distinguished cluster, then no additional updates to graph
\hat{H}
are required. Otherwise, we perform a
vertex splitting
operation, that we apply to supernode
t
, to construct a new supernode
v_{X}
. The corresponding collection
E^{\prime}
of edges is defined as follows. Let
e=(a,b)
be an edge of
H
, with exactly one endpoint in
X
; assume w.l.o.g. that
a\in X
and
b\in Y
, where
Y\in{\mathcal{X}}
is some cluster.
Let
2^{j}
be the smallest power of
2
that is greater than
\operatorname{skip}(e)
.
Assume first that
e
is a regular edge in
H
.
Notice that, if
Y
is not a distinguished cluster, then, for all
j\leq j^{\prime}\leq\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
,
graph
\hat{H}
already contains an edge
(t,v_{Y})
, that is a copy of
e
, whose length is
1
, and weight is
2^{j^{\prime}}
. We add to
E^{\prime}
a copy
(v_{X},v_{Y})
of

e
, whose length is
1
, and weight is
2^{j^{\prime}}
. Once the vertex splitting operation is completed, we will delete from
\hat{H}
all copies of
e
that are incident to
t
. If
Y
is a distinguished cluster, then, for all
j\leq j^{\prime}\leq\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
, we insert a copy
(v_{X},t)
of the edge
e
into
E^{\prime}
, whose length is
1
and weight is
2^{j^{\prime}}
.
Assume now that
e
is a special edge of
H
, and denote its length in
H
by
\ell(e)=2^{i}
. Consider a pair
i\leq i^{\prime}\leq\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
and
j\leq j^{\prime}\leq\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
of indices. As before, if
Y
is not a distinguished cluster, then graph
\hat{H}
already contains an edge
(t,v_{Y})
, that is a copy of
e
, whose length is
2^{i^{\prime}}
, and weight is
2^{j^{\prime}}
. We add to
E^{\prime}
a copy
(v_{X},v_{Y})
of

e
, whose length is
2^{i^{\prime}}
, and weight is
2^{j^{\prime}}
. Once the vertex splitting operation is completed, we will delete from
\hat{H}
all copies of
e
that are incident to
t
. If
Y
is a distinguished cluster, then we insert a copy
(v_{X},t)
of the edge
e
into
E^{\prime}
, whose length is
2^{i^{\prime}}
and weight is
2^{j^{\prime}}
. The case where
b\in X
and
a\not\in X
is treated similarly.

Lastly, it remains to consider updates to the clusters in
{\mathcal{X}}
. All such updates can be implemented by the following two operations: (i) given a cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
, and a subset
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
of vertices, insert
X^{\prime}
into
{\mathcal{X}}
(we call this update
cluster splitting
); and (ii) given
a cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
and a vertex
x\in X
, delete
x
from
X
(we call this update
vertex deletion
).

Consider first a cluster splitting operation, that is applied to cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
, through which a new cluster
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
is inserted into
{\mathcal{X}}
. Assume first that
X
is not a distinguished cluster. We apply a vertex splitting operation to supernode
v_{X}
, inserting a new supernode
v_{X^{\prime}}
into
\hat{H}
. The corresponding set
E^{\prime}
of edges is defined as follows.
Let
e=(a,b)
be an edge of
H
, with exactly one endpoint in
X^{\prime}
; assume w.l.o.g. that
a\in X^{\prime}
and
b\in Y
, where
Y\in{\mathcal{X}}
is some cluster.
Let
2^{j}
be the smallest power of
2
that is greater than
\operatorname{skip}(e)
.
Assume first that
e
is a regular edge in
H
.
Notice that, if
Y\neq X
, then, for all
j\leq j^{\prime}\leq\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
,
graph
\hat{H}
already contains an edge
(v_{X},v_{Y})
, that is a copy of
e
, whose length is
1
, and weight is
2^{j^{\prime}}
. We add to
E^{\prime}
a copy
(v_{X^{\prime}},v_{Y})
of

e
, whose length is
1
, and weight is
2^{j^{\prime}}
. If
Y=X
, then, for all
j\leq j^{\prime}\leq\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
, we insert a copy
(v_{X^{\prime}},v_{X})
of the edge
e
into
E^{\prime}
, whose length is
1
and weight is
2^{j^{\prime}}
.
Assume now that
e
is a special edge of
H
, and denote its length in
H
by
\ell(e)=2^{i}
. Consider a pair
i\leq i^{\prime}\leq\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
and
j\leq j^{\prime}\leq\left\lceil\log n\right\rceil
of indices. As before, if
Y\neq X
, then graph
\hat{H}
already contains an edge
(v_{X},v_{Y})
, that is a copy of
e
, whose length is
2^{i^{\prime}}
, and weight is
2^{j^{\prime}}
. We add to
E^{\prime}
a copy
(v_{X^{\prime}},v_{Y})
