of

e
, whose length is
2^{i^{\prime}}
, and weight is
2^{j^{\prime}}
. If
Y=X
, then we insert a copy
(v_{X^{\prime}},v_{X})
of the edge
e
into
E^{\prime}
, whose length is
2^{i^{\prime}}
and weight is
2^{j^{\prime}}
. The case where
b\in X^{\prime}
and
a\not\in X^{\prime}
is treated similarly.

If
X^{\prime}
is a distinguished cluster, then so is
X
, and no updates to
\hat{H}
are required. If
X
is a distinguished cluster but
X^{\prime}
is not a distinguished cluster, then the update procedure to graph
\hat{H}
is almost identical to that when a distinguished cluster becomes a non-distinguished cluster. Specifically, we apply a vertex-splitting operation to
t
, following which a supernode
v_{X^{\prime}}
representing the cluster
X^{\prime}
is inserted into
\hat{H}
. The remaining details a straightforward and are omitted here.

Lastly, assume that a vertex
x
is deleted from some cluster
X\in{\mathcal{X}}
. In this case, we consider every edge
e\in E(H)
that is incident to
x
one by one. Let
y
denote the other endpoint of
e
. If
X
is not a distinguished cluster, then we delete every copy of
e
that is incident to
v_{X}
from
\hat{H}
. Otherwise, we delete every copy of
e
that is incident to
t
from
\hat{H}
.

To summarize, we view a cluster-splitting operation that is applied to a cluster
X
as consisting of two steps. In the first step, a new cluster
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
is inserted into
{\mathcal{X}}
, and in the second step, the vertices of
X^{\prime}
are deleted from
X
. Recall that, following such cluster-splitting updates, it is possible that for some edge
e\in E(H)
whose endpoints lie in different clusters, the value
\operatorname{skip}(e)
increases. In such a case, we delete from
\hat{H}
all copies of
e
, whose weight is below the new value
\operatorname{skip}(e)
. This concludes the description of the algorithm for maintaining the graph
\hat{H}
.

It is easy to verify that the total time that is required in order to compute and maintain graph
\hat{H}
is asymptotically bounded by the total time required to maintain graph
H
and the collection
{\mathcal{X}}
of clusters times
O(\log^{6}n)
.
Indeed, from
Claim

5.11
, every cluster in
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}_{2}
has
O(\log n)
close descendants, and so it may have at most
O(\log^{2}n)
descendant clusters overall. Therefore, every vertex of
H
may belong to
O(\log^{2}n)
clusters over the course of the algorithm. For each edge
e=(x,y)
, for every pair
X,X^{\prime}
of clusters that contain
x
and
y
respectively, at any time during the algorithm,
O(\log^{2}n)
copies of
e
connecting the supernodes corresponding to
X
and
X^{\prime}
may be present in
\hat{H}
. Overall, at most
O(\log^{5}n)
copies of an edge of
H
may ever be present in
\hat{H}
, and so the total time that is required in order to compute and maintain graph
\hat{H}
is asymptotically bounded by the total time required to maintain graph
H
and the collection
{\mathcal{X}}
of clusters times
O(\log^{6}n)
.

Solving the
DAG-like SSSP
Problem on
\hat{H}

So far we have defined a dynamic graph
\hat{H}
with lengths and weights on edges and two special edges
s
and
t
, that undergoes an online sequence of edge-deletion and vertex-splitting updates, such that Property
S1
holds.
We consider an instance of the
DAG-like SSSP
problem defined on graph
\hat{H}

with parameter
\Gamma=64n\cdot 2^{8\sqrt{\log N}}
, parameter
d
replaced by
\frac{\Lambda}{4}
, and
\epsilon=\frac{1}{10}
.
We apply the algorithm from
Theorem

2.7
to the resulting instance of the
DAG-like SSSP
problem.
Recall that the total update time of this algorithm is bounded by:

\tilde{O}\left((n^{\prime})^{2}+|E(\hat{H})|+\Gamma\cdot n^{\prime}\right)\leq\tilde{O}\left(n\cdot(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot 2^{8\sqrt{\log N}}\right)

Overall, the total time that is required for Part 1 of the algorithm, and also for maintaining graph
\hat{H}
and for maintaining the data structures for the
DAG-like SSSP
problem from
Theorem

2.7
is bounded by:

c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+2c_{2}}.

Computing the Cut-Witness

Once the algorithm for the
DAG-like SSSP
problem terminates, we are guaranteed that graph
\hat{H}
contains no
s
-
t
path
Q
with
\sum_{e\in E(Q)}\hat{\ell}(e)\leq\frac{\Lambda}{4}
and
\sum_{e\in E(Q)}w(e)\leq\Gamma
.

We now define new lengths
\ell^{\prime}(e)
for edges
e\in E(H)
, as follows.
For every regular edge
e\in E(H)
, we set
\ell^{\prime}(e)=0
.
Consider now a special edge
e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}
. If both endpoints of
e
lie in
B
at the end of the algorithm, then we set
\ell(e)=0
. Otherwise, if
e\not\in E^{\mathsf{bad}}
, then we set
\ell^{\prime}(e)=\ell(e)
. Lastly, if
e\in E^{\mathsf{bad}}
, and not both endpoints of
e
lie in
B
, we set
\ell^{\prime}(e)=\ell(e)+\operatorname{span}(e)\cdot\frac{\eta\cdot\log n}{8\Delta\cdot 2^{7\sqrt{\log N}}}
.
We now show that
\left\{\ell^{\prime}(e)\right\}_{e^{\prime}\in E(H)}
is a valid cut-witness.

Claim 5.15
.

\left\{\ell^{\prime}(e)\right\}_{e^{\prime}\in E(H)}
is a valid cut-witness.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{E}
denote all edges of
G
with both endpoints in
B
at the end of the algorithm. It is easy to verify that, for each edge
e\in\tilde{E}
, we have set
\ell^{\prime}(e)=0
. Additionally, for every edge
e
that is incident to
s
or to
t
, we have set
\ell^{\prime}(e)=0
. Next, we bound
\sum_{e\in E(H)}\ell^{\prime}(e)
, as follows:

\begin{split}\sum_{e\in E(H)}\ell^{\prime}(e)&\leq\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}\setminus\tilde{E}}\ell(e)+\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{bad}}\setminus\tilde{E}}\operatorname{span}(e)\cdot\frac{\eta\cdot\log n}{8\Delta\cdot 2^{7\sqrt{\log N}}}\\
&\leq\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}\setminus\tilde{E}}\ell(e)+\frac{\eta\cdot\log n}{8\Delta\cdot 2^{7\sqrt{\log N}}}\cdot\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{bad}}}\operatorname{span}(e)\\
&\leq\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}\setminus\tilde{E}}\ell(e)+\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\log n}{2}\\
&\leq\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}\setminus\tilde{E}}\ell(e)+\frac{\Lambda\cdot\Delta}{2\eta\log^{4}n}.\end{split}

(we have used Inequality
9
and the fact that

\Lambda=(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\frac{\eta\cdot\log^{5}n}{\Delta}
).

Lastly, we prove that the distance from
s
to
t
in
H
, with respect to edge lengths
\ell^{\prime}(\cdot)
, is at least
\frac{\Lambda}{32}
. Assume for contradiction that there is an
s
-
t
path
Q
in the current graph
H
, such that
\sum_{e\in E(Q)}\ell^{\prime}(e)<\frac{\Lambda}{32}
.
Recall that graph
G
contains no regular edge connecting a vertex of
A
to a vertex of
B
. Therefore, path
Q
contains at least one special edge. Moreover, if we discard the first and the last regular edges on
Q
, and denote by
Q^{\prime}
the resulting path, then regular and special edges alternate on
Q^{\prime}
. Since the length of every special edge in
H
is at least
1
, we get that the number of regular edges on
Q
, that we denote by
m_{r}
, is bounded by
2\sum_{e\in E(Q)}\ell^{\prime}(e)+3<\frac{\Lambda}{8}
.

Let
\hat{E}(Q)\subseteq E(Q)
be the set of edges that also lie in the contracted graph
\hat{H}
, and denote
\hat{E}(Q)=(\hat{e}_{1},\ldots,\hat{e}_{z})
, where the edges are indexed in the order of their appearence on
Q
.
For all
1\leq i\leq z
, let
\hat{e}_{i}^{\prime}
be the copy of edge
e_{i}
in
\hat{H}
that has the shortest length and the smallest weight.
Then the sequence
(\hat{e}_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\hat{e}_{z}^{\prime})
of edges defines an
s
-
t
path in graph
\hat{H}
, that we denote by
\hat{Q}
. Notice that
\hat{E}(Q)
may not contain an edge with both endpoints in the current set
B
. Therefore, for every special edge
e^{\prime}_{i}\in\hat{E}(Q)
,
\hat{\ell}(\hat{e}^{\prime}_{i})=\ell(\hat{e}_{i})\leq\ell^{\prime}(\hat{e}_{i})
, while for every regular edge
e\in\hat{E}(Q)
,
\hat{\ell}(e)=1
.
We conclude that the length of path
\hat{Q}
in graph
\hat{H}
is bounded by
\sum_{e\in E(Q)}\ell^{\prime}(e)+m_{r}\leq\frac{\Lambda}{4}
.
To reach a contradiction, it is now enough to show that
\sum_{\hat{e}^{\prime}_{i}\in\hat{E}(Q)}w(\hat{e}^{\prime}_{i})\leq\Gamma
.

Indeed, recall that, for every edge
e\in E^{\mathsf{bad}}
, we have set
\ell^{\prime}(e)=\ell(e)+\operatorname{span}(e)\cdot\frac{\eta\cdot\log n}{8\Delta\cdot 2^{7\sqrt{\log N}}}
.
Since
\sum_{e\in E(Q)}\ell^{\prime}(e)\leq\frac{\Lambda}{32}
, we get that:

\sum_{e\in E(Q)\cap E^{\mathsf{bad}}}\operatorname{span}(e)\cdot\frac{\eta\cdot\log n}{8\Delta\cdot 2^{7\sqrt{\log N}}}\leq\frac{\Lambda}{32}.

Therefore:

\sum_{e\in E(Q)\cap E^{\mathsf{bad}}}\operatorname{span}(e)\leq\frac{\Lambda\cdot\Delta\cdot 2^{7\sqrt{\log N}}}{4\eta\log n}=\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot 2^{7\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot\log^{4}n}{4},

since
\Lambda=(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\frac{\eta\cdot\log^{5}n}{\Delta}
.
But then:

\sum_{e\in E(Q)}\operatorname{skip}(e)\leq n+2\sum_{e\in E(Q)\cap E^{\mathsf{bad}}}\operatorname{span}(e)\leq n+\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot 2^{7\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot\log^{4}n}{2}\leq\frac{\Gamma}{4},

since
\Gamma=64n\cdot 2^{8\sqrt{\log N}}
. Since, for all
1\leq i<z
,
w(\hat{e}^{\prime}_{i})\leq 2\operatorname{skip}(\hat{e}_{i})
, we get that:

\sum_{\hat{e}^{\prime}_{i}\in E(\hat{Q})}w(\hat{e}^{\prime}_{i})\leq 2\sum_{e\in E(Q)}\operatorname{skip}(e)\leq\frac{\Gamma}{2},

a contradiction.

5.3
Completing the Algorithm

Throughout the algorithm, for every special edge
e=(x,y)
in graph
H
, we maintain a counter
n(e)
, that counts the number of
s
-
t
paths containing
e
that the algorithm reported in response to queries that contain
e
, since
\ell(e)
was doubled last, or since the beginning of the algorithm – whatever happened later.

The algorithm contains at most
\Delta
iterations, and terminates when either (i) all iterations are completed; or (ii) the algorithm for the
DAG-like SSSP
problem returns “FAIL”; or (iii) one of the algorithms for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem or for the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem that we use to maintain the clusters of
{\mathcal{X}}
returns “FAIL”. In the latter case, we terminate our algorithm with a “FAIL”; we have shown that this may only happen with probability at most
1/2
. In the second case, we return the cut-witness as described above.

We now proceed to describe the
i
th iteration. We use the algorithm for the
DAG-like SSSP
problem from
Theorem

2.7
to compute a simple
s
-
t
path
P_{i}
in the current graph
\hat{H}
, with
\sum_{e\in E(\hat{H})}\hat{\ell}(e)\leq(1+10\epsilon)\cdot\frac{\Lambda}{4}\leq\frac{\Lambda}{2}
, in time
O(|E(P_{i})|)
. We denote by
\hat{e}_{1},\hat{e}_{2},\ldots,\hat{e}_{z}
the sequence of the edges on this path. For all
1\leq j\leq z
, let
e_{j}=(x_{j},y_{j})
be the edge corresponding to
\hat{e}_{j}
in graph
H
. Notice that
x_{1}=s
, and, for all
1\leq j<z
, there is a cluster of
{\mathcal{X}}
that contains both
y_{j}
and
x_{j+1}
; we denote this cluster by
X_{j}
.
Cluster
X_{j}
cannot be a distinguished cluster, since path
P_{i}
is simple.
Lastly, there must be a distinguished cluster
X_{z}\in{\mathcal{X}}
, that contains
y_{z}
.

Recall that, if Case 1 happened, then
|U^{0}|\geq 2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
. Moreover,
|U|\geq\gamma
holds throughout the algorithm, where
\gamma=|U^{0}|-256(n-|B^{0}|)(\log N)^{64}\geq(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
. At the end of the algorithm,
|V(G)\setminus B|\leq(n-|B^{0}|)+\Delta\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)
holds. Therefore, if Case 1 happens, then
U
remains a distinguished cluster throughout the algorithm.

For all
1\leq j<z
, we query Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X_{j})
for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem with vertices
y_{j}
and
x_{j+1}
. Let
Q_{j}\subseteq H[X_{j}]
be the simple path connecting
y_{j}
to
x_{j+1}
that the algorithm returns, whose length is at most
d_{X_{j}}
. Assume first that either Case 1 did not happen; or Case 1 happened and
X_{z}\neq U
. In this case, we select an arbitrary vertex
x_{z+1}\in B\cap X_{z}
, and query Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X_{j})
for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem with vertices
y_{z}
and
x_{z+1}
. Let
Q_{z}\subseteq H[X_{z}]
be the simple path connecting
y_{z}
to
x_{z+1}
that the algorithm returns, whose length is at most
d_{X_{z}}
. If
Q_{z}
contains any vertex of
B
as an inner vertex, then we truncate it, so it connects
y_{z}
to a vertex of
B
, and does not contain any vertex of
B
as an inner vertex.
We then append an edge connecting the last vertex of
Q_{z}
to
t
to the path
Q_{z}
. (For all
1\leq j\leq z
, if
X_{j}
is the singleton cluster, then we simply let
Q_{j}
contain a single vertex
x_{j}=y_{j+1}
).
If Case 1 happened and
X_{z}=U
, then we query Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(U)
with vertex
y_{z}
, to obtain a simple path
Q_{z}
connecting
y_{z}
to some vertex
b\in B
, such that
Q_{z}
has length at most
d_{U}
and no inner vertices of
Q_{z}
belong to
B
. We then append the edge
(b,t)
at the end of path
Q_{z}
.

By concatenating
e_{1},Q_{1},e_{2}\ldots,e_{z},Q_{z}
, we obtain an
s
-
t
path in the current graph
H
, that we denote by
P^{\prime}_{i}
. Since the initial path
P_{i}
was simple, all custers in
{\mathcal{X}}
are mutually disjoint, and each of the paths
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{z}
is simple, we get that
P^{\prime}_{i}
is a simple path as well. We now bound the length of
P^{\prime}_{i}
. Recall that
\sum_{j=1}^{z}\ell(e_{j})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{z}\hat{\ell}(\hat{e}_{j})\leq\frac{\Lambda}{2}
.

Recall that, for every cluster
X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}_{2}
,
d_{X}=\min\left\{\frac{|X^{0}|\cdot\eta}{\Delta},d\right\}
.
Notice that clusters
X_{1},\ldots,X_{z-1}
may not contain vertices of
B
. Since, at the end of the algorithm,
|B|\geq|B^{0}|-\Delta
, we get that:

\sum_{j=1}^{z-1}|X_{j}|\leq n-|B|\leq n-|B^{0}|+\Delta\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|).

Moreover, for every cluster
X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\prime}
, algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X)
terminates once
|X|
falls below
|X^{0}|/2
, and so:

\sum_{j=1}^{z-1}|X^{0}_{j}|\leq 2\sum_{j=1}^{z-1}|X_{j}|\leq 4(n-|B^{0}|).

Altogether, we get that:

\sum_{j=1}^{z-1}d_{X_{j}}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{z-1}\frac{|X^{0}_{j}|\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\leq\frac{4(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\leq\frac{\Lambda}{4},

since
\Lambda=(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\frac{\eta\cdot\log^{5}n}{\Delta}
.

Note also that either
d_{X_{z}}\leq d_{U}
(if
X_{z}
is the special cluster), or
d_{X_{z}}\leq d
(otherwise) must hold.
Recall that
d_{U}=\max\left\{(\log N^{\prime})^{64},\left(|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+\Delta\right)\frac{\eta}{4\Delta\cdot 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}}\right\}
. Since
\Lambda=(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\frac{\eta\cdot\log^{5}n}{\Delta}
, while
|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+\Delta\leq 2(n-|B^{0}|)
and
\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\eta}{\Delta}>2^{4\sqrt{\log N}}
from Inequality
2
, we get that
d_{U}\leq\frac{\Lambda}{4}
.
Recall also that
d=\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\eta}{4\Delta\cdot 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}}\leq\frac{\Lambda}{4}
.
Therefore,
d_{z}\leq\frac{\Lambda}{4}
must hold. Overall, we get that:

\sum_{e\in E(P^{\prime})}\ell(e)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{z}\ell(e_{j})+\sum_{j=1}^{z-1}d_{X_{j}}\leq d_{X_{z}}\leq\Lambda,

as required.
We increase the counter
n(e)
for every edge
e\in E(P^{\prime})
. If, for any such edge
e
, the counter
n(e)
reaches
\eta
, then we double the length of
e
, and we reset
n(e)=0
.

Notice that the time required to respond to the queries posed to algorithms
{\mathcal{A}}(X_{1}),\ldots,{\mathcal{A}}(X_{z-1})
, and Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X_{z})
(if
X_{z}
is a regular cluster) or Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(X_{z})
(if it is a special cluster) is already accounted for in the analysis of the running times of these algorithms. The additional time that is required in order to respond to the query is bounded by
O(|E(P_{i})|)
.

Overall, the additional time that is required in order to respond to all queries is bounded by
\sum_{i=1}^{\Delta}O(|E(P_{i})|)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\Delta}O(|E(P^{\prime}_{i})|)
.
Recall that, for every special edge
e\in E(H)
, the length of
e
in
H
is doubled once it participates in
\eta
paths that the algorithm returns. Once the length of
e
reaches
\Lambda
, it may no longer participate in any path that the algorithm returns. Therefore, every special edge may participate in at most
O(\eta\cdot\log\Lambda)\leq O(\eta\log N)
paths
P^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,P^{\prime}_{\Delta}
. Since there are at most
n
special edges in
H
, and since regular and special edges alternate on the paths
P^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,P^{\prime}_{\Delta}
, we get that

\sum_{i=1}^{\Delta}|E(P^{\prime}_{i})|\leq O(n\eta\log N)\leq O(n\Delta\log N)\leq O(n\cdot(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\log N)
, since
\eta\leq\Delta
and
\Delta\leq n-|B^{0}|
from the problem definition.
The total additional time that is required in order to respond to all queries is then bounded by
O(n\cdot(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\log N)
, and the total running time of the algorithm is bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+2c_{2}}\right)\leq c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r+7c_{2}}.

6
Layered Connectivity Data Structure and an Algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem

The goal of this section is to prove the second part of
Theorem

4.12
, by showing that an algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem implies an algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem with the required running time. In order to prove this theorem, we define a new data structure, that we call a
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
, and provide an algorithm for maintaining this data structure in a graph that undergoes online increases in the lengths of its edges. The same data structure, and the same algorithm for maintaining it, will also be used in
Section

7
in order to prove the first part of
Theorem

4.12
, namely that an algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem implies an algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem with the required running time. Because of this, we define both the
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
, and the algorithm for maintaining this data structure slightly more generally than what is needed for the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem. But first we start with intuition.

Recall the definition of the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem. At a high level, the input is a perfect well-structured graph
G=(L,R,E)
with a proper assignment of lengths
\ell(e)
on its edges
e\in E
that are given in the adjacency list representation, together with a subset
\beta\subseteq V(G)
of vertices with
|\beta|\geq\frac{99|V(G)|}{100}
, and parameters
N\geq W^{0}(G)
,
1\leq\Delta\leq\frac{|\beta|}{(\log N)^{128}}
,
1\leq\eta\leq\Delta
, and
d\geq(\log N)^{64}
, such that
\frac{\Delta\cdot d}{\eta}\leq|V(G)|-|\beta|+\Delta\cdot(\log N)^{64}
, and every special edge has length
1
. Over the course of the algorithm, vertices may be deleted from set
\beta
, and the lengths of some edges may be increased, but the length of every special edge incident to a vertex that currently lies in
\beta
remains
1
.

The algorithm consists of at most
\Delta
iterations.
At the beginning of every iteration
i
, the algorithm is given a vertex
x\in V(G)
, and it must return a simple path
P_{i}
in
G
connecting
x
to any vertex
y\in\beta
, such that the length of the path is at most
d
, and no inner vertices on the path lie in
\beta
. After that, the length of some special edges on path
P_{i}
may be doubled, and vertex
y
is deleted from
\beta
. Recall that, for any edge
e\in E(G)
, if
\ell(e)
is doubled at times
\tau^{\prime}
and
\tau
with
\tau^{\prime}<\tau
, then at least
\eta
paths that the algorithm returns during the time interval
[\tau^{\prime},\tau)
must contain
e
.
The algorithm may, at any time, produce a strongly well-structured cut
(X,Y)
in
G
of sparsity
\Phi_{G}(X,Y)\leq\frac{(\log N)^{64}}{d}
, such that
w(X)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap X|
holds. Let
J^{\prime}\subseteq Y
denote the set of vertices that serve as endpoints of the edges of
E_{G}(X,Y)
. The vertices of
X\cup J^{\prime}
are then deleted from
G
, and the algorithm continues with the resulting graph
G=G[Y\setminus J^{\prime}]
.

A natural way to solve this problem is to simply maintain a collection

{\mathcal{Q}}=\left\{K(v)\mid v\in V(G)\right\}
of paths in
G
, where for each vertex
v\in V(G)
, path
K(v)
connects
v
to some vertex of
\beta
, such that the length of every path in
{\mathcal{Q}}
is at most
d
, and every vertex of
\beta
serves as endpoint of a small number of such paths.
Then, whenever a query vertex
x
arrives, we could simply return the path
K(x)
in response to the query, possibly truncating it to ensure that no inner vertices on the path belong to
\beta
.
As the time progresses, some of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
may however be destroyed. For example, as the lengths of some special edges are doubled, the lengths of some paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
may become too large, so we can no longer use them in order to respond to queries. Additionally, some vertices may be deleted from
\beta
, and so paths connecting vertices of
G
to such vertices can no longer be used in order to respond to queries. Lastly, when our algorithm returns a sparse cut
(A,B)
, and some of the vertices are deleted from
G
following this, it is possible that, for some of the paths
K(v)\in{\mathcal{Q}}
, some inner vertices on
K(v)
are deleted from
G
, while the first endpoint of the path remain in
G
. Whenever, for some vertex
v\in V(G)
, the corresponding path
K(v)\in{\mathcal{Q}}
is destroyed, we say that vertex
v
became
disconnected
. We need to ensure that, before we respond to each query, every vertex of
G
has a valid path
K(v)\in{\mathcal{Q}}
. Therefore, whenever a vertex
v
becomes disconnected, we need to compute a new path
K(v)
connecting it to some vertex that currently lies in
\beta
. The difficulty is that, even if the number of vertices that are currently disconnected is quite small, if we attempt to connect them to the vertices of
\beta
directly, the amount of time that we need to invest to compute such a routing may be potentially as large as
\Omega(|V(G)|\cdot(|V(G)|-|\beta|)
. We note that we can ensure that the total number of vertices that ever become disconnected over the course of the algorithm (counting every time a vertex becomes disconnected separately, even if the same vertex is disconnected several times), is relatively small. However, there may be a large number of iterations, in each of which a small number of vertices become disconnected. Therefore, computing a new collection of paths connecting these disconnected vertices to
\beta
from scratch in every iteration is prohibitively expensive in terms of the running time. A different approach that could address this issue is the following: let
U
be the set of vertices that are currently disconnected, and let
U^{\prime}=V(G)\setminus U
. Instead of connecting the vertices of
U
to the vertices of
\beta
directly, we can instead try to connect them to the vertices of
U^{\prime}
, and then exploit the paths in
\left\{K(v)\mid v\in U^{\prime}\right\}
in order to route the vertices of
U
to the vertices of
\beta
. In doing so we will only need to explore a relatively small subgraph of
G
, and we can use the algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, whose existence is assumed in the statement of
Theorem

4.12
, in order to do so in time roughly
O(W(G)^{1+o(1)}\cdot|U|)
. The difficulty with this approach is that, as we compose the routing paths with each other, iteration after iteration, we may obtain routing paths that are very long. To conclude, we need to balance between the need to ensure that all paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
are sufficiently short, with the need to ensure that the total running time invested in reconnecting the disconnected vertices to
\beta
is not too high. We use a natural
layering
approach, by defining a
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
, which we describe below.

So far we provided intuition for the
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
in the context of obtaining an algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem. We will also exploit this data structure in our algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem. Intuitively, instead of the set
\beta
of vertices that is given as part of input to the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem, in order to solve the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, we will select a random set
\beta
of such vertices of an appropriate cardinality, and then embed an expander that is defined over the vertices of
\beta
into the input graph
G
. The
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
data structure will then be used to maintain paths connecting every vertex of
G
to some vertex of
\beta
. We will also maintain a similar data structure in graph
\overleftarrow{G}
, that is obtained by reversing the direction of every edge in
G
, obtaining a collection of paths that, for every vertex
v\in V(G)
, connects some vertex of
\beta
to
v
.
We now formally define the
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
data structure, followed by an algorithm for maintaining such a data structure in dynamic graphs that arise from the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
and
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problems.
Lastly, we design an algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem that relies on this data structure.

6.1
The Layered Connectivity Data Structure

We assume that we are given a perfect well-structured graph
G=(L,R,E)
with
|V(G)|=n
, and a proper assignment
\ell(e)
of lengths to the edges
e\in E
, that are given in the adjacency-list representation. Recall that for every vertex
v\in V(G)
, its weight
w(v)
is the length of the unique special edge that is incident to
v
in
G
. We denote
W=\sum_{v\in V(G)}w(v)
, and we let
H
be the subdivided graph corresponding to
G
(see
Definition

4.1
). We assume further that we are given a parameter
N\geq\max\left\{n,W/32\right\}
that is greater than a large enough constant, together with additional parameters
d^{\prime}\geq 1
,
\rho\geq\log^{2}N
, and
1\leq r\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
, such that
d^{\prime}\leq\min\left\{\frac{2^{r\sqrt{\log N}}}{\log^{8}N},\frac{n}{8}\right\}
. Lastly, we are given a collection
\beta\subseteq V(H)
of vertices, that we refer to as
centers
, with
|\beta|\geq\frac{W}{d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{2}N}
.

Let
\lambda=\left\lceil\log(64N)\right\rceil+1
.
A
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
for graph
G
consists of the following ingredients:

•

The first ingredient is a partition
(U_{0},U_{1},\ldots,U_{\lambda})
of
V(H)
into disjoint subsets, such that
U_{0}=\beta
. For all
0\leq i\leq\lambda
, we refer to the vertices in set
U_{i}
as
layer-
i
vertices
, and we sometimes refer to the vertices of
U_{\lambda}
as
disconnected vertices
. We also denote by
U^{\geq i}=\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=i}^{\lambda}U_{i^{\prime}}
and by
U^{\leq i}=\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=0}^{i}U_{i^{\prime}}
. Similarly, we denote by
U^{>i}=\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=i+1}^{\lambda}U_{i^{\prime}}
and by
U^{<i}=\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=0}^{i-1}U_{i^{\prime}}
.

•

The second ingredient is collections
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{Q}}_{\lambda-1}
of paths in graph
H
. For all
1\leq i\leq\lambda-1
,
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}=\left\{K(v)\mid v\in U_{i}\right\}
, where, for every vertex
v\in U_{i}
, path
K(v)
connects vertex
v
to some vertex
v^{\prime}\in U^{<i}
. We say that vertex
v^{\prime}
is the
parent-vertex
of
v
, and vertex
v
is a
child-vertex
of
v^{\prime}
. We also say that
v
is a
descendant
of
v^{\prime}
. The descendant relation is transitive: if
v
is a descendant of
v^{\prime}
, and
v^{\prime}
is a descendant of
v^{\prime\prime}
, then
v
is a descendant of
v^{\prime\prime}
. Additionally, every vertex is a descendant of itself. If vertex
x
is a descendant of vertex
y
, then we say that
y
is an
ancestor
of
x
. Note that ancestor-descendant relation is only determined by the endpoints of the paths in
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
, and it is independent of the inner vertices on these paths.
For every vertex
x\in\beta
and index
0\leq i<\lambda
, we denote by
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}(x)
the set of all vertices
v\in U_{i}
that are descendants of
x
, and we denote by
{\mathcal{D}}(x)=\bigcup_{i=0}^{\lambda-1}{\mathcal{D}}_{i}(x)
the set of all descendant-vertices of
x
.

Lastly, we require that the following properties hold for a
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
:

L1.

For all
1\leq i<\lambda
, the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
have length at most
4d^{\prime}\log^{10}N
each, and they cause edge-congestion at most
4d^{\prime}\log^{9}N
in
H
;

L2.

For every vertex
x\in\beta
, for every layer
1\leq i\leq\lambda-1
,
|{\mathcal{D}}_{i}(x)|\leq\rho
; and

L3.

For every layer
1\leq i\leq\lambda-1
, every vertex
v\in U^{<i}\setminus U^{0}
, may serve as an endpoint of at most one path in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
.

The
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
data structure must also maintain, for every vertex
x\in\beta
and layer
1\leq i<\lambda
, the collection
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}(x)\subseteq U_{i}
of layer-
i
descendants of
x
. Additionally, for every layer
1\leq i\leq\lambda-1
and vertex
v\in U^{<i}
, if
v
serves as an endpoint of a path
K(u)\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
, then we maintain a pointer from
v
to
u
.

In order to construct and maintain a
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
, as graph
G
undergoes updates, we will employ a procedure that we call
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
, and describe next.

6.2
Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}

As before, we assume that we are given a perfect well-structured graph
G=(L,R,E)
with
|V(G)|=n
, and a proper assignment
\ell(e)
of lengths to the edges
e\in E
, that are given in the adjacency-list representation, together with a parameter
N\geq\max\left\{n,W/32\right\}
that is greater than a large enough constant, and parameters
d^{\prime}\geq 1
,
\rho\geq\log^{2}N
, and
1\leq r\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
, where
W=\sum_{v\in V(G)}w(v)
, and
d^{\prime}\leq\min\left\{\frac{2^{r\sqrt{\log N}}}{\log^{8}N},\frac{n}{8}\right\}
.
We additionally assume that
\frac{W\cdot\log^{3}N}{|\beta|}\leq\frac{\rho}{16}
, and that the length of every edge in
G
is at most
d^{\prime}
.
Lastly, we assume that we are given a subdivided graph
H
corresponding to
G
, in the adjacency-list representation, and a subset
\beta\subseteq V(H)
of its vertices, with
|\beta|\geq\frac{W}{d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{2}N}
.

A valid input to Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
consists of an index
1\leq i<\lambda
of a layer, and a valid
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
for
G
, such that, for all
i\leq i^{\prime}<\lambda
,
U_{i^{\prime}}=\emptyset
, and
|U_{\lambda}|\leq 2^{\lambda-i}
.
We denote
k=|U_{\lambda}|
.
A valid output of Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
is one of the following:

•

either a valid
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
, in which, for all
i<i^{\prime}<\lambda
,
U_{i^{\prime}}=\emptyset
and
|U_{\lambda}|\leq\frac{k}{4\lambda}
holds; additionally, layers
U_{1},\ldots,U_{i-1}
, together with the corresponding collections of paths
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{Q}}_{i-1}
must remain the same as in the input
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
; or

•

a strongly well-structured
\frac{1}{4d^{\prime}}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A),w(B)\geq\frac{k}{2^{14}\log N}
and
w(A)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap A|
; or

•

A weakly well-structured cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in
H
with
|A^{\prime}|\geq\frac{511|V(H)|}{512}
,
|A^{\prime}\cap\beta|<\frac{2|\beta|}{\log^{3}N}
, and
|E_{H}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|\leq\frac{|V(H)|}{1024d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N}
.

Next, we show an algorithm, that, given a valid input to Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
, produces a valid output for the procedure. The algorithm relies on Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, whose existence was assumed in the statement of
Theorem

4.12
.

Claim 6.1
.

Suppose that, for some parameter
r\geq 1
, there exists a randomized algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, that, given as an input an instance
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of the problem with
|V(G)|=n
,
has running time at most
c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}}
.
Then there is a randomized algorithm, that, given a valid input to Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
, where graph
G
and its corresponding subdivided graph
H
are given in the adjacency-list representation, with the same parameter
r
, either produces a valid output for the procedure, or returns “FAIL”. The running time of the algorithm is at most

O\left(c_{1}\cdot W\cdot k\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+14}\right)
, and the probability that it returns “FAIL” is at most
\frac{1}{N^{10}}
.

The remainder of this subsection is dedicated to the proof of
Claim

6.1
. We denote by
{\mathcal{A}}
the algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, whose existence is assumed in the statement of the claim.

The most natural way to design an algorithm for Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
is to simply cast it as a special case of the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, in the underlying graph
H
, with the set
A=U_{\lambda}
of source vertices, and the set
B=\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=0}^{i-1}U_{i^{\prime}}
of destination vertices. For technical reasons, we must allow every vertex
x\in\beta
to serve as an endpoint of at least
\Theta\left(\frac{W\cdot\operatorname{poly}\log N}{|\beta|}\right)
of the resulting routing paths, while each of the remaining vertices of
B
may serve as an endpoint of at most one path. To accommodate this requirement, we slightly modify graph
H
. The modification essentially replaces every vertex
x\in\beta
with
\Theta\left(\frac{W\cdot\operatorname{poly}\log N}{|\beta|}\right)
distinct vertices, while ensuring that the resulting graph remains a uniform well-structured graph. The main difficulty with this approach, however, is that, for every vertex
x\in\beta
, and every layer
1\leq i^{\prime}<i
, there may be up to
\rho
vertices of
U_{i^{\prime}}
that are descendants of
x
. It is possible that we will connect a distinct vertex of
U_{\lambda}
to each such descendant vertex of
x
via the routing computed by the algorithm for the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem. The vertices of
U_{\lambda}
from which these routing paths originate are then added to layer
U_{i}
, and, as the result, the number of descendants of
x
in
U_{i}
may become too high. In order to overcome this difficulty, we will iteratively solve
{4\lambda}
instances of the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, with the goal of constructing, for every vertex
v\in U_{\lambda}
, a collection
{\mathcal{R}}(v)
of at most
{4\lambda}
paths that originate at
v
.
Let
U^{\prime}\subseteq U_{\lambda}
be the subset of vertices
v
with
|{\mathcal{R}}(v)|\geq 2\lambda
, and let
{\mathcal{R}}=\bigcup_{v\in U^{\prime}}{\mathcal{R}}(v)
. Our goal is to ensure that set
U^{\prime}
contains almost all vertices of
U_{\lambda}
, the paths in
{\mathcal{R}}
cause low edge-congestion, and they terminate at the vertices of
\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=0}^{i-1}U_{i^{\prime}}
. We also need to ensure that every vertex of
\beta
serves as an endpoint to at most
\Theta\left(\frac{W\cdot\operatorname{poly}\log N}{|\beta|}\right)
paths in
{\mathcal{R}}
, while every vertex of
\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=1}^{i-1}U_{i^{\prime}}
serves as an endpoint of at most one such path.
Intuitively, by sending
\frac{1}{2\lambda}
flow units on every path in
{\mathcal{R}}
, we obtain a fractional flow where every vertex in
U^{\prime}
sends at least
1
flow unit to the vertices of
\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=0}^{i-1}U_{i^{\prime}}
, and, for every vertex
x\in\beta
, the descendants of
x
, including
x
itself, receive at most
\rho
flow units. We can then compute an integral flow, which defines, for every vertex
v\in U^{\prime}
, a path
K(v)
connecting
v
to some vertex in
\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=0}^{i-1}U_{i^{\prime}}
, such that, for every vertex
x\in\beta
, at most
\rho
such paths terminate at the descendant vertices of
x
.

We now provide a formal description of the procedure.
Recall that we have denoted
k=|U_{\lambda}|
. We assume first that
k\geq 2^{20}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N
. We show an algoritm for the special case where
k<2^{20}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N
later.
We start by describing the modified graph
H^{\prime}
, to which the algorithm for the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem will be applied iteratively.
Throughout, we use a parameter
M=\frac{W\cdot\log^{3}N}{|\beta|}
. Since
\frac{W\cdot\log^{3}N}{|\beta|}\leq\frac{\rho}{16}
from the problem definition, we get that
M\leq\frac{\rho}{16}
.

6.2.1
Modified Graph
H^{\prime}
.

Recall that we are given as input a perfect well-structured graph
G
, and its corresponding subdivided graph
H
. Both graphs are given in the adjacency-list representation. We first define the graph
H^{\prime}
, and then describe an algorithm to compute its adjacency-list representation efficiently.

Graph
H^{\prime}
is defined as follows.
We start with
H^{\prime}=H
, and the bipartition
(L^{\prime\prime},R^{\prime\prime})
of its vertices, that is identical to the bipartition
(L^{\prime},R^{\prime})
of
V(H)
. We then inspect every vertex
x\in\beta
one by one. If
x\in L^{\prime\prime}
, then we add the collection
\left\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{M-1}\right\}
of new vertices to graph
H^{\prime}
, that we call
copies
of
x
. The vertices in
\left\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{M-1}\right\}
are also added to
R^{\prime\prime}
, and, for all
1\leq j\leq M-1
, we add a regular edge
(x,x_{j})
to graph
H^{\prime}
. If
x\in R^{\prime\prime}
, then we also add a collection
\left\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{M-1}\right\}
of new vertices to
H^{\prime}
, called copies of
x
, but now we add
x_{1}
to
L^{\prime\prime}
, and
x_{2},\ldots,x_{M^{\prime}-1}
to
R^{\prime\prime}
. We add a special edge
(x,x_{1})
, and, for all
2\leq j\leq M-1
, we add a regular edge
(x_{1},x_{j})
to
H^{\prime}
. This concludes the definition of graph
H^{\prime}
.

Note that
H^{\prime}
is a uniform well-structured graph. Since
M=\frac{W\cdot\log^{3}N}{|\beta|}
, and since
|V(H)|=W
, we get that
|V(H^{\prime})|\leq O(W\log^{3}N)
, and moreover,
|E(H^{\prime})\setminus E(H)|\leq O(W\log^{3}N)
. It is easy to modify the adjacency-list representation of
H
to obtain the adjacency-list representation of
H^{\prime}
, by inserting all vertices of
V(H^{\prime})\setminus V(H)
and all edges
E(H^{\prime})\setminus E(H)
into the former, in time
O(W\log^{3}N)
.

6.2.2
Parameters for the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
Problem Instances

Our algorithm will perform
4\lambda
iterations. In every iteration, we will apply Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem to graph
H^{\prime}
, with suitably chosen sets
A^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime}
of vertices of
H^{\prime}
. We now define the parameters that will be used in each such instance of the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, and verify that this setting of the parameter indeed defines a valid instance of the problem.

We start by letting
N^{\prime}=64N\log^{3}N
.
It is easy to see that:

\log N^{\prime}\leq\log N+6\log\log N\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{64c_{2}r}\right)\log N,

(10)

since
r\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
and
N
is large enough. Additionally,
\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}\leq\sqrt{\log N+6\log\log N}\leq\sqrt{\log N}+\sqrt{6\log\log N}
. Therefore:

2^{\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}\leq 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot\log^{6}N\leq 2^{2\sqrt{\log N}}.

(11)

We will use these facts later.

We also use parameters
\Delta^{\prime}=k
, and
{\eta^{\prime}=2^{20}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N}
. In all instances of the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem that we construct, we will ensure that
U_{\lambda}\subseteq A^{\prime}
, while set
B^{\prime}
contains all vertices of
U_{0}
and their copies, and additionally,
|V(H^{\prime})\setminus B^{\prime}|\leq 4\lambda k
holds. We show that all such resulting instances are valid instances of the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem in the following simple claim, whose proof is deferred to Section
C.1
of Appendix.

Claim 6.2
.

Let
A^{\prime},B^{\prime}
be any pair of disjoint subsets of
V(H^{\prime})
with
U_{\lambda}\subseteq A^{\prime}
, and with all vertices of
U_{0}
and their copies lying in
B^{\prime}
, such that
|V(H^{\prime})\setminus B^{\prime}|\leq 4\lambda k
. Then
(H^{\prime},A^{\prime},B^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is a valid instance of the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem.

We are now ready to describe our algorithm, that consists of at most
4\lambda
iterations. The algorithm starts with a collection
{\mathcal{R}}=\emptyset
of paths, and in every iteration, at most
k
paths are added to
{\mathcal{R}}
. We now describe a single iteration.

6.2.3
Description of an Iteration

We describe the
j
th iteration, for
1\leq j\leq 4\lambda
.
We construct a set
B_{j}
of vertices of
H^{\prime}
as follows. We add to
B_{j}
all vertices of
\beta
and their copies. Additionally, we add to
B_{j}
all vertices of
U^{<i}\setminus U_{0}
, except for those that serve as endpoints of the paths that currently lie in
{\mathcal{R}}
. Notice that set
V(H^{\prime})\setminus B_{j}
contains all vertices of
U_{\lambda}
, and, additionally, every vertex
v\in U^{<i}\setminus U_{0}
that serves as an endpoint of some path that currently belongs to
{\mathcal{R}}
. Since at most
k
paths are added to
{\mathcal{R}}
in every iteration, and since the number of iterations is bounded by
4\lambda
, we get that
|V(H^{\prime})\setminus B_{j}|\leq 4\lambda\cdot k
.
We also set
A_{j}=U_{\lambda}
, and we consider the resulting instance
(H^{\prime},A_{j},B_{j},\Delta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},N^{\prime})
of the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem. From
Claim

6.2
, this is a valid
r
-restricted instance of the problem, and therefore we can apply Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
to this instance.
Recall that Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
may return “FAIL”, with probability at most
1/2
. In this case, we simply repeat Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
, until either it does not return “FAIL”, or until it returns “FAIL” in at least
\log^{3}N
consecutive iterations. In the latter case, we terminate our algorithm and return “FAIL”, and we say that iteration
j
of the algorithm terminated with a “ FAIL”. Notice that the probability that iteration
j
terminates with a “FAIL” is at most
2^{-\log^{3}N}
. We now assume that iteration
j
did not terminate with a “FAIL”, and we consider the output of the last execution of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
. Let
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}
be the routing that the algorithm returned. We consider two cases, depending on whether
|{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}|\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{64\lambda}\right)k
or not.

Case 1:
|{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}|\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{64\lambda}\right)k

Recall that the paths in
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}
cause congestion at most
4\eta^{\prime}\cdot\log N^{\prime}
.
We can assume w.l.o.g. that, for every path
R\in{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}
, if
x\in B_{j}
is an inner vertex on path
R
, then
x
also serves as an endpoint of another path in
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}
(as otherwise we can truncate path
R
, so that it terminates at
x
).

We say that a path
Q\in{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}
is
long
if
Q
contains at least
d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{10}N
special edges, and we say that it is
short
otherwise. We use the next simple claim in order to show that
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}
contains many short paths.

Claim 6.3
.

The number of long paths in
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}
is at most
\frac{k}{64\lambda}
.

Proof.

Let
E^{\prime}
be the set of all special edges of
H^{\prime}
that participate in the paths in
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}
. We claim that
|E^{\prime}|\leq 6\lambda k
. Indeed, we can partition
E^{\prime}
into two subsets: set
E^{\prime}_{1}
containing all special edges
e
, such that at least one endpoint of
e
lies in
V(H^{\prime})\setminus B_{j}
, and set
E^{\prime}_{2}
containing all remaining edges. Since
|V(H^{\prime})\setminus B_{j}|\leq 4\lambda k
, we get that
|E^{\prime}_{1}|\leq 4\lambda k
. From the above discussion, for every edge
e\in E^{\prime}_{2}
, at least one endpoint of
e
must serve as an endpoint of a path in
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}
, and so
|E^{\prime}_{2}|\leq|{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}|\leq k
. Therefore, overall,
|E^{\prime}|\leq|E^{\prime}_{1}|+|E^{\prime}_{2}|\leq 6\lambda k
. Since the paths in
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}
cause congestion at most
4\eta^{\prime}\log N^{\prime}
, we get that:

\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}}|E(Q)\cap E^{\prime}|\leq|E^{\prime}|\cdot 4\eta^{\prime}\cdot\log N^{\prime}\leq 24\eta^{\prime}\lambda k\log N^{\prime}\leq 512k\eta^{\prime}\log^{2}N
, since
\lambda=\left\lceil\log(64N)\right\rceil+1\leq 8\log N
and
\log N^{\prime}\leq 2\log N
.

Assume for contradiction that there are more than
\frac{k}{64\lambda}
long paths in
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}
. Then:

\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}}|E(Q)\cap E^{\prime}|\geq\frac{k}{64\lambda}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{10}N\geq\frac{k}{2^{9}}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{9}N>512k\eta^{\prime}\log^{2}N

since
\lambda=\left\lceil\log(64N)\right\rceil+1\leq 8\log N
,
\eta^{\prime}=2^{20}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N
, and
N
is sufficiently large. This is a contradiction since we have shown that
\sum_{Q\in{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}}|E(Q)\cap E^{\prime}|\leq 512k\eta^{\prime}\log^{2}N
.

We let
{\mathcal{R}}_{j}\subseteq{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}
be the set of all short paths, so
|{\mathcal{R}}_{j}|\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{32\lambda}\right)k
. Since the paths in
{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
are short, and since regular and special edges alterante on each path, the length
of each such path is at most
4d^{\prime}\log^{10}N
. The paths in
{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
cause congestion at most
4\eta^{\prime}\cdot\log N^{\prime}\leq d^{\prime}\log^{8}N
, since

\log N^{\prime}\leq 2\log N
from Inequality
10
,
\eta^{\prime}=2^{20}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N
, and we have assumed that
N
is large enough.
We add the paths of
{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
to set
{\mathcal{R}}
, and continue to the next iteration.

Case 2:
|{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}|<\left(1-\frac{1}{64\lambda}\right)k

We now consider the second case, where
|{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}|<\left(1-\frac{1}{64\lambda}\right)k
. Let
A^{\prime}_{j}\subseteq A_{j}
and
B^{\prime}_{j}\subseteq B_{j}
be the sets of vertices that do not serve as endpoints of the paths in
{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}
, so
|A^{\prime}_{j}|,|B^{\prime}_{j}|\geq\frac{k}{64\lambda}
. Consider the cut
(\tilde{Y},\tilde{Y}^{\prime})
in graph
H^{\prime}
that the algorithm for the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem returns, and recall that

A^{\prime}_{j}\subseteq\tilde{Y}
,
B^{\prime}_{j}\subseteq\tilde{Y}^{\prime}
, and additionally:

|E_{H^{\prime}}(\tilde{Y},\tilde{Y}^{\prime})|\leq\frac{64\Delta^{\prime}}{\eta^{\prime}\log^{4}(|V(H^{\prime})|)}+\frac{256|{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}_{j}|}{\eta^{\prime}}\leq\frac{512k}{\eta^{\prime}}\leq\frac{k}{1024d^{\prime}\log^{6}N},

since
\eta^{\prime}=2^{20}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N
.

Since
A^{\prime}_{j}\subseteq\tilde{Y}
, we get that
|\tilde{Y}\cap U_{\lambda}|\geq\frac{k}{64\lambda}
. Next, we show that
|\tilde{Y}|\leq O(k\log N)
,

|A^{\prime}_{j}|\geq|\tilde{Y}\cap\beta|\cdot\log N
,
and that
\tilde{Y}^{\prime}
must contain at least
|\beta|\cdot\left(1-\frac{2}{\log^{3}N}\right)
vertices of
\beta
, in the following simple observation, whose proof is deferred to Section
C.2
of Appendix.

Observation 6.4
.

|\tilde{Y}|\leq O(k\log N)
,
|\tilde{Y}^{\prime}\cap\beta|\geq\max\left\{|\beta|\cdot\left(1-\frac{2}{\log^{3}N}\right),|\beta|-\frac{2k}{\log^{3}N}\right\}
, and
|A^{\prime}_{j}|\geq|\tilde{Y}\cap\beta|\cdot\log N
.

If Case 2 happens, then we transform the cut
(\tilde{Y},\tilde{Y}^{\prime})
in
H^{\prime}
into either a weakly well-structured cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in
H
with
|A^{\prime}|\geq\frac{511|V(H)|}{512}
,
|A^{\prime}\cap\beta|<\frac{2|\beta|}{\log^{3}N}
, and
|E_{H}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|\leq\frac{W}{1024d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N}
; or into a strongly well-structured
\frac{1}{4d^{\prime}}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A),w(B)\geq\frac{k}{2^{14}\log N}
, and
w(A)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap A|
. We do so in three steps. In the first step, we obtain a weakly well-structured cut
(X_{1},X^{\prime}_{1})
in
H
. If this cut has the required properties,
namely that
|X_{1}|\geq\frac{511|V(H)|}{512}
,
|X_{1}\cap\beta|<\frac{2|\beta|}{\log^{3}N}
, and
|E_{H}(X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime})|\leq\frac{W}{1024d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N}
,
then we return this cut and terminate the algorithm. Otherwise, in the second step, we compute a weakly well-structured cut
(X_{2},X^{\prime}_{2})
in
G
. Finally, in the third step, we compute the required strongly well-structured cut
(A,B)
in
G
.

Step 1: a Weakly Well-Structured Cut in
H
.

We start by defining a cut
(X_{1},X^{\prime}_{1})
in
H
with
X_{1}=\tilde{Y}\cap V(H)
and
X^{\prime}_{1}=\tilde{Y}^{\prime}\cap V(H)
. Clearly,
|E_{H}(X_{1},X^{\prime}_{1})|\leq|E_{H^{\prime}}(\tilde{Y},\tilde{Y}^{\prime})|\leq\frac{k}{1024d^{\prime}\log^{6}N}
. Moreover,
|X_{1}|\geq|A^{\prime}_{j}|\geq\frac{k}{64\lambda}
.
Furthermore, from
Observation

6.4
, we get that
|X_{1}|\leq|\tilde{Y}|\leq O(k\log N)
;
|X_{1}|\geq|A^{\prime}_{j}|\geq|\tilde{Y}\cap\beta|\cdot\log N=|X_{1}\cap\beta|\cdot\log N
; and
|X_{1}^{\prime}\cap\beta|\geq|\tilde{Y}^{\prime}\cap\beta|\geq\max\left\{|\beta|\cdot\left(1-\frac{2}{\log^{3}N}\right),|\beta|-\frac{2k}{\log^{3}N}\right\}
.

Next, we turn the cut
(X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime})
into a weakly well-structured one. In order to do so, we consider every vertex
v\in X_{1}
one by one. When vertex
v\in X_{1}
is considered, we check whether there is a regular edge
(v,u)
that is incident to
v
and belongs to
E_{H}(X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime})
. If so, then
v\in L^{\prime}
must hold. We then we move
v
from
X_{1}
to
X^{\prime}_{1}
. Notice that this move may introduce at most one new edge to the cut
E_{H}(X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime})
– the special edge that is incident to
v
, while at least one edge that is incident to
v
is removed from
E_{H}(X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime})
.
Therefore, throughout this transformation,
|E_{H}(X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime})|
cannot grow, and
|X_{1}|
decreases by at most
|E_{H}(X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime})|\leq\frac{k}{d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N}
. Since, at the beginning of Step 1,
|X_{1}|\geq\frac{k}{64\lambda}\geq\frac{k}{256\log N}
held, at the end of this step,
|X_{1}|\geq\frac{k}{512\log N}
holds, and
moreover
|X_{1}|\geq\frac{|X_{1}\cap\beta|\cdot\log N}{2}
. Since we did not remove any vertices from
X^{\prime}_{1}
, it is still the case that

|X_{1}^{\prime}\cap\beta|\geq\max\left\{|\beta|\cdot\left(1-\frac{2}{\log^{3}N}\right),|\beta|-\frac{2k}{\log^{3}N}\right\}
.
It is easy to verify that
every edge in
E_{H}(X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime})
is a special edge, and so
(X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime})
is a weakly well-structured in
H
.
Notice that the time required for this step is bounded by
O(\operatorname{Vol}_{H}(X_{1}))\leq O(|X_{1}|\cdot W)\leq O(k\cdot W\cdot\log N)
.

We now consider two cases. The first case happens if
|X_{1}^{\prime}|<\frac{k}{512}
. Then
we are guaranteed that:

|E_{H}(X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime})|\leq\frac{k}{1024d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N}\leq\frac{|V(H)|}{1024d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N}.

Additionally, we get that
|X_{1}^{\prime}|\leq\frac{k}{512}\leq\frac{|V(H)|}{512}
, and

|X_{1}^{\prime}\cap\beta|\geq|\tilde{Y}^{\prime}\cap\beta|\geq|\beta|\cdot\left(1-\frac{2}{\log^{3}N}\right)
. Therefore,
|X_{1}|\geq\frac{511|V(H)|}{512}
, and
|X_{1}\cap\beta|<\frac{2|\beta|}{\log^{3}N}
. We return cut
(X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime})
in
H
and terminate the algorithm.

From now on, we assume that
|X_{1}^{\prime}|\geq\frac{k}{512}
. We denote
\varphi=\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}
.
Since
|E_{H}(X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime})|\leq\frac{k}{d^{\prime}\log^{6}N}
, while
|X_{1}|\geq\frac{k}{512\log N}
, the sparsity of the cut
(X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime})
in
H
is at most
\frac{1}{d^{\prime}\log^{4}N}=\frac{\varphi}{\log^{4}N}
. Moreover,
(X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime})
is a weakly well-structured cut in
H
and
|X_{1}\cap\beta|<\frac{2k}{\log^{3}N}
.

Step 2: a Weakly Well-Structured Cut in
G
.

Next, we turn the cut
(X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime})
in
H
into a
cut
(X_{2},X_{2}^{\prime})
in
G
, in a natural way: we let
X_{2}=X_{1}\cap V(G)
, and
X_{2}^{\prime}=X_{2}\cap V(G)
.
Recall that the length of every edge in
G
is at most
d^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{\varphi}
. From
Observation

4.4
,
(X_{2},X_{2}^{\prime})
is a weakly well-structured cut in
G
of sparsity at most
\frac{8\varphi}{\log^{4}N}
, with
w(X_{2})\geq\frac{|X_{1}|}{8}\geq\frac{k}{2^{12}\log N}
and
w(X_{2}^{\prime})\geq\frac{|X_{1}^{\prime}|}{8}\geq\frac{k}{2^{12}\log N}
. The time required to compute the cut
(X_{2},X_{2}^{\prime})
in
G
given the cut
(X_{1},X_{1}^{\prime})
in
H
is bounded by
O(|V(H)|)\leq O(W)
. Clearly,
|X_{2}\cap\beta|\leq|X_{1}\cap\beta|<\frac{2k}{\log^{3}N}
.

Step 3: a Strongly Well-Structured Cut in
G
.

Recall that for every special edge
e\in E(G)
,
\ell(e)\leq d^{\prime}\leq\frac{n}{8}
(from the definition of the input to Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
), while
\sum_{e\in E(G)}\ell(e)\geq\frac{n}{2}
.
We can now apply the algorithm from
Observation

4.5
to cut
(X_{2},X_{2}^{\prime})
compute a strongly well-structured cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A)\geq\frac{w(X_{2})}{4}\geq\frac{k}{2^{14}\log N}
and
w(B)\geq\frac{w(X_{2}^{\prime})}{4}\geq\frac{k}{2^{14}\log N}
, whose sparsity is at most
\frac{16\varphi}{\log^{4}N}\leq\frac{1}{4d^{\prime}}
.

We now show that
w(A)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap A|
.
Recall that the algorithm from
Observation

4.5
starts with the cut
(A,B)=(X_{2},X_{2}^{\prime})
, and considers every special edge
e=(u,v)\in E_{G}(B,A)
in turn. For each such edge, it either moves
u
from
B
to
A
, or it moves
v
from
A
to
B
.
We partition the set
A\cap\beta
of vertices into three subsets: set
\beta_{1}
contains all vertices
v\in A\cap\beta
that lied in
X_{2}
; recall that
|\beta_{1}|\leq|X_{2}\cap\beta|\leq\frac{2k}{\log^{3}N}
.
We now define the remaining two sets
\beta_{2}
and
\beta_{3}
.
Each vertex
v\in(\beta\cap A)\setminus\beta_{1}
was added to set
A
by the algorithm from

Observation

4.5
. Therefore, for each such vertex
v
, there is a special edge
(v,u)\in E_{G}(X_{2}^{\prime},X_{2})
. If
u\in\beta
holds (in which case
u\in\beta_{1}
holds), we add
v
to
\beta_{2}
, and otherwise we add it to
\beta_{3}
. It is easy to verify that
|\beta_{2}|\leq|\beta_{1}|\leq\frac{2k}{\log^{3}N}
. Consider now a vertex
v\in\beta_{3}
, and the corresponding special edge
e=(v,u)
. Since
w(v),w(u)\geq 1
, it is easy to see that
|\beta_{3}|\leq w(A)/2
. Overall, we get that:

|\beta\cap A|=|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|+|\beta_{3}|\leq\frac{4k}{\log^{3}N}+\frac{w(A)}{2}\leq 0.51w(A).

Therefore,
w(A)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap A|
, as required.

The running time of the algorithm from
Observation

4.5
is bounded by
O(\operatorname{Vol}_{G}(X_{2}))\leq O(\operatorname{Vol}_{H}(X_{1}))\leq O(|X_{1}|\cdot|V(H)|)\leq O(k\cdot W\cdot\log N)
.
We return the cut
(A,B)
and terminate the algorithm.

Overall, the total time we have spent on iteration
j
when Case 2 happens, excluding the time required to execute Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
, is bounded by
O(k\cdot W\cdot\log N)
.
This concludes the description and the analysis of a single iteration.

6.2.4
Constructing a New
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
.

If any iteration terminated with a “FAIL”, then the algorithm terminates with a “FAIL”. Since the number of iterations is bounded by
4\lambda\leq 32\log N
, and the probability that a single iteration terminates with a “FAIL” is bounded by
2^{-\log^{3}N}
, we get that the overall probability that our algorithm implementing procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
terminates with a “FAIL” is bounded by
\frac{1}{N^{10}}
. We assume from now on that no iteration terminated with a “ FAIL”.
If, in any iteration, Case 2 occurred, then the algorithm terminated with a cut.

We assume from now on that neither of the
4\lambda
iterations terminated with a “FAIL”, and that, in each iteration, Case 1 occurred. Therefore, for all
1\leq j\leq 4\lambda
, we have computed a collection

{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
of paths, with
|{\mathcal{R}}_{j}|\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{32\lambda}\right)k
, such that the length of every path in
{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
is at most
4d^{\prime}\log^{10}N
.
Let
{\mathcal{R}}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{4\lambda}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
. Since, for all
1\leq j\leq 4\lambda
, the paths in
{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
cause congestion at most
d^{\prime}\log^{8}N
, and
\lambda=\left\lceil\log(64N)\right\rceil+1\leq 4\log N
, we get that the paths in
{\mathcal{R}}
cause congestion at most
4d^{\prime}\log^{9}N
.
For every vertex
v\in U_{\lambda}
, let
{\mathcal{R}}(v)\subseteq{\mathcal{R}}
be the set of paths that originate at
v
. Clearly,
|{\mathcal{R}}(v)|\leq 4\lambda
holds.

We say that a vertex
v\in U_{\lambda}
is
good
if
|{\mathcal{R}}(v)|\geq 2\lambda
, and we say that it is bad otherwise. We claim that at most
\frac{k}{4\lambda}
vertices of
U_{\lambda}
may be bad. Indeed, let
\Pi
be the collection of all pairs
(v,j)
, where
v\in U_{\lambda}
and
1\leq j\leq 4\lambda
, such that no path of
{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
originates at
v
. Since, for all
1\leq j\leq 4\lambda
,
|{\mathcal{R}}_{j}|\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{32\lambda}\right)k
, we get that
|\Pi|\leq\frac{k}{32\lambda}\cdot 4\lambda=\frac{k}{8}
. However, if more than
\frac{k}{4\lambda}
vertices of
U_{\lambda}
are bad, then
|\Pi|>\frac{k}{4\lambda}\cdot 2\lambda\geq\frac{k}{2}
, a contradiction.
We let
U^{\prime}\subseteq U_{\lambda}
be the set of all good vertices, so
|U^{\prime}|\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{4\lambda}\right)k
.

Let
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq{\mathcal{R}}
be a set of path that contains, for every vertex
v\in U^{\prime}
, exactly
2\lambda
paths that originate at
v
. From our construction, the paths in
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
have length at most
4d^{\prime}\log^{10}N
each, and they cause congestion at most
4d^{\prime}\log^{9}N
. Additionally, every vertex
x\in\beta
, the number of paths in
{\mathcal{R}}
that terminate at
x
is bounded by:

(4\lambda)\cdot M\leq\frac{4\lambda\cdot W\log^{3}N}{|\beta|}\leq\frac{\lambda\rho}{4},

since
M=\frac{W\cdot\log^{3}N}{|\beta|}
and
\frac{W\cdot\log^{3}N}{|\beta|}\leq\frac{\rho}{16}
from the problem definition.
Our algorithm also ensures that vertex
y\in U^{<i}\setminus\beta
may serve as an endpoint of at most one such path. Consider now some vertex
x\in\beta
, and let
{\mathcal{D}}^{<i}(x)
be the set of all descendant vertices of
x
that belong to layers
U_{1},\ldots,U_{i-1}
, including vertex
x
itself. From Property
L2
of
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
,
|{\mathcal{D}}^{<i}(x)|\leq i\cdot\rho
.
Let
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{x}\subseteq\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
denote the collection of all paths
Q\in\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
, such that
Q
terminates either at one of the vertices in
{\mathcal{D}}^{<i}(x)
. Then
|\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{x}|\leq\frac{\lambda\rho}{4}+\lambda\cdot\rho\leq 1.5\lambda\cdot\rho
.

Notice that for every path
Q\in\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
, if
v\in U^{<i}
is the last endpoint of
Q
, then we can compute a vertex
x\in\beta
with
v\in{\mathcal{D}}^{<i}(x)
in time
O(1)
, using the current
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
. We compute the collection
\beta^{\prime}\subseteq\beta
of vertices, where a vertex
x\in\beta^{\prime}
if and only if some path in
\tilde{R}
terminates at a vertex of
{\mathcal{D}}^{<i}(x)
. This set of vertices can be computed in time
O(|\tilde{R}|)\leq O(\lambda k)\leq O(k\log N)
.

Let
V^{\prime}\subseteq U^{<i}
be the set of vertices that serve as endpoints of the paths in
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
. From our construction, every vertex
v\in V^{\prime}\setminus\beta
serves as an endpoint of exactly one such path.
Next, we construct an auxiliary directed flow network
\tilde{H}
. We start by letting
V(\tilde{H})=U^{\prime}\cup V^{\prime}\cup\beta^{\prime}
. For every path
Q\in\tilde{R}
, if
v
is the first endpoint of
Q
, and
v^{\prime}
is the last endpoint of
Q
then we add a directed edge
e=(v,x)
to the flow network, whose capacity is
1
. We say that edge
e

corresponds to the path
Q
.
For every vertex
u\in V^{\prime}
, if
x\in\beta^{\prime}
is the ancestor of
v
, then we add an edge
(u,x)
of capacity
1
to the graph.
Additionally, we add a source vertex
s
, and connect it to every vertex in
U^{\prime}
with an edge of capacity
1
, and a destination vertex
t
. Every vertex
x\in\beta^{\prime}
connects to
t
with an edge of capacity
\rho
. This concludes the description of the directed flow network
\tilde{H}
. We claim that the value of the maximum
s
-
t
flow in this network is
|U^{\prime}|
. Indeed, since the total capacity of all edges incident to
s
is
|U^{\prime}|
, the value of the maximum
s
-
t
flow cannot be greater than
|U^{\prime}|
. Consider now the following fractional flow in
\tilde{H}
: we send one flow unit on every edge incident to
s
, and, for every path
Q\in\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
, we send
\frac{1}{2\lambda}
flow units on its corresponding edge
(v,u)\in E(\tilde{H})
, and the same amount of flow on edge
(u,x)
, where
x\in\beta^{\prime}
is the ancestor of
u
. Since, for every vertex
v\in U^{\prime}
, set
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
contains exactly
2\lambda
paths originating at
v
, the total amount of flow leaving
v
is exactly
1
.
Since every vertex
u\in V^{\prime}
is an endpoint of at most one path in
\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
, if
x\in\beta^{\prime}
is the ancestor of
u
, then edge
(u,x)
carries at most
1
flow unit.

Consider now some vertex
x\in\beta^{\prime}
, and recall that
|\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{x}|\leq 1.5\lambda\cdot\rho
. Since, for every path
Q\in\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{x}
, the corresponding edge carries
\frac{1}{2\lambda}
flow units, the total amount of flow entering
x
is at most
\frac{1.5\lambda\cdot\rho}{2\lambda}\leq\rho
. We set the flow on the edge
(x,t)
to be equal to the total amount of flow entering
x
. Overall, we obtain a valid
s
-
t
flow in
\tilde{H}
, whose value is
|U^{\prime}|
.

Next, we employ the standard Ford-Fulkerson algorithm in order to compute an integral flow of value
|U^{\prime}|
in
\tilde{H}
. We start by setting the flow on every edge of
\tilde{H}
to
0
, and then perform at most
|U^{\prime}|\leq k
iterations. In every iteration, we compute a residual flow network, along with an augmenting path, and then modify the current flow accordingly. Clearly, every iteration can be executed in time
O(|E(\tilde{H})|)\leq O(k\log N)
, while the number of iterations is bounded by
k
. Altogether, the running time of the algorithm that computes the flow network
\tilde{H}
, and computes a maximum integral
s
-
t
flow in it is bounded by
O(k^{2}\log N)\leq O(k\cdot W\log N)
.

We are now ready to construct a new
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
. The sets
U_{0},\ldots,U_{i-1}
of vertices, and the sets
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{Q}}_{i-1}
of paths remain unchanged.
For all
i<i^{\prime}<\lambda
, we set
U_{i^{\prime}}=\emptyset
.
We also set
U_{i}=U^{\prime}
and
U_{\lambda}=V(H)\setminus\left(\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=0}^{i}U_{i^{\prime}}\right)
. Since
|U^{\prime}|\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{4\lambda}\right)k
, we get that
|U_{\lambda}|\leq\frac{k}{4\lambda}
.
For every vertex
v\in U^{\prime}
, we consider the unique edge
e
of
\tilde{H}
that leaves the vertex
v
and carries one flow unit, and we let
Q\in\tilde{\mathcal{R}}
be the path corresponding to
e
. We then set
K(v)=Q
, and we let
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}=\left\{K(v)\mid v\in U_{i}\right\}
. Since, for every vertex
x\in\beta^{\prime}
, the capacity of the edge
(x,t)
in
\tilde{H}
is
\rho
, this guarantees that the number of descendant-vertices of
x
in
U_{i}
is bounded by
\rho
, as required.
For every vertex
u\in V^{\prime}
, if
x\in\beta^{\prime}
is its ancestor, then the capacity of the edge
(u,x)
is
1
, so
u
may serve as an endpoint in at most
1
path in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
.
Additionally, our construction guarantees that the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
have length at most
4d^{\prime}\log^{9}N
each, and they cause edge-congestion at most
4d^{\prime}\log^{9}N
in
H
, as required. Therefore, we obtain a valid
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
.

6.2.5
Analysis of the Running Time

Recall that the time required to construct the adjacency-list representation of graph
H^{\prime}
from the adjacency-list of
H
is bounded by
O(W\log^{3}N)
. The algorithm consists of
4\lambda=O(\log N)
iterations. In every iteration, we apply Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem to instance
(H^{\prime},A_{j},B_{j},\Delta^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},N^{\prime})
at most
O(\log^{3}N)
times.
Recall that
|V(H^{\prime})|\leq O(W\cdot\log^{3}N)
, and
|V(H^{\prime})\setminus B_{j}|\leq 4\lambda k\leq O(k\log N)
. Therefore,
the running time of each execution of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
is bounded by:

\begin{split}&c_{1}\cdot|V(H^{\prime})|\cdot(|V(H^{\prime})|-|B_{j}|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left(c_{1}\cdot W\cdot k\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+4}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left(c_{1}\cdot W\cdot k\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+10}\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{64c_{2}r}\right)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+4}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left(c_{1}\cdot W\cdot k\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+10}\right)\end{split}

(We have used the fact that, from Inequality
11
,

2^{\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}\leq 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot\log^{6}N
, and from Inequality
10
,
\log N^{\prime}\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{64c_{2}r}\right)\cdot\log N
.

The time spent on executing Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
in all
4\lambda=O(\log N)
iterations is then bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot W\cdot k\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+14}\right).

If the algorithm terminates with a cut, then we may need to invest additional
O(k\cdot W\cdot\log N)
time in order to convert the cut returned by Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
into a desired well structured cut in
H
or in
G
. Lastly, if the algorithm terminates with a new
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
, then the additional time that is required in order to compute the flow network
\tilde{H}
, the maximum integral
s
-
t
flow in it, and then compute the corresponding set
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
of paths, is bounded by
O(k\cdot W\cdot\log N)
. Overall, we get that the running time of the algorithm is bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot W\cdot k\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+14}\right).

6.2.6
Special Case:
k<2^{20}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N
.

It now remains to consider a special case where
k<2^{20}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N
.
In this case, we employ the following simple algorithm. We start with
U^{\prime}=\emptyset
,
{\mathcal{R}}=\emptyset
, and
T=V(H)\setminus U_{\lambda}
.
In every iteration, we will add a single path to
{\mathcal{R}}
, connecting some vertex in
U_{\lambda}
to a vertex of
T
.
For every vertex
x\in\beta
, we maintain a counter
n_{x}
, that counts the number of paths in
{\mathcal{R}}
that terminate at the vertices of
\bigcup_{i^{\prime}<i}{\mathcal{D}}_{i^{\prime}}(x)
. Once this number reaches
\rho
, we remove vertex
x
, and all vertices in
\bigcup_{i^{\prime}<i}{\mathcal{D}}_{i^{\prime}}(x)
from
T
. We also delete from
T
any vertex that serves as an endpoint of a path in
{\mathcal{R}}
, except for the vertices that lie in
\beta
. We initialize
n_{x}=0
for all
x\in\beta
. We will ensure that
|{\mathcal{R}}|\leq k<2^{20}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N
. This ensures that the total number of vertices
x\in\beta
for which
n_{x}=\rho
holds at the end of the algorithm is bounded by
\frac{k}{\rho}
. From Requirement
L2
of the
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
, for each such vertex
x\in\beta
,
|{\mathcal{D}}(x)|\leq\lambda\cdot\rho
, and so the total number of vertices that are ever deleted from
T
over the course of the algorithm is bounded by
\frac{k}{\rho}\cdot\lambda\cdot\rho+k\leq 2\lambda\cdot k\leq 8k\log N
. Therefore, throughout the algorithm,
|V(H)\setminus T|\leq k+8k\log N\leq 9k\log N\leq 2^{24}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{7}N
holds.

We consider every vertex
v\in U_{\lambda}
one by one. When vertex
v
is considered, we either compute a path
K(v)
connecting
v
to a vertex of
T
, such that no inner vertices on
K(v)
lie in
T
; or we compute a set
S_{v}\subseteq V(H)\setminus T
of vertices with no outgoing edges, that contains
v
. In the former case, the length of
K(v)
is at guaranteed to be at most
|V(H)\setminus T|\leq 2^{14}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{7}N\leq 4d^{\prime}\log^{10}N
. We add vertex
v
to set
U^{\prime}
, add path
K(v)
to set
{\mathcal{R}}
, and, if
u\in T
is the other endpoint of path
K(v)
, and
x\in\beta
is the vertex for which
u\in{\mathcal{D}}(x)
, we increase the counter
n_{x}
, and, if
u\not\in\beta
, delete
u
from
T
. If
n_{x}
reaches
\rho
, then we delete from
T
all vertices of
\bigcup_{i^{\prime}<i}{\mathcal{D}}_{i^{\prime}}(x)
. We then continue to the next iteration.
In the latter case, if we obtain a set
S_{v}\subseteq V(H)\setminus T
of vertices with no outgoing edges with
v\in S_{v}
, we just continue to the next iteration.

If, at the end of this procedure,
|U^{\prime}|\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{4\lambda}\right)k
, then we obtain a new
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
by keeping the layers
U_{0},\ldots,U_{i-1}
unchanged, together with the corresponding collections
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{Q}}_{i-1}
of paths, setting
U_{i}=U^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}={\mathcal{R}}=\left\{K(v)\mid v\in U^{\prime}\right\}
.
Note that the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
cause congestion at most
k\leq 2^{20}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N\leq 4d^{\prime}\log^{9}N
.
We also let
U_{\lambda}
contain all vertices that originally lied in
U_{\lambda}
and were not added to
U^{\prime}
, and, for all
i<i^{\prime}<\lambda
, we set
U_{i^{\prime}}=\emptyset
.
From our construction, for every vertex
x\in\beta
,
|{\mathcal{D}}_{i}(x)|\leq\rho
holds, as required, and every vertex
v\in U^{<i}
serves as endpoint of at most one path in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
.
It is immediate to verify that we obtain a valid
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
, with
|U_{\lambda}|\leq\frac{k}{4\lambda}
.

Assume now that, at the end of the algorithm,
|U^{\prime}|<\left(1-\frac{1}{4\lambda}\right)k
holds.
Consider the cut
(\tilde{Y},\tilde{Y}^{\prime})
in
H
, where
\tilde{Y}=\bigcup_{v\in U_{\lambda}\setminus U^{\prime}}S_{v}
and
\tilde{Y}^{\prime}=V(H)\setminus\tilde{Y}
. Clearly,
E_{H}(\tilde{Y},\tilde{Y}^{\prime})=\emptyset
. Moreover, from our construction,
|\tilde{Y}|\geq|U_{\lambda}\setminus U^{\prime}|\geq\frac{k}{4\lambda}\geq\frac{k}{512\log N}
.
At the same time,
\tilde{Y}\subseteq V(H)\setminus T
, so
|\tilde{Y}|\leq|V(H)\setminus T|\leq 2^{24}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{7}N
.
Since the total number of vertices
x\in\beta
with
n_{x}=\rho
at the end of the algorithm is bounded by
\frac{k}{\rho}\leq\frac{W}{\rho}
, while, from the problem definition,
\frac{W\cdot\log^{3}N}{|\beta|}\leq\frac{\rho}{16}
, we get that

W\leq\frac{|\beta|\cdot\rho}{16\log^{3}N}
, and
|\tilde{Y}^{\prime}\cap\beta|\geq|\beta|-\frac{W}{\rho}\geq|\beta|\left(1-\frac{2}{\log^{3}N}\right)
.

Since
E_{H}(\tilde{Y},\tilde{Y}^{\prime})=\emptyset
, cut
(\tilde{Y},\tilde{Y}^{\prime})
is weakly well-structured in
H
, and it is
\frac{1}{d^{\prime}\log^{4}N}
-sparse. If
|\tilde{Y}^{\prime}|<\frac{k}{512}
,
then we return cut
(\tilde{Y},\tilde{Y}^{\prime})
in
H
, that is guaranteed to have all required properties. Otherwise, we transform cut
(\tilde{Y},\tilde{Y}^{\prime})
into a a strongly well-structured
\frac{1}{4d^{\prime}}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A),w(B)\geq\frac{k}{2^{14}\log N}
and
w(A)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap A|
, exactly as before, and output this cut as the outcome of the algorithm.

We now provide an algorithm to efficiently implement the above procedure. Throughout the algorithm, we denote
Z=V(H)\setminus T
. Each vertex
v\in Z
is classified as either
marked
or
unmarked
; a vertex
v\in Z
is marked only if we have correctly established that no path connects
v
to a vertex of
T
. Notice that vertices may leave
T
, but they cannot join
T
. Therefore, if, at some time
\tau
during the algorithm, no path conencts
v
to vertices of
T
, then this remains so for the remainder of the algortihm. For every vertex
v\in Z
that is unmarked, we maintain a heap
h(v)
that contains all unmarked vertices
u\in Z
with edge
(v,u)\in E(H)
, and also all vertices
u^{\prime}\in T
with edge
(v,u^{\prime})\in E(H)
.

At the beginning of the algorithm,
Z=U_{\lambda}
, and all vertices in
Z
are unmarked. Notice that the time that is required in order to construct the initial heaps
h(v)
for vertices
v\in Z
is bounded by
O(|U_{\lambda}|\cdot|V(H)|)\leq O(k\cdot W)
. Next, we perform iterations. In every iteration, we select an arbitrary vertex
v\in Z
, and perform a DFS, starting from
v
, in the graph that is obtained from
H
by deleting the marked vertices, until a vertex of
T
is reached. In other words, when the DFS search arrives at some vertex
z
, it only explores edges
(z,u)
with
u\in h(z)
. Whenever the DFS search backtracks from a vertex
a
, we know that no path connects
a
to a vertex of
T
, so we mark
a
. We then examine all edges
(b,a)\in E(H)
with
b\in Z
, and delete
a
from the corresponding heap
h(b)
. The time that the DFS spent on exploring vertex
a
, on marking it, and on deleting it from the heaps of vertices
b\in Z
with
(b,a)\in E(H)
is
charged
to vertex
a
itself. Excluding the time charged to the newly marked vertices, the DFS search from a single vertex
v
takes time
O(|Z|)=O(k\log N)
. If, as the result of the search, we arrive at a vertex of
T
, then we obtain the desired path
K(v)
. Otherwise, vertex
v
becomes marked as well, and we process it like all other marked vertices. Notice that the number of such DFS searches that our algorithm performs is bounded by
|U_{\lambda}|\leq k
, and so the total time spent on the DFS searches, excluding the time charged to the marked vertices, is bounded by
O(k^{2}\log N)\leq O(k\cdot W\cdot\log N)
.

Recall that, throughout the algorithm,
|Z|=|V(H)\setminus T|\leq O(k\log N)
holds. Every vertex in
Z
may be marked at most once. When a vertex
a\in Z
is marked, we need to consider all vertices
b\in Z
with
(b,a)\in E(H)
, and delete
a
from the heap
h(b)
. Since
|Z|\leq O(k\log N)
, we spend
O(k\log N)
to process each newly marked vertex, and
O(k^{2}\log^{2}N)\leq O(k\cdot W\log^{2}N)
time on all marked vertices throughout the algorithm.

Once every vertex of
U_{\lambda}
is either added to
U^{\prime}
or is marked, the algorithm terminates. The set
\tilde{Y}
of vertices can then be constructed by simply including all marked vertices of
Z
in it. From the above discussion, the total running time of the algorithm for this special case is
O(k\cdot W\cdot\log^{2}N)
.
This completes the proof of
Claim

6.1
.

6.3
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
Problem

Suppose we are given a perfect well-structured graph
G
with a proper assignment
\ell(e)
of lengths to its edges, together with a parameter
d^{\prime}\geq 1
, and a subset
\beta\subseteq V(G)
of vertices of
G
.
Assume further that
G
undergoes an online sequence of updates of the following two kinds: (i) double the length of a special edge
e\in E(G)
; and (ii) delete a vertex
v\in\beta
from the set
\beta
.
Intuitively, the purpose of the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem is to maintain the
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
in
G
with
U_{\lambda}=\emptyset
, except that we slightly relax Requirement
L1
, and allow the lengths of the paths in
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
to be bounded by
16d^{\prime}\log^{10}N
, instead of
4d^{\prime}\log^{10}N
.
The parameter
\rho
corresponding to the data structure is
\rho=\frac{2^{12}\cdot W^{0}\cdot\log^{3}N}{|\beta|}
, where
W^{0}
is the initial total weight of all vertices of
G
.
The algorithm is allowed, at any time, to return a strongly well-structured
\frac{1}{4d^{\prime}}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap A|
. Following that, if
w(A)\leq w(B)
, then the vertices of
A
, and the endpoints of the edges in
E_{G}(A,B)
are deleted from
G
, while otherwise the vertices of
B
, and the endpoints of the edges in
E_{G}(A,B)
are deleted from
G
. In either case, the algorithm must continue with the resulting graph. The algorithm is also allowed, at any time, to return a weakly well-structured cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in graph
H
– the subdivided graph corresponding to
G
– with
|A^{\prime}|\geq\frac{511|V(H)|}{512}
,
|A^{\prime}\cap\beta|<\frac{2|\beta|}{\log^{3}N}
, and
|E_{H}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|\leq\frac{|V(H)|}{1024d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N}
, after which the algorithm terminates. It is also allowed, at any time, to terminate with a “FAIL”, but we require that the probability that this ever happens is bounded by
1/N^{7}
. We model this algorithm as consisting of a number of phases, where every phase consists of a number of iterations. In every iteration, the algorithm must either (i) produce a valid
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
for the current graph
G
with
U_{\lambda}=\emptyset
(with the relaxed Requirement
L1
), after which the phase terminates; or (ii) produce a strongly well-structured
\frac{1}{4d^{\prime}}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
, after which
G
is updated as described above, and some vertices may be deleted from
\beta
; or (iii) produce a weakly well-structured cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in graph
H
, with
|A^{\prime}|\geq\frac{511|V(H)|}{512}
,
|A^{\prime}\cap\beta|<\frac{2|\beta|}{\log^{3}N}
, and
|E_{H}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|\leq\frac{|V(H)|}{1024d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N}
, after which the algorithm terminates. At the end of each phase, the algorithm is given a collection of special edges of
G
whose lengths are doubled, and a collection of vertices that are deleted from
\beta
, and then a new phase begins. The algorithm only needs to continue as long as
|\beta|
remains sufficiently large (it most contain at least
1/4
of the vertices from the original set
\beta
), and as long as the total increase in the lengths of the edges is not too large. We now define the total increase of edge lengths formally, followed by a formal defintion of the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem.

Definition 6.5
(Total increase in edge lengths).

Let
G
be a perfect well-structured graph with a proper assignment
\ell(e)
of lengths to its edges, that undergoes an online sequence of updates, consisting of the following operations: (i) double the length of a special edge
e\in E(G)
; and (ii) delete a vertex from
V(G)
, together with its incident edges. Consider any time
\tau
during the update sequence, and denote by
E^{0}
the set of special edges in the initial graph
G
. For each edge
e\in E^{0}
, let
\ell^{0}(e)
be the length of
e
at the beginning of the algorithm, and let
\ell^{\tau}(e)
be the length of
e
at time
\tau
if
e\in E(G)
at time
\tau
; or the length of
e
just before it was deleted from
\tau
otherwise. Then the total increase in edge lengths at time
\tau
is:
\sum_{e\in E^{0}}\left(\ell^{\tau}(e)-\ell^{0}(e)\right)
.

We are now ready to define the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem.

Definition 6.6
(
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem).

The input to the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem consists of a perfect well-structured graph
G=(L,R,E)
with
|V(G)|=n
, and a proper assignment
\ell(e)
of lengths to the edges
e\in E
, that are given in the adjacency list representation, together with a subdivided graph
H
corresponding to
G
in the adjacency-list representation. Let
W^{0}
denote the weight of all vertices in this initial graph
G
. Additionally, we are given a parameter
N\geq\max\left\{n,W^{0}/8\right\}
that is greater than a large enough constant, and parameters
d^{\prime}\geq 1
, and
1\leq r\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
, such that
d^{\prime}\leq\min\left\{\frac{2^{r\sqrt{\log N}}}{\log^{8}N},\frac{n}{8}\right\}
. Lastly, we are given a set
\beta\subseteq V(H)
of vertices, with
|\beta|\geq\frac{W^{0}}{256d^{\prime}}
. We denote by
\beta^{0}
the initial set
\beta
.

The algorithm consists of a number of phases, and every phase is partitioned into iterations. In every iteration, the algorithm is required to return one of the following:

O1.

either a valid
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
in the current graph
G
with the parameter
