\rho=\frac{2^{12}\cdot W^{0}\cdot\log^{3}N}{|\beta^{0}|}
, in which Requirement
L1
is relaxed to allow the lengths of the paths in
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
to be bounded by
16d^{\prime}\log^{10}N
and
U_{\lambda}=\emptyset
;

O2.

or a strongly well-structured
\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap A|
;

O3.

or a weakly well-structured cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in graph
H
, with
|A^{\prime}|\geq\frac{511|V(H)|}{512}
,
|A^{\prime}\cap\beta|<\frac{2|\beta|}{\log^{3}N}
, and
|E_{H}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|\leq\frac{|V(H)|}{1024d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N}
.

In the case of Outcome
O3
, the algorithm terminates. In the case of Outcome
O2
, the endpoints of the edges in
E_{G}(A,B)
are deleted from
G
. Additionally, if
w(A)\leq w(B)
, then the vertices of
A
are deleted from
G
, and otherwise the vertices of
B
are deleted from
G
. All the vertices of
\beta
that are deleted from
G
are also deleted from
\beta
, along with some additional vertices that may be deleted from
\beta
(given by the adversary). In case of Outcome
O1
, the current phase terminates, and the algorithm is given a collection of special edges of
G
, whose lengths are doubled, together with a list of vertices that are deleted from
\beta
.

Once the total increase in the edge lengths becomes greater than
8n
, or at least
3/4
of vertices in the initial set
\beta
are deleted from
\beta
, the algorithm terminates. The algorithm may also return “FAIL” and terminate at any time, but we require that the probability that this ever happens is bounded by
\frac{1}{N^{7}}
.

Next, we provide an algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem, that relies on the algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, whose existence is assumed in
Theorem

4.12
. We then show that the algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem with the properties required in
Theorem

4.12
follows immediately from the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem. This algorithm will also be used as one of the main ingredients in our algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, that will complete the proof of
Theorem

4.12
.

6.4
An Algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
Problem

In this subsection, we provide an algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem, that is summarized in the following theorem.

Theorem 6.7
.

Suppose that, for some parameter
r\geq 1
, there exists a randomized algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, that, given as an input an instance
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of the problem with
|V(G)|=n
,
has running time at most
c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}}
.
Then there is a randomized algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem, for the same value of parameter
r
, whose running time is at most:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+26}\right),

where
\Lambda
is the total increase in the edge lengths over the course of the algorithm,
\beta^{\prime}
is the set of all vertices deleted from
\beta
over the course of the algorithm (including the vertices that are deleted from
H
following updates to graph
G
), and
W^{0}
is the total initial weight of the vertices in the input graph
G
.

The remainder of this subsection is dedicated to the proof of
Theorem

6.7
. The algorithm is quite straightforward: in every iteration we simply apply the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
problem from
Claim

6.1
to the current graph
G
, and the current
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
. The only subtlety is that the algorithm requires that the length of every edge in
G
is at least
d^{\prime}
. If this is not the case, and
e=(u,v)
is a special edge of length greater than
d^{\prime}
, then we can define a strongly well-structured cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
A=\left\{u\right\}
and
B=V(G)\setminus\left\{u\right\}
, whose sparsity is at least
1/d^{\prime}
(since
w(u),w(v)\geq d^{\prime}
), and return this cut. We also need to verify that, whenever we apply the algorithm from
Claim

6.1
to the current graph
G
, we indeed obtain a valid instance of the
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
problem. The main difficulty is in the analysis of the running time of this algorithm, that we discuss below.

6.4.1
The Data Structures

Recall that we are given as input a perfect well-structured graph
G=(L,R,E)
with
|V(G)|=n
, and a proper assignment
\ell(e)
of lengths to the edges
e\in E
, that are given in the adjacency list representation, together with a subdivided graph
H=(L^{\prime},R^{\prime},E^{\prime})
corresponding to
G
in the adjacency-list representation.
We denote by
W^{0}
the weight of all vertices in this initial graph
G
. Recall that we are also given a parameter
N\geq\max\left\{n,W^{0}/8\right\}
that is greater than a large enough constant, and parameters
d^{\prime}\geq 1
, and
1\leq r\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
, such that
d^{\prime}\leq\min\left\{\frac{2^{r\sqrt{\log N}}}{\log^{8}N},\frac{n}{8}\right\}
.
We also denote by
\beta^{0}
the initial set
\beta\subseteq V(H)
of vertices that is given as part of the problem input; recall that
|\beta^{0}|\geq\frac{W^{0}}{256d^{\prime}}
.
Throughout, we use the parameter
\rho=\frac{2^{12}\cdot W^{0}\cdot\log^{3}N}{|\beta^{0}|}
.

Recall that graph
H
is obtained from
G
by replacing every special edge
e=(u,v)
with a new path, that we denote by
P^{*}(e)
, and call
the path representing
e
in
H
. The number of special edges on
P^{*}(e)
is exactly
\ell(e)
. Therefore,
|V(H)|=\sum_{v\in V(G)}w(v)
, and initially,
|V(H)|=W^{0}
.

Assume that at some time during the algorithm’s execution, the length
\ell(e)
of a special edge
e\in E(G)
is doubled. Then for every special edge
e^{\prime}=(x,y)
on path
P^{*}(e)
, we replace the edge by a path
(x,x^{\prime},y^{\prime},y)
, where
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}
are new vertices, edges
(x,x^{\prime})
and
(y^{\prime},y)
are special, and edge
(x,y^{\prime})
is regular; we call this process the
splitting
of edge
e^{\prime}
. Notice that, since
(x,y)
is a special edge of
H
,
x\in R^{\prime}
and
y\in L^{\prime}
must hold. We add
x^{\prime}
to
L^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}
to
R^{\prime}
. It is easy to verify that
H
remains the subdivided graph of
G
after this update. Since the total increase in the lengths of the edges of
G
over the course of the algorithm is bounded by
\Lambda
, the total time required to maintain graph
H
in the adjacency-list representation, once the representation of the initial graph
H
is given, is bounded by
O(\Lambda)
.

Our algorithm maintains a valid
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
for the current graph
G
, with the parameter
\rho
defined above, and with Requirement
L1
relaxed to allow the lengths of the paths in
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
to be bounded by
16d^{\prime}\log^{10}N
. However, whenever a new path is added to set
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
, we require that its initial length is bounded by
4d^{\prime}\log^{10}N
.

At the beginning of the algorithm, we set
U_{0}=\beta
,
U_{1}=U_{2}=\cdots=U_{\lambda-1}=\emptyset
, and
U_{\lambda}=V(H)\setminus\beta
. For all
1\leq i<\lambda
, we also set
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}=\emptyset
. It is easy to verity that this is a valid
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
. Throughout, we denote
{\mathcal{Q}}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
.
We also maintain, for every vertex
x\in\beta
and layer
1\leq i<\lambda
, the set
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}(x)
of the descendants of
x
at layer
i
, and, for every vertex
v\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}{\mathcal{D}}_{i}(x)
, a pointer from
v
to
x
. At the beginning of the algorithm, for all

x\in\beta
and
1\leq i<\lambda
, we set
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}(x)=\emptyset
. Additionally, for every layer
1\leq i<\lambda
, for every vertex
v\in U^{<\lambda}
, if
v
serves as an endpoint of a path
K(u)\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
, then we maintain a pointer from
v
to
u
.

For every edge
e\in E(H)
, we will maintain a list
S(e)
of all vertices

v\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}U_{i}
with
e\in K(v)
. The task of maintaining these lists is quite straightforward. We will ignore it when we describe the remainder of the algorithm below, but we will show how to extend the algorithm in order to maintain these lists efficiently later.

For every vertex
v\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}U_{i}
, we will maintain the length
\hat{\ell}(v)
of the path
K(v)\in{\mathcal{Q}}
.
When the path
K(v)
is first added to
{\mathcal{Q}}
, we initialize its length
\hat{\ell}(v)
. Then whenever a special edge on path
K(v)
undergoes splitting, we increase
\hat{\ell}(v)
by
1
.
The time that is required in order to maintain the values
\hat{\ell}(v)
for vertices
v\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}U_{i}
is clearly subsumed by the time required to compute and to maintain the collection
{\mathcal{Q}}
of paths. In our description of the remainder of the algorithm below, we will ignore the task of maintaining these values.

Before we provide the algorithm, we need one more definition, and a simple bound. We denote by
G^{0}
the initial graph
G
. For every vertex
v\in V(G^{0})
, we denote by
w^{0}(v)
the weight of
v
at the beginning of the algorithm. If
v
lies in
G
at the end of the algorithm, then we let
w^{*}(v)
be the weight of
v
at the end of the algorithm, and otherwise we let
w^{*}(v)
be the weight of
v
just before it is deleted from
G
. We also denote
W^{*}=\sum_{v\in V(G^{0})}w^{*}(v)
. Since the total increase in the lengths of edges over the course of the algorithm is at most
8n
, we get that
W^{*}\leq W^{0}+16n
. Since
N\geq\max\left\{n,W^{0}/8\right\}
, we get that
W^{*}\leq 32N
.

6.4.2
The Algorithm

We now describe the algorithm for executing a single phase.
We denote by
M
the multiset of all vertices that ever lied in
U_{\lambda}
over the course of the algorithm. If a vertex is added to and removed from
U_{\lambda}
multiple times during the algorithm, we add multiple copies of that vertex to
M
. We will bound the running time of the algorithm in terms of
|M|
, and then later we will bound
|M|
. Whenever our algorithm computes a strongly well-structured
\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap A|
, we call Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}
that
we describe below. Following this procedure, the vertices that serve as endpoints of the edges in
E_{G}(A,B)
, along with the vertices of
A
(if
w(A)\leq w(B)
) or the vertices of
B
(otherwise) are deleted from
G
.
We partition the execution of a single phase into three stages.

First stage.

The first stage continues as long as the current graph
G
contains an edge of length greater than
d^{\prime}
. Let
e=(u,v)
be any such edge, and recall that it must be a special edge. Consider the cut
(A,B)
in
G
, where
A=\left\{u\right\}
, and
B=V(G)\setminus\left\{u\right\}
. It is immediate to verify that this cut is strongly well-structured. Moreover, since
w(u)=w(v)=\ell(e)
, while
|E_{G}(A,B)|=1
, the sparsity of the cut
(A,B)
is at most
\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}
, and
w(A)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap A|
(as
|A|=1
). We then output the cut
(A,B)
, and call Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}
that is described below.
This completes the description of the algorithm for the first stage of a phase. At the end of the first stage, graph
G
does not contain any edge whose length is greater than
d^{\prime}
.

Second stage.

The second stage is executed as long as
U_{\lambda}\neq\emptyset
.
We let
1\leq i<\lambda
be the smallest index for which
\sum_{j=i+1}^{\lambda}|U_{j}|\geq 2^{\lambda-i-1}
holds. Notice that, since
|U_{\lambda}|\geq 1
, if we choose
i=\lambda-1
, then
\sum_{j=i+1}^{\lambda}|U_{j}|\geq 2^{\lambda-i-1}=1
holds, so such an index
i
must exist.
We need the following claim.

Claim 6.8
.

\sum_{j=i}^{\lambda}|U_{j}|\leq 2^{\lambda-i}
.

Proof.

Assume otherwise, that is,
\sum_{j=i}^{\lambda}|U_{j}|>2^{\lambda-i}
. Since we did not choose index
i-1
instead of
i
, it must be the case that
i=1
. Recall however that
\lambda=\left\lceil\log(64N)\right\rceil+1
, and so
2^{\lambda-1}\geq 64N
. Therefore,
\sum_{j=1}^{\lambda}|U_{j}|\geq 64N
.
However,
|V(H)|\leq W^{*}<64N
must hold, a contradiction.

For every layer
i\leq j\leq\lambda-1
and vertex
v\in U_{j}
, we delete
v
from
U_{j}
and add it to
U_{\lambda}
. We also delete path
K(v)
from set
{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}
, and delete
v
from the set
{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(x)
, where
x
is its ancestor-vertex in
\beta
.
We denote by
k=|U_{\lambda}|
, so
2^{\lambda-i-1}\leq k\leq 2^{\lambda-i}
.
Notice that the running time that we have spent so far in this iteration is
O(2^{\lambda-i})+O(\lambda\cdot\log n)\leq O(k)+O(\log^{2}N)
.

Next, we apply the algorithm for Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
from
Claim

6.1
to the current graphs
G
,
H
, and the index
i
. For brevity of notation, we denote this procedure by
\Pi
. We verify that we obtain a valid input to Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
.

First, the current weight of all vertices in
G
is bounded by
W^{*}\leq 32N
, so
N\geq\max\left\{n,W/32\right\}
holds. The inequalities
1\leq r\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
and
1\leq d^{\prime}\leq\min\left\{\frac{2^{r\sqrt{\log N}}}{\log^{8}N},\frac{n}{8}\right\}
hold from the definition of the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem. The length of every edge in
G
is at most
d^{\prime}
, and, for all
i\leq j<\lambda
,
U_{j}=\emptyset
holds, while
|U_{\lambda}|\leq 2^{\lambda-i}
from
Claim

6.8
.
Recall that we have set
\rho=\frac{2^{12}\cdot W^{0}\cdot\log^{3}N}{|\beta^{0}|}
.
Since
|\beta^{0}|\leq W^{0}
, we get that
\rho\geq\log^{2}N
. Let
W=\sum_{v\in V(G)}w(v)
.
From the problem definition,
W\leq W^{*}\leq 32W^{0}
, and
|\beta|\geq\frac{|\beta^{0}|}{4}
. Therefore:

\frac{W\cdot\log^{3}N}{|\beta|}\leq\frac{128W^{0}\cdot\log^{3}N}{|\beta^{0}|}\leq\frac{\rho}{16},

as required.

It remains to verify that
|\beta|\geq\frac{W}{d^{\prime}\log^{2}N}
holds. From the definition of the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem,
|\beta^{0}|\geq\frac{W^{0}}{256d^{\prime}}
, and, throughout the algorithm,
|\beta|\geq\frac{|\beta^{0}|}{4}\geq\frac{W^{0}}{1024d^{\prime}}
holds. Since the total weight
W
of the vertices of
G
is bounded by
W^{*}\leq 32W^{0}
, and
N
is sufficiently large, we get that
|\beta|\geq\frac{W^{0}}{1024d^{\prime}}\geq\frac{W}{2^{15}d^{\prime}}\geq\frac{W}{d^{\prime}\log^{2}N}
holds. Therefore, we obtain a valid input for Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
.
Recall that the running time of procedure
\Pi
is bounded by:

\begin{split}&O\left(c_{1}\cdot W\cdot k\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+14}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left(c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot k\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+14}\right),\end{split}

and the probability that it returns “FAIL” is at most
\frac{1}{N^{10}}
. If the procedure returns “FAIL”, then we terminate our algorithm, and return “FAIL” as well. Otherwise, we consider three cases.

In the first case, the procedure returns a weakly well-structured cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in
H
with
|A^{\prime}|\geq\frac{511|V(H)|}{512}
,
|A^{\prime}\cap\beta|<\frac{2|\beta|}{\log^{3}N}
, and
|E_{H}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|\leq\frac{|V(H)|}{1024d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N}
. In this case, we return the cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, and the algorithm terminates. We say that Procedure
\Pi
produced a type-1 outcome in this case.

In the second case, the procedure returns a valid
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
, in which, for all
i<i^{\prime}<\lambda
,
U_{i^{\prime}}=\emptyset
and
|U_{\lambda}|\leq\frac{k}{4\lambda}
holds; additionally, layers
U_{1},\ldots,U_{i-1}
, together with the corresponding collections of paths
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{Q}}_{i-1}
remain the same as in the input
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
. In this case we say that Procedure
\Pi
produced a type-2 outcome. Notice that, at the beginning of the procedure,
|U_{\lambda}|=k
held, while at the end of the procedure,
|U_{\lambda}|\leq\frac{k}{4\lambda}
. Therefore, at least
k\left(1-\frac{1}{4\lambda}\right)
vertices were removed from
U_{\lambda}
by this procedure. For each such vertex
v
, we
charge
the vertex
O\left(c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+14}\right)
units for the execution of the procedure, so that the total charge to all vertices that were removed from
U_{\lambda}
by the procedure is at least the total running time of the procedure.

Lastly, we consider the third case, where Procedure
\Pi
returned a strongly well-structured
\frac{1}{4d^{\prime}}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A),w(B)\geq\frac{k}{2^{14}\log N}
and
w(A)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap A|
. In this case, we say that the outcome of Procedure
\Pi
is of type
3
.
We then output the cut
(A,B)
, and call Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}
that is described below.
Recall that, following this cut, all vertices that serve as endpoints of the edges in
E_{G}(A,B)
, and also either all vertices of
A
or all vertices of
B
, are deleted from
G
. Let
Z
denote the set of all vertices that are deleted from
G
.
Notice
w(Z)\geq\min\left\{w(A),w(B)\right\}\geq\frac{k}{2^{14}\log N}
. For every vertex
v\in Z
, we charge the vertex
O\left(c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot w(v)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+15}\right)
units for the execution of the iteration, so that the total charge to all vertices of
Z
is at least as high as the running time of the iteration.

Third Stage.

The second stage terminates once we obtain a valid
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
(with relaxed Requirement
L1
), with
U_{\lambda}=\emptyset
. Recall that, following this, the algorithm is given a set
E^{\prime}\subseteq E(G)
of special edges whose lengths need to be doubled, and a set
\hat{\beta}\subseteq\beta
of vertices, that need to be deleted from
\beta
.
For every special edge
e\in E^{\prime}
, we consider each of the special edges
e^{\prime}\in P^{*}(e)
one by one. We start by performing a splitting update of the edge
e^{\prime}
, that introduces two new special edges
e_{1},e_{2}
in graph
H
, that replace edge
e^{\prime}
, and two new vertices, that we add to set
U_{\lambda}
.
We initialize
S(e_{1}^{\prime})=S(e_{2}^{\prime})=\emptyset
. For every vertex
v\in S(e)
, we update the corresponding path
K(v)
by splitting the edge
e^{\prime}
, and we update the length
\hat{\ell}(v)
of the path by increasing it by (additive)
2
. If
\hat{\ell}(v)
becomes greater than
16d^{\prime}\log^{10}N
, then let
U_{j}
be the layer to which
v
belongs. We delete
v
from
U_{j}
and add it to
U_{\lambda}
. We also delete the path
K(v)
from
{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}
, and we say that path
K(v)
is
destroyed
. We also delete
v
from set
{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(x)
, where
x\in\beta
is the ancestor of
v
. Let
\Lambda^{\prime}
denote the total initial length of the edges in
E^{\prime}
, so the lengths of all edges increased by additive
\Lambda^{\prime}
in this iteration, and the number of edge-splitting operations performed in graph
H
is bounded by
\Lambda^{\prime}
. Since, from Requirement
L1
, for all
1\leq i<\lambda
, the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
cause edge-congestion at most
4d^{\prime}\log^{9}N
, and since
\lambda\leq O(\log N)
, we get that, for every edge
e^{\prime}\in E(H)
,
|S(e)|\leq O(d^{\prime}\log^{10}N)
. Therefore, the total running time of the algorithm for Stage 3 so far is
O(\Lambda^{\prime}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{10}N)
.

Next, we process the vertices of
\hat{\beta}
, that are deleted from set
\beta
.
We consider the vertices
x\in\hat{\beta}
one by one. For each such vertex
x
, for every layer
1\leq j<\lambda
, and for every descendant
v\in{\mathcal{D}}_{i}(x)
of
x
at layer
j
, we delete
v
from
U_{j}
and add it to
U_{\lambda}
. We also delete the path
K(v)
from
{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}
(we may sometimes say that the path is
destroyed
).
This completes the description of the algorithm for the third stage of a phase. Recall that, from Property
L2
of the
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
, for every vertex
x\in\beta
, for every layer
1\leq j\leq\lambda-1
,
|{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(x)|\leq\rho
. Therefore,
the total number of vertices that are added to
U_{\lambda}
due to the deletion of their ancestors from
\beta
is bounded by
O(|\hat{\beta}|\cdot\rho\cdot\lambda)\leq O(|\hat{\beta}|\cdot\rho\cdot\log N)
.
The running time of Stage 3 is bounded by
O(\Lambda^{\prime}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{10}N)+O(|\hat{\beta}|\cdot\rho\cdot\log N)\leq O(\Lambda^{\prime}\cdot W^{0}\cdot\log^{10}N)+O(|\hat{\beta}|\cdot\rho\cdot\log N)
, since
d^{\prime}\leq n\leq W^{0}
from the problem definition.
This completes the description of the algorithm for a single phase.

Recall that
\Lambda
bounds the total increase in the lengths of edges of the course of the algorithm.
Let
\hat{W}
denote the total weight of all vertices that were ever deleted from
G
over the course of the algorithm, and let
\beta^{\prime}
be the set of all vertices that were deleted from
\beta
over the course of the algorithm. Observe that the vertices of
\beta^{0}\setminus\beta^{\prime}
remain in
G
throughout the algorithm, and so:

\hat{W}\leq W^{*}-|\beta^{0}\setminus\beta^{\prime}|\leq W^{0}+2\Lambda-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|.

(12)

From the above discussion, the total running time of algorithms for stages 1, 2 and 3 (excluding the running times of the executions of Procedure
\Pi
and Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}
), over the course of the entire algorithm, is bounded by:

O(|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho\cdot\log N)+O(\Lambda\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{10}N).

Since, from the problem definition,
d^{\prime}\leq n\leq W^{0}
,
this is bounded by:

O(|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho\cdot\log N)+O(\Lambda\cdot W^{0}\cdot\log^{10}N).

Next, we bound the total running time of all executions of Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
over the course of the algorithm. Observe first that type-1 outcome of the procedure may only occur once over the course of the algorithm. The running time of Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
in case of type-1 outcome is bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot k\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+14}\right),

where
k\leq|V(H)\setminus\beta|
.
Recall that
\beta^{\prime}
is the set of all vertices that were deleted from
\beta
over the course of the algorithm, so
|\beta|\geq|\beta^{0}|-|\beta^{\prime}|
. Recall also that
|V(H)|\leq W^{*}\leq W^{0}+2\Lambda
. Therefore,
k\leq|V(H)\setminus\beta|\leq W^{*}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\leq W^{0}+2\Lambda-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|
. We then get that the total running time of Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
when type-1 outcome occurs is bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+\Lambda+|\beta^{\prime}|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+14}\right).

Recall that we have charged the running times of the executions of Procedure
\Pi
in which type-2 or type-3 outcomes occured to vertices that were removed from
U_{\lambda}
by the procedure (in case of type-2 outcome), or to the weights of the vertices that were deleted from
G
following the procedure. Recall also that
M
is the multiset of all vertices that ever lied in
U_{\lambda}
.
The total running time of the remaining executions of Procedure
\Pi
is bounded by:

\begin{split}&O\left(c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot(\hat{W}+|M|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+15}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left(c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot(W^{0}+|\beta^{\prime}|-|\beta^{0}|+\Lambda+|M|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+15}\right),\end{split}

from Inequality
12
.
The total running time of the algorithm, excluding Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}
, is therefore bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda+|M|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+15}\right).

Next, we describe Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}
and analyze its running time, and then bound
|M|
.

6.4.3
Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}

Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}
is called whenever our algorithm produces a strongly well-structured
\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap A|
. Let
Z
be the set of vertices of
G
that contains all endpoints of the edges in
E_{G}(A,B)
. Additionally, if
w(A)\leq w(B)
, we add the vertices of
A
to
Z
, and we add the vertices of
B
to
Z
otherwise. Recall that, following this cut, the vertices of
Z
are deleted from
G
. Additionally, some vertices may be deleted from
\beta
. We denote the latter set of vertices by
\hat{\beta}
, and we assume that
Z\cap\beta\subseteq\hat{\beta}
.

We start by updating the graph
H
, as follows: we delete the vertices of
Z
from
H
, and, for every special edge
e\in E(G)
that is incident to a vertex of
Z
, we delete all vertices and edges on the corresponding path
P^{*}(e)
from
H
.
For every vertex
v
that is deleted from
H
, if
U_{j}
is the layer of the
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
to which
v
belongs, we delete
v
from
U_{j}
and from set
{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(x)
, where
x\in\beta
is the ancestor of
v
, and we also delete path
K(v)
from
{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}
.
Note that the update of graph
H
takes time
O(w(Z))
. We denote by
Z^{\prime}
the set of vertices that were deleted from
H
, so
|Z^{\prime}|\leq O(w(Z))
.

Next, we consider the vertices
x\in\hat{\beta}
one by one. For each such vertex
x
, for every layer
1\leq j<\lambda
, and for every descendant
v\in{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(x)
of
x
at layer
j
, we delete
v
from
U_{j}
and add it to
U_{\lambda}
. We also delete the path
K(v)
from
{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}
(we may sometimes say that the path is
destroyed
). Additionally, if
x
remains in
H
, we add it to
U_{\lambda}
as well.
Let
n^{\prime}
be the number of vertices that were added to
U_{\lambda}
so far. Recall that, from Property
L2
of the
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
, for every vertex
x\in\hat{\beta}
, for every layer
1\leq j\leq\lambda-1
,
|{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(x)|\leq\rho
. Therefore,
n^{\prime}\leq|\hat{\beta}|\cdot\lambda\cdot\rho\leq 8|\hat{\beta}|\cdot\rho\cdot\log N
. The running time of the algorithm so far is bounded by
O(w(Z)+|\hat{\beta}|\cdot\rho\cdot\log N)
.

Lastly, it is possible that, for some vertices
v\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{\lambda-1}U_{j}
, some vertex of
K(v)
was deleted from
H
by the procedure, while vertex
v
itself was not deleted. Note that, if the ancestor
x\in\beta
of
v
was
deleted from
\beta
, then we have already removed
v
from its corresponding layer.
Let
V^{\prime}\subseteq\bigcup_{j=1}^{\lambda-1}U_{j}
denote the set of all vertices
v\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{\lambda-1}U_{j}
, such that
v\not\in Z^{\prime}
, the ancestor
x\in\beta
of
v
is not in
\hat{\beta}
, and some vertex on path
K(v)
is in
Z^{\prime}
.
We further partition set
V^{\prime}
into two subsets: set
V^{\prime}_{1}
contains all vertices
v\in V^{\prime}
, such that the last vertex
u
on path
K(v)
lies in
Z^{\prime}
; while set
V^{\prime}_{2}
contains all remaining vertices.

From Requirement
L3
of
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
,
|V^{\prime}_{1}|\leq\lambda\cdot Z^{\prime}\leq O(w(Z)\cdot\log N)
. Since the
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
data structure maintains, for every vertex
v\in V^{\prime}
a pointer from the last endpoint
u
of
K(v)
to vertex
v
itself, set
V^{\prime}_{1}
can be computed in time
O(|V^{\prime}_{1}|)\leq O(w(Z)\cdot\log N)
.

In order to compute the set
V^{\prime}_{2}
of vertices, consider the collection
E_{G}(A,B)
of edges. Recall that, for every edge
e\in E_{G}(A,B)
, we have defined a path
P^{*}(e)
that represents edge
e
in graph
H^{\prime}
. Let
E^{\prime}\subseteq E(H)
be the set of edges that contains, for each edge
e\in E_{G}(A,B)
, the first and the last edge on path
P^{*}(e)
. We claim that, for every vertex
v\in V^{\prime}_{2}
,
path
K(v)
must contain an edge of
E^{\prime}
.

Observation 6.9
.

For every vertex
v\in V^{\prime}_{2}
,
path
K(v)
must contain an edge of
E^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Assume first that
w(A)\leq w(B)
.
Consider the graph
H\setminus E^{\prime}
, and let
(a,b)
be any edge in this graph with
a\in Z^{\prime}
and
b\not\in Z^{\prime}
. From the definition of the set
Z^{\prime}
of vertices and the set
E^{\prime}
of edges, it must be the case that
a
is the last endpoint of some path
P^{*}(e)
for an edge
e\in E_{G}(A,B)
; equivalently,
a\in V(G)
is the tail of one of the edges in
E_{G}(A,B)
. But then
a\in L^{\prime}
, and it only has a single incoming edge in
H
, which must belong to
E^{\prime}
.
Consider now some vertex
v\in V_{2}^{\prime}
, and its corresponding path
K(v)
. Assume for contradiction that
K(v)
does not contain an edge of
E^{\prime}
. Since
K(v)
connects two vertices of
V(H)\setminus Z^{\prime}
, and contains at least one vertex of
Z^{\prime}
as an inner vertex, it must contain an edge
(a,b)
with
a\in Z^{\prime}
and
b\not\in Z^{\prime}
. But the only edge entering
a
lies in
E^{\prime}
, a contradiction.

If
w(A)>w(B)
, then the proof is the same, except that we consider an edge
(a,b)
with
a\not\in Z^{\prime}
and
b\in Z^{\prime}
. In this case,
b
must be a head of an edge of
E_{G}(A,B)
, and so it has no outgoing edges in
H\setminus E^{\prime}
.

In order to compute the set
V^{\prime}_{2}
of vertices, we consider the edges
e\in E_{G}(A,B)
one by one. When edge
e
is considered, we let
e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime\prime}
be the first and the last edges on the corresponding path
P^{*}(e)
. We then add to
V^{\prime}_{2}
all vertices that lie in set
S(e^{\prime})\cup S(e^{\prime\prime})
. Notice that, from Requirement
L1
of the
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
, the congestion caused by the set
{\mathcal{Q}}
of paths is bounded by
O(d^{\prime}\lambda\log^{9}N)\leq O(d^{\prime}\log^{10}N)
, and so for every edge
e^{*}\in E(H)
,
|S(e^{*})|\leq O(d^{\prime}\log^{10}N)
. Altogether, we get that:

|V_{2}^{\prime}|\leq O\left(|E_{G}(A,B)|\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{10}N\right)
.
Since cut
(A,B)
is
\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}
-sparse, we get that
|E_{G}(A,B)|\leq\frac{\min\left\{w(A),w(B)\right\}}{d^{\prime}}\leq\frac{w(Z)}{d^{\prime}}
. Overall, we get that
|V_{2}^{\prime}|\leq O(w(Z)\cdot\log^{10}N)
, and set
V^{\prime}_{2}
can be computed in time
O(w(Z)\cdot\log^{10}N)
. From our discussion,
|V^{\prime}|\leq O(w(Z)\cdot\log^{10}N)
, and it can be computed in time
O(w(Z)\cdot\log^{10}N)
.

For every vertex
v\in V^{\prime}
, let
1\leq j\leq\lambda
be the index of the layer to which
v
belongs. If
j<\lambda
, then we delete
v
from
U_{j}
and add it to
U_{\lambda}
, and we also delete the path
K(v)
from
{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}
. Additionally, if
x\in\beta
is the ancestor of
v
, then we delete
v
from set
{\mathcal{D}}_{j}(x)
.

From our discussion, the total number of vertices that were added to
U_{\lambda}
over the course of the procedure is bounded by
O(w(Z)\cdot\log^{10}N)+O(|\hat{\beta}|\cdot\rho\cdot\log N)
, and the running time of the procedure is bounded by
O(w(Z)\cdot\log^{10}N)+O(|\hat{\beta}|\cdot\rho\cdot\log N)
.

Recall that we have denoted by
\hat{W}
the total weight of all vertices that were ever deleted from
G
over the course of the algorithm, and by
\beta^{\prime}
the set of all vertices that remain in
\beta
at the end of the algorithm. Recall also that, from Inequality
12
,

\hat{W}\leq W^{0}+2\Lambda-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|
.
Therefore, the total number of vertices that were ever added to set
U_{\lambda}
by Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}
is bounded by:

O(\hat{W}\cdot\log^{10}N)+O(|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho\cdot\log N)\leq O\left((W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\log^{10}N\right),

and the total running time of all calls to Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}
over the course of the algorithm is bounded by

O(\hat{W}\cdot\log^{10}N)+O(|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho\cdot\log N)\leq O\left((W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\log^{10}N\right).

The total running time of the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem is then bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda+|M|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+15}\right).

(13)

6.4.4
Bounding
|M|
and the Running Time of the Algorithm

Recall that
M
is the multiset containing all vertices that were ever added to

U_{\lambda}
over the course of the phase. We partition the set
M
into six subsets,
M_{1},\ldots,M_{6}
, depending on the process through which the vertices were added to
U_{\lambda}
, and we then bound the cardinality of each set separately.

Set
M_{1}
.

Recall that at the beginning of the algorithm, every vertex of
V(H)\setminus\beta^{0}
is added to
U_{\lambda}
. Set
M_{1}
contains all these vertices, and, additionally, whenever a new vertex is inserted into graph
H
due to the splitting of a special edge, this new vertex, which is then added to
U_{\lambda}
, is also added to
M_{1}
.
Lastly, if Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
ever terminates with a type-1 outcome, then all vertices that were added to
U_{\lambda}
in the same iteration, just prior to that execution of Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
, are also added to set
M_{1}
.
Since the vertices of
\beta^{0}\setminus\beta^{\prime}
always lie in
U_{0}
and are never added to
U_{\lambda}
, it is easy to see that
|M_{1}|
is asymptotically bounded by the total number of vertices ever present in
H
, excluding the vertices of
|\beta^{0}\setminus\beta^{\prime}|
. Therefore,
|M_{1}|\leq O(W^{*}-|\beta^{0}\setminus\beta^{\prime}|)\leq O(W^{0}+2\Lambda-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|)
.

Set
M_{2}
.

The second set
M_{2}
contains all vertices
v
that were added to
U_{\lambda}
when the length of the current path
K(v)\in{\mathcal{Q}}
exceeded
16d^{\prime}\log^{10}N
.
Recall that, when path
K(v)
was added to
{\mathcal{Q}}
, its length was bounded by
4d^{\prime}\log^{10}N
. Therefore, between the time path
K(v)
was added to
{\mathcal{Q}}
, and the time it was destroyed, its edges underwent at least
4d^{\prime}\log^{10}N
splitting updates.
Recall that the total increase in the lengths of all edges in
G
is denoted by
\Lambda
, so the total number of edge splitting updates in graph
H
is bounded by
\Lambda
. Since every edge of
H
may belong to at most
4d^{\prime}\lambda\log^{9}N\leq 32d^{\prime}\log^{10}N
paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
, we get that the total number of edge splitting updates that the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
undergo over the course of the algorithm is bounded by
O(\Lambda\cdot d^{\prime}\log^{10}N)
. Since each destroyed path must undergo at least
4d^{\prime}\log^{10}N
edge-splitting updates, we get that
|M_{2}|\leq O(\Lambda)
.

Set
M_{3}
.

The third set,
M_{3}
, contains all vertices
v
that were added to
U_{\lambda}
because the ancestor-vertex of
v
was deleted from
\beta
during Phase 3 of the algorithm. Note that, from Property
L2
of the

Layered Connectivity Data Structure
, every vertex
x\in\beta
has at most
\lambda\cdot\rho
descendants at any time. Therefore,
|M_{3}|\leq O(|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho\cdot\lambda)\leq O(|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho\cdot\log N)
.

Set
M_{4}
.

The fourth set,
M_{4}
, contains all vertices that were added to
U_{\lambda}
by Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}
. As we showed already,
|M_{4}|\leq O\left((W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\log^{10}N\right)
.

Set
M_{5}
.

The fifth set,
M_{5}
, contains all vertices that were added to
U_{\lambda}
during the iterations of Stage 2, just prior to the call to Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
, that resulted in a type-3 outocme of the procedure.
Recall that in each such iteration, the number of vertices that were added to
U_{\lambda}
was bounded by
k
, while the total weight of the vertices that are subsequently deleted from
G
is at least
\Omega\left(\frac{k}{\log N}\right)
. Therefore,
|M_{5}|\leq O(\hat{W}\cdot\log N)\leq O\left((W^{0}+2\Lambda-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|)\cdot\log N\right)
from Inequality
12
,

So far we have defined five subsets
M_{1},\ldots,M_{5}
of the set
M
, and we have shown that:

\sum_{j=1}^{5}|M_{j}|\leq O\left((W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\log^{10}N\right).

(14)

Set
M_{6}
.

We let the sixth set,
M_{6}
, contain all remaining vertices of
M
. Each such vertex
v
must have been added to
U_{\lambda}
during an iteration of the second stage, just prior to a call to Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
, that resulted in a type-2 outcome.
Consider any such iteration of Phase 2, and let
1\leq i<\lambda
be the layer that was chosen by the iteration. We then say that this iteration of Phase 2 is a
type-
i
iteration
. For brevity, in the remainder of the proof, we use “type-
i
iteration” only to refer to type-
i
iterations of Phase 2 in which Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
resulted in a type-2 outcome.
Recall that, at the beginning of such an iteration,
\sum_{j=i+1}^{\lambda}|U_{j}|\geq 2^{\lambda-i-1}
holds, and the vertices of
\bigcup_{j=i}^{\lambda-1}U_{j}
are then moved from their current layers to
U_{\lambda}
. Let
k=\sum_{j=i}^{\lambda}|U_{j}|
, so
2^{\lambda-i-1}\leq k\leq 2^{\lambda-i}
. Then at the beginning of the iteration, at most
k
vertices were added to
U_{\lambda}
, while at the end of the iteration,
|U_{\lambda}|\leq\frac{k}{4\lambda}
held. Moreover, layers
U_{1},\ldots,U_{i-1}
remain unchanged over the course of the iteration.

From the description of our algorithm, for any pair
1\leq i^{\prime},i^{\prime\prime}\leq\lambda
of indices, a vertex may only move directly from layer
U_{i^{\prime}}
to layer
U_{i^{\prime\prime}}
if either
i^{\prime}=\lambda
or
i^{\prime\prime}=\lambda
.
At the beginning of a type-
i
iteration,
|U^{>i}|\geq 2^{\lambda-i-1}
holds, while at the end of the iteraiton,
|U^{>i}|\leq\frac{k}{4\lambda}\leq\frac{2^{\lambda-i}}{4\lambda}
.
Therefore, between every pair of type-
i
iterations, at least
2^{\lambda-i-1}-\frac{2^{\lambda-i}}{4\lambda}\geq 2^{\lambda-i-2}
vertices must have moved from
\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=1}^{i}U_{i^{\prime}}
to
U_{\lambda}
.
For all
1\leq i<\lambda
, we denote by
M^{i}_{6}\subseteq M_{6}
the set of vertices that were added to
M_{6}
during type-
i
iterations.
We start by bounding the number of type-
i
iterations, for
1\leq i<\lambda
.

Claim 6.10
.

There is a large enough constant
c^{*}
, such that for all
1\leq i<\lambda
, the total number of type-
i
iterations is bounded by
\frac{c^{*}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\log^{10}N}{2^{\lambda-i}}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction. The base case is when
i=1
.
From the above discussion, between every pair of type-1 iterations, at least
2^{\lambda-3}
new vertices must have been moved from
U_{1}
to
U_{\lambda}
. All such vertices must belong to
M_{1}\cup\cdots\cup M_{5}
. Since
\sum_{j=1}^{5}|M_{j}|\leq O\left((W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\log^{10}N\right)
, and
c^{*}
is a sufficiently large constant, we get that the total number of type-
1
iterations is bounded by:

O\left(\frac{(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\log^{10}N}{2^{\lambda}}\right)\leq\frac{c^{*}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\log^{10}N}{2^{\lambda-1}}.

Consider an index
1<i<\lambda
, and assume that the claim holds for all indices
1\leq i^{\prime}<i
. We define a potential function
\Phi_{i}=\sum_{j=i+1}^{\lambda}|U_{j}|
. Recall that, at the beginning of a type-
i
iteration,
\Phi_{i}=\sum_{j=i+1}^{\lambda}|U_{j}|\geq 2^{\lambda-i-1}
holds, while at the end of the iteration,
|U_{\lambda}|\leq\frac{2^{\lambda-i}}{4\lambda}
, and for all
i<i^{\prime}<\lambda
,
U_{i^{\prime}}=\emptyset
, so
\Phi_{i}\leq\frac{2^{\lambda-i}}{4\lambda}
. Therefore, in every type-
i
iteration,
\Phi_{i}
decreases by at least
2^{\lambda-i-1}\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{2\lambda}\right)\geq 0.48\cdot 2^{\lambda-i}
. The potential
\Phi_{i}
is increased whenever vertices are added to sets
M_{1},\ldots,M_{5}
, and the total increase due to the addition of such vertices to
M_{1},\ldots,M_{5}
is bounded by
\sum_{j=1}^{5}|M_{j}|
. Additionally, for all
1\leq i^{\prime}<i
, each type-
i^{\prime}
iteration may increase the potential
\Phi_{i}
by at most
\frac{2^{\lambda-i^{\prime}}}{4\lambda}
. Since, from the induction hypothesis, the number of type-
i^{\prime}
iterations is bounded by
\frac{c^{*}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\log^{10}N}{2^{\lambda-i^{\prime}}}
,
the total increase in the potential
\Phi_{i}
due to type-
i^{\prime}
iterations is bounded by:

\frac{c^{*}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\log^{10}N}{2^{\lambda-i^{\prime}}}\cdot\frac{2^{\lambda-i^{\prime}}}{4\lambda}\leq\frac{c^{*}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\log^{10}}{4\lambda}.

Therefore, the total increase in the potential
\Phi_{i}
over the course of the entire algorithm is bounded by:

\begin{split}\sum_{j=1}^{5}|M_{j}|+&(i-1)\cdot\frac{c^{*}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\log^{10}N}{4\lambda}\\
&\leq 0.26c^{*}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\log^{10}N.\end{split}

Since every successful type-
i
iteration decreases the potential
\Phi_{i}
by at least
0.48\cdot 2^{\lambda-i}
, we get that the total number of type-
i
iterations is bounded by:

\frac{0.26c^{*}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\log^{10}N}{0.48\cdot 2^{\lambda-i}}\leq\frac{c^{*}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\log^{10}N}{2^{\lambda-i}}.

Since, for all
1\leq i<\lambda
, every type-
i
iteration adds at most
O(2^{\lambda-i})
vertices to
U_{\lambda}
, we get that
|M_{6}^{i}|\leq O\left((W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\log^{10}N\right)
, and altogether:

|M_{6}|=\sum_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}|M_{6}^{i}|\leq O\left((W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\lambda\cdot\log^{10}N\right).

Combining this with Inequality
14
, and substituting
\lambda\leq O(\log N)
, we get that:

|M|\leq O\left((W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot\log^{11}N\right).

Lastly, substituting this bound on
|M|
into the bound from Equation
13
, we get that the total running time of the algorithm so far is bounded by:

\begin{split}&O\left(c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda+|M|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+15}\right)\\
&\quad\leq O\left(c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+26}\right)\end{split}

6.4.5
Maintaining the Additional Data Structures

It remains to show an algorithm that maintains, for every edge
e\in E(H)
, the set
S(e)
of vertices, whose corresponding path
K(v)
contains
e
. Additionally, for every vertex
x\in\beta
, we need to maintain, for all
1\leq i<\lambda
, the set
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}(x)
of the descendants of
x
at layer
i
, and, for every vertex
v\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}U_{i}
, a pointer to its unique ancestor in
\beta
. Lastly, for every layer
1\leq i\leq\lambda-1
and vertex
v\in\bigcup_{j=1}^{i-1}U_{j}
, if
v
serves as an endpoint of a path
K(u)\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
, then we need to maintain a pointer from
v
to
u
.
We now show how to extend our algorithm, in a straightforward manner, to maintain these additional data structures.

At the beginning of the algorithm, for every edge
e\in E(H)
, we set
S(e)=\emptyset
, and for every vertex
x\in\beta
and index
1\leq i<\lambda
, we set
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}(x)=\emptyset
. Whenever a new vertex
v
is added to any layer
U_{i}
, for
1\leq i<\lambda
, we inspect its corresponding path
K(v)
, that is added to
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
. For every edge
e\in E(K(v))
, we add vertex
v
to the list
S(e)
. Let
y
denote the last endpoint of path
K(v)
, and let
x\in\beta
be the unique ancestor of
y
. We then add
v
to
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}(x)
, and we add a pointer from
v
to
x
, and another pointer from
y
to
v
.

Whenever any vertex
u
is deleted from a set
U_{i}
, for any
1\leq i<\lambda
, we again inspect the corresponding path
K(v)
, and, for every edge
e\in E(K(v))
, we delete
v
from set
S(v)
. If
y
is the other endpoint of path
K(v)
, then we delete the pointer from
y
to
v
. Additionally, if
x\in\beta
is the unique ancestor of
v
at level
0
, then we delete
v
from
{\mathcal{D}}_{i}(x)
. Note that the time required to execute all these updates is asymptotically bounded by the time required to compute all paths
K(v)
that are ever added to
{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Therefore, the total update time of the algorithm remains bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+26}\right).

Recall that the algorithm from Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
may terminate with a “FAIL”, and if this happens, our algorithm terminates with a “FAIL” as well. However, the number of calls to Procedure
\mathsf{ReconnectLayer}
is bounded by
|M|\leq\tilde{O}((W^{0})^{2})\leq N^{3}
, and the probability that a single call terminates with a “FAIL” is bounded by
\frac{1}{N^{10}}
. Therefore, overall, the probability that our algorithm terminates with a “FAIL” is bounded by
\frac{1}{N^{7}}
.

6.5
An Algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
Problem

In this subsection we prove the second part of
Theorem

4.12
, by showing that an algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem implies an algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem with the required running time. We summarize this result in the following theorem.

Theorem 6.11
.

Suppose that, for some parameter
r\geq 1
, there exists a randomized algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, that, given as an input an instance
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of the problem with
|V(G)|=n
,
has running time at most
c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}}
. Then there exists a randomized algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem, that, on input
(G,\beta,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
has running time at most:
c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(G)\cdot(W^{0}(G)-|\beta|+\Delta)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r}
.

The remainder of this subsection is dedicated to the proof of
Theorem

6.11
.
Recall that we are given as input a perfect well-structured graph
G=(L,R,E)
with a proper assignment of lengths
\ell(e)
to its edges
e\in E
, that are given in the adjacency-list representation, such that the length of every special edge is
1
.
We are also given a subset
\beta\subseteq V(G)
of vertices with
|\beta|\geq\frac{99|V(G)|}{100}
, and parameters
N\geq W^{0}(G)
,
1\leq\Delta\leq\frac{|\beta|}{(\log N)^{128}}
,
1\leq\eta\leq\Delta
, and
d\geq(\log N)^{64}
, such that
\frac{\Delta\cdot d}{\eta}\leq|V(G)|-|\beta|+\Delta\cdot(\log N)^{64}
. Lastly, we are given an integer
1\leq r\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
, and we are guaranteed that
d\leq 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N}}
.

Over the course of the algorithm, vertices may be deleted from set
\beta
, and the lengths of some edges may be increased, but the length of every special edge incident to a vertex that currently lies in
\beta
is always
1
. We denote by
\beta^{0}
the initial set
\beta
of vertices. Once
|\beta|
falls below
\frac{|\beta^{0}|}{2}
, the algorithm terminates.

Recall that the algorithm consists of at most
\Delta
iterations.
At the beginning of every iteration
i
, the algorithm is given a vertex
x\in V(G)
, and it must return a simple path
P_{i}
in
G
connecting
x
to any vertex
y\in\beta
, such that the length of the path is at most
d
, and no inner vertices on the path lie in
\beta
(if
x\in\beta
, that we require that path
P_{i}
only consists of the vertex
x
). After that, the length of some special edges on path
P_{i}
may be doubled, and vertex
y
is deleted from
\beta
. In this case, we say that vertex
y
was deleted from
\beta

directly
. If
\tau^{\prime}>\tau
are two times at which the length of some edge
e\in E(G)
is doubled, then we are guaranteed that the algorithm returned at least
\eta
paths in response to queries during the time interval
(\tau,\tau^{\prime}]
that contained
e
.

The algorithm may, at any time, produce a strongly well-structured cut
(X,Y)
in
G
of sparsity
\Phi_{G}(X,Y)\leq\frac{(\log N)^{64}}{d}
, such that
w(X)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap X|
holds. Let
J^{\prime}\subseteq Y
denote the set of vertices that serve as endpoints of the edges of
E_{G}(X,Y)
. The vertices of
X\cup J^{\prime}
are then deleted from
G
, and the algorithm continues with the resulting graph
G=G[Y\setminus J^{\prime}]
. Let
Z
be the set of vertices that are deleted from
G
following this cut. Notice that the vertices of
Z\cap\beta
are deleted from graph
G
, and hence from set
\beta
as well. We say that the vertices of
Z\cap\beta
are deleted from
\beta

indirectly
.

Let
\Lambda
denote the total increase in the lengths of edges over the course of the algorithm, and denote
W^{0}=W^{0}(G)=n
. We also denote the initial graph
G
by
G^{0}
, and the set of all vertices that were deleted from
\beta
over the course of the algorithm, either directly or indirectly, by
\beta^{\prime}
. We need the following claim and its corollary, whose proofs are deferred to Section
C.3
and
C.4
of Appendix, respectively.

Claim 6.12
.

\Lambda\leq 4n-4|\beta^{0}|+5\Delta\cdot(\log N)^{64}\leq 4n
and
|\beta^{\prime}|\leq 45n-45|\beta^{0}|+56\Delta\cdot(\log N)^{64}
.

Corollary 6.13
.

Throughout the algorithm,
\sum_{v\in V(G)\setminus\beta}w(v)\leq 0.2|\beta|
.

Note that the subdivided graph
H
of
G
is identical to graph
G
, so we can use the adjacency list representation of
G
also as the adjacency list representation of
H
. We define a parameter
d^{\prime}=\frac{d}{(\log N)^{64}}
. Recall that, from the definition of the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem, we are guaranteed that
d\leq 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N}}
, and also that
d\leq\frac{\eta}{\Delta}\left(n-|\beta^{0}|+\Delta\cdot(\log N)^{64}\right)\leq n
, since
\eta\leq\Delta
, and
\Delta\leq\frac{|\beta^{0}|}{(\log N)^{128}}
from the problem definition.
Therefore,
d^{\prime}\leq\min\left\{\frac{2^{r\sqrt{\log N}}}{\log^{8}N},\frac{n}{8}\right\}
. Since
|\beta^{0}|\geq\frac{99n}{100}
from the problem definition, and
d^{\prime}\geq 1
(as
d\geq(\log N)^{64}
), we get that
|\beta^{0}|\geq\frac{W^{0}}{256d^{\prime}}
.

We apply the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem from
Theorem

6.7
to graph
G
, set
\beta
of vertices, and parameters
N,d^{\prime}
, and
r
. From our discussion, this is a valid input to the problem. Recall that the algorithm uses a parameter
\rho=\frac{2^{12}\cdot W^{0}\cdot\log^{3}N}{|\beta^{0}|}
. Since, from the definition of the
\mathsf{MaintainSpecialCluster}
problem,
|\beta^{0}|\geq\frac{99n}{100}
, while
W^{0}=n
, we get that
\rho=O(\log^{3}N)
. Our algorithm consists of at most
\Delta
phases, that correspond to the phases of the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem. Recall that the purpose of each such phase is to construct a valid
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
with
U_{\lambda}=\emptyset
. Our algorithm will only respond to queries at the end of each phase, once such a data structure is obtained.

We now consider a single phase of the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem, and one of its iterations. Assume first that the algorithm produces
a strongly well-structured
\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap A|
. Since
d^{\prime}=\frac{d}{(\log N)^{64}}
, the sparsity of the cut is at most
\frac{(\log N)^{64}}{d}
. We claim that
w(A)<w(B)
must hold in the following simple obsevation, whose proof is deferred to Section
C.5
of appendix.

Observation 6.14
.

w(A)<w(B)
must hold.

We delete from
G
the vertices of
A
, and the endpoints of the edges of
E_{G}(A,B)
that lie in
B
, and continue with the resulting graph.

Next, we show that the algorithm to the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
may never produce a weakly well-structured cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in graph
H
, with
|A^{\prime}|\geq\frac{511|V(H)|}{512}
,
|A^{\prime}\cap\beta|<\frac{2|\beta|}{\log^{3}N}
, and
|E_{H}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|\leq\frac{|V(H)|}{d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N}
.
Indeed, assume for contradiction that the algorithm produces such a cut. Since every special edge incident to a vertex of
\beta
has length
1
, we get that
|V(H)\setminus\beta|=\sum_{v\in V(G)\setminus\beta}w(v)|\leq 0.2|\beta|
from
Corollary

6.13
.
But then:

|A^{\prime}|\leq|V(H)\setminus\beta|+\frac{2|\beta|}{\log^{3}N}\leq 0.21|\beta|.

Since
|V(H)|\geq|\beta|
, it is then impossible that
|A^{\prime}|\geq\frac{511|V(H)|}{512}
, a contradiction.

Therefore, if the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem does not produce a a strongly well-structured
\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A)\geq 1.8|\beta\cap A|
, it must produce a valid

Layered Connectivity Data Structure
, with
U_{\lambda}=\emptyset
. In this case, our algorithm is ready to respond to a query. Let
x
be a query vertex that it receives. If
x\in\beta
, then we return a path consisting of the vertex
x
itself. Otherwise, we construct a path
P
connecting
x
to some vertex
y\in\beta
, as follows. Since
U_{\lambda}=\emptyset
, there must be an integer
1\leq i<\lambda
with
x\in U_{i}
. We consider the path
K(x)\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
, that connects
x
to some vertex
x_{1}\in U_{i_{1}}
, for some
0\leq i_{1}<i
. If
x^{\prime}\in\beta
, then we terminate this process, and otherwise we consider the path
K(x_{1})
, which is concatenated to path
K(x)
. After at most
\lambda
such iterations, we must obtain a path
P
, that connects
x
to some vertex
y\in\beta
. Since the length of every path in set
\bigcup_{j=1}^{\lambda-1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}
is at most
4d^{\prime}\log^{10}N
, the final length of the resulting path is bounded by
4\lambda d^{\prime}\log^{10}N\leq 32d^{\prime}\log^{11}N<d
, since
d^{\prime}=\frac{d}{(\log N)^{64}}
, and
N
is sufficiently large. In time
O(d)
, we can turn this path into a simple path, and truncate it so that it still connects
x
to some vertex of
\beta
, but the inner vertices on the path do not belong to
\beta
. The time required to respond to a single query is bounded by
O(d)
, and the total time required to respond to all queries is bounded by
O(d\cdot\Delta)\leq O\left(n\cdot(n-|\beta^{0}|+\Delta)\cdot(\log N)^{64}\right)
,
since, from the problem definition,
\Delta\cdot d\leq\eta\cdot\left(|V(G)|-|\beta|+\Delta\cdot(\log N)^{64}\right)
, and
\eta\leq\Delta\leq|\beta|\leq n
.

Recall that, in our setting,
\rho=O(\log^{3}N)
, and so the running time of the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem from

Theorem

6.7
is bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+|\beta^{\prime}|+\Lambda)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+29}\right).

Since, from
Claim

6.12
,

\Lambda\leq 4n-4|\beta^{0}|+5\Delta\cdot(\log N)^{64}
and
|\beta^{\prime}|\leq 45n-45|\beta^{0}|+56\Delta\cdot(\log N)^{64}
, while
n=W^{0}
, we get that the total running time of the algorithm is bounded by:

\begin{split}&O\left(c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta^{0}|+\Delta)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+100}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot(W^{0}-|\beta|+\Delta)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r}.\end{split}

Finally, recall that the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem may terminate with a “FAIL”, with probability at most
\frac{1}{N^{7}}
. If this happens, then our algorithm terminates with a “FAIL” as well, but the probability for this happening is bounded by
\frac{1}{N^{7}}
.

7
From the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
Problem to
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
Problem – Comleting the Proof of
Theorem

4.12

The goal of this section is to complete the proof of
Theorem

4.12
, by providing an algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, that is summarized in the following theorem.

Theorem 7.1
.

Suppose that, for some parameter
r\geq 1
, there exists a randomized algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, that, given as an input an instance
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of the problem with
|V(G)|=n
,
has running time at most
c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}}
. Then there exists a randomized algorithm for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, that, on input
(G,\Delta,\eta,d,N)
has running time at most:
c_{1}\cdot(W^{0}(G))^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r}+c_{1}\cdot W^{0}(G)\cdot\Delta\cdot\log^{4}N
.

We assume that there exists a randomized algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, for some integer
r\geq 1
, that, on an input an instance
(G,A,B,\Delta,\eta,N)
of the problem with
|V(G)|=n
,
has running time at most
c_{1}\cdot n\cdot(n-|B|)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}}
.

Recall that the input to the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem consists of a perfect well-structured
n
-vertex graph
G=(L,R,E)
with a proper assignment
\ell(e)
of lengths to its edges. Recall that we have defined vertex weights for perfect well-structured graphs, where the weight
w(v)
of a vertex
v
is the length of the unique special edge incident to
v
. For every vertex
v\in V(G)
, we denote by
w^{0}(v)
its initial weight, and we denote
W^{0}(G)=\sum_{v\in V(G)}w^{0}(v)
. Additionally, we are given a parameter
N\geq W^{0}(G)
, that is greater than a large enough constant, with
r\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil
,
and parameters
1\leq\Delta\leq N
,
1\leq\eta\leq\Delta
, and
(\log N)^{64}\leq d\leq 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N}}
, such that
\frac{\Delta\cdot d}{\eta}\leq n
. In particular:

d\leq\frac{n\eta}{\Delta}\leq n.

(15)

Since
d\geq(\log N)^{64}
, we get that
(\log N)^{64}\leq n\leq N
, and so:

\log\log N\leq\log n\leq\log N.

(16)

Since we have assumed that
N
is greater than a large enough constant, and in particular that
N>2^{2^{c_{2}}}
, we can also assume that
n
is greater than a large enough constant.
We also get following inequality that will be useful for us later:

\log(16N)=4+\log N\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{32c_{2}r}\right)\log N.

(17)

Additionally,
\sqrt{\log(16N)}\leq\sqrt{4+\log N}\leq 2+\sqrt{\log N}
, and so:

2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log(16N)}}\leq 2^{2c_{2}}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\leq 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot\log N,

(18)

since
N
is sufficiently large.

The algorithm consists of at most
\Delta
iterations.
At the beginning of every iteration
i
, the algorithm is given two vertices
x,y\in V(G)
, and it must return a simple path
P_{i}
in
G
connecting
x
to
y
, whose length is at most
d
. (We may sometimes say that the algorithm is given a
short-path
query between the pair
(x,y)
of vertices, and it returns the path
P_{i}
in response to the query).
The lengths of some of the special edges on path
P_{i}
are then doubled.
If
e
is a special edge of
G
, and
\tau<\tau^{\prime}
are consecutive times during the algorithm’s execution when the length of
e
was doubled, then we are guaranteed that the number of the paths
P_{i}
that the algorithm returned in response to the queries during time interval
(\tau,\tau^{\prime}]
with
e\in E(P_{i})
is at least
\eta
.

The algorithm may, at any time, produce a strongly well-structured cut
(X,Y)
in
G
of sparsity
\Phi_{G}(X,Y)\leq\frac{(\log N)^{64}}{d}
.
If
w(X)\leq w(Y)
, then let
Z=X
and
Z^{\prime}=Y
; otherwise, let
Z=Y
and
Z^{\prime}=X
. We also let
J^{\prime}\subseteq Z^{\prime}
denote the set of vertices that serve as endpoints of the edges of
E_{G}(X,Y)
. The vertices of
Z\cup J^{\prime}
are then deleted from
G
, and the algorithm continues with the resulting graph
G=G[Z^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}]
. This modification ensures that
G
remains a perfect well-structured graph. Once
|V(G)|\leq n/2
holds, the algorithm terminates, even if fewer than
\Delta
iterations have passed.
The algorithm may also, at any time, return “FAIL”, but the probability of this ever happening must be bounded by
1/N^{4}
.

We can assume w.l.o.g. that, in the initial graph
G
, the length of every edge is at most
d
. Indeed, if
(x,y)
is an edge of
G
of length greater than
d
, then cut
(A,B)
with
A=\left\{x\right\}
and
B=V(G)\setminus\left\{x\right\}
is a strongly well-structured cut of sparsity at most
\frac{1}{d}
, since
w(x),w(y)=d
, and
|E_{G}(A,B)|=1
. As long as special edges of length greater than
d
lie in
G
, we can therefore produce
\frac{1}{d}
-sparse cuts that will result in the removal of such edges from
G
. The time required to perform this preprocessing step is
O(|E(G)|)\leq O(W^{0}(G))
. We will also ensure that, throughout the algorithm, every edge of
G
has length at most
2d
. Therefore, if we denote by
W^{(\tau)}
the total weight of all edges at some time
\tau
during the algorithm’s execution, then
W^{(\tau)}\leq 2n\cdot d\leq 2n^{2}
must hold (we have used Inequality
15
). Since the algorithm terminates once the number of vertices falls below
n/2
, the total weight
W
of all vertices of
G
satisfies
\frac{n}{2}\leq W\leq 2n^{2}
, at all times, and therefore, at all times the following inequality holds:

\log n-1\leq\log W\leq 4\log n.

(19)

Throughout, we denote by
G^{0}
the initial graph
G
, and by
E^{\mathsf{spec}}
be the set of all special edges of
G^{0}
.
For each such edge
e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}
, let
\ell^{0}(e)
be the length of
e
at the beginning of the algorithm, and let
\ell^{*}(e)
be defined as follows. If
e
lies in graph
G
at the end of the algorithm, then
\ell^{*}(e)
is the length of edge
e
at the end of the algorithm. Otherwise,
\ell^{*}(e)
is the length of the edge
e
just before it was deleted from
G
. We will use the following simple observation to bound
\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}}\ell^{*}(e)
.
The proof is deferred to Section
D.1
of Appendix.

Observation 7.2
.

\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}}\ell^{*}(e)\leq 2\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}}\ell^{0}(e)+\frac{2d\Delta}{\eta}\leq 2\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}}\ell^{0}(e)+2n.

Throughout the algorithm, instead of working with graph
G
, it will be convenient for us to work with its corresponding subdivided graph
G^{+}
(see
Definition

4.1
). We denote the two sides of the bipartition of the vertices of
G^{+}
by
L^{+}
and
R^{+}
, respectively. Recall that graph
G^{+}
is obtained from
G
by replacing every special edge
e=(u,v)
with a new path, that we denote by
P^{*}(e)
, and call
the path represeinting
e
in
G^{+}
. The number of special edges on path
P^{*}(e)
is exactly
\ell(e)
. For every special edge
e^{\prime}
on path
P^{*}(e)
, we say that
e
is the
parent-edge
of
e^{\prime}
, and that
e^{\prime}
is the
child-edge
of
e
.

Assume that at some time during the algorithm’s execution, the length
\ell(e)
of an edge
e\in E(G)
is doubled. Then for every special edge
e^{\prime}=(x,y)
on path
P^{*}(e)
, we replace the edge by a path
(x,x^{\prime},y^{\prime},y)
, where
x^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}
are new vertices, edges
(x,x^{\prime})
and
(y^{\prime},y)
are special, and edge
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
is regular; we call this process the
splitting
of edge
e^{\prime}
. Notice that, since
(x,y)
is a special edge of
G^{+}
,
x\in R^{+}
and
y\in L^{+}
must hold. We add
x^{\prime}
to
L^{+}
and
y^{\prime}
to
R^{+}
. It is easy to verify that
G^{+}
remains the subdivided graph of
G
after this update.

In order to simplify the notation, we will denote
G^{+}
by
H
from now on, and we will also denote the bipartition
(L^{+},R^{+})
by
(L^{\prime},R^{\prime})
. Recall that
H
is a uniform perfect well-structured graph. The initial number of vertices in
H
is
W^{0}
. As the algorithm progresses and edges of
H
are subdivided, we will denote by
W
the current number of vertices in
H
, which is equal to
\sum_{v\in V(G)}w(v)
.

From now on we will mostly work with graph
H
. In every iteration, we are given a pair
(x,y)
of vertices of
H
, and we need to return a simple path
P
connecting
x
to
y
in
H
, of length at most
d
. Such a path can be easily trasformed into a path in graph
G
of length at most
d
connecting
x
to
y
. After that, some special edges on path
P
are split.
From the definition of the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem, we are guaranteed that the following property holds throughout the algorithm.

Q’1.

Consider any special edge
e
of
H
that did not lie in the original graph
H=(G^{0})^{+}
. Assume that
e
was added to
H
at some time
\tau
during the algorithm’s execution, and was later split at some time
\tau^{\prime}>\tau
(so after time
\tau^{\prime}
, edge
e
no longer lies in
H
). Then the number of paths
P
that the algorithm returned in response to queries during the time interval
(\tau,\tau^{\prime}]
with
e\in E(P)
is at least
\eta
.

Notice that
W^{0}=2\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}}\ell^{0}(e)
, and
the total number of vertices that ever belonged to
H
over the course of the algorithm is bounded by
\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}}2\ell^{*}(e)\leq 4\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}}\ell^{0}(e)+4n\leq 2W^{0}+4n\leq 6W^{0}
,
from
Observation

7.2
. In particular,
|V(H)|\leq 6W^{0}
holds at all times.

Throughout, we use a parameter
d^{\prime}
, that is the smallest integral power of
2
that is greater than
\frac{d}{(\log N)^{28}}
, and we set
\varphi=\frac{4}{d^{\prime}}
. Intuitively, if, at any time during the algorithm’s execution, the length of any special edge
e=(x,y)
in
G
becomes at least
d^{\prime}/2
, then we can immediately produce a cut
(A,B)
with
A=\left\{x\right\}
and
B=V(G)\setminus\left\{x\right\}
. It is easy to verify that
(A,B)
is a strongly well-structured cut in
G
. Moreover, since
w(x),w(y)=\ell(e)
, the sparsity of the cut is at most
\varphi
, and so our algorithm could output this cut. In reality our algorithm is somewhat more complex, but it uses a similar method to ensure that, at all times, the lengths of all edges in
G
are bounded by
d^{\prime}
. Additionally, as long as the algorithm does not terminate,
\sum_{e\in E(G)}\ell(e)\geq\frac{|V(G)|}{2}\geq\frac{n}{4}>d^{\prime}
must hold.

Let
W
be the total weight of all vertices of
G
at any time during the algorithm’s execution.
Then
W\geq|V(G)|\geq\frac{n}{2}\geq\frac{d}{2}
(from Inequality
15
). Therefore, throughout the algorithm:

d^{\prime}\leq\frac{2d}{\log^{28}N}\leq\frac{4W}{\log^{28}N}

(20)

holds.
As long as the lengths of all edges in
G
are bounded by
d^{\prime}/2
, our algorithm may produce, at any time, a weakly well-structured cut
(A,B)
in the current graph
H
, of sparsity at most
\frac{\varphi}{4}
. Let
A^{\prime}=A\cap V(G)
and
B^{\prime}=B\cap V(G)
. From
Observation

4.4
,
(A,B)
is a weakly well-structured cut in
G
of sparsity at most
2\varphi
. Furthermore, using the algorithm from
Observation

4.5
, we can transform cut
(A,B)
into a strongly well-structured cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
in
G
, of sparsity at most
4\varphi
.
We then return cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
, following which graph
G
is updated with the deletion of the vertices on the side of the cut whose current weight is smaller, and the endpoints of the edges in
E_{G}(A^{*},B^{*})
. We update graph
H
by deleting from it every vertex that was deleted from
G
, and, additionally, for every edge
e
that was deleted from
G
, its corresponding path
P^{*}(e)
is deleted from
H
. As before, the algorithm is allowed to halt and return “FAIL” at any time, but we are required that this happens with probability at most
1/N^{4}
.

7.1
Partition into Phases and Probability of Failure

Our algorithm is partitioned into phases. A phase terminates if one of the following happened: (i) the algorithm returned “FAIL”, in which case we say that the phase is of
type 1
; or (ii) at least
\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
vertices have been deleted from graph
H
during the current phase, where
\hat{W}
is the number of vertices in
H
at the beginning of the phase, in which case we say that the phase is of
type 2
; or (iii) at least
\Delta^{\prime}=\frac{\Delta}{\log^{30}N}
queries have been responded to; in which case we say that the phase is of
type 3
.
We will ensure that the probability that the algorithm returns “FAIL” in a single phase is bounded by
\frac{1}{2}
.
Since the total number of vertices that ever belong to graph
H
is bounded by
6W^{0}\leq O(N)
, we get that the total number of type-2 and type-3 phases is bounded by
O(\log^{30}N\cdot\log N)\leq\log^{32}N
.
If
\left\lceil 16\log N\right\rceil
consecutive type-1 phases occur, then we terminate the algorithm and return “FAIL”. Notice that, for each type-2 phase and type-3 phase, the probability that it is followed by
\left\lceil 16\log N\right\rceil
consecutive type-1 phases is bounded by
\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{\left\lceil 16\log N\right\rceil}\leq\frac{1}{N^{16}}
. Therefore, overall, the probability that the algorithm ever returns “FAIL” is bounded by
\frac{(\log N)^{32}}{N^{16}}<\frac{1}{N^{4}}
, as required. The total number of phases in the algorithm is then bounded by
O(\log^{32}N\cdot\log N)\leq O(\log^{33}N)
.

At a very high level, our algorithm follows a somewhat standard framework, though the specific details are quite different. As a first step, we select a random subset
T
of vertices of
H
, that we call
terminals
, where every vertex of
H
is selected to be added to
T
independently with probability
\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}
. Next, we will attempt to embed an expander
X
, whose vertex set is
T
, into graph
H
, so that every embedding path has length
\widetilde{O}(d^{\prime})
, and the paths cause edge-congestion
O(\operatorname{poly}\log n)
. We show that, with high probability, if we are unable to compute such an embedding, then we can compute a cut
(A,B)
in
H
, that can be transformed into a
\frac{(\log N)^{64}}{d}
-sparse strongly well-structured cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in
G
with
w(A^{\prime}),w(B^{\prime})\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
. We then output this cut, and update graphs
G
and
H
with the corresponding deletions of vertices and edges, which must lead to the deletion of at least
\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
vertices from
H
. Therefore, with high probability, if we cannot successfully compute an expander
X
, and embed it into
H
, the current phase will terminate. Assume now that we have successfully computed an expander
X
over the set
T
of roughly
\frac{\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}}
vertices, and embedded it into
H
.
Then we will employ the
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
from
Section

6
, with the set
V(X)
of vertices serving as the set
\beta
of centers, in order to maintain a collection
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\operatorname{out}}
of short paths, connecting every vertex of
V(H)
to some vertex of
V(X)
. We will also employ the same data structure on graph
\overleftarrow{G}
, that is obtained from
G
by reversing the direction of each of its edges, to maintain a collection
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\operatorname{in}}
of short paths, that, for every vertex
v\in V(H)
, contains a path that connects some vertex of
V(X)
to
v
.

We now formally describe the algorithm for executing a single phase.
We assume w.l.o.g. that, at the beginning of the phase, graph
G
does not contain any edge of length greater than
d^{\prime}
. Otherwise, as long as such an edge
e=(x,y)
lies in
G
, then cut
(A,B)
with
A=\left\{x\right\}
and
B=V(G)\setminus\left\{x\right\}
is a strongly well-structured cut of sparsity
\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}\leq\varphi
, since
w(x)=d^{\prime}
, and
|E_{G}(A,B)|=1
. As long as special edges of length greater than
d^{\prime}
lie in
G
, we can therefore produce
\varphi
-sparse cuts that will result in the removal of such edges from
G
. The time required to perform this preprocessing step, including updating the graph
H
, is
O(|V(H)|)\leq O(W^{0})
.
Therefore, we assume that, at the beginning of the phase,
G
does not contain edges of length greater than
d^{\prime}
. We denote by
\hat{W}
the number of vertices in
H
at the beginning of the phase.

We start with a preprocessing step, in which we sample the vertices of
H
to be added to the set
T
. Then we describe our two main data structures. The first data structure maintains a large expander that is defined over the set
T
of vertices, while the second data structure is
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
in graphs
G
and
\overleftarrow{G}
.

7.2
Preprocessing Step: Subsampling

Recall that the number of vertices in
H
at the beginning of the phase, denoted by
\hat{W}
, is bounded by
12W^{0}\leq 12N
.

In this step, we construct a set
T
of vertices of
H
, that we call
terminals
, as follows: every vertex
v\in V(H)
is added to
T
with probability
\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}
independently. This is the only randomized part in our algorithm for the
Maximum Bipartite Matching
problem. Next, we will define four “bad” events, and we will show that each of them may only happen with low probability. Eventually, if our algorithm returns “FAIL” and halts, we will guarantee that at least one of these bad events must have happened. This will ensure that the probability that the phase terminates with the “FAIL” is at most
\frac{1}{2}
, as required.

7.2.1
Bad Event
{\cal{E}}_{1}

Observe first that the expected cardinality of
T
is
\frac{\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}}
. We say that bad event
{\cal{E}}_{1}
happens if
|T|>\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}}
or
|T|<\frac{\hat{W}}{2d^{\prime}}
. From
Lemma

2.5
,
\text{\bf Pr}\left[|T|>\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}}\right]\leq 2^{-8\hat{W}/d^{\prime}}\leq\frac{1}{512}
, and
\text{\bf Pr}\left[|T|<\frac{\hat{W}}{2d^{\prime}}\right]\leq e^{-\hat{W}/(8d^{\prime})}\leq\frac{1}{512}
, since
d^{\prime}\leq\frac{4\hat{W}}{\log^{28}N}
from Inequality
20
.
Overall, we get that
\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{1}\right]\leq\frac{1}{256}
, and, if
{\cal{E}}_{1}
does not happen, then
\frac{\hat{W}}{2d^{\prime}}\leq|T|\leq\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}}
holds.

Before we define the remaining two bad events, we need to inspect the well-structured cuts of
H
more closely.

7.2.2
Well-Structured Cuts and Their Structure

Recall that a cut
(A,B)
of
H
is weakly well-structured iff all edges of
E_{H}(A,B)
are special. Let
E^{\prime}
be any subset of special edges of
H
. We denote by
{\mathcal{C}}(E^{\prime})
the collection of all weakly well-structured cuts
(A,B)
in
H
with
E_{H}(A,B)=E^{\prime}
. Next, we investigate the structure of the cuts in
{\mathcal{C}}(E^{\prime})
.

Let
J(E^{\prime})
denote the set of all vertices
v\in V(H)
, such that some edge of
E^{\prime}
leaves
v
, and let
J^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
denote the set of all vertices
u\in V(H)
, such that some edge of
E^{\prime}
enters
u
. Next, we denote by
S^{*}(E^{\prime})
the set of all vertices
v\in H
, such that there is a path in
H\setminus E^{\prime}
connecting a vertex of
J(E^{\prime})
to
v
. Notice that, for every cut
(A,B)\in{\mathcal{C}}(E^{\prime})
, it must be the case that
S^{*}(E^{\prime})\subseteq A
. Indeed, assume otherwise, and let
v\in S^{*}(E^{\prime})
be any vertex that lies in
B
. Since
J(E^{\prime})\subseteq A
must hold, there is a path
P
connecting a vertex of
A
to
v
, such that
P
is disjoint from the edges of
E^{\prime}
. But then some edge of
P
must lie in
E_{H}(A,B)
, contradicting the fact that
E_{H}(A,B)=E^{\prime}
.

Similarly, we denote by
S^{**}(E^{\prime})
the set of all vertices
v\in H
, such that there is a path in
H\setminus E^{\prime}
connecting
v
to a vertex of
J^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
. From the same arguments as above, for every cut
(A,B)\in{\mathcal{C}}(E^{\prime})
,
S^{**}(E^{\prime})\subseteq B
must hold.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}
be the set of all strongly connected components of
H\setminus\left(S^{*}(E^{\prime})\cup J(E^{\prime})\cup S^{**}(E^{\prime})\cup J^{\prime}(E^{\prime})\right)
, where for each
X\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}
, we view
X
as a set of vertices corresponding to the connected component. Denote
{\mathcal{X}}(E^{\prime})={\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}\cup\left\{S^{*}(E^{\prime})\cup J(E^{\prime}),S^{**}(E^{\prime})\cup J^{\prime}(E^{\prime})\right\}
. Clearly,
{\mathcal{X}}(E^{\prime})
is a partition of
V(H)
. Consider again any cut
(A,B)\in{\mathcal{C}}(E^{\prime})
. As observed already,
S^{*}(E^{\prime})\cup J(E^{\prime})\subseteq A
and
S^{**}(E^{\prime})\cup J^{\prime}(E^{\prime})\subseteq B
must hold. Moreover, it is easy to see that, since
E_{H}(A,B)=E^{\prime}
, for all
X\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\prime}
, either
X\subseteq A
, or
X\subseteq B
must hold. From the definition of the sets
S^{*}(E^{\prime}),S^{**}(E^{\prime})
of vertices, graph
H\setminus E^{\prime}
contains no path connecting a vertex of
S^{*}(E^{\prime})\cup J(E^{\prime})
to a vertex lying outside this set. Similarly, no path in
H\setminus E^{\prime}
may connect a vertex of
V(H)\setminus\left(S^{**}(E^{\prime})\cup J^{\prime}(E^{\prime})\right)
to a vertex of
S^{**}(E^{\prime})\cup J^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
. It is now easy to verify that, for every pair
X,X^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{X}}(E^{\prime})
of sets, it cannot be the case that
H\setminus E^{\prime}
contains both a path
P
connecting a vertex of
X
to a vertex of
X^{\prime}
, and a path
P^{\prime}
connecting a vertex of
X^{\prime}
to a vertex of
X
. Indeed, if such paths did exist, then neither of these paths could contain vertices of
S^{*}(E^{\prime})\cup J(E^{\prime})\cup S^{**}(E^{\prime})\cup J^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
, and so the vertices of
X\cup X^{\prime}
should have belonged to a single strongly connected component of
H\setminus\left(S^{*}(E^{\prime})\cup J(E^{\prime})\cup S^{**}(E^{\prime})\cup J^{\prime}(E^{\prime})\right)
.

We can now define an arbitrary topological ordering
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{q})
of the sets in
{\mathcal{X}}(E^{\prime})
, such that
X_{1}=S^{**}(E^{\prime})\cup J(E^{\prime})
,
X_{q}=S^{*}(E^{\prime})\cup J^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
, and for all
1\leq i<j\leq q
, there is no edge of
E(H)\setminus E^{\prime}
that connects a vertex of
X_{j}
to a vertex of
X_{i}
. Notice that, for all
1\leq i\leq q
, we can define a cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
with
A_{i}=X_{i+1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{q}
and
B_{i}=X_{1}\cup\cdots\cup X_{i}
. From our discussion so far, the only edges that connect vertices of
A_{i}
to vertices of
B_{i}
are the edges of
E^{\prime}
, and so for each such
1\leq i<r
, cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
lies in
{\mathcal{C}}(E^{\prime})
. We call all cuts in
\left\{(A_{i},B_{i})\mid 1\leq i\leq q\right\}

canonical cuts
corresponding to
E^{\prime}
, and denote the set of all such cuts by
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
. Notice that
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}(E^{\prime})
, and it is possible that
{\mathcal{C}}(E^{\prime})\setminus{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
.
Note that the definition of the set
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
of cuts depends on the topological ordering of the sets in
{\mathcal{X}}(E^{\prime})
, and there may be several such topological orderings. We assume that we are given some tie-breaking rule that selects a single topological ordering of the sets in
{\mathcal{X}}(E^{\prime})
, and we define
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
with respect to that topological ordering. We also assume that the above unique topological ordering can be computed in time
O(n)
, given the sets in
{\mathcal{X}}(E^{\prime})
.
We are now ready to define the second bad event.

7.2.3
Bad Event
{\cal{E}}_{2}

We start with intuition. Recall that our goal is to embed an expander over the set
T
of vertices into
H
, with polylogarithmic congestion. Intuitively, if we are not able to do so, we will construct a cut
(A,B)
in
H
, such that
|T\cap A|,|T\cap B|\geq\Omega(|T|/\operatorname{poly}\log N)
, and
|E_{H}(A,B)|
is sufficiently low. If this happens, we would like to be able to compute a sparse and a roughly balanced cut in graph
H
. If we denote by
E^{\prime}=E_{H}(A,B)
, then ideally we would like to ensure that some canonical cut corresponding to
E^{\prime}
has this property. The second bad event happens if this is not the case for some such cut
(A,B)
. Next, we formally define the second bad event, and show that it can only happen with small probability.

We say that bad event
{\cal{E}}_{2}
happens if there is a weakly well-structured cut
(A,B)
in
H
, with
|A\cap T|,|B\cap T|\geq\frac{|T|}{\log^{4}N}
, and
|E_{H}(A,B)|\leq\frac{|T|}{\log^{6}N}
, such that, if we denote by
