E^{\prime}=E_{H}(A,B)
, then for every canonical cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
, either
|A^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
, or
|B^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
holds.

Claim 7.3
.

\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{2}\right]\leq\frac{1}{64}.

Proof.

Recall that, if event
{\cal{E}}_{1}
does not happen, then
\frac{\hat{W}}{2d^{\prime}}\leq|T|\leq\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}}
holds, and so, if
E^{\prime}
is a set of special edges with
|E^{\prime}|\leq\frac{|T|}{\log^{6}N}
, then
|E^{\prime}|\leq\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\log^{6}N}
.

For every subset
E^{\prime}
of at most
\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\log^{6}N}
special edges, we define a bad event
{\cal{E}}(E^{\prime})
to be the event that there is some cut
(A,B)
in
H
with
E_{H}(A,B)=E^{\prime}
, such that
|A\cap T|,|B\cap T|\geq\frac{|T|}{\log^{4}N}
, and for every canonical cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
, either
|A^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
, or
|B^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
holds.
We bound
\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}(E^{\prime})\right]
for each such set
E^{\prime}
of edges separately, and then use the union bound in order to bound
\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{2}\right]
.

Fix any subset
E^{\prime}
of at most
\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\log^{6}N}
special edges. Let
X\in{\mathcal{X}}(E^{\prime})
be the set of vertices with maximum cardinality. We claim that Event
{\cal{E}}(E^{\prime})
may only happen if
|X|\geq\hat{W}-\frac{2\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
, in the following simple observation.

Observation 7.4
.

If
{\cal{E}}(E^{\prime})
happened, then
|X|\geq\hat{W}-\frac{2\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
must hold.

Proof.

Assume otherwise, that is, Event
{\cal{E}}(E^{\prime})
happened, but
|X|<\hat{W}-\frac{2\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
.
Denote
{\mathcal{X}}(E^{\prime})=(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{q})
, where the sets are listed according to their topological order, and assume that
X=X_{i}
.

Assume first that
|X_{i}|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{4}
. Then either
|\bigcup_{i^{\prime}<i}X_{i^{\prime}}|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
, or
|\bigcup_{i^{\prime}>i}X_{i^{\prime}}|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
. In the former case, cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
is a canonical cut with
|A_{i}|,|B_{i}|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
, while in the latter case, cut
(A_{i-1},B_{i-1})
is a canonical cut with
|A_{i-1}|,|B_{i-1}|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
, a contradiction (if
i=1
then we are guaranteed that
(A_{i},B_{i})
is the cut with the desired properties, and if
i=q
, then we are guaranteed that
(A_{q-1},B_{q-1})
is such a cut).

Assume next that
|X_{i}|<\frac{\hat{W}}{4}
. In this case, we let
1<j<q
be the smallest index for which
\sum_{j^{\prime}<j}|X_{j^{\prime}}|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{2}
. It is easy to verify that
\sum_{j^{\prime}<j}|X_{j^{\prime}}|\leq\frac{3\hat{W}}{4}
must hold, and so cut
(A_{j},B_{j})\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
satisfies that both
|A_{j}|,|B_{j}|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
, a contradiction.

We assume from now on that
|X|\geq\hat{W}-\frac{2\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
holds for the set
E^{\prime}
of edges. Let
Y=V(H)\setminus X
, so
|Y|\leq\frac{2\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
. Consider now any cut
(A,B)\in{\mathcal{C}}(E^{\prime})
, and assume that
|A\cap T|,|B\cap T|\geq\frac{|T|}{\log^{4}N}
. Notice that all vertices of
X
must lie on one side of the cut
(A,B)
. If
X\subseteq A
, then
B\subseteq Y
, and, since
|T\cap B|\geq\frac{|T|}{\log^{4}N}
, we get that
|T\cap Y|\geq\frac{|T|}{\log^{4}N}
. Similarly, if
X\subseteq B
, then
A\subseteq Y
, and, since

|T\cap A|\geq\frac{|T|}{\log^{4}N}
, we get that
|T\cap Y|\geq\frac{|T|}{\log^{4}N}
. In either case, we conclude that Event
{\cal{E}}(E^{\prime})
may only happen if
|X|\geq\hat{W}-\frac{2\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
and
|T\cap Y|\geq\frac{|T|}{\log^{4}N}
.

We start by bounding the probability that
|T\cap X|<\frac{\hat{W}}{4d^{\prime}}
. Since we have assumed that
|X|\geq\hat{W}-\frac{2\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{2}
, and since every vertex is selected into
T
independently with probability
1/d^{\prime}
, we get that
\text{\bf E}\left[|T\cap X|\right]\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{2d^{\prime}}
. From
Lemma

2.5
,
\text{\bf Pr}\left[|T\cap X|<\frac{\hat{W}}{4d^{\prime}}\right]\leq e^{-\hat{W}/(16d^{\prime})}
.

Assume now that
|T\cap X|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{4d^{\prime}}
, so in particular
|T|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{4d^{\prime}}
. In this case, if
|T\cap Y|\geq\frac{|T|}{\log^{4}N}
, then
|T\cap Y|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{4d^{\prime}\log^{4}N}
must hold.
Since
\text{\bf E}\left[|T\cap Y|\right]=\frac{|Y|}{d^{\prime}}\leq\frac{2\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\log^{5}N}
, from
Lemma

2.5
,
\text{\bf Pr}\left[|T\cap Y|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{4d^{\prime}\log^{4}N}\right]\leq 2^{-\hat{W}/(4d^{\prime}\log^{4}N)}\leq e^{-\hat{W}/(16d^{\prime})}
.

Altogether, we get that:

\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}(E^{\prime})\right]\leq\text{\bf Pr}\left[|T\cap X|<\frac{\hat{W}}{4d^{\prime}}\right]+\text{\bf Pr}\left[|T\cap Y|\geq\frac{|T|}{\log^{4}N}\mid|T\cap X|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{4d^{\prime}}\right]\leq 2\cdot e^{-\hat{W}/(16d^{\prime})}.

Observe that
H
has
\hat{W}
special edges, and so the number of sets
E^{\prime}
containing at most
\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\log^{6}N}
special edges is bounded by:

(2\hat{W})^{8\hat{W}/(d^{\prime}\log^{6}N)}\leq(24N)^{8\hat{W}/(d^{\prime}\log^{6}N)}\leq 2^{32\hat{W}/(d^{\prime}\log^{5}N)}.

(We have used the fact that
\hat{W}\leq 12\hat{W}^{0}\leq 12N
). Using the union bound over all sets
E^{\prime}
of at most
\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\log^{6}N}
special edges, we get that the probability that event
{\cal{E}}(E^{\prime})
happens for any such set of edges is bounded by:

2^{\frac{32\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\log^{5}N}}\cdot 2\cdot 2^{-\frac{\hat{W}}{16d^{\prime}\log^{4}N}}\leq 2^{-\frac{\hat{W}}{32d^{\prime}\log^{4}N}}\leq\frac{1}{512},

since
d^{\prime}\leq\frac{8\hat{W}}{\log^{28}N}
from Inequality
20
.

Finally, if Event
{\cal{E}}_{2}
happened, then either
{\cal{E}}_{1}
happened, or, for some set
E^{\prime}
of at most
\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\log^{6}N}
special edges, event
{\cal{E}}(E^{\prime})
has happened. Therefore, overall,
\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{2}\right]\leq\frac{1}{256}+\frac{1}{512}\leq\frac{1}{64}
.

7.2.4
Bad Event
{\cal{E}}_{3}

We start again with intuition. Once we construct the expander
X
and embed it into
H
, we will need to route the remaining vertices of
H
to the vertices of
T
via paths that cause congestion roughly
\widetilde{O}(d^{\prime})
. If we are unable to do so, we will compute a sparse cut that separates some vertices of
H
from most terminals in
T
. Denote this cut by
(A,B)
. The bad scenairo that we would like to avoid is when one side of the cut (say
A
) contains almost all vertices of
H
, while the other side of the cut contains few vertices of
H
, but it contains most terminals. If this happens, then the vertices of
B
will be subsequently deleted from
H
, thereby destroying the expander that we have constructed. As we need to invest a large amount of time into constructing the expander
X
and embedding it into
H
, we would like to avoid such a situation. Let
E^{\prime}=E_{G}(A,B)
. If some canonical cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
is roughly balanced with respect to the vertices of
H
, then we can return cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
instead of cut
(A,B)
, and ensure that a sufficiently large number of vertices is deleted from
H
, and the current phase can be terminated. However, if
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
contains no such cut, then it is not clear how to bypass the problem mentioned above. The third bad event is designed to capture exactly this scenario. We now formally define it and show that it is unlikely to happen.

We say that the bad event
{\cal{E}}_{3}
happens if there is
a weakly well-structured cut
(A,B)
in
H
, with
|E_{H}(A,B)|\leq\frac{|T|}{\log^{6}N}
, such that one of the sides of the cut contains at least
\frac{\hat{W}}{2}
vertices of
H
and at most
\frac{|T|}{32}
vertices of
T
, and additionally, if we denote by
E^{\prime}=E_{H}(A,B)
, then for every canonical cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
, either
|A^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
, or
|B^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
holds.

Claim 7.5
.

\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{3}\right]\leq\frac{1}{64}.

Proof.

As before, if event
{\cal{E}}_{1}
does not happen, and
E^{\prime}
is a set of special edges with
|E^{\prime}|\leq\frac{|T|}{\log^{6}N}
, then
|E^{\prime}|\leq\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\log^{6}N}
must hold.
For every subset
E^{\prime}
of at most
\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\log^{6}N}
special edges, we define a bad event
{\cal{E}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
to be the event that there is some weakly well-structured cut
(A,B)
in
H
with
E_{H}(A,B)=E^{\prime}
, such that one side of the cut cut contains at least
\hat{W}/2
vertices of
H
and at most
\frac{|T|}{32}
vertices of
T
, and additionally,
for every canonical cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
, either
|A^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
, or
|B^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
holds.
We bound
\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})\right]
for each such set
E^{\prime}
of edges separately, and then use the union bound in order to bound
\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{3}\right]
.

Fix any subset
E^{\prime}
of at most
\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\log^{6}N}
special edges. Let
X\in{\mathcal{X}}(E^{\prime})
be the set of vertices with maximum cardinality. Using the same arguments as in the proof of
Claim

7.3
, Event
{\cal{E}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
may only happen if
|X|\geq\hat{W}-\frac{2\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
. We assume from now on that
|X|\geq\hat{W}-\frac{2\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
holds.
Let
Y=V(H)\setminus X
, so that
|Y|\leq\frac{2\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
.

Consider now some weakly well-structured cut
(A,B)
in
H
with
E_{H}(A,B)=E^{\prime}
, and assume that one of the sets
Z\in\left\{A,B\right\}
contains at least
\hat{W}/2
vertices of
H
and at most
\frac{|T|}{32}
vertices of
T
. Since set
X
must be contained in one of the sides of the cut, it must be the case that
X\subseteq Z
, and
|T\cap X|\leq\frac{|T|}{32}=\frac{|T\cap X|+|T\cap Y|}{32}
. Therefore,
|T\cap X|\leq\frac{|T\cap Y|}{31}
must hold.

Using the same reasoning as in the proof of
Claim

7.3
,
\text{\bf Pr}\left[|T\cap X|<\frac{\hat{W}}{4d^{\prime}}\right]\leq e^{-\hat{W}/(16d^{\prime})}
.

Assume now that
|T\cap X|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{4d^{\prime}}
. In this case, if
|T\cap X|\leq\frac{|T|}{32}
holds, then
|T\cap Y|\geq 31|T\cap X|\geq\frac{31\hat{W}}{4d^{\prime}}
must hold.
Notice that, since
|Y|\leq\frac{2\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
,
\text{\bf E}\left[|T\cap Y|\right]\leq\frac{2\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\log^{5}N}
. From
Lemma

2.5
,
\text{\bf Pr}\left[|T\cap Y|\geq\frac{31\hat{W}}{4d^{\prime}}\right]\leq 2^{-31\hat{W}/(4d^{\prime})}\leq e^{-\hat{W}/(16d^{\prime})}
.

Altogether, we get that:

\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})\right]\leq\text{\bf Pr}\left[|T\cap X|<\frac{\hat{W}}{4d^{\prime}}\right]+\text{\bf Pr}\left[|T\cap X|\leq\frac{|T|}{32}\mid|T\cap X|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{4d^{\prime}}\right]\leq 2\cdot e^{-\hat{W}/(16d^{\prime})}.

As in the proof of
Claim

7.3
, the number of sets
E^{\prime}
containing at most
\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\log^{6}N}
special edges is bounded by
2^{32\hat{W}/(d^{\prime}\log^{5}N)}
.

Using the union bound over all sets
E^{\prime}
of at most
\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\log^{6}N}
special edges, we get that the probability that event
{\cal{E}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
happens for any such set of edges is bounded by:

2^{\frac{32\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\log^{5}N}}\cdot 2\cdot 2^{-\frac{\hat{W}}{16d^{\prime}\log^{4}N}}\leq 2^{-\frac{\hat{W}}{32d^{\prime}\log^{4}N}}\leq\frac{1}{512},

since
d^{\prime}\leq\frac{8\hat{W}}{\log^{28}N}
from Inequality
20
.

Finally, if Event
{\cal{E}}_{3}
happened, then either event
{\cal{E}}_{1}
happened, or, for some set
E^{\prime}
of at most
\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\log^{6}N}
special edges, event
{\cal{E}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime})
has happened. Therefore, overall,
\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{3}\right]\leq\frac{1}{256}+\frac{1}{512}\leq\frac{1}{64}
.

7.2.5
Bad Event
{\cal{E}}_{4}

Recall that for every edge
e\in E(G)
, there is a corresponding path
P^{*}(e)
, containing
2\ell(e)
vertices, that represents
e
in graph
H
. Recall that we have ensured that, at the beginning of the phase, the length of every edge
e\in E(G)
is at most
d^{\prime}
. The fourth bad event,
{\cal{E}}_{4}
, happens if there is some edge
e\in E(G)
, such that at least
16\log N
vertices of
P^{*}(e)
are in
T
.

Claim 7.6
.

\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{4}\right]\leq\frac{1}{64}
.

Proof.

For every special edge
e\in E(G)
, let
{\cal{E}}^{\prime\prime}(e)
be the bad event that at least
16\log N
vertices of
P^{*}(e)
are added to
T
. Since the total number of vertices on
P^{*}(e)
is
2\ell(e)\leq 2d^{\prime}
, and every edge is chosen to be added to
T
with probability
\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}
, the expectation of
|P^{*}(e)\cap T|
is at most
2
. From
Lemma

2.5
, the probability of
{\cal{E}}^{\prime\prime}(e)
is then bounded by
2^{-16\log N}\leq N^{-16}
.

By using the union bound over all special edges
e\in E(G)
, and since the number of such edges is bounded by
n\leq N
, we get that:

\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{4}\right]\leq N\cdot N^{-16}\leq\frac{1}{64}.

Lastly, we let
{\cal{E}}
be the bad event that either of the events
{\cal{E}}_{1},{\cal{E}}_{2}
,
{\cal{E}}_{3}
, or
{\cal{E}}_{4}
happened. Clearly:

\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}\right]\leq\sum_{i=1}^{4}\text{\bf Pr}\left[{\cal{E}}_{i}\right]\leq\frac{1}{16}.

Note that we can check, in time
O(N)
, whether events
{\cal{E}}_{1}
and
{\cal{E}}_{4}
hold. If either of these events holds, we terminate the algorithm and return “FAIL”. Therefore, we assume from now on that neither of the events
{\cal{E}}_{1},{\cal{E}}_{4}
happened.

As the phase progresses, vertices may be deleted from graphs
G
and
H
. We view the set
T
of terminal vertices as static, that is, it does not change even as some vertices of
T
are deleted from
H
.

7.3
Finalizing Cuts

In this subsection we define a special kinds of cuts in graph
H
, that we call
finalizing
cuts. If, at any time during the execution of a phase, our algorithm computes such a cut in the current graph
H
, we will be able to terminate the current phase, by either correctly establishing that at least one of the events
{\cal{E}}_{2},{\cal{E}}_{3}
must have happened, or by providing a strongly well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in the current graph
G
, such that
w(A),w(B)\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
. In the former case, we will terminate the current phase with a “FAIL”, while in the latter case, once cut
(A,B)
is processed, and the corresponding vertices and edges are deleted from
H
, we are guaranteed that at least
\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
vertices have been deleted from
H
since the beginning of the phase, and so the current phase terminates. We are now ready to define finalizing cuts in
H
.

Definition 7.7
(Finalizing Cut).

Let
(A,B)
be a cut in graph
H
at any time during the execution of a phase. We say that the cut
(A,B)
is a
finalizing
cut, if the following hold:

•

all edges in
E_{H}(A,B)
are special edges;

•

|E_{H}(A,B)|\leq\frac{|T|}{8\log^{6}N}
; and

•

one of the sides of the cut
Z\in\left\{A,B\right\}
contains at least
|V(H)|-\frac{\hat{W}}{32}
vertices of
H
, and at most
|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{7}{\log^{4}N}\right)
vertices of
T
.

Next, we show that, if our algorithm ever produces a finalizing cut during a phase, then the phase can be terminated, in the following claim.

Claim 7.8
.

Suppose, at any time during the execution of a phase, there is a finalizing cut
(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
in the current graph
H
. Assume further that neither of the events
{\cal{E}}_{2},{\cal{E}}_{3}
has happened, and that every edge in the current graph
G
has length at most
d^{\prime}/2
. Then there is a strongly well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
in the current graph
G
, such that
w(A^{*}),w(B^{*})\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
. Moreover, there is a deterministic algorithm, that, given the graph
G
at the beginning of the current phase (that we denote by
G_{0}
), the sequence of the strongly well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cuts
(A_{1},B_{1}),\ldots(A_{q},B_{q})
in
G
that the algorithm has produced during the current phase so far, the current graphs
G
and
H
, and the finalizing cut
(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
in the current graph
H
, either correctly estabishes that at least one of the events
{\cal{E}}_{2},{\cal{E}}_{3}
has happened, or computes a strongly well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
in the current graph
G
, such that
w(A^{*}),w(B^{*})\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
. The running time of the algorithm is
O(\hat{W}^{2})
.

The remainder of this subsection is dedicated to the proof of
Claim

7.8
.
We denote by
G_{0}
the graph
G
at the beginning of the current phase, and by
H_{0}
its corresponding subdivided graph
H
. The main idea of the proof is to exploit the cuts
(A_{1},B_{1}),\ldots(A_{q},B_{q})
in graph
G
, together with the finalizing cut
(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
in graph
H
, in order to compute another well-structured cut
(\tilde{S},\tilde{S}^{\prime})
in
H_{0}
, such that
|E_{H}(\tilde{S},\tilde{S}^{\prime})|\leq\frac{|T|}{\log^{6}N}
, and one side of the cut contains all but at most
\frac{\hat{W}}{16}
vertices of
H_{0}
, but only a relatively small fraction of the vertices of
T
. Let
E^{\prime\prime}=E_{H}(\tilde{S},\tilde{S}^{\prime})
. By inspecting all canonical cuts in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime\prime})
, we will then either correctly establish that at least one of the events
{\cal{E}}_{2},{\cal{E}}_{3}
happened, or we will compute a cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
in
G
with the required properties.

In the following technical claim, whose proof is deferred to Section
D.2
of Appendix, we provide an algorithm for computing the cut
(\tilde{S},\tilde{S}^{\prime})
in
H_{0}
with the desired properties.

Claim 7.9
.

There is a deterministic algorithm, that, given the graph
G_{0}
, the sequence of the strongly well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cuts
(A_{1},B_{1}),\ldots(A_{q},B_{q})
in
G
that the algorithm has produced during the current phase so far, the current graphs
G
and
H
, such that all edge lengths in
G
are bounded by
d^{\prime}/2
, and the finalizing cut
(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
in the current graph
H
, computes a weakly well-structured cut
(\tilde{S},\tilde{S}^{\prime})
in
H_{0}
, such that
|E_{H_{0}}(\tilde{S},\tilde{S}^{\prime})|\leq\frac{|T|}{\log^{6}N}
, and one side
\tilde{Z}\in\left\{\tilde{S},\tilde{S}^{\prime}\right\}
of the cut contains all but at most
\hat{W}/8
vertices of
H_{0}
, and at most
\left(1-\frac{2}{\log^{4}N}\right)\cdot|T|
vertices of
T
. The
running time of the algorithm is
O(\hat{W}^{2})
.

We are now ready to complete the proof of
Claim

7.8
. Let
E^{\prime\prime}=E_{H_{0}}(\tilde{S},\tilde{S}^{\prime})
. We construct, in time
O(|E(H_{0})|)\leq O(\hat{W}^{2})
, the collection
{\mathcal{X}}(E^{\prime\prime})
of subsets of
H_{0}
associated with the set
E^{\prime\prime}
of edges, and then examine all canonical cuts
(A,B)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime\prime})
.
For each such cut, we check whether
|A|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
, or
|B|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
hold.
From the definition of canonical cuts, we can check this, for every cut
(A,B)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime\prime})
, in time
O(|V(H)|)\leq O(\hat{W})
. We first claim that if, for each such canonical cut
(A,B)
, either
|A|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
or
|B|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
holds, then at least one of the events
{\cal{E}}_{2},{\cal{E}}_{3}
must have happened.

Observation 7.10
.

If, for every canonical cut
(A,B)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime\prime})
, either
|A|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
or
|B|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
holds, then at least one of the events
{\cal{E}}_{2},{\cal{E}}_{3}
must have happened.

Proof.

Recall that one side
\tilde{Z}\in\left\{\tilde{S},\tilde{S}^{\prime}\right\}
of the cut
(\tilde{S},\tilde{S}^{\prime})
in
H
contains all but at most
\hat{W}/8
vertices of
H_{0}
, and at most
\left(1-\frac{2}{\log^{4}N}\right)\cdot|T|
vertices of
T
. Denote the other side of the cut by
\tilde{Z}^{\prime}
. We now consider two cases. The first case happens if
|\tilde{Z}\cap T|\leq\frac{|T|}{32}
. Then
\tilde{Z}
contains at least
\frac{\hat{W}}{2}
vertices of
H
and at most
\frac{|T|}{32}
vertices of
T
. Since, for every canonical cut
(A,B)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime\prime})
, either
|A|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
or
|B|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
holds, we conclude that Event
{\cal{E}}_{3}
must have happened.

Otherwise,
|\tilde{Z}\cap T|\geq\frac{|T|}{32}
, and, since
|\tilde{Z}\cap T|\leq\left(1-\frac{2}{\log^{4}N}\right)\cdot|T|
, we get that
|\tilde{Z}\cap T|,|\tilde{Z}^{\prime}\cap T|\geq\frac{|T|}{\log^{4}N}
. Since, for every canonical cut
(A,B)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime\prime})
, either
|A|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
or
|B|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
holds, we conclude that Event
{\cal{E}}_{2}
must have happened.

If, for every canonical cut
(A,B)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime\prime})
, either
|A|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
or
|B|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
hold, then we terminate the algorithm, and report that at least one of the events
{\cal{E}}_{2},{\cal{E}}_{3}
has happened. We assume from now on that there is a canonical cut
(A,B)\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(E^{\prime\prime})
with
|A|,|B|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{5}N}
. Recall that
(A,B)
is a weakly canonical cut in graph
H_{0}
, and
|E_{H_{0}}|\leq\frac{|T|}{\log^{6}N}\leq\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\log^{6}N}\leq\frac{8}{d^{\prime}\log N}\cdot\min\left\{|A|,|B|\right\}\leq\frac{\varphi}{64}\min\left\{|A|,|B|\right\}
. Therefore,
(A,B)
is a weakly canonical cut of sparsity at most
\frac{\varphi}{64}
in
H_{0}
. Recall also that every edge in
G_{0}
has length at most
\frac{d^{\prime}}{2}=\frac{2}{\varphi}
.

For every vertex
v\in V(G_{0})
, we denote the weight of
v
in
G_{0}
by
w_{0}(v)
, and, for every subset
S\subseteq V(G_{0})
of vertices, we denote
w_{0}(S)=\sum_{v\in S}w_{0}(v)
. Similarly, for every vertex
v\in V(G)
, we denote the weight of
v
in
G
by
w(v)
, and for a subset
S\subseteq V(G)
of vertices, we denote
w(S)=\sum_{v\in S}w(v)
. Note that, for every vertex
v\in V(G)
,
w_{0}(v)\leq w(v)
.

Consider now the cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in
G_{0}
, where
A^{\prime}=A\cap V(G_{0})
and
B^{\prime}=B\cap V(G_{0})
. From
Observation

4.4
,
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
is a weakly well-structured cut in
G_{0}
, whose sparsity is at most
\frac{\varphi}{8}
, with
w_{0}(A^{\prime})\geq\frac{|A|}{8}\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{8\log^{5}N}
, and
w_{0}(B^{\prime})\geq\frac{|B|}{8}\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{8\log^{5}N}
.

Recall that
d^{\prime}\leq\frac{2d}{(\log N)^{28}}\leq\frac{n}{256}\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{128}\leq\frac{\sum_{e\in E(G_{0})}\ell(e)}{2}
. Therefore, the length of every edge in
G_{0}
is bounded by
\frac{\sum_{e\in E(G_{0})}\ell(e)}{2}
. We call now apply the algorithm from
Observation

4.5
to graph
G_{0}
, parameter
\frac{\varphi}{8}
, and the cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in
G_{0}
, to compute a cut
(A^{\prime\prime},B^{\prime\prime})
in
G_{0}
with
w_{0}(A^{\prime\prime})\geq\frac{w_{0}(A^{\prime})}{4}\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{32\log^{5}N}
and
w_{0}(B^{\prime\prime})\geq\frac{w_{0}(B^{\prime})}{4}\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{32\log^{5}N}
, such that
(A^{\prime\prime},B^{\prime\prime})
is a strongly well-structured cut in
G_{0}
, whose sparsity is at most
\varphi/4
.
The running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O(|E(G_{0})|)\leq O(\hat{W}^{2})
.

Lastly, we define the cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
in the current graph
G
as follows:
A^{*}=A^{\prime\prime}\cap V(G)
and
B^{*}=B^{\prime\prime}\cap V(G)
. Since
(A^{\prime\prime},B^{\prime\prime})
is a strongly well-structured cut in
G_{0}
, and
G\subseteq G_{0}
, it is easy to verify that
(A^{*},B^{*})
is a strongly well-structured cut in
G
. Moreover, since the current phase has not terminated yet, the total weight of all vertices deleted so far from
G
is bounded by
\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
, so
w(A^{*})\geq w_{0}(A^{*})\geq w_{0}(A^{\prime\prime})-\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}\geq\frac{w_{0}(A^{\prime\prime})}{2}>\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
.
Similarly,
w(B^{*})\geq w_{0}(B^{*})\geq\frac{w_{0}(B^{\prime\prime})}{2}>\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
. Since
|E_{G}(A^{*},B^{*})|\leq E_{G_{0}}(A^{\prime\prime},B^{\prime\prime})
, we conclude that cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
has sparsity at most
\varphi
. We output the cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
as the outcome of the algorithm. From the above discussion, the running time of the algorithm is bounded by
O(\hat{W}^{2})
.

7.4
First Data Structure: Expander
X
and its Embedding

Throughout the phase, we will maintain an expander
X
, that is defined over the terminals, and is embedded into
H
. We start by providing the algorithm to initialize this data structure, and then provide the algorithm for maintaining it throughout the phase.

7.4.1
Initialization

In this step, we will either compute an expander
X
over the set
T
of vertices and embed it into
H
via short paths that cause low congestion; or we will compute a sparse cut
(A,B)
in
H
, following which a sufficient number of vertices will be deleted from
H
, so that the current phase will terminate.
We summarize the algorithm for initializing the expander in the following lemma, whose statement uses the constant
\alpha_{0}
from
Theorem

2.1
.

Lemma 7.11
.

There is a randomized algorithm, that either returns “FAIL”, or computes one of the following:

•

either a finalizing cut in
H
;

•

or a strongly well-structured
1/d^{\prime}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A),w(B)\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{(\log N)^{30}}
;

•

or a graph
X^{*}
with
V(X^{*})=T
, such that
X^{*}
has maximum vertex degree at most
24
, and it is an
\frac{\alpha_{0}}{81}
-expander. In the latter case, the algorithm additionally computes a set
F\subseteq E(X^{*})
of at most
\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{500}
edges, and an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
of
X^{*}\setminus F
into
H
via paths that have length at most
O\left(d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{16}\right)
each, that cause congestion
O\left((\log N)^{13}\right)
.

The probability that the algorithm returns “FAIL” is at most
\frac{1}{N^{7}}
, and the running time of the algorithm is bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot\hat{W}^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+3}\right).

The remainder of this subsubsection is dedicated to proving
Lemma

7.11
.

Recap of the Cut-Matching Game

We use the Cut-Matching Game – a framework that was initially proposed by
[
KRV09
]
. The specific variant of the game is due to
[
KKOV07
]
. Its slight modification (that we also use) was proposed in
[
CGL+20
]
, and later adapted to directed graphs in
[
BGS20
]
; the same modification was used in
[
CK24
]
.
We also note that
[
Lou10
]
extended the original cut-matching game of
[
KRV09
]
to directed graphs, though we do not use this variation here.
We now define the variant of the game that we use.

The game is played between two players: the Cut Player and the Matching Player.
The game starts with a directed graph
X
that contains
\hat{n}
vertices and no edges, and proceeds in iterations. In the
i
th iteration, the Cut Player chooses a cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
in
X
with
|A_{i}|,|B_{i}|\geq\hat{n}/10
, and
|E_{X}(A_{i},B_{i})|\leq\hat{n}/100
. It then computes two arbitrary equal-cardinality sets
A_{i}^{\prime},B_{i}^{\prime}
of vertices with
|A^{\prime}_{i}|\geq\hat{n}/4
, such that either
A_{i}\subseteq A^{\prime}_{i}
, or
B_{i}\subseteq B^{\prime}_{i}
holds. The matching player then needs to return two perfect matchings: a matching
{\mathcal{M}}_{i}\subseteq A^{\prime}_{i}\times B^{\prime}_{i}
, and a matching
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq B^{\prime}_{i}\times A^{\prime}_{i}
, both of cardinality
|A^{\prime}_{i}|
. The edges of
{\mathcal{M}}_{i}\cup{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{i}
are added to
X
, and the iteration terminates.
If there is no cut
(A,B)
in
X
with
|A|,|B|\geq\hat{n}/10
, and
|E_{X}(A,B)|\leq\hat{n}/100
, then the game must terminate, though we may choose to terminate it earlier.
The following bound on the number of iterations in this variant of the Cut-Matching Game was shown by
[
BGS20
]
, extending a similar result of
[
KKOV07
]
for undirected graphs.

Theorem 7.12
(Lemma 7.4 in
[
BGS20
]
; see also Theorem 7.3 and the following discussion).

The number of iterations in the above Cut-Matching Game is bounded by
O(\log\hat{n})
.

From the above theorem, there is an absolute constant
c_{\mathsf{CMG}}>0
, such that, after
c_{\mathsf{CMG}}\log\hat{n}
iterations of the above game, for every cut
(A,B)
in
X
with
|A|,|B|\geq\hat{n}/10
,
|E_{X}(A_{i},B_{i})|>\hat{n}/100
must hold. Notice that the maximum vertex degree in the final graph
X
is bounded by
2c_{\mathsf{CMG}}\log\hat{n}
.

Implementing the Cut Player

There are several known algorithms that we could use in order to implement the cut player. For example,
[
BGS20
]
, generalizing the results of
[
CGL+20
]
to directed graphs, provided such an algorithm, whose running time is
\widehat{O}(\hat{n})
(see Theorem 7.3 in
[
BGS20
]
). We use instead the algorithm of
[
CK24
]
, whose analysis is very simple, even though its running time is somewhat higher. The algorithm is summarized in the following theorem, that uses the constant
\alpha_{0}
from
Theorem

2.1
.

Theorem 7.13
(Theorem 8.5 in
[
CK24
]
).

There is a deterministic algorithm, that, given an
\hat{n}
-vertex directed graph
G
with maximum vertex degree at most
O(\log\hat{n})
, returns one of the following:

•

Either a cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
|E_{G}(A,B)|\leq\hat{n}/100
and
|A|,|B|\geq\hat{n}/10
; or

•

another directed graph
X^{\prime}
with
|V(X^{\prime})|\geq\hat{n}/4
and maximum vertex degree at most
18
, such that
X^{\prime}
is an
\alpha_{0}
-expander, together with a subset
F\subseteq E(X^{\prime})
of at most
\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot\hat{n}}{1000}
edges of
X^{\prime}
, and an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
X^{\prime}\setminus F
into
G
via paths of length at most
O(\log^{2}\hat{n})
, that cause congestion at most
\tilde{\eta}\leq O(\log^{2}\hat{n})
.

The running time of the algorithm is
\widetilde{O}(\hat{n}^{2})
.

Next, we discuss the implementation of the matching player, which is different from that of
[
CK24
]
: to make the algorithm more efficient, we rely on Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, whose existence we have assumed.

Implementing the Matching Player

We use the parameter
N^{\prime}=\max\left\{N,\hat{W}\right\}
, so that
|V(H)|\leq N^{\prime}
and
N\leq N^{\prime}\leq 12N
holds, and additional parameters

\hat{\Delta}=\left\lceil\frac{|T|}{4}\right\rceil
and
\eta^{\prime}=(\log N^{\prime})^{8}
. Throughout the algorithm, we will sometimes compute disjoint subsets
A,B
of vertices of
H
of cardinality
\hat{\Delta}
each, and we will view
(H,A,B,\hat{\Delta},\eta^{\prime},N^{\prime})
as an instance of the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem. We show that this must be indeed a valid instance of the problem, in the following simple observation whose proof is deferred to Section
D.3
of Appendix.

Observation 7.14
.

For any pair
(A,B)
of disjoint subsets of
V(H)
of cardinality
\hat{\Delta}
each,
(H,A,B,\hat{\Delta},\eta^{\prime},N^{\prime})
is a valid input to the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem.

The matching player will be implemented by applying Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
for the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem to instances
(H,A,B,\hat{\Delta},\eta^{\prime},N^{\prime})
, where
A,B
are disjoint subsets of
V(H)
of cardinality
\hat{\Delta}
each. Throughout, we will use a parameter
{z=\frac{4|T|}{\log^{4}N}}
.

Proof of
Lemma

7.11
: the Cut-Matching Game

We let
\tilde{c}
be a large enough constant, so that,
in
Theorem

7.13
the parameter
\tilde{\eta}\leq\tilde{c}\log^{2}\hat{n}
.
We start with the graph
X
, whose vertex set is
T
, and edge set is
\emptyset
, and then run the Cut-Matching game on it. Recall that the number of iterations in the game is bounded by
c_{\mathsf{CMG}}\cdot\log\hat{n}
.
This ensures that the graph
X
that is maintained over the course of the game has maximum vertex degree
O(\log\hat{n})
.

Description of a Single Iteration

We now describe the execution of the
i
th iteration.
First, we apply the algorithm from
Theorem

7.13
to the current graph
X
. We say that the current iteration is a
type-1 iteration
, if the algorithm returns
a cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
in
X
with
|E_{X}(A_{i},B_{i})|\leq\hat{n}/100
and
|A_{i}|,|B_{i}|\geq\hat{n}/10
, and otherwise we say that it is a type-2 iteration. Assume first that the current iteration is a type-1 iteration. Then we compute two arbitrary disjoint subsets
A^{\prime}_{i},B^{\prime}_{i}
of
V(X)
of cardinality
\left\lceil\frac{\hat{n}}{4}\right\rceil=\left\lceil\frac{|T|}{4}\right\rceil=\hat{\Delta}
each, such that either
A_{i}\subseteq A^{\prime}_{i}
or
B_{i}\subseteq B^{\prime}_{i}
holds. We treat
(A^{\prime}_{i},B^{\prime}_{i})
as the move of the cut player. Recall that the running time of the algorithm from
Theorem

7.13
is
\widetilde{O}(\hat{n}^{2})\leq\widetilde{O}(\hat{W}^{2})
.

Next, we consider two instances of the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem: instance

(H,A_{i}^{\prime},B_{i}^{\prime},\hat{\Delta},\eta^{\prime},N^{\prime})
, and instance
(H,B_{i}^{\prime},A_{i}^{\prime},\hat{\Delta},\eta^{\prime},N^{\prime})
. We apply Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
to each of these two instances.
If the algorithm returns “FAIL” on any of these instances, we repeat it up to
\left\lceil 10\log N\right\rceil
times, or until it terminates with a valid output, whichever happens first. If, for any of the two instances of the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, the algorithm
returned “FAIL” repeatedly in each of its
\left\lceil 10\log N\right\rceil
applications, then we terminate the algorithm and return “FAIL”. Since the probability that an algorithm for the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem returns “FAIL” is at most
1/2
, the probability that the current iteration termintates with a “FAIL” is bounded by
\frac{2}{N^{10}}
. We assume from now on that the last application of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
to each of the two instances did not terminate with a “FAIL”. We denote by
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
the routings that these algorithms return, where
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}
is the routing from
A^{\prime}_{i}
to
B^{\prime}_{i}
. Recall that the running time of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
on each of these two instances is bounded by:

\begin{split}&c_{1}\cdot\hat{W}^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left(c_{1}\cdot\hat{W}^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log(12N))^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+1}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left(c_{1}\cdot\hat{W}^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+1}\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{32c_{2}r}\right)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+1}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left(c_{1}\cdot\hat{W}^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+1}\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{32c_{2}r}\right)^{32c_{2}r}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left(c_{1}\cdot\hat{W}^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+1}\right)\\
\end{split}

(we have used Inequalities
17
and
18
). Since Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
may be applied up to
O(\log N)
times to the resulting instances of the
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem, the total time spent in a single iteration by the executions of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
is bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot\hat{W}^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+2}\right).

We now consider two cases. The first case happens when both
|{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}|,|{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}|\geq\hat{\Delta}-z
. In this case, we say that the current iteration is of
type (1a)
. Recall that the paths in each of the sets
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}
,
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
cause congestion at most
4\eta^{\prime}\cdot\log N^{\prime}\leq 4(\log N^{\prime})^{9}\leq 4(\log(12N))^{9}\leq(\log N)^{10}
, since
\eta^{\prime}=(\log N^{\prime})^{8}
and
N^{\prime}\leq 12N
.
Since regular and special edges must alternate on each such path, and since the total number of special edges is at most
\hat{W}/2
,
\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}}|E(P)|\leq\hat{W}\cdot(\log N)^{10}
. Similarly,
\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}}|E(P)|\leq\hat{W}\cdot(\log N)^{10}
.
We say that a path
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}\cup{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
is
long
, if it contains more than
8d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{14}
edges. Clearly, the number of long paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}
is bounded by:

\frac{\hat{W}\cdot(\log N)^{10}}{8d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{14}}\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{2d^{\prime}(\log N)^{4}}\leq\frac{|T|}{\log^{4}N}\leq z.

(We have used the fact that
|T|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{2d^{\prime}}
, as Event
{\cal{E}}_{1}
did not happen.)
Similarly, at most
z
paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
are long.
We discard all long paths from both
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
. At the end of this process, every path in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}\cup{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
has length at most
8d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{14}
, and
|{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}|,|{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}|\geq\hat{\Delta}-2z
.

The set
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}
of paths naturally defines a matching
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq A^{\prime}_{i}\times B^{\prime}_{i}
, where for every path
Q\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
whose endpoints are
a\in A^{\prime}_{i},b\in B^{\prime}_{i}
, we add an edge
e=(a,b)
to the matching. We also let
Q(e)=Q
be its embedding into
G
. Similarly, the set
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
of paths defines a matching
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}\subseteq B^{\prime}_{i}\times A^{\prime}_{i}
, together with an embedding
Q(e)\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
for each edge
e\in{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime\prime}
. Recall that matching
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{i}
has cardinality at least
\hat{\Delta}-2z
, while
|A^{\prime}_{i}|=|B^{\prime}_{i}|=\hat{\Delta}
. Therefore, by adding a set
F^{\prime}_{i}
of at most
2z
additional edges (that we refer to as
fake edges
), we can turn matching
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{i}
into a perfect one. Similarly, we add a collection
F^{\prime\prime}_{i}
of at most
2z
fake edges to
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
in order to turn it into a perfect matching. The edges of
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}_{i}\cup{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
are then added to graph
X
, and the current iteration terminates.

Assume now that one of the two sets
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i},{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
computed by Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
contains fewer than
\hat{\Delta}-z
paths; we assume w.l.o.g. that it is
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}
. In this case we say that the current iteration is of
type (1b)
.

Let
A^{\prime\prime}_{i}\subseteq A^{\prime}_{i},B^{\prime\prime}_{i}\subseteq B^{\prime}_{i}
be the sets of vertices that do not serve as endpoints of the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}
, so
|A^{\prime\prime}_{i}|,|B^{\prime\prime}_{i}|\geq z
. Recall that the algorithm must also return a cut
(Z,Z^{\prime})
in
H
, such that:

|E_{H}(Z,Z^{\prime})|\leq\frac{64\hat{\Delta}}{\eta^{\prime}\log^{4}\hat{W}}+\frac{256|{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}|}{\eta^{\prime}}\leq\frac{512\hat{\Delta}}{\eta^{\prime}}\leq\frac{256|T|}{(\log N^{\prime})^{8}}\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}(\log N)^{7}},

since
\eta^{\prime}=(\log N^{\prime})^{8}
,
\hat{\Delta}=\left\lceil\frac{|T|}{4}\right\rceil
,
|T|\leq\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}}
since Event
{\cal{E}}_{1}
did not happen, and
N^{\prime}\geq N
.
We are also guaranteed that
A^{\prime\prime}_{i}\subseteq Z
and
B^{\prime\prime}_{i}\subseteq Z^{\prime}
, so
|Z\cap T|,|Z^{\prime}\cap T|\geq z=\frac{4|T|}{\log^{4}N}
.
We use the following simple claim, that either computes a finalizing cut in
H
, or returns a strongly well-structured
1/d^{\prime}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A),w(B)\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{(\log N)^{30}}
.
The proof is deferred to Section
D.4
of Appendix.

Claim 7.15
.

There is a deterministic algorithm, that, given a cut
(Z,Z^{\prime})
in graph
H
with
|Z\cap T|,|Z^{\prime}\cap T|\geq\frac{4|T|}{\log^{4}N}
and
|E_{H}(Z,Z^{\prime})|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}(\log N)^{7}}
, either computes a finalizing cut in
H
, or returns a strongly well-structured
1/d^{\prime}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A),w(B)\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{(\log N)^{30}}
. The running time of the algorithm is
O(\hat{W}^{2})
.

If the algorithm from
Claim

7.15
returns a finalizing cut in
H
, then we output this cut as the outcome of the algorithm. Otherwise, we output the
1/d^{\prime}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A),w(B)\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{(\log N)^{30}}
that the algorithm returns.

Finally, assume that the current iteration
i
is of type 2. In this case, the algorithm for the Cut Player from
Theorem

7.13
computed a directed graph

X^{\prime}
with
|V(X^{\prime})|\geq\hat{n}/4
and maximum vertex degree at most
18
, such that
X^{\prime}
is an
\alpha_{0}
-expander, together with a subset
F^{\prime}\subseteq E(X^{\prime})
of at most
\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot n}{1000}
edges of
X^{\prime}
, and an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
X^{\prime}\setminus F^{\prime}
into
X
via paths of length at most
O(\log^{2}\hat{n})
, that cause congestion at most
\tilde{c}\log^{2}\hat{n}
.

Denote
\hat{F}=\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=1}^{i-1}(F^{\prime}_{i}\cup F^{\prime\prime}_{i})
– the collection of the fake edges that we have computed so far. Since for all
1\leq i^{\prime}<i
,
|F^{\prime}_{i}\cup F^{\prime\prime}_{i}|\leq 4z
and
i\leq c_{\mathsf{CMG}}\log n
, we get that
|\hat{F}|\leq 4c_{\mathsf{CMG}}z\log\hat{n}
.

We also denote
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}=\bigcup_{i^{\prime}=1}^{i-1}({\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}\cup{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i})
. Note that
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
defines an embedding of
X\setminus\hat{F}
into
H
. All paths in
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
have lengths at most
8d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{14}
. Each set
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}\cup{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
causes congestion at most
2(\log N)^{10}
, and so the total congestion caused by the paths in
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
is bounded by
\hat{\eta}=O\left((\log N)^{11}\right)
.
We now terminate the execution of the Cut-Matching Game.

As our next step, we will construct a set
F\subseteq E(X^{\prime})
of fake edges, and an embedding of the remaining edges of
X^{\prime}
into
H
. The latter is done by comining the embedding
{\mathcal{P}}
of
X^{\prime}\setminus F^{\prime}
into
X
with the embedding
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
of
X\setminus\hat{F}
into
H
.

Constructing the Set
F
of Fake Edges for
X^{\prime}
and Embedding
X^{\prime}\setminus F
into
H

We start with
F=F^{\prime}
. Next, we process every edge
e\in E(X^{\prime})\setminus F^{\prime}
one by one. When an edge
e=(u,v)
is processed, we consider the path
P^{\prime}(e)\in{\mathcal{P}}
, that serves as an embedding of
e
into graph
X
. Recall that the length of the path is at most
O(\log^{2}\hat{n})
. Let
P(e)=(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{k})
, where
k\leq O(\log^{2}\hat{n})\leq O(\log^{2}N)
.
If path
P(e)
contains any edges of
\hat{F}
, then we add
e
to the set
F
of fake edges. Otherwise,
for all
1\leq j\leq k
, we consider the path
Q(e_{j})\in\hat{\mathcal{Q}}
, that serves as the embedding of the edge
e_{j}
into graph
G
. Recall that the length of
Q
is at most
8d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{14}
. By concatenating the paths
Q(e_{1}),\ldots,Q(e_{k})
, we obtain a path
P^{\prime}(e)
connecting
u
to
v
in
G
, whose length is at most
O\left(d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{16}\right)
. We turn
P^{\prime}(e)
into a simple path in time
\tilde{O}(d^{\prime})
, obtaining the final path
\hat{P}(e)
, that will serve as an embedding of the edge
e
.

Once every edge of
X^{\prime}\setminus F^{\prime}
is processed, we obtain a final set
F
of fake edges, and, for every remaining edge
e\in E(X^{\prime})\setminus F
, a simple path
\hat{P}(e)
of length
O\left(d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{16}\right)
, that serves as the embedding of
e
in
G
. Recall that
|V(X^{\prime})|\geq\hat{n}/4
, and graph
X^{\prime}
is an
\alpha_{0}
-expander with maximum vertex degree at most
18
.
Next, we bound the congestion caused by the paths in set
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}=\left\{\hat{P}(e)\mid e\in E(X^{\prime})\setminus F\right\}
in
H
, and the cardinality of the set
F
of fake edges.

Consider any edge
e\in E(H)
. Recall that
e
may participate in at most
\hat{\eta}=O\left((\log N)^{11}\right)
paths in set
\hat{\mathcal{Q}}=\left\{Q(e^{\prime})\mid e^{\prime}\in E(X)\setminus\hat{F}\right\}
. Every edge
e^{\prime}\in E(X)\setminus\hat{F}
, whose corresponding path
Q(e^{\prime})
contains the edge
e
, may in turn participate in at most
O(\log^{2}N)
paths of
{\mathcal{P}}
. Therefore, overall, every edge
e
of
G
participates in at most
O\left((\log N)^{13}\right)
paths of
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
.

In order to bound the number of the fake edges in
F
,
recall that
|\hat{F}|\leq 4c_{\mathsf{CMG}}z\log N
. Since the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}
cause congestion at most
\tilde{c}\log^{2}\hat{n}=\tilde{c}\log^{2}N
, we get that every edge of
\hat{F}
may participate in at most
\tilde{c}\log^{2}N
paths of
{\mathcal{P}}
. Therefore,
|F\setminus\hat{F}|\leq(4c_{\mathsf{CMG}}z\log N)\cdot(\tilde{c}\log^{2}N)\leq 4\tilde{c}\cdot c_{\mathsf{CMG}}\cdot z\cdot\log^{3}N\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{2000}
,
since
z=\frac{4|T|}{\log^{4}N}
, and
N
is sufficiently large.
Since
|F^{\prime}|\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{1000}
, we get that
|F|\leq\frac{3\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{2000}
.

Note that graph
X^{\prime}
has all required properties, except that
V(X^{\prime})
may not contain all terminals. In our last step, we fix this problem, by connecting the terminals that do not lie in
X^{\prime}
to the vertices of
X^{\prime}
.

Extending the Expander to All Terminals

Let
T_{0}\subseteq T
be the set of terminals that lie in
X^{\prime}
, so
|T_{0}|\geq\left\lceil\frac{|T|}{4}\right\rceil
. We partition the terminals of
T\setminus T_{0}
into three subsets
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
arbitrarily, so that every subset contains at most
\left\lceil\frac{|T|}{4}\right\rceil
terminals. We now perform last three iterations, that we will denote by iterations
(i+1),(i+2)
and
(i+3)
. In each of these three iterations, we will only invoke the algorithm of the Matching Player, in order to connect the vertices in one of the subsets
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
to
T_{0}
.

We now describe iteration
(i+1)
. The remaining two iterations are performed similarly. We let
A^{\prime}_{i+1}
be any subset of
T_{0}
containing exactly
\hat{\Delta}=\left\lceil\frac{|T|}{4}\right\rceil
vertices. We also let
B^{\prime}_{i+1}
be any subset of
\hat{\Delta}
terminals with
T_{1}\subseteq B^{\prime}_{i+1}
, so that
A^{\prime}_{i+1}
and
B^{\prime}_{i+1}
are disjoint.
Next, we consider two instances of the
r
-restricted
\mathsf{RouteAndCut}
problem: instance

(H,A_{i+1}^{\prime},B_{i+1}^{\prime},\hat{\Delta},\eta^{\prime},N^{\prime})
, and instance
(H,B_{i+1}^{\prime},A_{i+1}^{\prime},\hat{\Delta},\eta^{\prime},N^{\prime})
. We apply Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
to each of these two instances.
As before, if either of these algorithms returns “FAIL”, then we repeat it at most
\left\lceil 10\log N\right\rceil
times, or until it terminates with a valid input – whatever happens first. If the algorithm returned “FAIL” in all
\left\lceil 10\log N\right\rceil
applications, then we terminate the current algorithm with a “FAIL”. We assume from now on that in the last application of algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
to both instances a valid output was computed, and we denote by
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i+1}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i+1}
the routings that these algorithms return, where
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i+1}
is the routing from
A^{\prime}_{i+1}
to
B^{\prime}_{i+1}
.

If either of the sets
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i+1},{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i+1}
of paths has cardinality less than
\hat{\Delta}-z
, then we employ the same algorithm as before to either compute a finalizing cut in
H
, or to compute a strongly well-structured
1/d^{\prime}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A),w(B)\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{(\log N)^{30}}
. We then terminate the algorithm and return the resulting cut.
We assume from now on that
|{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i+1}|,|{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i+1}|\geq\hat{\Delta}-z
.
We use the set
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i+1}
of paths as before (except that we ingore those paths that terminate at vertices of
B^{\prime}_{i+1}\setminus T_{1}
), in order to compute a matching
M^{\prime}_{i+1}\subseteq T_{0}\times T_{1}
, with
|M^{\prime}_{i+1}|\geq|T_{1}|-2z
, together with an embedding of the edges of
M^{\prime}_{i+1}
into the graph
H
, via paths of lengths at most
8d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{14}
, that cause
congestion at most
O(\log^{10}N)
. We then add a collection
F^{\prime}_{i+1}
of at most
2z
fake edges to
M^{\prime}_{i+1}
to turn it into a matching of cardinality
|T_{1}|
. Similarly, we use the paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime\prime}_{i+1}
, in order to compute a matching
M^{\prime\prime}_{i+1}\subseteq T_{1}\times T_{0}
, with
|M^{\prime\prime}_{i+1}|=|T_{1}|
, together with a subset
F^{\prime\prime}_{i+1}\subseteq M^{\prime\prime}_{i+1}
of at most
2z
fake edges, and an embedding of the edges of
M^{\prime\prime}_{i+1}\setminus F^{\prime\prime}_{i+1}
into the graph
H
, via paths of lengths at most
8d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{14}
, that cause
congestion at most
O(\log^{10}N)
. The remaining two iterations are executed similarly, except that we use sets
T_{2}
and
T_{3}
of terminals instead of
T_{1}
.

Consider now the final graph
X^{*}
, that is obtained from
X^{\prime}
by adding the vertices of
T_{1}\cup T_{2}\cup T_{3}
to it, and the edges of
{\mathcal{M}}^{*}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{3}(M^{\prime}_{i+j}\cup M^{\prime\prime}_{i+j})
. Then
V(X^{*})=T
, maximum vertex degree in
X^{*}
is at most
18+6=24
. Moreover, from
Observation

2.2
, graph
X^{*}
is an
\frac{\alpha_{0}}{81}
-expander.

Recall that each of the sets
\left\{F^{\prime}_{i+j},F^{\prime\prime}_{i+j}\mid 1\leq j\leq 3\right\}
of fake edges has cardinality at most
2z\leq\frac{8|T|}{\log^{4}N}
. We add the fake edges from all these sets to the set
F
of fake edges. Since initially
|F|\leq\frac{3\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{2000}
, and since the number of edges that we added to
F
in this step is bounded by
\frac{100|T|}{\log^{4}N}
, we get that
|F|\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{500}
holds. For every edge
e\in{\mathcal{M}}^{*}\setminus F
, we have computed an embedding path
\tilde{P}(e)
, that is added to the set
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
of paths. The final set
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
of paths is an embedding of
X^{*}\setminus F
into
H
, where the length of every path is
O\left(d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{15}\right)
, and the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
cause congestion
O\left((\log N)^{16}\right)
.

Running Time and Failure Probability Analysis

The algorithm has
O(\log\hat{n})\leq O(\log N)
iterations.
The probability that it returns “FAIL” in a single iteration is at most
\frac{2}{N^{10}}
, and so the probability that the algorithm ever returns “FAIL” is at most
\frac{1}{N^{9}}
.

the total time spent in a single iteration on the executions of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}
is bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot\hat{W}^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+2}\right).

Additionally, in every iteration we use the algorithm for the Cut Player from
Theorem

7.13
, whose running time is
\widetilde{O}(\hat{W}^{2})
.

The algorithm from
Claim

7.15
, whose running time is
O(\hat{W}^{2})
, may only be incurred once over the course of the execution of our algorithm.

Additionally, when computing the embedding
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
of
X^{*}\setminus F
into
G
, we spend time at most
O\left(d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{16}\right)
per edge of
X^{\prime}
, and, since
|E(X^{\prime})|\leq O(\hat{W})
, the time required to compute the embedding is bounded by:
O\left(\hat{W}\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{15}\right)\leq O\left(\hat{W}^{2}\right)
, since
d^{\prime}\leq\frac{2d}{(\log N)^{28}}
and
d\leq n
from Inequality
15
.
Overall, the running time of the algorithm is bounded by:

\begin{split}&O\left(c_{1}\cdot\hat{W}^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+3}\right)+\tilde{O}(\hat{W}^{2})\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left(c_{1}\cdot\hat{W}^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+3}\right).\end{split}

7.4.2
Maintaining the Expander

At the beginning of the phase, we execute the algorithm from
Lemma

7.11
. If the algorithm terminated with a “FAIL”, which may only happen with probability at most
\frac{1}{N^{7}}
, then we terminate the algorithm for the current phase and return “FAIL”. If the algorithm returned
a finalizing cut in
H
, then we apply the algorithm from
Claim

7.8
to this cut. If the algorithm from
Claim

7.8
estabishes that at least one of the events
{\cal{E}}_{2},{\cal{E}}_{3}
happened, we terminate the phase with a “FAIL”. Otherwise, we obtain a strongly well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
in the current graph
G
, such that
w(A^{*}),w(B^{*})\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
. In this case, we output the cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
. Once this cut is processed, at least
\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
vertices are deleted from
H
, and the current phase terminates. Finally, if the algorithm from

Lemma

7.11
returns a strongly well-structured
1/d^{\prime}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A),w(B)\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{(\log N)^{30}}
, then as before we output this cut, and, once it is processed, at least
\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
vertices are deleted from
H
, and the current phase terminates. Therefore, we assume from now on that the algorithm from
Lemma

7.11
computed a graph
X^{*}
, that we will denote for simplicity by
X
from now on. Recall that
V(X)=T
, maximum vertex degree in
X
is at most
24
, and
X
is an
\frac{\alpha_{0}}{81}
-expander.
Recall that we are also given a set
F\subseteq E(X)
of at most
\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{500}
fake edges, and an embedding
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
of
X\setminus F
into
H
.
We let
d^{\prime\prime}=\Theta\left(d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{15}\right)
and
\eta^{\prime\prime}=\Theta\left((\log N)^{13}\right)
be such that every path in
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
has length at most
d^{\prime\prime}
, and the congestion caused by the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
is at most
\eta^{\prime\prime}
. For convenience, we will denote the set
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
of paths by
{\mathcal{Q}}
from now on, and, for every edge
\hat{e}\in E(X)\setminus F
, we denote by
Q(\hat{e})\in{\mathcal{Q}}
its corresponding embedding path.

Recall that, since Event
{\cal{E}}_{1}
did not happen, we are guaranteed that
\frac{\hat{W}}{2d^{\prime}}\leq|T|\leq\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}}
. As the phase progresses, some vertices and edges may be deleted from
X
and from
G
. We will view the set
T
of terminals as a static set, so even if some vertices of
T
are deleted from
X
or from
G
, the set
T
remains unchanged.

Throughout the algorithm, for every edge
e\in E(H)
, we maintain a collection
S^{\prime}(e)\subseteq E(X)
of edges
\hat{e}\in E(X)\setminus F
, such that
e\in Q(\hat{e})
. Clearly, the time required to compute these initial sets
S^{\prime}(e)
of edges, for all
e\in E(H)
, is subsumed by the time required to compute the embedding of
X\setminus F
into
H
. Additionally, for every edge
\hat{e}\in E(X)\setminus F
, we maintain the length
\hat{\ell}(\hat{e})
of the path
Q(\hat{e})
, which is equal to the number of edges on the path, and must initially be bounded by
d^{\prime\prime}
.

As the phase progresses, graph
H
undergoes changes, that may require updating the expander
X
and its embedding. Specifically, we need to ensure that, even as vertices and edges are deleted from
H
,
V(X)\subseteq V(H)
, and, for every edge
\hat{e}\in E(X)\setminus F
,
Q(\hat{e})\subseteq H
continue to hold. Additionally, as edges in
H
undergo splitting, some of the embedding paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
may become longer, and we may need to delete the corresponding edges from
X
, in order to ensure that the lengths of all embedding paths are suitably bounded. Lastly, we need to ensure that, throughout the phase, for every pair
x,y\in V(X)
of vertices, there is a path connecting
x
to
y
in
X\setminus F
, whose length is at most
O(\log N)
. Whenever our algorithm discovers that this is no longer the case, it may compute a sparse cut
(A,B)
in
X
, and delete the vertices of the smaller side of the cut from
X
. We now describe all updates that graph
X
undergoes over the course of the phase formally. We will ensure that, despite these updates, throughout the phase,
|V(X)|\geq|T|/2
holds.

First Type of Updates: Due to Edge Splitting

Recall that, as the algorithm progresses, the lengths of some edges
e\in E(G)
may be doubled. Whenever this happens, we update the corresponding path
P^{*}(e)
in
H
, by splitting every special edge
e^{\prime}\in E(P^{*}(e))
. The latter is done by replacing the special edge
e^{\prime}=(x,y)
with a path
(x,z,z^{\prime},y)
, where
z,z^{\prime}
are new vertices, and
(x,z)
,
(z^{\prime},y)
are new special edges. Notice that once edge
e^{\prime}
is split, it no longer lies in graph
H
. For every edge
\hat{e}\in E(X)\setminus F
that lies in
S^{\prime}(e^{\prime})
, we consider the corresponding embedding path
Q(\hat{e})
, that must contain the edge
e^{\prime}
. We replace
e^{\prime}
with the new path
(x,z,z^{\prime},y)
on
Q(\hat{e})
, and insert
\hat{e}
into the sets
S^{\prime}((x,z))
,
S^{\prime}((z^{\prime},y))
, that correspond to the new special edges
(x,z)
and
(z^{\prime},y)
. We also update the length
\hat{\ell}(\hat{e})
of path
Q(\hat{e})
, which increases by additive
2
. If, as the result of this procedure, the length of path
Q(\hat{e})
became greater than
8d^{\prime\prime}
, then edge
\hat{e}
is deleted from graph
X
.

We denote by
\hat{E}_{1}
the set of all edges that were deleted from graph
X
over the course of the phase due to this type of updates. We now show that the number of such edges must be small.

Claim 7.16
.

|\hat{E}_{1}|\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{5000}
.

Proof.

Recall that, over the course of the phase, the algorithm only needs to respond to at most
\Delta^{\prime}
queries. Given such a query between a pair
(x,y)
of vertices, the algorithm must return a path
P
of length at most
d
connecting
x
to
y
. Denote by
\tilde{\ell}(P)
the length of the path
P
at the time when it was returned in response to the query. Then the lengths of some edges on path
P
are doubled.
If
{\mathcal{P}}
is the set of all paths that the algorithm returned in response to queries over the course of the phase, then
\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\tilde{\ell}(P)\leq\Delta^{\prime}\cdot d
must hold.

Let
G_{0}
denote the graph
G
at the beginning of the phase.
Recall that, if
e
is any edge of
G_{0}
, and
\tau,\tau^{\prime}
are two consecutive times during the current phase when the length of
e
is doubled, then
e
must have participated in at least
\eta
paths that the algorithm returned during the time interval
(\tau,\tau^{\prime}]
. However, the first time that the length of
e
is doubled during the current phase may occur even before it participates in
\eta
such paths.
For every edge
e\in E(G_{0})
, denote by
\tilde{\ell}^{0}(e)
the length of
e
at the beginning of the current phase. If
e\in E(G)
at the end of the phase, then we denote by
\tilde{\ell}^{*}(e)
the length of
e
at the end of the phase; otherwise,
\tilde{\ell}^{*}(e)
is the length of
e
just before it is deleted from
G
.

Let
E^{\prime}\subseteq E(G_{0})
be the set of all edges
e
with
\tilde{\ell}^{*}(e)>2\tilde{\ell}^{0}(e)
, so
E^{\prime}
contains all edges of
G_{0}
whose lengths were doubled at least twice.
Consider now any edge
e\in E^{\prime}
, and assume that
\tilde{\ell}^{*}(e)=2^{i}
. Let
\tau
be the time during the current phase when the length of
e
was doubled from
2^{i-2}
to
2^{i-1}
, and let
\tau^{\prime}
be the time when it was doubled from
2^{i-1}
to
2^{i}
. Then
e
participated in at least
\eta
paths that were returned by the algorithm in response to queries during the time interval
(\tau,\tau^{\prime}]
. For each such path
P
, it contributed
2^{i-1}=\frac{\tilde{\ell}^{*}(e)}{2}
to
\tilde{\ell}(P)
. Therefore, every edge
e\in E^{\prime}
contributes at least
\frac{\eta\cdot\tilde{\ell}^{*}(e)}{2}
to
\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\tilde{\ell}(P)
. We conclude that
\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}}\tilde{\ell}^{*}(e)\leq\frac{2}{\eta}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\tilde{\ell}(P)\leq\frac{2\Delta^{\prime}\cdot d}{\eta}
.

Notice that, for every edge
e\in E(G)
, at any time during the execution of the algorithm, the number of special edges on
P^{*}(e)
is equal to the current length of
e
, while the splitting of a speical edge on
P^{*}(e)
increases the number of special edges on
P^{*}(e)
by
1
. Therefore, if the current length of
e
is
\ell(e)
, then the edges of
P^{*}(e)
underwent
\ell(e)-\tilde{\ell}^{0}(e)
splittings since the beginning of the algorithm.

Consider the graph
H
at the beginning of the current phase, that we denote by
H_{0}
.
Note that, whenever a special edge
e\in E(H)
undergoes a splitting, the edge itself is deleted from
H
, and instead two new edges are inserted. Consider now a special edge
e\in E(H)
, and suppose it underwent a splitting at some time. We say that this splitting operation is
unimportant
if
e\in E(H_{0})
, and we say that it is
important
otherwise. Note that, if an edge
e\in E(H)
undergoes an important splitting update, then its parent-edge must lie in
E^{\prime}
. Therefore, from the above discussion, the total number of important splitting operations in graph
H
is bounded by
\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}}\tilde{\ell}^{*}(e)
.

Consider now some edge
\hat{e}\in E(X)\setminus F
, and its embedding path
Q(\hat{e})
. Let
\mu(\hat{e})
be the total number of important splitting operations that the edges of path
Q(\hat{e})
underwent over the course of the algorithm.
Since the embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}
of
X\setminus F
into
H
causes congestion at most
\eta^{\prime\prime}
, we get that
\sum_{e\in X\setminus F}\mu(\hat{e})\leq\eta^{\prime\prime}\cdot\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}}\tilde{\ell}^{*}(e)\leq\frac{2\Delta^{\prime}\cdot d\cdot\eta^{\prime\prime}}{\eta}
. On the other hand, if
\hat{e}\in\hat{E}_{1}
, then the length of
Q(\hat{e})
increased from
d^{\prime\prime}
to
8d^{\prime\prime}
over the course of the algorithm, and so the edges on
Q(\hat{e})
must have undergone at least
6d^{\prime\prime}
important splitting operations so
\mu(\hat{e})\geq 6d^{\prime\prime}
. Therefore:

|\hat{E}^{\prime}|\leq\frac{2\Delta^{\prime}\cdot d\cdot\eta^{\prime\prime}}{6\eta d^{\prime\prime}}\leq O\left(\frac{\Delta\cdot d}{\eta\cdot d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{33}}\right)\leq O\left(\frac{n}{d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{33}}\right)\leq O\left(\frac{\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{33}}\right)<\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{5000},

since
d^{\prime\prime}=\Theta\left(d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{16}\right)
,
\Delta^{\prime}=\frac{\Delta}{(\log N)^{30}}
, and
\eta^{\prime\prime}=\Theta\left((\log N)^{13}\right)
. We have also used the fact that
\frac{\Delta\cdot d}{\eta}\leq n
from the problem definition, that
\hat{W}\geq\frac{n}{2}
, and that
|T|>\frac{8\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}}
, since Event
{\cal{E}}_{1}
did not happen.

Second Type of Updates: Due to Sparse Cuts in
X

Throughout the execution of the phase, we may sometimes find cuts
(A,B)
in
X\setminus F
, with
|E_{X\setminus F}(A,B)|\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2^{20}}\cdot\min\left\{|A|,|B|\right\}
. Denote
A
and
B
by
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
, where
Z
is the smaller of the two sets, breaking ties arbitrarily. We then delete the vertices of
Z
from graph
X
.
Notice that
|E_{X\setminus F}(A,B)|\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2^{20}}\cdot|Z|
. We
charge
the vertices of
Z
for the edges of
E_{X\setminus F}(A,B)
, where each vertex
v\in Z
is charged
\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2^{20}}
units, so the total charge is at least
|E_{X\setminus F}(A,B)|
.

Let
\hat{E}_{2}
be the set of edges in the initial graph
X
, that is defined as follows. Initially,
\hat{E}_{2}=\emptyset
. Whenever our algorithm computes a cut

(A,B)
in
X\setminus F
, with
|E_{X\setminus F}(A,B)|\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2^{20}}\cdot\min\left\{|A|,|B|\right\}
, we add the edges of
E_{X\setminus F}(A,B)
to set
\hat{E}_{2}
. From the charging scheme that we just described, it is easy to verify that, at the end of the algorithm,
|\hat{E}_{2}|\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{2^{20}}
holds.

Third Type of Updates: Due to Sparse Cuts in
G

Recall that our algorithm may, at any time, produce a strongly well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in graph
G
. If
w(A)\leq W(B)
, then the vertices of
A
are deleted from
G
, and otherwise the vertices of
B
are deleted from
G
. Additionally, the endpoints of the edges of
E_{G}(A,B)
are also deleted from
G
, and graph
H
is also updated with corresponding edge- and vertex-deletions. This may require updating graph
X
, for example, if some of the embedding paths in
{\mathcal{Q}}
use vertices or edges that were deleted from
H
. We emphasize that edges and vertices may only be deleted from
G
via this procedure, and the only other updates that graph
G
undergoes is the doubling of the lengths of its edges.

Let
(A_{1},B_{1}),\ldots,(A_{q},B_{q})
denote the sequence of all strongly well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cuts in
G
that our algorithm produced during the current phase, indexed in the order in which the algorithm produced them. If the last sparse cut that the algorithm produced caused the total number of vertices that were deleted from
H
over the course of the phase to reach
\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
(after which the current phase is immediately terminated), then we do not include that cut in this sequence. For all
1\leq i\leq q
, denote by
\tau_{i}
the time when cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
was produced, by
E_{i}=E_{G}(A_{i},B_{i})
at time
\tau_{i}
, and by
J_{i}\subseteq A_{i},J^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq B_{i}
the vertices that serve as endpoints of the edges in
E_{i}
(with respect to
G^{(\tau_{i})}
– graph
G
at time
\tau_{i}
).
If
w(A_{i})\geq w(B_{i})
, then we denote
Z_{i}=A_{i}\setminus J_{i}
, and otherwise we denote
Z_{i}=B_{i}\setminus J^{\prime}_{i}
. We also let
\overline{Z}_{i}=V(G^{(\tau_{i})})\setminus Z_{i}
contain the remaining vertices of the current graph
G^{(\tau_{i})}
. For convenience, if
Z_{i}=A_{i}\setminus J_{i}
, then we say that cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
is of
type 1
, and otherwise we say that it is of
type 2
.

We denote by
G_{0}
the graph
G
at the beginning of the phase, and, for all
1\leq i\leq q
, we denote by
G_{i}
the graph
G
that is obtained after cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
has been processed. Therefore, for all
1\leq i\leq q
, graph
G_{i}
is obtained from graph
G_{i-1}
by deleting the vertices of
\overline{Z}_{i}
from it, or, equivalently,
G_{i}=G_{i-1}[Z_{i}]
.

We denote by
H_{0}
the graph
H
at the beginning of the current phase. For all
1\leq i\leq q
, we denote by
H^{\prime}_{i}
the graph
H
just before the cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
is processed, and we denote by
H_{i}
the graph
H
immediately after cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
is processed.
Therefore, graph
H_{i}
is obtained from
H^{\prime}_{i}
by deleting from it all vertices and regular edges that were deleted from
G_{i-1}
; additionally, for every special edge
e
that was deleted from
G_{i-1}
, all vertices and edges on the corresponding path
P^{*}(e)
are deleted from
H^{\prime}_{i}
. We denote
V(H_{i})
by
Z^{*}_{i}
, and we denote by
\overline{Z}^{*}_{i}=V(H^{\prime}_{i})\setminus V(H_{i})
the set of all vertices that were deleted from
H^{\prime}_{i}
when cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
of
G
was processed.

We note that, in addition to the updates due to processing the cuts
(A_{j},B_{j})
, the only other kind of updates that graph
H
undergoes is the splitting of some of its special edges, that happens whenever the length of the corresponding parent-edge is doubled. Therefore, for all
1\leq i\leq q
, graph
H^{\prime}_{i}
can be obtained from
H_{i-1}
by executing a sequence of special edge splitting operations.

Consider some index
1\leq i\leq q
, and consider an edge
e=(x,y)\in E_{i}
. Recall that this edge is represented by a path
P^{*}(e)
in graph
H
. Additionally, every regular edge
e^{\prime}
that is incident to
x
or
y
in
G
is present in graph
H
. We let
\Gamma(e)\subseteq E(H^{\prime}_{i})
contain all regular edges that are incident to
x
or
y
in
H^{\prime}_{i}
, and all edges of
P^{*}(e)
.
We then set
\Gamma_{i}=\bigcup_{e\in E_{i}}\Gamma(e)
. Notice that, if
(A_{i},B_{i})
is a type-1 cut (so
Z_{i}=A_{i}\setminus J_{i}
), then graph
H^{\prime}_{i}\setminus\Gamma_{i}
contains no edge connecting a vertex of
Z^{*}_{i}
to a vertex of
\overline{Z}^{*}_{i}
. Similarly, if
(A_{i},B_{i})
is a type-2 cut (so
Z_{i}=B_{i}\setminus J^{\prime}_{i}
), the graph
H^{\prime}_{i}\setminus\Gamma_{i}
contains no edge connecting a vertex of
\overline{Z}^{*}_{i}
to a vertex of
Z^{*}_{i}
. Therefore, it may be convenient to think of the update to graph
H^{\prime}_{i}
due to the cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
in
G
as follows: first, we delete the edges of
\Gamma_{i}
from
H^{\prime}_{i}
. Then we obtain a sparsity-
0
cut in the resulting graph (with the cut being
(Z^{*}_{i},\overline{Z}^{*}_{i})
if
(A_{i},B_{i})
is a type-1 cut, or
(\overline{Z}^{*}_{i},Z^{*}_{i})
otherwise). After that we delete the vertices of one of the sides of the cut (vertices of
\overline{Z}^{*}_{i}
) from the resulting graph, obtaining graph
H_{i}
.

Before we describe the updates to the expander
X
, recall that
X
undergoes other types of updates, in which edges and vertices may be deleted from it. But for the sake of analyzing the updates of type 3, it will be helpful for us to ignore the other types of updates that expander
X
undergoes. Therefore, we denote the initial graph
X
by
X_{0}
, and, for
1\leq i\leq q
, we denote by
X_{i}
the graph that is obtained from
X_{i-1}
once the cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
is processed. Except for updating the graph
X
while processing the cuts
(A_{i},B_{i})
, we currently do not allow any other updates to
X
. We will show how combine all types of updates to
X
together later.

Fix again an index
1\leq i\leq q
, and consider an edge
e=(x,y)\in E_{i}
. Recall that we have defined a collection
\Gamma(e)
of edges of
H^{\prime}_{i}
, that includes all edges on the path
P^{*}(e)
, and all regular edges that are incident to
x
or to
y
in
H^{\prime}_{i}
. We define the corresponding subset
\hat{\Gamma}(e)
of edges of
X_{i-1}\setminus F
:
\hat{\Gamma}(e)=\bigcup_{e^{\prime}\in\Gamma(e)}S^{\prime}(e^{\prime})
. In other words, an edge
\hat{e}\in E(X_{i-1})\setminus F
belongs to the set
\hat{\Gamma}(e)
if and only if its corresponding embedding path
Q(\hat{e})
contains at least one edge of
\Gamma(e)
. We need the following simple observation.

Observation 7.17
.

For every edge
e=(x,y)\in E_{i}
,
|\hat{\Gamma}(e)|\leq 2\eta^{\prime\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
T(e)
be the set of all terminals that lie on path
P^{*}(e)
.
Since we have assumed that Event
{\cal{E}}_{4}
did not happen,
|T(e)|\leq 16\log N
.

We classify the edges in set
\hat{\Gamma}(e)
into three different types, and bound the number of edges of each type separately. Consider any edge
\hat{e}\in\hat{\Gamma}(e)
.

We say that
\hat{e}
is of type 1, if at least one of its endpoints lies on
P^{*}(e)
. On this case, at least one endpoint of
\hat{e}
belongs to set
T(e)
. Since
|T(e)|\leq 16\log N
, and since the degree of every vertex in
X
is at most
24
, we get that the total number of type-1 edges in
\hat{\Gamma}(e)
is bounded by
O(\log N)\leq\eta^{\prime\prime}
.

We say that edge
\hat{e}
is of type 2, if it is not of type 1, and, additionally, its embedding path
Q(\hat{e})
contains at least one edge of
P^{*}(e)
. Since every inner vertex on
P^{*}(e)
has in- and out-degree
1
, and the endpoints of
\hat{e}
do not lie on
P^{*}(e)
, it must be the case that
P^{*}(e)\subseteq Q(\hat{e})
. Since the embedding of
X\setminus F
into
H
causes congestion at most
\eta^{\prime\prime}
, we get that the total number of type-2 edges in
\hat{\Gamma}
is bounded by
\eta^{\prime\prime}
.

If edge
\hat{e}
is not of the first two types, then we say that it is of type 3. We claim that no type-3 edges may exist. Indeed, assume for contradiction that some edge
\hat{e}\in\hat{\Gamma}(e)
is of type
3
. Then path
Q(\hat{e})
must contain some regular edge
e^{\prime}
of
H^{\prime}_{i}
that is incident to
x
or
y
; assume w.l.o.g. that
e^{\prime}
is incident to
x
. However,
x
is not an endpoint of
\hat{e}
, and so it is not an endpoint of
Q(\hat{e})
. Since the only edge leaving
x
in
G
is
e
, path
Q(\hat{e})
must contain the first edge on
P^{*}(e)
. But then
\hat{e}
must belong to one of the first two types, a contradiction.

Fix again an index
1\leq i\leq q
. We now define the set
\hat{\Gamma}_{i}
of edges of
X_{i-1}\setminus F
, as follows:
\hat{\Gamma}_{i}=\bigcup_{e\in E_{i}}\hat{\Gamma}(e)
.
Note that, equivalently,
\hat{\Gamma}_{i}
contains all edges
\hat{e}
of
X_{i-1}\setminus F
, whose embedding path
Q(\hat{e})
contains at least one edge of
\Gamma_{i}
. Notice that, from
Observation

7.17
,
|\hat{\Gamma}_{i}|\leq 2|E_{i}|\cdot\eta^{\prime\prime}
.
We denote
\hat{E}_{3}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{q}\hat{\Gamma}_{i}
.
We need the following simple observation.

Observation 7.18
.

\sum_{i=1}^{q}|E_{i}|\leq\frac{\varphi\cdot\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
, and
|\hat{E}_{3}|\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{2^{20}}
.

Proof.

Fix an index
1\leq i\leq q
. Since cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
is
\varphi
-sparse in
G_{i-1}
,
|E_{i}|\leq\varphi\cdot\min\left\{w(A_{i}),w(B_{i})\right\}
. Moreover, once cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
is processed, the total weight of all vertices in
G
decreases by at least
\min\left\{w(A_{i}),w(B_{i})\right\}\geq\frac{|E_{i}|}{\varphi}
, and so at least
\frac{|E_{i}|}{\varphi}
vertices are deleted from
H^{\prime}_{i}
to obtain
H_{i}
.
Since we have assumed that, by the time that cut
(A_{q},B_{q})
has been processed, fewer than
\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
vertices were deleted from
H
, we get that

{\sum_{i=1}^{q}|E_{i}|\leq\frac{\varphi\cdot\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}}
.

From
Observation

7.17
, for all
1\leq i\leq q
,
|\hat{\Gamma}_{i}|\leq 2|E_{i}|\cdot\eta^{\prime\prime}
. Therefore:

|\hat{E}_{3}|=\sum_{i=1}^{q}|\hat{\Gamma}_{i}|\leq 2\eta^{\prime\prime}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{q}|E_{i}|\leq\frac{2\varphi\cdot\eta^{\prime\prime}\cdot\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}\leq O\left(\frac{\hat{W}}{d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{17}N}\right)\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot\hat{W}}{2^{21}\cdot d^{\prime}}\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{2^{20}},

since
\eta^{\prime\prime}=\Theta(\log^{13}N)
,
\varphi=\frac{4}{d^{\prime}}
, and
N
is large enough.

We are now ready to describe the type-3 updates to the expander
X
. Fix an index
1\leq i\leq q
. In order to update expander
X_{i-1}
following the cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
in graph
G_{i-1}
that our algorithm produced, we start by deleting the edges of
\hat{\Gamma}_{i}
from
X_{i-1}
.
For each such edge
\hat{e}\in\hat{\Gamma}_{i}
, we consider every edge
e\in Q(\hat{e})
, and update the set
S^{\prime}(e)
of edges by deleting
\hat{e}
from it.
Next, we denote
T_{i}=V(X_{i-1})\cap Z^{*}_{i}
, and
\overline{T}_{i}=V(X_{i-1})\cap\overline{Z}_{i}^{*}
. If
\overline{T}_{i}\neq\emptyset
, then we delete the vertices of
\overline{T}_{i}
from
X_{i-1}
, obtaining the final graph
X_{i}
. We now discuss this latter update in more detail, presenting a slightly different view of this update, that will be helpful for us later.

Assume first that
(A_{i},B_{i})
in
G_{i-1}
is a type-1 cut, so
Z_{i}=A_{i}\setminus J_{i}
. As observed already, in this case, graph
H^{\prime}_{i}\setminus\Gamma_{i}
contains no edge connecting a vertex of
Z^{*}_{i}
to a vertex of
\overline{Z}^{*}_{i}
. It is then easy to verify that graph
X_{i-1}\setminus\hat{\Gamma}_{i}
contains no edge connecting a vertex of
T_{i}
to a vertex of
\overline{T}_{i}
. Indeed, if such an edge
\hat{e}
existed, then its corresponding path
Q(\hat{e})
would connect a vertex of
Z^{*}_{i}
to a vertex of
\overline{Z}^{*}_{i}
in
H_{i}^{\prime}
, and so it must contain an edge of
\Gamma_{i}
. But then
\hat{e}\in\hat{\Gamma}_{i}
holds, a contradiction. Therefore, if
(A_{i},B_{i})
is a type-1 cut, and
T_{i},\overline{T}_{i}\neq\emptyset
, then
(T_{i},\overline{T}_{i})
is a cut of sparsity
0
in
X_{i-1}\setminus\hat{\Gamma}_{i}
.

Next, assume that
(A_{i},B_{i})
is a type-2 cut. As observed already, in this case, graph
H^{\prime}_{i}\setminus\Gamma_{i}
contains no edge connecting a vertex of
\overline{Z}^{*}_{i}
to a vertex of
Z^{*}_{i}
. From the same arguments as above, graph
X_{i-1}\setminus\hat{\Gamma}_{i}
contains no edge connecting a vertex of
\overline{T}_{i}
to a vertex of
\overline{T}_{i}
. Therefore, if
(A_{i},B_{i})
is a type-2 cut, and
T_{i},\overline{T}_{i}\neq\emptyset
, then
(\overline{T}_{i},T_{i})
is a cut of sparsity
0
in
X_{i-1}\setminus\hat{\Gamma}_{i}
.

To conclude, we can view the update to graph
X_{i-1}
as follows: first, we delete all edges of
\hat{\Gamma}_{i}
from
X_{i-1}
. Then we either delete all vertices from
X_{i-1}
(if
T_{i}=\emptyset
), or delete no vertices from
X_{i-1}
(if
\overline{T}_{i}=\emptyset
), or we obtain a sparsity-
0
cut in the resulting graph
X_{i-1}\setminus\hat{\Gamma}
, and we delete all vertices on one side of this cut from
X_{i-1}
.
This view of the update procedure will be helpful for us later.
It is easy to verify that, at the end of this update, the collection
\left\{Q(\hat{e})\in{\mathcal{Q}}\mid\hat{e}\in E(X_{i})\setminus F\right\}
defines a valid embedding of graph
X_{i}\setminus F
into graph
H_{i}
. Indeed, we have ensured that
T_{i}\subseteq V(H_{i})
. Additionally, for every edge
\hat{e}\in E(X_{i})\setminus F
, both endpoints of
\hat{e}
lie in set
Z^{*}_{i}
, and, since path
Q(\hat{e})
is disjoint from the edges of
\Gamma_{i}
, it may not use vertices of
\overline{Z}^{*}_{i}
, and must therefore be contained in
H_{i}
.

Lastly, we would like to claim that
|V(X_{q})|
remains sufficiently large compared to
|T|=|V(X_{0})|
, but unfortunately this may not be the case. Instead we will show that, if
1\leq i\leq q
is the smallest index, for which
|X_{i}|\leq|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{8}{\log^{4}N}\right)
holds, then we can compute a finalizing cut in graph
G_{i-1}
.
In our algorithm, we will monitor the number of vertices in the current graph
X_{i}
, and, if it ever falls below
|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{8}{\log^{4}N}\right)
, we will compute a finalizing cut, and apply the algorithm from
Claim

7.8
to it, after which the current phase will be terminated, so in particular no further cuts
(A_{j},B_{j})
will be produced by the algorithm during the current phase. Therefore, essentially we only need to consider the case where
|V(X_{q-1})|\geq|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{8}{\log^{4}N}\right)
but
|V(X_{q})|<|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{8}{\log^{4}N}\right)
. The following claim summarizes the algorithm that will allow us to complete the current phase in such a case. For a vertex
v\in V(G_{0})
, we denote by
\hat{w}(v)
its weight at the beginning of current phase, and for a subset
S
of vertices of
G_{0}
, we denote by
\hat{w}(S)=\sum_{v\in S}\hat{w}(v)
.

Claim 7.19
.

Suppose that
|V(X_{q-1})|\geq|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{8}{\log^{4}N}\right)
but
|V(X_{q})|<|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{8}{\log^{4}N}\right)
. Then there is a deterministic algorithm, that, given graphs
G_{0},X_{0}
, and the sequence
(A_{1},B_{1}),\ldots,(A_{q},B_{q})
of cuts in
G
, and graph
H_{q-1}
, computes a finalizing cut
(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
in
H_{q-1}
. The running time of the algorithm is
O(\hat{W}^{2})
.

Proof.

For convenience, in this proof we denote graph
G_{q-1}
by
G^{\prime}
and graph
H_{q-1}
by
H^{\prime}
.
We compute the finalizing cut
(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
in
H^{\prime}
as follows. First, for every vertex
v\in V(G^{\prime})
, if
v\in A_{q}
, then we add
v
to
\tilde{A}
, and otherwise we add
v
to
\tilde{B}
. Next, we consider every special edge
e=(x,y)
in
G^{\prime}
. If
x,y\in A_{q}
, then we add all inner vertices on path
P^{*}(e)
to
\tilde{A}
. If
x,y\in B_{q}
