, then we add all inner vertices on path
P^{*}(e)
to
\tilde{B}
.

Next, we consider two cases. The first case happens if
(A_{q},B_{q})
is a type-1 cut, so
V(G_{q})=A_{q}\setminus J_{q}
. For each remaining special edge (that must have first endpoint in
A_{q}
and last endpoint in
B_{q}
), we add all inner vertices of
P^{*}(e)
to
\tilde{B}
.

Note that, for every special edge
e
of
G^{\prime}
, the first and the last edges on path
P^{*}(e)
are special edges, so the resulting cut
(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
in
H^{\prime}
is weakly well-structured. Clearly:

|E_{H^{\prime}}(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})|=|E_{G^{\prime}}(A_{q},B_{q})|\leq\varphi\cdot\min\left\{w(A_{q}),w(B_{q})\right\}\leq\frac{4}{d^{\prime}}\cdot\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}\leq\frac{8|T|}{\log^{30}N},

since
\varphi=\frac{4}{d^{\prime}}
.
We have also used the fact that, since the current phase has not terminated yet,
w(A_{q}),w(B_{q})\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
, and that, since Event
{\cal{E}}_{1}
did not happen,
|T|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{2d^{\prime}}
.

Notice that, from our construction,
\tilde{A}=V(H_{q})\cup J_{q}
. All vertices of
\tilde{B}
are deleted from
H^{\prime}
when the cut
(A_{q},B_{q})
is processed, and, since the current phase did not terminate yet,
|\tilde{B}|\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
. Since
\tilde{A}=V(H_{q})\cup J_{q}
, we get that
|\tilde{A}\cap T|\leq|T_{q}|+|J_{q}|
. Recall that we have assumed that
|T_{q}|=|V(X_{q})|<|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{8}{\log^{4}N}\right)
. Additionally,
|J_{q}|\leq|E_{G^{\prime}}(A_{q},B_{q})|\leq\frac{8|T|}{\log^{30}N}
. Altogether, we conclude that
|\tilde{A}\cap T|\leq|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{7}{\log^{4}N}\right)
, and
|\tilde{A}|\geq|V(H^{\prime})|-\frac{\hat{W}}{32}
. Therefore, cut
(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
is a finalizing cut in graph
H^{\prime}
.

The case where
(A_{q},B_{q})
is a type-2 cut is treated similarly, except that now, for every special edge
e
of
G^{\prime}
whose endpoints belong to different sets in
\left\{A_{q},B_{q}\right\}
, we place all inner vertices of
P^{*}(e)
in
\tilde{A}
.
Using the same reasoning as before, all edges of
E_{H^{\prime}}(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
are special edges, and
|E_{H^{\prime}}(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})|\leq\frac{8|T|}{\log^{30}N}
holds.
Our construction ensures that
\tilde{B}=V(H_{q})\cup J^{\prime}_{q}
. Using the same argument as above, we conclude that
|\tilde{B}\cap T|\leq|T_{q}|+|J^{\prime}_{q}|\leq|T_{q}|+|E_{H^{\prime}}(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})\leq|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{7}{\log^{4}N}\right)
. Similarly,
|\tilde{B}|\geq|V(H^{\prime})|-\frac{\hat{W}}{32}
, and so cut
(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
is finalizing.

Whenever our algorithm computes a strongly well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
in the current graph
G
, we will first compute the corresponding sets
E_{i}
,
\Gamma_{i}
, and
\hat{\Gamma}_{i}
of edges, together with the set
T_{i}
of terminals, before applying any changes to graphs
G
and
H
. If
|T_{i}|\geq|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{8}{\log^{4}N}\right)
, then we proceed with updating the graphs
G,H
and
X
, as described above. But otherwise, we use the algorithm from

Claim

7.19
in order to compute a finalizing cut
(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
in the current graph
H
, and then employ the algorithm from
Claim

7.8
, that either correctly establishes that at least one of the events
{\cal{E}}_{2},{\cal{E}}_{3}
has happened (in which case we terminate the phase with a “FAIL”), or computes a strongly well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
in the current graph
G
, such that
w(A^{*}),w(B^{*})\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
. In the latter case, we do not process the cut
(A_{i},B_{i})
, but instead output the cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
, and update both graphs
G
and
H
accordingly, following which at least
\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
vertices must be deleted from
H
, and the current phase terminates.

Combining All Three Types of Updates

We now complete our description of the algorithm for maintaining the expander
X
and the embedding of
X\setminus F
into
H
throughout the phase. We will also maintain another graph
X^{\prime}
. Initially,
X^{\prime}=X
. Whenever a type-3 update
(A_{i},B_{i})
occurs, we update the graph
X^{\prime}
as described above, but we will ignore type-1 and type-2 updates when maintaining
X^{\prime}
. We will sometimes refer to
X^{\prime}
as the “shadow expander”, while we refer to
X
as the “expander”, even though, as the algorithm progresses, it is possible that both graphs stop being expanders.

In order to maintain the graph
X
, whenever a type-1 or a type-2 update arrives, we process it exactly as describe above. Assume now that the
i
th type-3 update
(A_{i},B_{i})
arrives.
In this case, we use graph
X^{\prime}
in order to compute the set
\hat{\Gamma}_{i}
of edges, and the partition
(T_{i},\overline{T}_{i})
of the vertices of
X^{\prime}
, as described above, and we update graph
X^{\prime}
accordingly. If, as the result,
|V(X^{\prime})|
falls below

|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{8}{\log^{4}N}\right)
, then we invoke the algorithm from
Claim

7.19
, that computes a finalizing cut
(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
in the current graph
H
. We then apply the algorithm from
Claim

7.8
to the resulting cut. If the algorithm
correctly estabishes that at least one of the events
{\cal{E}}_{2},{\cal{E}}_{3}
has happened, then we terminate our algorithm and return “FAIL”. Otherwise,
we obtain a strongly well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
in the current graph
G
, such that
w(A^{*}),w(B^{*})\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
.
We output this cut, and update the graphs
G
and
H
accordingly, following which at least
\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
vertices are deleted from
H
and the current phase terminates.

Assume now that
|V(X^{\prime})|\geq|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{8}{\log^{4}N}\right)
continues to hold after the current update. We then update graph
X
as follows: we delete the edges of
\hat{\Gamma}_{i}
, and the vertices of
\overline{T}_{i}
from it.
This completes the description of the algorithm for maintaining graph
X
. It is easy to verify that, at all times,
X\subseteq X^{\prime}
holds. Therefore, when the
i
th type-3 update is processed, immediately after the edges of
\hat{\Gamma}_{i}
are deleted from
X
, one of the cuts
(T_{i}\cap V(X),\overline{T}_{i}\cap V(X))
,
(\overline{T}_{i}\cap V(X),T_{i}\cap V(X))
must be a cut of sparsity
0
in the resulting graph
X
.

Let
E^{*}=F\cup\hat{E}_{1}\cup\hat{E}_{2}\cup\hat{E}_{3}
. Recall that
|F|\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{500}
, and we have shown that

|\hat{E}_{1}|\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{5000}
(in
Claim

7.16
),
|\hat{E}_{2}|\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{2^{20}}
, and
|\hat{E}_{3}|\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{2^{20}}
(in
Observation

7.18
).
Altogether, we get that
|E^{*}|\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{400}
holds throughout the phase.
We now prove that, throughout the phase,
|V(X)|\geq|T|/2
holds.

Claim 7.20
.

Throughout the phase,
|V(X)|\geq|T|/2
.

Proof.

Assume otherwise. Consider the first time
\tau
at which
|V(X)|<|T|/2
holds. For convenience, we denote by
X_{0}
the original graph
X
, which is an
\alpha_{0}
-expander, with
|T|
vertices and we denote by
\tilde{X}
the graph
X\setminus E^{*}
.

We construct the set
{\mathcal{Y}}
of subsets of vertices of
X_{0}
, as follows. Initially,
{\mathcal{Y}}=\emptyset
. Whenever a type-2 update arrives, with a cut
(A,B)
of the current graph
X
, if
|A|\leq|B|
, then we add
A
to
{\mathcal{Y}}
(recall that in this case, the vertices of
A
are deleted from
X
); otherwise, we add
B
to
{\mathcal{Y}}
. Clearly the cardinality of the set that we just added to
{\mathcal{Y}}
is bounded by
|T|/2
. Moreover, since the edges of
E_{X}(A,B)
belong to
E^{*}
, cut
(A,B)
has sparsity
0
in
\tilde{X}
.

Assume now that a type-3 update arrives, and let
T^{\prime}
denote the set of vertices deleted from
X
in the current iteration. We then add
T^{\prime}
to
{\mathcal{Y}}
. Since we did not terminate the current phase after the current iteration,
|V(X^{\prime})|\geq|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{8}{\log^{4}N}\right)
must currently hold, and so
|T^{\prime}|\leq\frac{8|T|}{\log^{4}N}\leq\frac{|T|}{2}
must hold. Moreover, as we have shown, one of the cuts
(T^{\prime},V(X)\setminus T^{\prime})
or
(V(X)\setminus T^{\prime},T^{\prime})
is a sparsity-
0
cut in graph
\tilde{X}
.

Let
{\mathcal{Y}}=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{q})
be the final collection of vertex subsets obtained after all updates that occured until time
\tau
have been processed, where the sets are indexed in the order in wich they were added to
{\mathcal{Y}}
. We also add the set
Y_{q+1}
that contains all vertices that lie in
X
at time
\tau
to
{\mathcal{Y}}
. From our assumption,
|X|\leq|T|/2
, and so for all
1\leq q^{\prime}\leq q+1
,
|Y_{q^{\prime}}|\leq|T|/2
.

For all
1\leq j\leq q
, let
Y^{\prime}_{j}=Y_{j+1}\cup\cdots\cup Y_{q}
. At the time when
Y_{j}
was added to
{\mathcal{Y}}
, it defined a cut of sparsity
0
in graph
\tilde{X}
. In other words, either
E_{\tilde{X}}(Y_{j},Y^{\prime}_{j})=\emptyset
, or

E_{\tilde{X}}(Y_{j},Y^{\prime}_{j})=\emptyset
. Let
\alpha
be such that
\max_{Y_{j}\in{\mathcal{Y}}}\left\{|Y_{j}|\right\}=\alpha\cdot|T|
. From our discussion so far,
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2}
. From
Observation

2.4
, there is a cut
(A,B)
in
\tilde{X}
with
|A|,|B|\geq|T|/4
, and
E_{\tilde{X}}(A,B)=\emptyset
.

Observe that
|E_{X_{0}}(A,B)|\leq|E^{*}|\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{400}
. Since
|A|,|B|\geq\frac{|T|}{4}
, we obtain a cut
(A,B)
in
X_{0}
, whose sparsity is below
\alpha_{0}
, contradicting the fact that
X_{0}
is an
\alpha_{0}
-expander.

7.5
Second Data Structure: Connecting to the Vertices of
X

At the beginning of the phase, we initialize the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem from
Theorem

6.7
on the current graph
G
, with
\beta=V(X)
, parameters
d^{\prime}
and
r
remaining unchanged, and parameter
N
replaced by
N^{\prime}=8N
.
We slightly modify the lengths of the special edges: for each such edge
e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}
, we define the new length
\ell^{\prime}(e)=\max\left\{\ell(e),2\ell^{0}(e)\right\}
. We define, for every vertex
v\in V(G)
, a weight
w^{\prime}(v)
to be the length
\ell^{\prime}(e)
of the unique special edge
e
incident to
v
, and we let
W^{\prime}=\sum_{v\in V(G)}w^{\prime}(v)
. Clearly,
W^{\prime}\leq 2\hat{W}
. As the lengths of special edges in the original graph
G
grow, we always set, for every edge
e\in E(G)
, the length
\ell^{\prime}(e)
that is used by the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem to
\ell^{\prime}(e)=\max\left\{\ell(e),2\ell^{0}(e)\right\}
, and we also define the weights
w^{\prime}(v)
of vertices
v\in V(G)
with respect to these edge lengths. Clearly, for all
e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}
,
\ell(e)\leq\ell^{\prime}(e)\leq 2\ell(e)
and for all
v\in V(G)
,
w(v)\leq w^{\prime}(v)\leq 2w(v)
always hold. We use
W^{\prime}
to denote the sum of the initial weights
w^{\prime}(v)
for
v\in V(G)
, so it does not change over the course of the algorithm.
Finally, the graphs
G
and the corresponding subdivided graph
H^{\prime}
that serve as input to the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem must be given in the adjacency-list representation. We can compute both adjacency-list representations in time
O(|V(H^{\prime})|^{2})\leq O((W^{\prime})^{2})\leq O((W^{0})^{2})
.
We prove that we obtain a valid input to the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem in the following simple claim, whose proof deferred to Section
D.5
of Appendix.

Claim 7.21
.

(G,\left\{\ell^{\prime}(e)\right\}_{e\in E(G)},H^{\prime},N^{\prime},d^{\prime},r,\beta)
is a valid input to the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem.

As the algorithm progresses, we will ensure that
\beta=V(X)
always holds. Since, from
Claim

7.20
,
|V(X)|\geq|T|/2
holds throghout the phase, this will ensure that at most half the vertices may be deleted from the initial set
\beta
over the course of the phase. From
Observation

7.2
,

\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}}\ell^{*}(e)\leq 2\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}}\ell^{0}(e)+2n
. Therefore,
the total increase in the lengths
\ell^{\prime}(e)
of edges
e\in E(G)
over the course of the entire algorithm is at most
2n\leq 8|V(G)|
, as required.

Consider the graph
H^{\prime}
, that is the subdivided graph corresponding to
G
and the lengths
\ell^{\prime}(e)
of its edges, and recall that
H
is the subdivided graph corresponding to
G
and the lengths
\ell(e)
of its edges. Recall that, for every edge
e\in E(G)
,
\ell(e)\leq\ell^{\prime}(e)\leq 2\ell(e)
. Since the lengths of all special edges are integral powers of
2
, for every special edge
e\in E(G)
, either
\ell^{\prime}(e)=\ell(e)
or
\ell^{\prime}(e)=2\ell(e)
holds. Therefore, graph
H^{\prime}
can be obtained from graph
H
as follows: for every edge
e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}
with
\ell^{\prime}(e)=2\ell(e)
, we subdivide every special edge
\hat{e}\in E(H)
that lies on the path
P^{*}(e)
representing
e
once, obtaining a path
\hat{P}(\hat{e})
with
2
special edges, representing edge
\hat{e}
in
H^{\prime}
. For all edges
\hat{e}\in E(H^{\prime})\cap E(H)
, we let
\hat{P}(\hat{e})=(\hat{e})
.

Recall that the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem may, at any time, produce Outcome
O3
, namely, a weakly well-structured cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in graph
H^{\prime}
, with
|A^{\prime}|\geq\frac{511|V(H^{\prime})|}{512}
,
|A^{\prime}\cap V(X)|<\frac{2|V(X)|}{\log^{3}N^{\prime}}
, and
|E_{H^{\prime}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|\leq\frac{|V(H^{\prime})|}{1024d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N^{\prime}}
.
Consider the cut
(A,B)
in
H
with
A=A^{\prime}\cap V(H)
and
B=B^{\prime}\cap V(H)
. We claim that
(A,B)
is a finalizing cut in
H
, in the following simple observation.

Observation 7.22
.

Cut
(A,B)
is a finalizing cut in
H
.

Proof.

Since, at the beginning of the phase,
|V(H^{\prime})|=W^{\prime}\leq 2\hat{W}
, and over the course of the phase, the total increase in the lengths
\ell^{\prime}(e)
of edges
e\in E(G)
, and hence in
|V(H^{\prime})|
, is at most
4n\leq 8\hat{W}
, we get that
|V(H^{\prime})|\leq 10\hat{W}
holds throughout the phase.

Consider any edge
e=(x,y)\in E_{H}(A,B)
. First, we claim that
e
is a special edge. Indeed, otherwise,
e\in E_{H^{\prime}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
must hold, contradicting the fact that
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
is a weakly well-structured cut. If
e\in E(H^{\prime})
, then
e\in E_{H^{\prime}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
must hold. Otherwise, at least one of the two special edges that lie on the path
\hat{P}(e)
representing
e
in
H^{\prime}
belong to
E_{H}(A,B)
. Therefore:

|E_{H}(A,B)|\leq|E_{H^{\prime}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|\leq\frac{|V(H^{\prime})|}{1024d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N^{\prime}}\leq\frac{10\hat{W}}{1024d^{\prime}\cdot\log^{6}N}\leq\frac{|T|}{8\log^{6}N},

since
|T|\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{2d^{\prime}}
, as Event
{\cal{E}}_{1}
did not happen.
Recall that

|A^{\prime}|\geq\frac{511|V(H^{\prime})|}{512}
, and so:

|B|\leq|B^{\prime}|\leq\frac{|V(H^{\prime})|}{512}\leq\frac{10\hat{W}}{512}\leq\frac{\hat{W}}{32}.

Therefore,
|A|\geq|V(H)|-\frac{\hat{W}}{32}
must hold. Lastly,

|A\cap V(X)|\leq|A^{\prime}\cap V(X)|<\frac{2|V(X)|}{\log^{3}N^{\prime}}<\frac{|T|}{2}
.
We conclude that
(A,B)
is a finalizing cut in
H
.

For brevity, whenever the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
on
G
produces Outcome
O3
, we say that it produces a finalizing cut
(A,B)
in
H
.

Recall that the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem uses a parameter
\rho=\frac{2^{12}\cdot W^{\prime}\cdot\log^{3}N^{\prime}}{|T|}\leq O\left(\frac{\hat{W}\log^{3}N}{|T|}\right)
, and that the total increase in the lengths of all edges is denoted by
\Lambda
; in our case,
\Lambda\leq O(n)
holds. Recall also that
\beta^{\prime}
denotes the set
V(X)
of vertices at the end of the algorithm. The running time of the algorithm from
Theorem

6.7
for this instance of the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem is then bounded by:

\begin{split}&O\left(c_{1}\cdot\hat{W}\cdot(\hat{W}+|T|\cdot\rho+\Lambda)\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+26}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left(c_{1}\cdot(W^{0})^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+29}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left(c_{1}\cdot(W^{0})^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+30}\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{32c_{2}r}\right)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+30}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq O\left(c_{1}\cdot(W^{0})^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+30}\right).\end{split}

(we have used Inequalities
17
and
18
.)

We denote the data structure for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem that we described above by
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{out}}
, and we denote the set
{\mathcal{Q}}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
of paths that it maintains by
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\operatorname{out}}
.
If a vertex
v
lies in layer
U_{\lambda}
, then we say that
v
is
disconnected
in
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{out}}
. For each vertex
v\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}U_{i}
, the unique path of
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
that originates at
v
is denoted by
K(v)
.

Next, we consider the graph
\overleftarrow{G}
, obtained from
G
by reversing the direction of its edges, and its corresponding subidivided graph
\overleftarrow{H}
. It is easy to verify that
\overleftarrow{G}
is a perfect well-structured graph. We define an instance of the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem on graph
\overleftarrow{G}
, with the edge lengths
\left\{\ell^{\prime}(e)\right\}_{e\in E(\overleftarrow{G})}
, set
\beta
of vertices, and parameters
N^{\prime},d^{\prime}
and
r
defined exactly as before, and then apply the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem from
Theorem

6.7
to this instance. We denote the data structure maintained by this algorithm by
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{in}}
. If a vertex
v
lies in layer
U_{\lambda}
, then we say that
v
is
disconnected
in
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{in}}
.
In order to avoid confusion, we denote the layers of this data structure by
U^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,U^{\prime}_{\lambda}
.
Recall that, for all
1\leq i<\lambda
, the algorithm maintains a collection
{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}=\left\{K(v)\mid v\in U^{\prime}_{i}\right\}
of paths in graph
\overleftarrow{H}
, where for all
v\in U_{i}
, path
K(v)
connects
v
to some vertex of
\bigcup_{i^{\prime}<i}U^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
. By reversing the direction of edges in this path, we obtain a path
K^{\prime}(v)
in
H
, that connects some vertex of
\bigcup_{i^{\prime}<i}U^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}
to
v
. We denote by
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}=\left\{K^{\prime}(v)\mid v\in U^{\prime}_{i}\right\}
, and by
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\operatorname{in}}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\lambda-1}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}_{i}
. To avoid confusion, for a vertex
v\not\in U_{\lambda}
, we use
K(v)
only to denote the path maintained by
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{out}}
, that is,
K(v)
is the unique path originating from
v
in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\operatorname{out}}
, and for
v\not\in U^{\prime}_{\lambda}
, we use
K^{\prime}(v)
to denote the unique path terminating at
v
in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\operatorname{in}}
.

If the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem in graph
\overleftarrow{G}
produces Outcome
O3
, then, using the same reasoning as in the proof of
Observation

7.22
, we obtain a finalizing cut
(A,B)
in graph
\overleftarrow{H}
. It is then immediate to verify that
(B,A)
is a finalizing cut in graph
H
. For brevity, whenever the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem in graph
\overleftarrow{G}
produces Outcome
O3
, we say that it produces a finalizing cut in
H
.

Assume now that the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem in graph
\overleftarrow{G}
produces Outcome
O2
; that is, it computes
a strongly well-structured
\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
\overleftarrow{G}
. It is then easy to see that
(B,A)
is a strongly well-structured
\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}
-sparse cut in
G
. Therefore, if
the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem in graph
\overleftarrow{G}
produces Outcome
O2
, we will say that it produces a strongly well-structured
\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}
-sparse cut in
G
.

From our discussion, the total running time of the algorithms that maintain data structures
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{in}}
and
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{out}}
, including the time that is needed in order to construct the corresponding two instances of the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem, and including the time that is needed in order to compute a finalizing cut in
H
, if any of the two algorithms produce Outcome
O3
, is bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot(W^{0})^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+30}\right).

7.6
Additional Data Structures

Recall that our algorithm maintains a graph
X
(that is initially an expander), a subset
F
of edges of
X
(the fake edges), and an embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}
of
X\setminus F
into
H
. Additionally, it maintains a
shadow expander

X^{\prime}
, which is initially identical to
X
, but, as the time progresses, we may ignore some of the updates to graph
X
and only apply a subset of such updates to
X^{\prime}
. We also maintain a subset
F^{\prime}
of edges of
X^{\prime}
, which is initially identical to
F
, and an embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
of
X^{\prime}\setminus F^{\prime}
into
H
, which is initially identical to
{\mathcal{Q}}
.
Throughout the algorithm,
X^{\prime}\subseteq X
and
F^{\prime}\subseteq F
hold. Additionally, for every edge
e\in E(X^{\prime})\setminus F
, its embedding path
Q^{\prime}(e)\in{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
is identical to the embedding path
Q(e)\in{\mathcal{Q}}
.
As the time progresses, vertices and edges may be deleted from
X
and from
X^{\prime}
. Whenever an edge
e\in F
is deleted from
X
, we delete it from
F
as well, and similarly, whenever an edge
e^{\prime}\in F^{\prime}
is deleted from
X^{\prime}
, we delete it from
F^{\prime}
as well. For every edge
e\in E(X)
, its embedding path
Q(e)\in{\mathcal{Q}}
remains unchanged throughout the algorithm, except that, whenever some special edge on path
Q(e)
undergoes splitting, we modify the path accordingly. Embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
of
X^{\prime}\setminus F^{\prime}
into
H
is maintained similarly.

For every edge
e\in E(X)\setminus F
, we maintain the length
\hat{\ell}(e)
of the embedding path
Q(e)
. We initialize the value
\hat{\ell}(e)
at the beginning of the phase, once the graph
X
and the embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}
of
X\setminus F
into
H
are computed. After that, whenever a special edge of
Q(e)
undergoes splitting, we increase
\hat{\ell}(e)
by (additive) 2. The time that is required in order to maintain the values
\hat{\ell}(e)
for edges
e\in E(X)\setminus F
is clearly subsumed by the time required to compute and to maintain the collection
{\mathcal{Q}}
of paths. In our description of the remainder of the algorithm below, we will ignore the task of maintaining these values.

For every edge
e\in E(H)
, we also maintain a list
S(e)
of all edges
\hat{e}\in E(X)\setminus F
, such that
e\in Q(\hat{e})
. The task of maintaining these lists is quite straightforward; we will ignore it when we describe the remainder of the algorithm below, but we will show how to extend the algorithm in order to maintain these lists efficiently later.

Throughout, we use a distance parameter
\hat{d}=\frac{2^{30}\log\hat{W}}{\alpha_{0}}
.
Throughout the phase, we maintain an arbitrary vertex
v\in V(X)
,
and two shortest-paths trees
\hat{\tau}^{\prime}
and
\hat{\tau}^{\prime\prime}
rooted at
v
in graph
X\setminus F
, where the edges in
\hat{\tau}^{\prime}
are directed away from
v
, and the edges in
\hat{\tau}^{\prime\prime}
are directed towards
v
. In other words, for every vertex
a\in V(X)
, the unique
s
-
a
path in
\hat{\tau}^{\prime}
is a shortest
s
-
a
path in
X\setminus F
, and the unique
a
-
s
path in
\hat{\tau}^{\prime\prime}
is a shortest
a
-
s
path in
X\setminus F
. Note that both trees can be computed in time
O(|T|)
, since
|V(X)|\leq|T|
, and maximum vertex degree in
X
is
O(1)
. Whenever an edge or a vertex are deleted from
X
, we recompute both trees from scratch, selecting a new root vertex
v\in V(X)
if needed. Since
|E(X)|\leq O(|T|)
, the trees need to be recomputed at most
O(|T|)
times, and so the total time spent over the course of the phase on computing the two trees is bounded by
O(|T|^{2})\leq O((W^{0})^{2})
.

7.7
The Remainder of the Algorithm

In this subsection we describe the remainder of the algorithm for a single phase of the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem.
At the beginning of the phase, we construct the subdivided graph
H
corresponding to the initial graph
G
, and then construct the set
T
of terminals, as described in Section
7.2
. We check whether either of the bad events
{\cal{E}}_{1}
or
{\cal{E}}_{4}
happened, and if so, we terminate the phase and return “FAIL”. We assume from now on that neither of these events happened. The running time of the algorithm so far is
O(|E(H)|)\leq O((W^{0})^{2})
.

We execute the algorithm from
Lemma

7.11
in order to construct the expander
X
. If the algorithm terminates with a “FAIL”, which may only happen with probability at most
\frac{1}{N^{7}}
, then we terminate the algorithm for the current phase and return “FAIL”. If the algorithm returns
a finalizing cut in
H
, then we apply the algorithm from
Claim

7.8
to this cut. If the algorithm from
Claim

7.8
estabishes that at least one of the events
{\cal{E}}_{2},{\cal{E}}_{3}
happened, we terminate the phase with a “FAIL”. Otherwise, we obtain a strongly well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
in the current graph
G
, such that
w(A^{*}),w(B^{*})\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
. In this case, we output the cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
. Once this cut is processed, at least
\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
vertices are deleted from
H
, and the current phase terminates. Finally, if the algorithm from

Lemma

7.11
returns a strongly well-structured
1/d^{\prime}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
with
w(A),w(B)\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{(\log N)^{30}}
, then as before we output this cut, and, once it is processed, at least
\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
vertices are deleted from
H
, and the current phase terminates. Therefore, we assume from now on that the algorithm from
Lemma

7.11
computed a graph
X
with
V(X)=T
, whose maximum vertex degree is at most
24
, such that
X
is an
\frac{\alpha_{0}}{81}
-expander.
Recall that we are also given a set
F\subseteq E(X)
of at most
\frac{\alpha_{0}\cdot|T|}{500}
fake edges, and an embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}
of
X\setminus F
into
H
. Every path in
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
has length at most
d^{\prime\prime}
, and the congestion caused by the paths in
{\mathcal{P}}^{*}
is at most
\eta^{\prime\prime}
.
The running time of the algorithm from
Lemma

7.11
is bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot\hat{W}^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+3}\right)\leq O\left(c_{1}\cdot(W^{0})^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+3}\right),

and the running time of the algorithm from
Claim

7.8
is
O(\hat{W}^{2})\leq O((W^{0})^{2})
.

We also initialize data structures
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{in}}
and
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{out}}
. Recall that the total time for maintaining both data structures is bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot(W^{0})^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+30}\right).

Recall that the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem, that is used to maintain data structures
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{in}}
and
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{out}}
may return “FAIL” with probability at most
\frac{1}{N^{7}}
. If any of these two instantiations of the algorithm terminate with a “FAIL”, we terminate the algorithm for the current phase, and return “FAIL”.

The running time of the algorithm so far, including the initialization of all data structures, and maintaining data structures
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{in}}
and
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{out}}
throughout the phase, is bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot(W^{0})^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+30}\right).

Our algorithm employs a cleanup procedure, that we describe below, once at the beginning of the phase, and once after each query is processed. The purpose of the procedure is to ensure that the following conditions hold:

C1.

The length of every path in
{\mathcal{Q}}
is bounded by
8d^{\prime\prime}
;

C2.

The depth of each of the trees
\hat{\tau}^{\prime},\hat{\tau}^{\prime\prime}
is at most
\hat{d}
; and

C3.

U_{\lambda}=\emptyset
and
U^{\prime}_{\lambda}=\emptyset
.

(here,
U_{\lambda}
and
U^{\prime}_{\lambda}
are the sets of disconnected vertices from data structures
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{out}}
and
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{in}}
, respectively).
Before we describe the cleanup procedure that ensures all these conditions, we describe our algorithm for responding to queries.

7.7.1
Responding to Queries

We assume that, at the time when a query
(x,y)
arrives, Conditions
C1
–
C3
hold. We start by computing a path
P_{1}
in graph
H
, connecting
x
to some vertex
x^{\prime}\in V(X)
, as follows. Since
U_{\lambda}=\emptyset
currently holds, there is some layer
0\leq i<\lambda
in data structure
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{out}}
, with
x\in U_{i}
. If
i=0
, then we let the path
P_{1}
contain a single vertex – vertex
x
itself. Otherwise, we use the unique path
K(x)\in{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
to connect
x
to some vertex
x_{1}\in U^{<i}
. Let
U_{i_{1}}
be the layer in which
x_{1}
lies, so
i_{1}<i
. We say that
K(x)
is the first
subpath
of
P_{1}
.
If
x_{1}\in V(X)
, then we have completed the construction of the path
P_{1}
. Otherwise, using a similar process, we connect
x_{1}
to some vertex
x_{2}\in U^{<i_{2}}
, via the path
K(x_{1})
, that becomes the second subpath of
P_{1}
and so on. After at most
\lambda-1
iterations, we are then guaranteed to reach a vertex
x^{\prime}\in V(X)
, thereby obtaining a path
P_{1}
connecting
x
to
x^{\prime}
. Since the length of every path in
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\operatorname{out}}
is at most
16d^{\prime}\log^{10}N^{\prime}
(from the relaxed condition
L1
), while
d^{\prime}\leq\frac{2d}{(\log N)^{28}}
and
\lambda\leq O(\log N)
, we get that the length of
P_{1}
is bounded by:

16d^{\prime}\lambda\log^{10}N^{\prime}\leq O\left(\frac{d}{(\log N)^{17}}\right).

Using a similar algorithm, together with data structure
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{in}}
, we compute a path
P_{2}
in graph
H
, of length at most
O\left(\frac{d}{(\log N)^{17}}\right)
, connecting some vertex
y^{\prime}\in V(X)
to
y
. The time required to compute the paths
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
is bounded by
O(|E(P_{1})|+|E(P_{2})|)
.

Next, we use the tree
\hat{\tau}^{\prime\prime}
to compute a path
P^{\prime}_{3}
in
X\setminus F
, connecting
x^{\prime}
to the root
v
of the tree
\hat{\tau}^{\prime\prime}
, and we use the tree
\hat{\tau}^{\prime}
to compute a path
P^{\prime}_{4}
in
X\setminus F
connecting
v
to
y^{\prime}
. By concatenating both paths, we obtain a path
P^{\prime}
in
X\setminus F
that connects
x^{\prime}
to
y^{\prime}
, whose length is at most
2\hat{d}
.

Denote
P^{\prime}=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{z})
. We compute a path
P_{3}
connecting
x^{\prime}
to
y^{\prime}
in graph
H
by concatenating the corresponding embedding paths
Q(e_{1}),\ldots,Q(e_{z})
in
{\mathcal{Q}}
. Recall that, from Condition
C1
, the length of each such path is bounded by
8d^{\prime\prime}
.
Since
\hat{d}=\frac{2^{30}\log\hat{W}}{\alpha_{0}}\leq O(\log N)
and

d^{\prime\prime}=\Theta\left(d^{\prime}\cdot(\log N)^{16}\right)\leq O\left(\frac{d}{(\log N)^{12}}\right)
, as
d^{\prime}\leq\frac{2d}{(\log N)^{28}}
, we get that the length of the resulting path
P_{3}
is bounded by:

2\hat{d}\cdot 8d^{\prime\prime}\leq O\left(\frac{d}{(\log N)^{11}}\right).

The time required to compute the path
P_{3}
is bounded by
|E(P_{3})|
.
By concatenating the paths
P_{1},P_{3}
and
P_{2}
, we obtain a path
P^{\prime}
connecting
x
to
y
in
H
, whose length is bounded by:

O\left(\frac{d}{(\log N)^{17}}\right)+O\left(\frac{d}{(\log N)^{11}}\right)<d,

since
N
is sufficiently large.
The total time spent on computing path
P^{\prime}
is
O(|E(P^{\prime})|)\leq O(d)
.
We then turn
P^{\prime}
into a simple
x
-
y
path of length at most
d
in graph
H
, in time
O(d)
. Finally, we convert
P^{\prime}
into a simple
x
-
y
path in graph
G
of length at most
d
in a straightforward way, in time
O(d)
, and return the path
P
.

The time to respond to a single query is bounded by
O(d)\leq O(n)\leq O(W^{0})
, and the time to respond to all queries in a single phase is bounded by
O(W^{0}\cdot\Delta^{\prime})\leq O\left(\frac{W^{0}\cdot\Delta}{(\log N)^{30}}\right)
(we have used the fact that
\Delta^{\prime}=\frac{\Delta}{\log^{30}N}
, and Inequality
15
).

7.7.2
Post-Query Updates

Recall that, after responding to a query, the algorithm receives a subset
E^{\prime}\subseteq E(P)
of special edges on the path
P
that was returned in response to the query, whose lengths need to be doubled. We can then compute a subset
E^{\prime\prime}\subseteq E^{\prime}
of edges, whose lengths
\ell^{\prime}(e)
that are used by the algorithms that maintain data structures
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{in}}
and
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{out}}
need to be doubled, and supply the set
E^{\prime\prime}
of edges to these two algorithms, in time
O(|E^{\prime}|)
.
Next, we consider the edges in
E^{\prime}
one by one. For each edge
e\in E^{\prime}
, we consider every special edge
e^{\prime}
on the path
P^{*}(e)
representing
e
in
H
. We split each such edge
e^{\prime}=(x,y)
, by replacing it with the path
(x,x^{\prime},y^{\prime},y)
in
H
. If any of the endpoints
x,y
of
e^{\prime}
are terminal vertices that lie in
T
, this operation does not affect them.
Notice that this operation inserts two new special edges into graph
H
: edge
e^{\prime}_{1}=(x,x^{\prime})
and edge
e^{\prime}_{2}=(y,y^{\prime})
. We initialize the lists
S(e^{\prime}_{1})=S(e^{\prime}_{2})=\emptyset
.

Next, we consider every edge
\hat{e}\in S(e^{\prime})
, one by one. For each such edge
\hat{e}
, we update the embedding path
Q(\hat{e})
by splitting the edge
e^{\prime}
, and also update the length
\hat{\ell}(e)
of the path by increasing it by (additive)
2
. We also insert
\hat{e}
into
S(e_{1}^{\prime})
and
S(e_{2}^{\prime})
. If the length
\hat{\ell}(e)
of
e
became greater than
8d^{\prime\prime}
, then we flag this edge, to be taken care of by the Cleanup procedure.

In order to bound the time required for post-query updates, let
E^{*}
be the set of all special edges that ever belonged to graph
H
over the course of the phase. Since, from
Observation

7.2
,
\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}}\ell^{*}(e)\leq 2\sum_{e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}}\ell^{0}(e)+4n\leq O(W^{0})
, and since, for each edge
e\in E(G)
, the number of its child edges that ever belonged to
H
over the course of the phase is asymptotically bounded
\ell^{*}(e)
, we get that
|E^{*}|\leq O(W^{0})
.

Recall that the congestion caused by the embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}
of
X\setminus F
into
H
is bounded by
O((\log N)^{13})
. Therefore, for every edge in
E^{*}
we spend
O((\log N)^{13})
time to process it when it is first inserted into
H
. The total time required for all post-query updates is then bounded by:

O(W^{0}\cdot(\log N)^{13}).

The total running time of the algorithm so far, including responding to queries, but excluding the Cleanup Procedure, is bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot(W^{0})^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+30}\right)+O\left(\frac{W^{0}\cdot\Delta}{(\log N)^{30}}\right).

7.7.3
The Cleanup Procedure

The cleanup procedure is executed once at the beginning of the phase, and then after every query, once all edge-splittings that follow the query are processed. The purpose of the procedure is to ensure that Properties
C1
–
C3
are satisfied. As part of the procedure, we will sometimes compute
\varphi
-sparse strongly well-structured cuts in graph
G
. Whenever this happens, we will call to another procedure, called
\mathsf{ProcessCut}^{\prime}
, that will process the resulting cut. We view Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}^{\prime}
as part of the cleanup procedure, and we describe it in the following subsection. We note that, if the cleanup procedure ever identifies a
\varphi
-sparse strongly well-structured cut
(A.B)
in
G
, our algorithm does not immediately output the cut, but rather calls to Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}^{\prime}
. The procedure may choose to either output the cut
(A,B)
, after which the vertices of the side of the cut with smaller weight and the endpoints of the edges in
E_{G}(A,B)
are deleted from
G
, and graphs
H
,
X
and
X^{\prime}
are updated accordingly; or it may instead compute another finalizing cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in
H
, and then call to
Claim

7.8
to process it. In the latter case the algorithm does not output the initial cut
(A,B)
, but after cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
is processed the phase will terminate.
We now turn to describe the cleanup procedure.

The procedure consists of iterations, which are executed as long as at least one of the conditions
C1
–
C3
is violated.

Assume first that Condition
C3
is violated. In this case we say that the current iteration is of type 1.
Assume w.l.o.g. that
U_{\lambda}\neq\emptyset
; the other case is symmetric. We execute a single iteration of the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem that maintains data structure
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{out}}
. We now consider three cases. If the algorithm produced Outcome

O1
, that is, it obtained a valid
Layered Connectivity Data Structure
with
U_{\lambda}=\emptyset
, then the current iteration terminates.
If the algorithm produced a finalizing cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in the current graph
H
, then the cut processed like decribed in Section
7.5
, after which the current phase terminates. Recall that the running time for processing the cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
is included in our analysis of the running time of the algorithm that maintains data structure
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{out}}
. Lastly, if the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem produces a a strongly well-structured
\frac{1}{d^{\prime}}
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in
G
, then we call to Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}^{\prime}
. The procedure will either produce a finalizing cut in
H
, that will subsequently terminate the current phase; or it will output the cut
(A,B)
, following which, if
w(A)\leq w(B)
, then the vertices of
A
, and the endpoints of the edges in
E_{G}(A,B)
are deleted from
G
, while otherwise, the vertices of
B
, and the endpoints of the edges in
E_{G}(A,B)
are deleted from
G
, and the current iteration terminates.
The total running time of all type-1 iterations of the Cleanup procedure executed over the course of the phase, excluding the running time of Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}^{\prime}
, is already included in the running times of the algorithms that maintain data structures
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{in}}
and
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{out}}
.

Assume now that Condition
C1
is violated. In this case we say that the current iteration is of type 2. Let
\hat{e}\in E(X)
be the edge with
\hat{\ell}(\hat{e})>8d^{\prime\prime}
; recall that all such edges were flagged by the algorithm that performed post-query updates. We then simply delete the edge
\hat{e}
from graph
X
.
We also recompute the shortest path threes
\hat{\tau}^{\prime}
and
\hat{\tau}^{\prime\prime}
in
X\setminus F
that are rooted at the same vertex
v
, in time
O(|E(X)|)\leq O(|T|)\leq O(W^{0})
.
The total running time of type-2 iterations over the course of the whole phase, excluding the time needed for recomputing the trees
\hat{\tau}^{\prime}
and
\hat{\tau}^{\prime\prime}
, is asymptotically bounded by the running time of the algorithm that performs post-query updates after every query.
Since at least one edge is deleted from
X
in each type-2 iteration, and
|E(X)|\leq O(|T|)\leq O(W^{0})
, the total time spent on recomputing the trees
\hat{\tau}^{\prime}
and
\hat{\tau}^{\prime\prime}
in all type-2 iterations of the phase is bounded by
O((W^{0})^{2})
.

Lastly, assume that Condition
C2
is violated. In this case we say that the current iteration is of type 3. Then we can compute, in time
O(1)
, two vertices
a pair
a,b\in V(X)
with
\mbox{\sf dist}_{X\setminus F}(a,b)>\hat{d}
.
Recall that
\hat{d}=\frac{2^{30}\log\hat{W}}{\alpha_{0}}
, and the maximum vertex degree in
X
is bounded by
D=24
. We can now use the algorithm from
Observation

2.3
to compute a cut
(A,B)
in
X\setminus F
of sparsity at most
\varphi^{\prime}=\frac{32D\log\hat{W}}{\hat{d}}\leq\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2^{20}}
. Let
Z\in\left\{A,B\right\}
be the set of vertices of smaller cardinality, breaking ties arbitrarily.
For every vertex
x\in Z
, we delete
x
from
V(X)
.
We supply the list of all vertices that are deleted from
X
to the algorithms for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem that are used to maintain data structures
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{in}}
and
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{out}}
.
Recall that the running time of the algorithm from
Observation

2.3
is bounded by
O(D\cdot|V(X)|)\leq O(\hat{W})\leq O(W^{0})
.
We also select an arbitrary vertex
v\in V(X)
, and compute shortest-path trees
\hat{\tau}^{\prime}
and
\hat{\tau}^{\prime\prime}
in
X\setminus F
rooted at
v
. Note that both trees can be computed in time
O(|E(X)|)\leq O(\hat{W})\leq O(W^{0})
.
Every time a type-3 iteration occurs, at least one vertex is deleted from
X
. Therefore, the total time that the algorithm spends on type-3 iterations over the course of the entire phase is bounded by
O((W^{0})^{2})
.

This completes the algorithm for the Cleanup procedure. From our discussion, the total running time of the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem so far, excluding Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}^{\prime}
, is bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot(W^{0})^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+30}\right)+O\left(\frac{W^{0}\cdot\Delta}{(\log N)^{30}}\right).

Next, we describe the algorithm for Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}^{\prime}
.

7.7.4
Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}^{\prime}

The input to Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}^{\prime}
is a strongly well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in graph
G
. Recall that, if our algorithm chooses to output the cut
(A,B)
, then the vertices of the side of the cut that has smaller weight will be deleted from
G
, together with the endpoints of the edges in
E_{G}(A,B)
. After that, graph
H
will be updated with a corresponding sequence of vertex- and edge-deletions, and expander graph
X
, together with the shadow expander
X^{\prime}
will also be updated, as described in Section
7.4.2
. If we apply all these updates directly, we may discover that too many vertices have been deleted from graph
X^{\prime}
. In such a case, we could use the algorithm from
Claim

7.19
, in order to compute a finalizing cut
(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
in graph
H
. However,
(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
is a valid finalizing cut in the original graph
H
, to which the updates following the cut
(A,B)
have not been applied yet. Therefore, our algorithm will, instead, simulate the updates to graphs
G
,
H
and
X^{\prime}
in order to find out whether the number of vertices that are deleted from
X^{\prime}
following these updates is too high. If so, it will apply the algorithm from
Claim

7.19
to compute a finalizing cut
(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
in graph
H
, followed by the algorithm from
Claim

7.8
, that either correctly establishes that at least one of the events
{\cal{E}}_{2},{\cal{E}}_{3}
has happened (in which the current phase terminates with “FAIL”), or computes a strongly well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
in the current graph
G
, such that
w(A^{*}),w(B^{*})\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
, which our algorithm then outputs; in this case, our algorithm does not output the cut
(A,B)
. The vertices of the side of the cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
that has smaller weight are then deleted from
G
, together with the endpoints of the edges of
E_{G}(A^{*},B^{*})
, and the current phase terminates. If our algorithm establishes that
|X^{\prime}|
remains sufficiently high even after we update the graphs
G
,
H
and
X^{\prime}
with the cut
(A,B)
, then it will simply output the cut
(A,B)
, and update the graphs
G,H,X^{\prime}
and
X
accordingly.

We now provide a formal description of Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}^{\prime}
. We assume that our algorithm maintains the graph
G_{0}
, which is the graph
G
at the beginning of the current phase, and the sequence of strongly well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cuts
(A_{1},B_{1}),\ldots,(A_{q},B_{q})
that it has output so far over the course of the phase. We also assume that it maintains the invariant that
|V(X^{\prime})|\geq|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{8}{\log^{4}N}\right)
. This invariant clearly holds at the beginning of the phase, when
V(X^{\prime})=T
.

We now assume that Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}^{\prime}
is called with a strongly well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cut
(A,B)
in the current graph
G
. We assume that so far the algorithm returned
q\geq 0
well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cuts, that we denote by
(A_{1},B_{1}),\ldots,(A_{q},B_{q})
, in the order in which the algorithm returned them.
We also denote the cut
(A,B)
by
(A_{q+1},B_{q+1})
, and let
E_{q+1}=E_{G}(A_{q+1},B_{q+1})
.
Let

J_{q+1}\subseteq A_{q+1},J^{\prime}_{q+1}\subseteq B_{q+1}
the vertices that serve as endpoints of the edges in
E_{q+1}
. As before, if
w(A_{q+1})\geq w(B_{q+1})
, then we denote
Z_{q+1}=A_{q+1}\setminus J_{q+1}
, and otherwise we denote
Z_{q+1}=B_{q+1}\setminus J^{\prime}_{q+1}
. We also let
\overline{Z}_{q+1}=V(G)\setminus Z_{q+1}
contain the remaining vertices of the current graph
G
. As before, if
Z_{q+1}=A_{q+1}\setminus J_{q+1}
, then we say that cut
(A_{q+1},B_{q+1})
is of
type 1
, and otherwise we say that it is of
type 2
.
We also denote by
G_{q}
and
H_{q}
the graphs
G
and
H
, respectively, at the beginning of Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}^{\prime}
, and we denote
z_{q+1}=\min\left\{w(A),w(B)\right\}
.

For every edge
e=(x,y)\in E_{q+1}
, we compute the set
\Gamma(e)
of edges in
H_{q}
. Recall that the set
\Gamma(e)
consists of all vertices on the path
P^{*}(e)
that represents
e
in graph
H_{q}
, as well as all regular edges insident to vertices
x
and
y
in
H_{q}
. It is easy to verify that
|\Gamma(e)|\leq|V(H_{q})|\leq O(\hat{W})
. Next, we compute the set
\Gamma_{q+1}=\bigcup_{e\in E_{q+1}}\Gamma(e)
of edges in
\hat{H}
.
The sets
\Gamma(e)
of edges for all
e\in E_{q+1}
, together with the set
\Gamma_{q+1}
of edges can be computed in time
O(|E_{q+1}|\cdot\hat{W})\leq O(\varphi z_{q+1}\cdot\hat{W})
.

We also compute the partition of the vertices of
H_{q}
into sets
Z^{*}_{q+1}
and
\overline{Z}^{*}_{q+1}
, as follows. Set
\overline{Z}^{*}_{q+1}
contains all vertices that lie in set
\overline{Z}_{q+1}
. Additionally, for every special edge
e
of
G
with both endpoints in
\overline{Z}_{q+1}
, set
\overline{Z}^{*}_{q+1}
contains all vertices on path
P^{*}(e)
. Similarly, set
Z^{*}_{q+1}
contains all vertices that lie in set
Z_{q+1}
, and additionally, for every special edge
e
of
G
with both endpoints in
Z_{q+1}
, set
Z^{*}_{q+1}
contains all vertices on path
P^{*}(e)
. It is easy to verify that
(Z^{*}_{q+1},\overline{Z}^{*}_{q+1})
is a partition of
V(H_{q+1})
, since cut
(A_{q+1},B_{q+1})
is strongly well-structured in
G
. The time required to compute the partition
(Z^{*}_{q+1},\overline{Z}^{*}_{q+1})
of
V(H_{q+1})
is bounded by
O(|V(H_{q+1})|)\leq O(\hat{W})
.

Next, we compute the set
\hat{\Gamma}_{q+1}=\bigcup_{e\in E_{q+1}}\hat{\Gamma}(e)
of edges in graph
X^{\prime}\setminus F
, where for an edge
e\in E_{q+1}
,
\hat{\Gamma}(e)=\bigcup_{e^{\prime}\in\Gamma(e)}S(e^{\prime})
. In other words, an edge
\hat{e}\in E(X^{\prime})\setminus F
belongs to the set
\hat{\Gamma}(e)
if and only if its corresponding embedding path
Q(\hat{e})
contains at least one edge of
\Gamma(e)
, or, equivalently, edge
\hat{e}
belongs to set
S(e^{\prime})
of edges for some edge
e^{\prime}\in\Gamma(e)
. From
Observation

7.17
, for every edge
e\in E_{q+1}
,
|\hat{\Gamma}(e)|\leq 2\eta^{\prime\prime}
.
Therefore,
|\hat{\Gamma}_{q+1}|\leq 2|E_{q+1}|\cdot\eta^{\prime\prime}\leq 2\varphi\cdot z_{q+1}\cdot\eta^{\prime\prime}\leq O(\varphi\cdot z_{q+1}\cdot(\log N)^{13})
.
Moreover, given an edge
e\in E_{q+1}
, set
\hat{\Gamma}(e)
can be computed in time
O(\hat{W}\cdot\eta^{\prime\prime})
. Indeed, recall that, from the proof of
Observation

7.17
, an edge
\hat{e}\in E(X)\setminus F
may lie in
\hat{\Gamma}(e)
in one of the following two cases: (i) an endpoint of
\hat{e}
lies in
T\cap P^{*}(e)
– all such edges
\hat{e}
can be identified in time
O(|E(P^{*}(e))|)\leq O(\hat{W})
; or (ii) if
e^{\prime}
is the first edge on
P^{*}(e)
and
\hat{e}\in S(e^{\prime})
– since
|S(e^{\prime})|\leq\eta^{\prime\prime}
, all such edges can be identified in time
O(\eta^{\prime\prime})
. Therefore, the time required to compute the set
\hat{\Gamma}_{q+1}
of vertices is bounded by
O(|E_{q+1}|\cdot\hat{W}\cdot\eta^{\prime\prime})\leq O(\varphi\cdot z_{q+1}\cdot\hat{W}\cdot\eta^{\prime\prime})\leq O(\varphi\cdot z_{q+1}\cdot\hat{W}\cdot(\log N)^{13})
.

We also compute a partition
(T_{q+1},\overline{T}_{q+1})
of the vertices of
X^{\prime}
, where
T_{q+1}=V(X^{\prime})\cap Z^{*}_{q+1}
and
\overline{T}_{q+1}=V(X^{\prime})\cap\overline{Z}^{*}_{q+1}
.
Notice that the running time of the procedure so far is bounded by:

O\left(\varphi z_{q+1}\cdot(\log N)^{13}\cdot\hat{W}\right)+O(\hat{W})\leq O\left(\frac{\hat{W}\cdot z_{q+1}\cdot(\log N)^{13}}{d^{\prime}}\right)+\leq O(\hat{W}\cdot z_{q+1}),

since
d^{\prime}\geq\frac{d}{(\log N)^{28}}
, and
d\geq(\log N)^{64}
from the definition of the problem.

Next, we consider two cases. The first case happens if
|T_{q+1}|\geq|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{8}{\log^{4}N}\right)
.
In this case we output the cut
(A_{q+1},B_{q+1})
, and then update our data structures accordingly: we delete the vertices of
\overline{Z}_{q+1}
from
G
, and we delete the vertices of
\overline{Z}^{*}_{q+1}
from
H
. We also delete the edges of
\hat{\Gamma}_{q+1}
and the vertices of
\overline{T}_{q+1}
from
X
and from
X^{\prime}
.
Finally, we update the algorithms that maintain data structures
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{in}}
and
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{out}}
with the vertices that were just deleted from
X
.
In this case, the running time of the procedure remains at most
O(\hat{W}\cdot z_{q+1})
.
Notice that, following this procedure, at least
z_{q+1}
vertices are deleted from
H
. We
charge
the running time of the procedure to the vertices that are deleted from
H
, where the charge to each vertex is
O(\hat{W})
.

We now consider the case where
|T_{q+1}|<|T|\cdot\left(1-\frac{8}{\log^{4}N}\right)
. In this case, we apply the algorithm from
Claim

7.19
to graph
G_{0}
and the sequence
(A_{1},B_{1}),\ldots,(A_{q+1},B_{q+1})
of cuts in
G
. Recall that the running time of the algorithm is
O(\hat{W}^{2})
, and it returns a finalizing cut
(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
in the current graph
H
.
Next, we apply the algorithm from
Claim

7.8

to graph
G_{0}
, the sequence
(A_{1},B_{1}),\ldots,(A_{q},B_{q})
of cuts in
G
, and the finalizing cut
(\tilde{A},\tilde{B})
in the current graph
H
. Recall that the running time of the algorithm from
Claim

7.8
is
O(\hat{W}^{2})
. If the algorithm from
Claim

7.8
estabishes that at least one of the events
{\cal{E}}_{2},{\cal{E}}_{3}
has happened, then we terminate the current phase and return “FAIL”. Otherwise, we obtain a strongly well-structured
\varphi
-sparse cut
(A^{*},B^{*})
in the current graph
G
, such that
w(A^{*}),w(B^{*})\geq\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}
.
Let
Z^{*}\in\left\{A^{*},B^{*}\right\}
be the side of smaller weight; if
w(A^{*})=w(B^{*})
, then we let
Z^{*}=B^{*}
. We delete the vertices of
Z^{*}
from
G
, together with the endpoints of the edges in
E_{G}(A^{*},B^{*})
. Every vertex
v
that was deleted from
G
is also deleted from
H
, and for every special edge
e
that was deleted from
G
, all vertices and edges on path
P^{*}(e)
are deleted from
H
. As the result, at least
\frac{\hat{W}}{\log^{30}N}

vertices are deleted from
H
and the current phase terminates.

Overall, the last execution of Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}^{\prime}
in a phase takes time
O(\hat{W}^{2})
. If
q^{*}
is the total number of times that the procedure is executed during the phase, then all but the last execution of the procedure take time at most:

\sum_{q^{\prime}=1}^{q^{*}-1}O(z_{q^{\prime}}\cdot\hat{W}))\leq O((W^{0})^{2}).

(Here, we used the fact that, from
Observation

7.2
, the total number of vertices that ever belonged to
H
is bounded by
O(W^{0})
.)
The total running time of all executions of Procedure
\mathsf{ProcessCut}^{\prime}
over the course of a phase is then bounded by
O(\hat{W}^{2})
, and the total running time of a single phase of the algorithm for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem remains at most:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot(W^{0})^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+30}\right)+O\left(\frac{W^{0}\cdot\Delta}{(\log N)^{30}}\right).

7.7.5
Maintaining the Additional Data Structures and the Final Accounting

Additional Data Structures.

It remains to show an algorithm that maintains, for every edge
e\in E(H)
, the set
S(e)\subseteq E(X)\setminus F
of edges. It is straightforward to augment our algorithm to maintain this data structure without increasing its asymptotic running time.

Once the algorithm from
Lemma

7.11
computes
the expander
X
and the embedding
{\mathcal{Q}}
of
X\setminus F
into
H
, we initialize the sets
S(e)
for all edges
e\in E(H)
in a straightforward way: we start by setting
S(e)=\emptyset
for all
e\in E(H)
. We then consider every edge
\hat{e}\in E(X)\setminus F
one by one. When edge
\hat{e}
is considered, we process every edge
e\in E(Q(\hat{e}))
, and add
\hat{e}
to the corresponding set
S(e)
of edges. Whenever some edge
\hat{e}\in E(X)\setminus F
is deleted from
X
, we similarly process every edge
e\in E(Q(\hat{e}))
, and we delete
\hat{e}
from set
S(e)
. Lastly, when an edge
e\in E(H)
undergoes splitting, and new special edges
e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime\prime}
are inserted into graph
H
as the result of this split, for every edge
\hat{e}\in S(e)
, we update the path
Q(\hat{e})
with the corresponding edge split, and we insert
\hat{e}
into the lists
S(e^{\prime})
and
S(e^{\prime\prime})
. The updates to the lists
\left\{S(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H)}
due to edge splitting were already taken into account in the running time analysis of the algorithm that processes post-query updates. The additional time that is required in order to maintain the lists
\left\{S(e)\right\}_{e\in E(H)}
is asymptotically bounded by the time required to initialize these lists at the beginning of the phase, which, in turn, is asymptotically bounded by the running time of the algorithm from
Lemma

7.11
.

Failure probability and running time analysis.

Recall that our algorithm may return “FAIL” in the following cases: (i) Event
{\cal{E}}
has happened – the probability for that is bounded by
1/16
; or (ii) the algorithm from
Lemma

7.11
for constructing the expander
X
returns “FAIL”: the probability for that is bounded by
\frac{1}{N^{7}}
; or (iii) the algorithm for the
\mathsf{ConnectToCenters}
problem, that is used in order to maintain data structures
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{in}}
and
\mbox{\sf DS}^{\operatorname{out}}
returns “FAIL”: this can happen with probability at most
\frac{1}{N^{7}}
for each of the two instantiations of the algorithm. Therefore, overall, the probability that the algorithm for a single phase terminates with a “FAIL” is bounded by
\frac{1}{2}
. From our analysis in
Section

7.1
, the number of phases in the algorithm is bounded by
O\left((\log N)^{33}\right)
. Since the running time of every phase is bounded by:

O\left(c_{1}\cdot(W^{0})^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+30}\right)+O\left(\frac{W^{0}\cdot\Delta}{(\log N)^{30}}\right),

using the fact that
c_{2}
is a large enough constant and
N
is sufficiently large, we get that the total running time of the algorithm is bounded by:

\begin{split}&O\left(c_{1}\cdot(W^{0})^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}(r-1)+8c_{2}+63}\right)+O\left(W^{0}\cdot\Delta\cdot\log^{3}N\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq c_{1}\cdot(W^{0})^{2}\cdot 2^{c_{2}\sqrt{\log N}}\cdot(\log N)^{16c_{2}r}+c_{1}\cdot W^{0}\cdot\Delta\cdot\log^{4}N.\end{split}

This completes the proof of
Theorem

4.12
.

Appendix A
Proofs Omitted from
Section

4

A.1
Proof of
Observation

4.4

Consider first any edge
e=(u,v)\in E(G)
with
u\in A
and
v\in B
. We claim that
e
must be a special edge. Indeed, assume otherwise, that is,
e
is a regular edge. Then
u\in A^{\prime}
and
v\in B^{\prime}
must hold. Moreover, edge
e=(u,v)
lies in graph
G^{+}
, so
e\in E_{G^{+}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
. Since cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in
G^{+}
is weakly well-structured, this is impossible, a contradiction. We conclude that every edge
e\in E_{G}(A,B)
must be a special edge, and so cut
(A,B)
is a weakly well-structured cut in
G
.

Next, we prove that
w(A)\geq\frac{|A^{\prime}|}{8}
; the proof that
w(B)\geq\frac{|B^{\prime}|}{8}
is symmetric.
We partition the vertices of
A^{\prime}
into three subsets. The first subset is
A^{\prime}_{1}=A
. If vertex
v\in A^{\prime}
does not lie in
A
, then there is some special edge
e=(x,y)\in E(G)
, such that
v
is an inner vertex on path
P(e)
. If any of the endpoints
x,y
of
e
lie in
A
, then we add vertex
v
to the second set
A^{\prime}_{2}
, and otherwise it is added to the third set,
A^{\prime}_{3}
.

Clearly,
w(A)\geq|A|\geq|A_{1}^{\prime}|
, and it is easy to verify that
w(A)\geq\frac{|A_{2}^{\prime}|}{2}
holds as well, since the weight of every vertex
x
in
G
is equal to the length of the unique special edge
e
that is incident to
x
, which, in turn, is equal to half the number of vertices on the corresponding path
P(e)
.
Therefore,
w(A)\geq\frac{|A^{\prime}_{1}|+|A^{\prime}_{2}|}{4}
must hold. Next, we show that
|A^{\prime}_{3}|\leq\frac{|A^{\prime}|}{2}
.

Indeed, let
\tilde{E}
be the collection of all special edges
e=(x,y)
in
G
with
x,y\in B
, such that at least one inner vertex on the corresponding path
P(e)
belongs to
A^{\prime}
. Clearly, for each such edge
e
, at least one edge
e^{\prime}\in P(e)
must belong to
E_{G^{+}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
. Therefore,
|\tilde{E}|\leq E_{G^{+}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\leq\varphi\cdot|A^{\prime}|
. Since we have assumed that graph
G
does not contain edges of length greater than
\frac{1}{4\varphi}
, we get that, for every edge
e\in\tilde{E}
, the number of vertices lying on the corresponding path
P(e)
is at most
\frac{1}{2\varphi}
. Therefore,
|A^{\prime}_{3}|\leq\frac{1}{2\varphi}\cdot|\tilde{E}|\leq\frac{|A^{\prime}|}{2}
. We conclude that
|A_{3}^{\prime}|\leq\frac{|A^{\prime}|}{2}
, and so
w(A)\geq\frac{|A^{\prime}_{1}|+|A^{\prime}_{2}|}{4}\geq\frac{|A^{\prime}|}{8}
. Using a similar reasoning, we get that
w(B)\geq\frac{|B^{\prime}|}{8}
.

Recall that
\Phi_{G^{+}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})=\frac{|E_{G^{+}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|}{\min\left\{w(A^{\prime}),w(B^{\prime})\right\}}=\frac{|E_{G^{+}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|}{\min\left\{|A^{\prime}|,|B^{\prime}|\right\}}
, since the weight of every vertex in
G^{+}
is
1
. Consider now any edge
e=(u,v)\in E_{G}(A,B)
. Since
(A,B)
is a well-structured cut in
G
,
e
must be a special edge, and moreover,
u\in A^{\prime}
,
v\in B^{\prime}
must hold. Therefore, at least one edge on the path
P(e)
must lie in
E_{G^{+}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
. We conclude that
|E_{G}(A,B)|\leq|E_{G^{+}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|
.
Altogether, we get that:

\Phi_{G}(A,B)=\frac{|E_{G}(A,B)|}{\min\left\{w(A),w(B)\right\}}\leq\frac{8|E_{G^{+}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|}{\min\left\{|A^{\prime}|,|B^{\prime}|\right\}}=8\Phi_{G^{+}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime}).

A.2
Proof of
Observation

4.5

We start with
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})=(A,B)
, and then consider every vertex
x\in A
one by one. If there is a special edge
e=(y,x)\in E_{G}(B^{\prime},A^{\prime})
, then if
w(A^{\prime})\leq w(B^{\prime})
currently holds, we move
y
from
B^{\prime}
to
A^{\prime}
, and otherwise we move
x
from
A^{\prime}
to
B^{\prime}
. Notice that, since graph
G
is well-structured, if a regular edge
(a,b)
lies in
G
, then
a\in L
and
b\in R
must hold. Additionally, since
(y,x)
is a special edge,
y\in R
and
x\in L
must hold. Therefore, the only edge entering
x
is the special edge
(y,x)
, and all edges leaving
x
are regular edges. By moving
x
from
A^{\prime}
to
B^{\prime}
we therefore cannot introduce any new edges to
E_{G}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, or any new special edges to
E_{G}(B^{\prime},A^{\prime})
. Similarly, the only edge leaving
y
is the special edge
(y,x)
, and all edges entering
y
are regular edges. Therefore, by moving
y
from
B^{\prime}
to
A^{\prime}
, we cannot introduce any new edges to
E_{G}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, or any new special edges to
E_{G}(B^{\prime},A^{\prime})
.

Once every vertex of
A
is processed, we obtain the final cut
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, that is strongly well-structured. Note that
|E_{G}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|\leq|E_{G}(A,B)|\leq\frac{\varphi}{2}\cdot\min\left\{w(A),w(B)\right\}
. Let
z=\min\left\{w(A),w(B)\right\}
, and let recall that
w(V(G))=2\sum_{e\in E}\ell(e)=2W
. Notice that
z\leq W
must hold.

If no vertices were ever removed from
A^{\prime}
, then
w(A^{\prime})=w(A)
holds. Otherwise, consider the last time when a vertex was removed from
A^{\prime}
. We denote the removed vertex by
v
. Then, just before
v
was moved from
A^{\prime}
to
B^{\prime}
,
w(A^{\prime})\geq w(B^{\prime})
held. Therefore, at the end of the algorithm,
w(A^{\prime})\geq W-w(v)\geq\frac{W}{2}
, since the weight
w(v)
of vertex
v
is the length of the unique special edge incident to
v
, which must be bounded by
\frac{W}{2}
. We conclude that, at the end of the algorithm,
w(A^{\prime})\geq\min\left\{w(A),\frac{W}{2}\right\}\geq\frac{w(A)}{4}
holds. Using similar reasoning,
w(B^{\prime})\geq\min\left\{w(B),\frac{W}{2}\right\}\geq\frac{w(B)}{4}
.
Moreover,
\min\left\{w(A^{\prime}),w(B^{\prime})\right\}\geq\min\left\{w(A),w(B),\frac{W}{2}\right\}\geq\frac{1}{2}\min\left\{w(A),w(B)\right\}
.
Altogether,
|E_{G}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})|\leq\frac{\varphi}{2}\cdot\min\left\{w(A),w(B)\right\}\leq\varphi\cdot\min\left\{w(A^{\prime}),w(B^{\prime})\right\}
.

The running time of the above algorithm is
O(\operatorname{Vol}(A))
. If
\operatorname{Vol}(B)<\operatorname{Vol}(A)
, then we can execute the same procedure by examining the vertices of
B
instead of
A
, in time
O(\operatorname{Vol}(B))
.

Appendix B
Proofs Omitted from
Section

5

B.1
Proof of
Observation

5.5

From Invariant
I1
, graph
H[X^{0}]
is a perfect well-structured graph. It is immediate to verify that the lengths
\ell(e)
of edges
e\in E(H)
define a proper assignment of lengths to the edges of
H[X^{0}]
. Recall that we have denoted by
W^{0}(X)
the total weight of all vertices in
H[X^{0}]
when
X
is added to
{\mathcal{X}}
, and, from
Observation

5.3
, we are guaranteed that
W^{0}(X)\leq N^{\prime}
, where
N^{\prime}=\max\left\{N,8n\cdot\log^{5}n\right\}
is the parameter that we have defined.
From the definition of the
s
-
t
-
SP
problem, we also get that
1\leq\eta\leq\Delta
and
1\leq\Delta\leq n-|B^{0}|\leq N\leq N^{\prime}
.
From our assumption that
N
is greater than a sufficiently large constant, with
N>2^{2^{c_{2}}}
, the same holds for
N^{\prime}
.

Since
X
is a non-leaf cluster,
|X^{0}|>\frac{\Delta\cdot(\log N)^{128}}{\eta}
must hold. Recall that
d_{X}=\min\left\{\frac{|X^{0}|\cdot\eta}{\Delta},d\right\}
. From Inequality
5
,
d\geq 2^{2\sqrt{\log N}}
. Therefore, if
d_{X}=d
, then
d_{X}\geq 2^{2\sqrt{\log N}}\geq 2^{\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}\geq(\log N^{\prime})^{64}
(from Inequality
4
, and since
N^{\prime}\geq N
is large enough). Otherwise:

d_{X}=\frac{|X^{0}|\cdot\eta}{\Delta}\geq(\log N)^{128}\geq(\log N^{\prime})^{64},

since
\log N^{\prime}\leq 2\log N\leq\log^{2}N

from Inequality
3
. In either case,
d_{X}\geq(\log N^{\prime})^{64}
holds.

Lastly, since
d_{X}=\min\left\{\frac{|X^{0}|\cdot\eta}{\Delta},d\right\}
, we get that
d_{X}\leq\frac{|X^{0}|\cdot\eta}{\Delta}
, and so
\frac{\Delta\cdot d_{X}}{\eta}\leq|X^{0}|
holds. This establishes that
(H[X^{0}],\Delta,\eta,d_{X},N^{\prime})
is a valid input instance for the
\mathsf{MaintainCluster}
problem.

In order to show that this instance is
r
-restricted, we need to show that
r\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}\right\rceil
and that
d_{X}\leq 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}
holds.
The former follows immediately since, from the definition of the
(r+1)
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem,
r+1\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}\right\rceil
. The latter also immediately follows since
d_{X}\leq d
, and since
d\leq 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N}}\leq 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}
from Inequality
5
.

B.2
Proof of
Observation

5.6

From Invariant
I1
, graph
H[X]
is a perfect well-structured graph, so every vertex of
X
is incident to exactly one special edge in
H[X]
. Clearly, both
H[Y^{\prime}]
and
H[Z^{\prime}]
are well-structured graphs. It remains to show that each of them is a strongly well-structured graph.

We start by proving that
H[Y^{\prime}]
is a strongly well-structured graph. It is enough to show that, for every vertex
v\in Y^{\prime}
, if
e
is the unique special edge that is incident to
v
in
G
, then the other endpoint
u
of
e
also lies in
Y^{\prime}
. Indeed, assume otherwise. Since
H[X]
is a perfect well-structured graph,
u\in X
must hold. It is impossible that
u\in J
, since in this case
u\in R
, and the unique special edge incident to
u
lies in the cut
E_{H}(Y,Z)
. Therefore,
u\in Z
must hold. Since
v\not\in J
, edge
e
must lie in
E_{H}(Z,Y)
. But from the definition of a strongly well-structured cut,
e
must be a regular edge, a contradiction.

In order to prove that
H[Z^{\prime}]
is a strongly well-structured graph, it is enough to show that, for every vertex
v\in Z^{\prime}
, if
e
is the unique special edge that is incident to
v
in
G
, then the other endpoint
u
of
e
also lies in
Z^{\prime}
. Assume otherwise. As before, since
H[X]
is a perfect well-structured graph,
u\in X
must hold. It is impossible that
u\in J^{\prime}
, since in this case
u\in L
, and the unique special edge incident to
u
lies in the cut
E_{H}(Y,Z)
. Therefore,
u\in Y
must hold. Since
v\not\in J^{\prime}
, edge
e
must lie in
E_{H}(Z,Y)
. But from the definition of a strongly well-structured cut,
e
must be a regular edge, a contradiction.

B.3
Proof of
Observation

5.7

Let
e=(u,v)
be a new right-to-left edge that was added to the
\mathcal{ATO}
as the result of the current transformation. Assume first that
v\in J^{\prime}
. Since
J^{\prime}\subseteq L
, edge
e
must be the unique special edge entering
v
, and, from the definition of the set
J^{\prime}
of vertices,
e\in E_{H[X]}(Y,Z)=E_{H}(Y,Z)
must hold. Therefore,
u\in J
must hold. Assume next that
v\in Z^{\prime}
. Since edge
e
is a right-to-left edge, it must be the case that
u\in Y^{\prime}\cup J=Y
. However, there can be no edges connecting
Y
to
Z^{\prime}
in
G[X]
, from the definition of the sets
J^{\prime}
and
Z^{\prime}
of vertices. Assume now that
v\in Y^{\prime}
. Since edge
e
is a right-to-left edge,
u\in J
must hold. However, as observed already,
J\subseteq R
must hold, and for every vertex
u\in J
, the only edge that leaves
u
is the unique special edge incident to
u
, whose other endpoint is in
J^{\prime}
. Therefore, it is impossible that
u\in J
and
v\in Y^{\prime}
holds for an edge
(u,v)
. Lastly, we need to consider the case where
v\in J
. Since
(u,v)
is a right-to-left edge, it must be the case that
u\in J
. But since
J\subseteq R
, it is impossible that an edge of
G
has both endpoints in
J
.

B.4
Proof of
Observation

5.8

As observed already, graph
H[U^{0}]
is a perfect well-structured graph. It is immediate to verify that the initial lengths
\ell(e)
of edges
e\in E(H)
define a proper assignment of lengths to the edges of
H[U^{0}]
. Moreover, initially, the length of every special edge of
G
is set to
1
.
Recall that
|U^{0}|>2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
, and that
|U^{0}\setminus\beta|\leq n-|B^{0}|
must hold. Therefore,
|\beta|\geq\frac{99|U^{0}|}{100}
holds, as required.
From our assumption that
N
is greater than a sufficiently large constant, so is
N^{\prime}
.

Recall that we have denoted by
W^{0}(U)
the total initial weight of all vertices in
H[U^{0}]
. It is immediate to verify that the initial weight of every vertex in
H[U^{0}]
is
1
, and so
W^{0}(U)\leq n\leq N\leq N^{\prime}
.
From the definition of the
s
-
t
-
SP
problem, we also get that
1\leq\eta\leq\Delta
and
1\leq\Delta\leq n-|B^{0}|\leq\frac{|\beta|}{(\log N)^{128}}
(the latter inequality follows since we have established that
|\beta|\geq\frac{99|U^{0}|}{100}
and
|U^{0}|\geq 2(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot(\log N)^{128}
).

Recall that
d_{U}=\max\left\{(\log N^{\prime})^{64},\left(|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+\Delta\right)\frac{\eta}{4\Delta\cdot 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}}\right\}
, so
d_{U}\geq(\log N^{\prime})^{64}
holds.
Lastly, we need to establish that
\frac{\Delta\cdot d_{U}}{\eta}\leq|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+\Delta\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{64}
.
Indeed, since

d_{U}=\max\left\{(\log N^{\prime})^{64},\left(|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+\Delta\right)\frac{\eta}{2\Delta\cdot 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}}\right\}
, we get that:

\frac{\Delta\cdot d_{U}}{\eta}=\max\left\{\frac{\Delta\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{64}}{\eta},\frac{|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|}{4\cdot 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}}+\frac{\Delta}{4\cdot 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}}\right\}\leq|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+\Delta\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{64}.

In order to show that this instance is
r
-restricted, we need to show that
r\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}\right\rceil
and that
d_{U}\leq 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}}
holds.
The former follows immediately since, from the definition of the
(r+1)
-restricted
s
-
t
-
SP
problem,
r+1\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N}\right\rceil\leq\left\lceil\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}\right\rceil
. The latter also immediately follows since:

\begin{split}d_{U}&=\max\left\{(\log N^{\prime})^{64},\left(|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+\Delta\right)\frac{\eta}{4\Delta\cdot 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}}\right\}\\
&\leq\max\left\{(\log N^{\prime})^{64},\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\eta}{2\Delta\cdot 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}}\right\}\\
&\leq\max\left\{(\log N^{\prime})^{64},2d\right\}\\
&\leq 2^{r\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}},\end{split}

since
d=\frac{(n-|B^{0}|)\cdot\eta}{4\Delta\cdot 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}}
and

d\leq 2^{r\cdot\sqrt{\log N}-1}\leq 2^{r\sqrt{\log N^{\prime}}-1}
from Inequality
5
.

B.5
Proof of
Observation

5.10

Recall that, at the beginning of the algorithm, for every edge
e\in E^{\mathsf{spec}}\setminus E^{*}
,
\ell(e)=1
, and for every vertex
v\in\tilde{U}
,
w(v)=1
holds.
For every edge
e\in(E^{\mathsf{spec}}\setminus E^{*})\cap H[U^{0}]
, if both endpoints of
e
lie in
U
at the end of the algorithm, then we set
\tilde{\ell}(e)
to be the length of
e
at the end of the algorithm, and otherwise we let
\tilde{\ell}(e)
be the length of
e
just before its endpoints are deleted from
U
.
Let
E^{\prime}\subseteq(E^{\mathsf{spec}}\setminus E^{*})\cap H[U^{0}]
be the set of all edges
e\in(E^{\mathsf{spec}}\setminus E^{*})\cap H[U^{0}]
with
\tilde{\ell}(e)>1
.
Then
\sum_{v\in\tilde{U}}\tilde{w}(v)\leq|\tilde{U}|+2\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}}\tilde{\ell}(e)
.

Let
q
be the total number of queries to algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(U)
, so that
q\leq\Delta
, and let
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}=\left\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{q}\right\}
be the paths returned by Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(U)
in response to the queries, where the paths are indexed in the order in which they were returned. For all
1\leq i\leq q
, let
C_{i}
denote the length of path
P_{i}
when it was added to
{\mathcal{Q}}
. Clearly,
C_{i}\leq d_{U}
, and
\sum_{i=1}^{q}C_{i}\leq q\cdot d_{U}\leq\Delta\cdot d_{U}
.

Consider now some edge
e\in E^{\prime}
, and recall that
\ell(e)
was doubled at least once over the course of Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}(U)
. Let
\tau
be the last time when the length of
e
was doubled. Then, prior to time
\tau
, there were at least
\eta
iterations
i
, during which the path
P_{i}
that was added to
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\prime}
contained
e
, and the length of
e
during the corresponding iteration was
\frac{\tilde{\ell}(e)}{2}
. In other words, edge
e
contributes at least
\frac{\tilde{\ell}(e)\eta}{2}
to
\sum_{i=1}^{q}C_{i}
. Altogether, we get that
\sum_{i=1}^{q}C_{i}\geq\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}}\frac{\tilde{\ell}(e)\eta}{2}
. Therefore:

\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}}\tilde{\ell}(e)\leq\frac{2\sum_{i=1}^{q}C_{i}}{\eta}\leq\frac{2\Delta\cdot d_{U}}{\eta}\leq|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+2\Delta\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{64},

since

d_{U}=\max\left\{(\log N^{\prime})^{64},\left(|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+\Delta\right)\frac{\eta}{4\Delta\cdot 2^{\sqrt{\log N}}}\right\}
.

Altogether, we get that:

\sum_{v\in\tilde{U}}\tilde{w}(v)\leq|\tilde{U}|+2\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}}\tilde{\ell}(e)\leq 4|U^{0}\setminus B^{0}|+8\Delta\cdot(\log N^{\prime})^{64},

since the set
\tilde{U}
contains all vertices of
U^{0}\setminus B^{0}
, and additionally all vertices of
B^{0}\cap U^{0}
that were deleted from
B^{0}
over the course of the algorithm, whose number is bounded by
\Delta
.

B.6
Proof of
Claim

5.11

Consider any cluster
X\in\tilde{X}^{\prime}_{2}
. Observe first that at most one child-cluster of
X
may be its close descendant. Indeed, assume otherwise, and let
X_{1},X_{2}
be two child-clusters of
X
that are its close descendants, and assume that
X_{1}
was added to
{\mathcal{X}}
before
X_{2}
. Then
|X^{0}_{1}|,|X^{0}_{2}|>|X^{0}|/2
must hold, and so
X^{0}_{1}\cap X^{0}_{2}\neq\emptyset
. But this is impossible since, after cluster
X_{1}
was added to
{\mathcal{X}}
, all vertices of
X_{1}^{0}
were deleted from
X
. Recall that, if
X_{1}
is a child-cluster of
X
, and it is a close descendant of
X
, then, when
X_{1}
was created,
\sum_{v\in X_{1}}w(v)\leq\sum_{v\in X}w(v)/2
held, following which the vertices of
X_{1}^{0}
were deleted from
X
.
We start with the following claim that bounds the number of close descendants of a cluster
X
, whose initial weights are not much lower than that of
X
.

Claim B.1
.

Consider a sequence
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{z})
of distinct clusters in
\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\prime}
, such that for all
1\leq i<z
,
X_{i+1}
is a child cluster of
X_{i}
, clusters
X_{2},\ldots,X_{z}
are close descendants of
X_{1}
, and for all
2\leq i\leq z
,
W^{0}(X_{i})\geq W^{0}(X_{1})/2
. Then
z\leq 16
must hold.

Proof.

For all
1\leq i\leq z
, let
\tau_{i}
be the time when cluster
X_{i}
was added to
{\mathcal{X}}
, and for all
1<i\leq r
, let
X^{\prime}_{i-1}
be the set of all vertices that lied in
X_{i-1}
just before time
\tau_{i}
, excluding the vertices of
X_{i}
.
For convenience, we also denote by
X^{\prime}_{z}=X_{z}
. For all
1\leq i<z
, we denote by
W_{i}
be the total weight of all vertices in set
X^{\prime}_{i}
at time
\tau_{i+1}
, and we denote by
W_{z}=W^{0}(X_{z})
. Note that the sets
X^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,X^{\prime}_{z}
of vertices are mutually disjoint.

Consider now an index
1<i\leq z
. Since
|X_{i}^{0}|\geq|X_{1}^{0}|/2
must hold, we get that
|X_{i}^{0}|\geq|X_{i-1}^{0}|/2
, and so, from our discussion, at time
\tau_{i}
, the total weight of the vertices of
X_{i}
was less than or equal to the total weight of the vertices of
X^{\prime}_{i-1}
. In other words, for all
1<i\leq z
,
W_{i-1}\geq W^{0}(X_{i})\geq W^{0}(X_{1})/2
.
From our definitions,
W_{z}=W^{0}(X_{z})\geq W^{0}(X_{1})/2
as well. Therefore:

\sum_{i=1}^{z}W_{i}\geq\frac{W^{0}(X_{1})\cdot z}{2}.

In the following observation, we show that
\sum_{i=1}^{z}W_{i}\leq 8W^{0}(X_{1})
must hold, and so
z\leq 16
, as required. The following observation then completes the proof of
Claim

B.1
.

Observation B.2
.

\sum_{i=1}^{z}W_{i}\leq 8W^{0}(X_{1})
.

Proof.

Recall that, from our definition, the sets
\left\{X^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,X^{\prime}_{z}\right\}
of vertices are mutually disjoint. For convenience, denote
\tau_{z+1}=\tau_{z}
, and let
Y=\bigcup_{i=1}^{z}X^{\prime}_{i}
, so
Y\subseteq X^{0}_{1}
. Recall that, for all
1\leq i<z
,
W_{i}
denotes the weight of the vertices in
X_{i}^{\prime}
at time
\tau_{i+1}
. In order to prove the observation, we will observe how the weights of the vertices of
Y
evolve during the time interval
(\tau_{1},\tau_{z}]
, except that, for all
1\leq i\leq z
, we will “freeze” the weights of the vertices in
X^{\prime}_{i}
at time
\tau_{i+1}
, so they are not allowed to grow anymore.

Specifically, for every vertex
v\in Y
, we denote by
\tilde{w}(v)
the weight of
v
at time
\tau_{1}
. For all
1\leq i\leq z
, for every vertex
v\in X_{i}
, we denote by
\tilde{w}^{\prime}(v)
the weight of
v
at time
\tau_{i+1}
. Clearly,

\sum_{v\in Y}\tilde{w}(v)\leq W^{0}(X_{1})
, and
\sum_{i=1}^{z}W_{i}=\sum_{v\in Y}\tilde{w}^{\prime}(v)
.
It now remains to bound
\sum_{v\in Y}(\tilde{w}^{\prime}(v)-\tilde{w}(v))
.
Let
\tilde{E}^{\prime}
be the set of all special edges that lie in
E(H[X^{0}_{1}])
.
For every special edge
e\in\tilde{E}^{\prime}
, we denote by
\tilde{\ell}(e)
the length of
e
at time
\tau_{1}
, and recall that
W^{0}(X_{1})=2\sum_{e\in\tilde{E}^{\prime}}\tilde{\ell}(e)
.

Recall that for each cluster
X\in\tilde{\mathcal{X}}_{2}^{\prime}
, we have defined a parameter
d_{X}=\min\left\{\frac{|X^{0}|\cdot\eta}{\Delta},d\right\}
. Since, for all
1<i\leq z
, cluster
X_{i}
is a descendant of
X_{1}
, we get that
|X^{0}_{i}|\leq|X^{0}|
, and so
d_{X_{i}}\leq d_{X_{1}}
.

Consider now the following process. Initially, every vertex
v\in Y
is assigned weight
\tilde{w}(v)
, and every special edge
e\in\tilde{E}^{\prime}
is assigned length
\tilde{\ell}(e)
. Then we obeserve the iterations of the algorithm that occured during the time interval
[\tau_{1},\tau_{z})
in the order in which they occur. Let
j
be any such iteration. Let
1\leq i\leq z
be the integer for which iteration
j
occurs during the time interval
[\tau_{i},\tau_{i+1})
. We then consider the cluster
X_{i}
, and denote by
P_{j}
the path that Algorithm
{\mathcal{A}}(X_{i})
returned in response to a
short-path
query during iteration
j
. Denote by
C_{j}
the length of
P_{j}
at the time when it was returned, and recall that
C_{j}\leq d_{X_{i}}\leq d_{X_{1}}
must hold. We also denote by
\tilde{E}_{j}\subseteq E(P_{j})
the set of special edges of
H[X_{i}]
, whose lengths were doubled as the result of this iteration. For every vertex
v\in X_{i}\cap Y
, that serves as an endpoint of an edge of
\tilde{E}_{j}
, we double the weight
\tilde{w}(v)
. For every edge
e\in\tilde{E}_{j}
, we also double its length
\tilde{\ell}(e)
.

It is easy to verify that, at the end of this process, the final weight of every vertex
v\in Y
is precisely
\tilde{w}^{\prime}(v)
.
For every special edge
e\in\tilde{E}^{\prime}
, let
\tilde{\ell}^{\prime}(e)
be the length of
e
at the end of this process. Then
\sum_{v\in Y}\tilde{w}^{\prime}(v)\leq 2\sum_{e\in\tilde{E}^{\prime}}\tilde{\ell}^{\prime}(e)
.
Let
r
be the total number of iterations of the algorithm that occur during the time interval
[\tau_{1},\tau_{z})
. Consider now any special edge
e\in\tilde{E}^{\prime}
. From the definition of our algorithm, if
\tau_{1}\leq\tau<\tau^{\prime}\leq\tau_{z}
are any two
times at which the length of
e
was doubled, then edge
e
participated in at least
\eta
of the paths of
\left\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{r}\right\}
that were returned by the above process during the time interval
[\tau,\tau^{\prime})
.

Recall that we have established that
\sum_{e\in\tilde{E}^{\prime}}\tilde{\ell}(e)=\frac{W^{0}(X_{1})}{2}
. Let
\tilde{E}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\tilde{E}^{\prime}
be the set of all edges
e
for which
\tilde{\ell}^{\prime}(e)>2\tilde{\ell}(e)
. Consider now any such edge
e\in\tilde{E}^{\prime\prime}
. Then the length of
e
must have been doubled at least twice during the process that we described above. Let
\tau<\tau^{\prime}
be the last two times during the process when the length of
e
was doubled. Then, at least
\eta
of the paths
of
\left\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{r}\right\}
